It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other marginalia <note>original: "marginalia"; handwritten notes or markings in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

### Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying <note>the use of software or "bots" to automatically search or download data</note>.

We also ask that you:

+ **Make non-commercial use of the files** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ **Refrain from automated querying** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR; the technology used to convert images of text into editable digital text</note>, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ **Maintain attribution** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ **Keep it legal** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page introduces the English translation of Frédéric Bastiat’s economic writings, establishing the relationship between his two most famous works.</meta>

## TRANSLATOR’S PREFACE.

---

Bastiat’s two great works on Political Economy—the *Economic Sophisms* <note>original: "Sophismes Économiques"</note> and the *Economic Harmonies* <note>original: "Harmonies Économiques"</note>—may be regarded as counterparts of each other. He himself viewed them in this way: “the one,” he says, “pulls down, the other builds up.” His objective in the *Sophisms* was to refute the fallacies of the Protectionist school <note>A school of thought advocating for government taxes on imported goods to protect domestic industries.</note>, which was then dominant in France. By doing so, he hoped to clear the way for the establishment of what he maintained to be the true system of economic science. He intended to base this system on a new and unique theory of value, which he later fully developed in the *Harmonies*. Whatever difference of opinion may exist among economists regarding the soundness of this theory, all must admire the irresistible logic of the *Sophisms* and the “bursts of wit and humor” which, as Mr. Cobden <note>Richard Cobden (1804–1865), a British manufacturer and statesman famous for his successful campaign against the Corn Laws.</note> has said, make that work as “amusing as a novel.”

<summary>The translator introduces Frédéric Bastiat’s two major works, explaining that the 'Sophisms' serves to debunk Protectionist myths while the 'Harmonies' seeks to build a new economic theory.</summary>
<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, Political Economy, Economic Sophisms, Economic Harmonies, Protectionism, Theory of Value, Richard Cobden</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page concludes the translator's preface, explaining that this volume of "Economic Sophisms" serves as a companion to Bastiat's "Economic Harmonies" to provide a complete view of his economic system.</meta>

# TRANSLATOR’S PREFACE.

Because Bastiat’s system has both a *destructive* and a *constructive* purpose—a *negative* as well as a *positive* design—it is perhaps only right, given his great reputation as an economist, to allow English readers to judge his system as a whole. Therefore, this translation of the *Economic Sophisms* <note>original: "Sophismes"</note> is intended to be a companion volume to the translation of the *Economic Harmonies* <note>original: "Harmonies"</note>.

I do not need to say anything further here by way of a preface, as the gifted author has explained the purpose of the work himself in a short but clear introduction.

->P. J. S.<- <note>Patrick James Stirling (1809–1891), the Scottish economist and translator who brought Bastiat's work to the English-speaking world.</note>

<summary>The translator explains that this volume completes the presentation of Bastiat’s economic system for English readers, serving as the "negative" or "destructive" counterpart to his "positive" work, the Economic Harmonies.</summary>
<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, Economic Sophisms, Economic Harmonies, Political Economy, Patrick James Stirling, translation, economic theory</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page begins the main text of Frédéric Bastiat’s "Economic Sophisms," following the Table of Contents. It introduces the First Series and the author's purpose for writing the work.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

## FIRST SERIES.*

### INTRODUCTION.

My goal in this small volume is to disprove some of the arguments used against Free Trade.

I do not intend to start a fight with the protectionists <note>Protectionists are those who support government policies like tariffs or taxes on imports to protect domestic industries from foreign competition.</note>; instead, I want to instill a core principle in the minds of those who hesitate because they have sincere doubts.

I am not one of those who say that protectionism is based purely on selfish interests. I believe instead that it is based on errors, or, if you will, on *incomplete truths*. Too many people genuinely fear liberty for us to assume that their concerns are not sincere.

It is perhaps aiming too high, but I admit my hope is that this small work will become a *manual* for those whose job it is to decide between these two principles. When people have not long been familiar with the doctrine of liberty, the sophisms <note>original: "sophisms." A sophism is a clever but false argument that seems convincing but is actually flawed or deceptive.</note> of protectionism, in one form or another, are constantly resurfacing. To clear up these errors when they return, a long process of analysis is necessary; however, not everyone has the time required for such a process—and legislators have less time than anyone else. This is why I am attempting to provide the analysis and its results in a simple, ready-to-use <note>original: "cut and dry."</note> format.

But it may be asked: Are the benefits of liberty so hidden that they can only be discovered by professional economists?

---

* The first series of *Economic Sophisms* <note>original: "Sophismes Économiques."</note> appeared at the end of 1845; the second series in 1848.—EDITOR.

<summary>In this introduction, Bastiat explains that his book is intended as a manual to help people—especially legislators—identify and debunk the common but false arguments (sophisms) used to justify trade protectionism.</summary>
<keywords>Free Trade, Protectionism, Sophisms, Economic Liberty, Public Policy, Legislation</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Frédéric Bastiat’s "Economic Sophisms," where he contrasts the simple but misleading arguments of protectionists with the more complex, logical proofs required to demonstrate the benefits of free trade.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

We must admit that our opponents have a clear advantage in this debate. They can state a half-truth in just a few words; however, to show that this truth is *incomplete*, we are forced to rely on long and tedious explanations.

This is due to the nature of the issue. Protectionism concentrates the benefits it creates in one specific area, while the harm it causes is spread across the general population. The benefit is visible to the naked eye, but the harm can only be seen by the mind's eye <note>Bastiat is arguing that while we can see the jobs protected in one factory, we cannot easily see the jobs lost across the rest of the country due to higher prices.</note>. With economic liberty, the opposite is true.

We see this pattern in almost every economic question.

You say, "Here is a machine that has put thirty workers out of a job."

Or, "Here is a big spender who stimulates every industry."

Or, "The conquest of Algeria <note>A reference to the French colonization of Algeria, which began in 1830 and was a major political topic in Bastiat's time.</note> has doubled the trade of the port city of Marseilles."

Or, "The government budget provides a living for a hundred thousand families."

Everyone understands you immediately. Your statements are simple, clear, and seemingly true. But what conclusions do you draw from them?

You conclude that machinery is an evil.

You conclude that luxury, wars of conquest, and high taxes are good for the economy.

Your theory is successful precisely because you can support it by pointing to obvious, undeniable facts.

On our side, we cannot simply look at the initial cause and its immediate result. We know that every effect becomes a new cause in its own right. To judge a policy fairly, we must follow it through the entire chain of consequences to its ultimate result. In other words, we are forced to *reason* our way through it.

But then the outcry begins: "You are theorists, philosophers, idealists, utopian dreamers, and rigid ideologues <note>original: "doctrinaires." In the 19th century, this was often a political insult for people who prioritized abstract principles over practical reality.</note>!" All the prejudices of the public are stirred up against us.

What should we do in this situation? We can only ask for the reader's patience and common sense, and set out our—

<summary>Bastiat explains that protectionist arguments are often more popular because their "benefits" are immediately visible, while the harmful consequences of such policies are indirect and require logical reasoning to uncover.</summary>
<keywords>protectionism, free trade, economic logic, machinery, taxation, Algeria, Marseilles, unintended consequences, economic theory</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Frédéric Bastiat’s "Economic Sophisms," where he explains his goal of clarifying economic logic to distinguish between truth and error in the debate over free trade and protectionism.</meta>

### INTRODUCTION. 3

...deductions, if we can, in a light so clear that truth and error must show themselves plainly, openly, and without disguise—and so that the victory, once gained, may rest clearly with either restriction or freedom.

And here I must make an essential observation.

Some extracts from this small book have already appeared in the *Journal des Économistes* <note>The premier French economic journal of the 19th century, where Bastiat frequently published his essays.</note>.

In a review—which was otherwise very favorable—written by the Viscount de Romanet, he assumes that I am calling for the total abolition of customs duties <note>Taxes on imported goods</note>. He is mistaken. I am calling for the abolition of the protectionist *system* <note>original: *régime*</note>. We do not refuse to pay taxes to the government, but we want, if possible, to persuade the citizens to stop taxing one another. Napoleon once said that "the customhouse should not be used to generate revenue, but as a way to protect domestic industry." We argue the opposite. We contend that the customhouse ought not to become a tool for mutual looting among the working classes, but that it may be used to raise revenue as legitimately as any other tax. 

I am so far from demanding the abolition of customs—or at least, speaking for myself—that I see that branch of tax revenue as our future safety net. I believe our resources are capable of providing the Treasury with huge returns; and to be blunt, I must add that, seeing how slowly sound economic ideas spread while government budgets grow so rapidly, I am inclined to rely more on the Treasury's desperate need for money than on the power of enlightened public opinion to drive commercial reform.

You might ask me then, "What is your conclusion?" I reply that here there is no need to reach a specific conclusion. I am simply fighting against sophisms <note>A sophism is a clever but false argument intended to deceive.</note>; that is all.

But you might object that it is not enough to tear down—one must also build up. That is true; but to destroy an error is to build up the truth that stands against it.

After all, I am not reluctant to state my wishes. I would like to see public opinion support a customs law written roughly in these terms:

> "Articles of primary necessity shall pay a duty of 5 percent of their value <note>original: *ad valorem*</note>."

<summary>Bastiat clarifies that his goal is to eliminate protectionist policies, not all customs duties, arguing that tariffs should be used for government revenue rather than as a tool for industries to exploit one another. He expresses hope that the government's need for tax money will eventually force a shift toward free trade.</summary>

<keywords>protectionism, free trade, customs duties, revenue, taxation, economic sophisms, Napoleon, Viscount de Romanet, ad valorem</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page concludes Bastiat’s preliminary thoughts on customs duties. He lists specific tax categories based on the type of goods being imported, while clarifying that these categories are more a matter of social policy than strict economic science.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

Articles of convenience <note>original: "Articles of convenience"; items that are not absolute necessities but contribute to a comfortable standard of living.</note>, 10 percent.

Articles of luxury, 15 to 20 percent.

I am aware that these distinctions belong to a way of thinking that is quite foreign to <term>Political Economy</term> <note>In the 19th century, this term referred to the study of how societies manage resources, production, and trade—what we now simply call "economics."</note> in its strict sense, and I am far from believing that they are as fair and useful as people generally assume. However, this subject is outside the scope of my current project.

<summary>Bastiat lists suggested tax rates for different classes of goods and admits that categorizing items by "convenience" or "luxury" is a social convention rather than a scientific economic principle.</summary>
<keywords>Political Economy, taxation, luxury goods, customs duties, economic theory</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page begins Chapter I of Bastiat's "Economic Sophisms," transitioning from the specific tax classifications mentioned on the previous page to a fundamental debate in economic theory: whether a society benefits more from abundance or from scarcity.</meta>

# I.

## ABUNDANCE AND SCARCITY.

---

Which is better for humanity and for society: abundance or scarcity?

"What!" you might exclaim, "can that even be a question? Has anyone ever claimed, or is it even possible to argue, that scarcity is the foundation of human well-being?"

Yes, this has been claimed, and it is defended every day. I do not hesitate to say that the *theory of scarcity* is by far the most popular one. It is the staple of conversation, newspapers, books, and political speeches <note>original: "tribune"; the platform from which a speaker addresses an assembly, referring here to parliamentary debates.</note>. As strange as it may seem, it is certain that <term>Political Economy</term> <note>The 19th-century term for the study of how societies manage resources and trade; what we now call economics.</note> will have fulfilled its practical mission once it has established beyond doubt and widely spread this very simple truth: "The wealth of humanity consists in the abundance of commodities."

Do we not hear it said every day, "The foreigner is about to flood us with their products"? If so, then people fear abundance.

Did not M. Saint-Cricq <note>Pierre de Saint-Cricq (1772–1854) was a French politician and a prominent advocate for protectionist trade policies.</note> exclaim, "Production is excessive"? He, too, feared abundance.

Do workers break machines? If they do, they fear an excess of production—they fear abundance.

Has not M. Bugeaud <note>Thomas Robert Bugeaud (1784–1849) was a French Marshal and politician who argued that high food prices were necessary to ensure the prosperity of farmers.</note> spoken these words: "Let bread be expensive, and farmers will get rich"? Now, bread can only be expensive if it is scarce. Therefore, M. Bugeaud praises scarcity.

Does not M. d'Argout <note>Antoine Maurice Apollinaire d'Argout (1782–1858) was a French statesman who served as Governor of the Bank of France.</note> argue against the sugar industry based on its very productivity? Does he not say, "Beetroot has no future, and its cultivation cannot be expanded, because a few acres devoted to its cultivation in each..."

<summary>Bastiat introduces the central conflict between the "theory of abundance" and the "theory of scarcity," noting that many politicians and workers paradoxically view plenty as a threat to prosperity.</summary>
<keywords>abundance, scarcity, Political Economy, protectionism, production, M. Saint-Cricq, M. Bugeaud, M. d'Argout</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of the "theory of scarcity." He transitions from specific examples of protectionist rhetoric to explaining the underlying logic that leads workers and politicians to believe that limiting production creates wealth.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
->**ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.**<-

department would supply the whole consumption of France?" <note>Referring to the argument on the previous page regarding whether a single region could provide enough for the whole country.</note> In his eyes, then, good lies in sterility and <term>dearth</term> <note>A scarcity or lack of something; specifically, a shortage of food.</note>, while evil lies in fertility and abundance.

*La Presse* and *Le Commerce* <note>original: "The Presse, the Commerce"; these were major French newspapers of the time.</note>, along with the majority of the daily papers, publish one or more articles every morning to demonstrate to the <term>Chambers</term> <note>The French legislative bodies: the Chamber of Peers and the Chamber of Deputies.</note> and the Government that it is sound policy to use laws to raise the price of all things by means of <term>tariffs</term> <note>Taxes on imported goods designed to protect domestic industries.</note>. And do the Chambers and the Government not obey these instructions? Now, tariffs can only raise prices by diminishing the *supply* of goods in the market! Therefore, the newspapers, the Chambers, and the Minister put the "theory of scarcity" into practice, and I am justified in saying that this theory is by far the most popular one.

How is it that in the eyes of workers, journalists <note>original: "publicists"; writers on current affairs or political topics.</note>, and statesmen, abundance appears to be something to be dreaded, while scarcity seems advantageous? I propose to trace this illusion to its source.

We notice that a man grows richer in proportion to the profit yielded by his efforts—that is to say, in proportion as he sells his product at a *higher price*. He sells at a higher price in proportion to the rarity or the scarcity of the article he produces. We conclude from this that, as far as he is personally concerned, scarcity enriches him. By applying this same reasoning to all other producers in turn, we construct the *theory of scarcity*. We then proceed to apply this theory: in order to favor producers generally, we raise prices artificially and cause a scarcity of all goods through prohibitions, trade restrictions, the suppression of machinery, and other similar means.

The same logic applies to abundance. We observe that when a product is plentiful, it sells at a lower price, and the producer earns less. If all producers are in the same situation, they are all poor. Therefore, people conclude it is abundance that ruins society. And as theories are soon put into practice, we see the law struggling against the abundance of goods.

This <term>sophism</term> <note>A clever but false argument, especially one used to deceive.</note> may make little impression when stated in its general form, but when applied to a specific set of facts, to a certain branch of industry, or to a given class of producers, it is extremely persuasive; and this is easily explained. It forms a...

<summary>Bastiat argues that the "theory of scarcity"—the idea that wealth is created by raising prices through limited supply—dominates French politics and media. He explains that this is a logical fallacy caused by applying the individual producer's desire for high prices to the economy as a whole.</summary>
<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, theory of scarcity, abundance, protectionism, tariffs, economic sophism, trade restriction, France, political economy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s exploration of the logical fallacies behind protectionism. He is currently dissecting the "theory of scarcity," arguing that it stems from a logical error—a "syllogism"—that only considers the individual as a producer (who benefits from high prices) rather than as a consumer (who benefits from abundance).</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>

### ABUNDANCE, SCARCITY.

...a <term>syllogism</term> <note>A form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two assumed premises. Bastiat is arguing that the logic used by protectionists is technically valid but misses crucial information.</note> which is not *false*, but *incomplete*. Now, what is *true* in a syllogism is always and necessarily present to the mind. But *incompleteness* is a negative quality—an absent <note>original: "datum"</note> fact—which is very possible, and indeed very easy, to leave out of account.

Man produces in order to consume. He is at once a producer and a consumer. The reasoning which I have just explained considers him only from the first of these points of view. Had the second been taken into account, it would have led to an opposite conclusion. In effect, might it not be said:—

> The consumer is richer in proportion as he *purchases* all things cheaper; and he purchases things cheaper in proportion to their abundance; therefore, it is abundance which enriches him. This reasoning, extended to all consumers, leads to the *theory of plenty*.

It is the notion of *exchange* imperfectly understood which leads to these illusions. If we consider our personal interest, we recognize distinctly that it is double. As *sellers*, we have an interest in <note>original: "dearness"</note> high prices, and consequently in scarcity; as *buyers*, we have an interest in cheapness, or what amounts to the same thing, in the abundance of commodities. We cannot, then, base our reasoning on one or the other of these interests before inquiring which of the two coincides and is identified with the general and permanent interest of mankind at large.

If man were a solitary animal, if he worked exclusively for himself, if he consumed directly the fruit of his labor—in a word, *if he did not exchange*—the theory of scarcity would never have appeared in the world. It is too evident that, in that case, abundance would be advantageous, from whatever quarter it came: whether from the result of his industry, from ingenious tools, from powerful machinery of his invention, or whether due to the fertility of the soil, the generosity <note>original: "liberality"</note> of nature, or even to a mysterious *influx* <note>original: "invasion"</note> of products brought by the waves and left by them upon the shore. No solitary man would ever have thought that, in order to encourage his labor and render it more productive, it was necessary to break in pieces the instruments which saved him effort, to neutralize the fertility of the soil, or give back to the sea the good things it had brought to his door. He would perceive at once that labor is...

<summary>Bastiat explains that the "theory of scarcity" arises from viewing the economy only from the seller's perspective, whereas a consumer's perspective reveals that abundance creates wealth. He uses the example of a solitary man to show that no one would naturally choose scarcity over plenty if they were working for their own direct consumption.</summary>

<keywords>syllogism, abundance, scarcity, theory of plenty, exchange, personal interest, producer vs consumer, Robinson Crusoe economics, Bastiat</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that labor is a means to an end (consumption), and that saving labor through technology or trade constitutes progress, despite the producer's desire for scarcity to drive up prices.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

...not an end, but a means; and that it would be absurd to reject the result for fear of doing injury to the means by which that result was accomplished. He would perceive that if he devotes two hours a day to providing for his wants, any circumstance (machinery, fertility, a free gift, no matter what) which saves him an hour of that labor—the result remaining the same—puts that hour at his disposal, and that he can devote it to increasing his enjoyments. In short, he would see *that to save labor is nothing else than progress*.

But *exchange* <note>The act of trading goods or services.</note> disturbs our view of a truth so simple. In the social state, and with the division of labor <note>original: "separation of employments"</note> to which it leads, the production and consumption of a commodity are not performed by the same individual. Each man comes to see his labor no longer as a means, but as an end. In relation to each commodity, exchange creates two interests: that of the producer and that of the consumer; and these two interests are always directly opposed to each other.

It is essential to analyze them and examine their nature.

Take the case of any producer whatever; what is his immediate interest? It consists of two things: first, that the fewest possible number of persons should devote themselves to his branch of industry; and second, that the greatest possible number of persons should be searching for the article he produces. Political economy explains it more concisely in these terms: supply very limited, demand very extended; or in other words: competition limited, demand unlimited.

What is the immediate interest of the consumer? That the supply of the product in question should be extended, and the demand restrained.

Seeing, then, that these two interests are in opposition to each other, one of them must necessarily coincide with social interests in general, and the other be antagonistic to them.

But which of them should legislation favor as being identical with the public good—if, indeed, it should favor either?

To discover this, we must inquire what would happen if the secret wishes of men were granted.

Insofar as we are producers, it must be allowed that the desire of every one of us is anti-social. Are we vine-dressers <note>People who cultivate and prune grapevines for wine production.</note>? It would give us no great regret if hail should shower down on...

<summary>Bastiat explains that while saving labor is progress for an individual, the system of exchange creates a conflict where producers profit from scarcity and consumers profit from abundance. He argues that the producer's interest is inherently "anti-social" because it relies on limiting supply to increase personal gain.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Frédéric Bastiat’s critique of the "theory of scarcity." <page-num>9</page-num>

-># ABUNDANCE, SCARCITY.<-

...all the vines in the world except our own: *this is the theory of scarcity.* Are we iron producers <note>original: "iron-masters"</note>? Our wish is that there should be no other iron in the market but our own, however much the public may need it; and for no other reason than that this need, keenly felt and poorly satisfied, will ensure us a higher price. This *is still* the theory of scarcity. Are we farmers? We say with Mr. Bugeaud <note>Thomas Robert Bugeaud (1784–1849) was a French Marshal and politician who advocated for agricultural protectionism to keep domestic prices high.</note>, "Let bread be expensive—that is to say, scarce—and farmers <note>original: "agriculturists"</note> will thrive." It is always the same theory: *the theory of scarcity*.

Are we doctors <note>original: "physicians"</note>? We cannot avoid seeing that certain physical improvements <note>original: "ameliorations"</note> that better the public health <note>original: "sanitary state"</note> of the country, the development of certain moral virtues such as moderation and temperance, the progress of knowledge that enables each person to take better care of their own health, and the discovery of certain simple remedies that are easy to apply would all be blows to our professional success. In as far as we are doctors, then, our secret wishes would be *anti-social*. I do not say that doctors actually form these secret wishes. On the contrary, I believe they would welcome with joy the discovery of a universal cure-all <note>original: "panacea"</note>; but they would not do this as doctors, but as human beings and as Christians. By a noble act of self-denial <note>original: "abnegation of self"</note>, the doctor puts himself in the consumer's point of view. But in practicing a profession from which he derives his own and his family's living, his desires—or, if you will, his interests—are *anti-social*.

Are we manufacturers of cotton fabrics <note>original: "stuffs"</note>? We desire to sell them at the price most profitable to ourselves. We would willingly consent to a ban <note>original: "interdict"</note> being placed on all rival manufacturers; and if we could venture to give this wish public expression, or hope to realize it with some chance of success, we would reach our end, to some extent, by indirect means. For example, by excluding foreign fabrics in order to diminish the *supply*, we would thus produce, forcibly and for our own profit, a *scarcity* of clothing.

In the same way, we might examine all other branches of industry, and we would always find that producers, acting as such, have anti-social views. “The shopkeeper,” says Montaigne <note>Michel de Montaigne (1533–1592) was a famous French philosopher; this quote comes from his essay "The Profit of One Man is the Loss of Another."</note>, “thrives only by the reckless spending <note>original: "irregularities"</note> of youth; the farmer by the high price of grain <note>original: "corn"</note>; the architect by the destruction of..."

<summary>Bastiat explains the "theory of scarcity," arguing that producers—from ironworkers to doctors—often have professional interests that conflict with society's well-being because they benefit from high prices and limited supply.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Frédéric Bastiat’s argument that the professional interests of "producers" (doctors, lawyers, manufacturers) often depend on scarcity, disease, or conflict, putting them at odds with the general welfare of society. He now contrasts this with the "consumer's" interest, which aligns perfectly with social progress.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

...houses; the officers of the law <note>original: "officers of justice"</note> thrive through lawsuits and quarrels. Ministers of religion gain their status and employment from our vices and our mortality. No doctor rejoices in the health of his friends, nor do soldiers rejoice in the peace of their country; and so it goes for all the rest.

It follows, then, that if the secret wishes of every producer were granted, the world would rapidly slide back into barbarism. The sail would replace steam, the oar would replace the sail, and general trade would be carried on by a freight wagon; the wagon would be replaced by the mule, and the mule by the peddler. Wool would exclude cotton, and cotton in its turn would exclude wool, continuing until the <term>dearth</term> <note>original: "dearth" – a scarcity or lack of something</note> of all things caused humanity itself to disappear from the face of the earth.

Suppose for a moment that legislative power and public force were placed at the disposal of Mimeral’s committee <note>The Committee of Industry, led by Auguste Mimeral (1780–1871), was a powerful lobby of French manufacturers that advocated for high tariffs and protectionism.</note>, and that each member of that association had the privilege of proposing and passing a favorite law. Is it difficult to guess what sort of industrial code the public would be forced to follow?

If we now proceed to consider the immediate interest of the consumer, we shall find that it is in perfect harmony with the general interest—with everything that the welfare of society requires. When a purchaser goes to market, he desires to find it well stocked. He wishes for seasons that are favorable <note>original: "propitious"</note> for all harvests; for increasingly marvelous inventions that bring more products and enjoyments within reach; for time and labor to be saved; for distances to be eliminated by the speed and perfection of transport; for a spirit of justice and peace to reduce the burden of taxation; and for barriers of every kind to be removed. In all of this, the interest of the consumer runs parallel with the public interest. The consumer may push his secret wishes to a <term>chimerical</term> <note>original: "chimerical" – imaginary, impossible, or highly unrealistic</note> and absurd length without these wishes ever becoming harmful to the public welfare. He may wish that food and shelter, the hearth and the home, education <note>original: "instruction"</note> and morality, security and peace, strength and health, could all be obtained without effort and in unlimited amounts—like the dust on the road or the water in a stream.

<summary>Bastiat argues that while producers benefit from scarcity and social problems, the consumer's desire for abundance and efficiency perfectly aligns with the general progress of society.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that the consumer's desire for abundance aligns with the general interest of society, whereas the producer's secret desire for scarcity (to keep prices high) is fundamentally anti-social.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

<header>ABUNDANCE AND SCARCITY</header>

...the air which we breathe; and yet the realization of his desires would not be at odds with the good of society.

It may be said that if these wishes were granted, the work of the producer would become more and more limited, and would eventually stop for lack of sustenance <note>original: "aliment"</note>. But why? Because, in this extreme scenario, all imaginable wants and desires would be fully satisfied. Man, like God <note>original: "Omnipotence"</note>, would create all things by a simple act of will. Well, in this hypothesis, what reason would we have to regret the end of industrial production?

I previously proposed the idea of an assembly composed of workers. In this assembly, each member, in his capacity as a producer, would have the power to pass a law embodying his *secret wish*. I argued that the code of laws which would come from that assembly would be systematized monopoly—the theory of scarcity reduced to practice.

In the same way, a chamber in which everyone consulted exclusively his own immediate interest as a consumer would tend to systematize liberty. It would suppress all restrictive measures and overthrow all artificial barriers—in a word, it would realize the *theory of plenty*.

Hence it follows:

That to consult exclusively the immediate interest of the producer is to consult an interest which is anti-social;

That to take as a basis exclusively the immediate interest of the consumer would be to take as a basis the general interest.

Let me enlarge on this view of the subject a little, at the risk of being <term>prolix</term> <note>original: "prolix" – wordy or long-winded</note>.

A radical antagonism exists between the seller and the buyer.*

The former desires that the subject of the bargain should be scarce, its supply limited, and its price high.

The latter desires that it should be *abundant*, its supply large, and its price low.

The laws, which should be at least neutral, take the part of the seller against the buyer, of the producer against the consumer, of dearness against cheapness,† of scarcity against abundance.

---

\* The author modified the terms of this proposition somewhat in a later <note>original: "posterior"</note> work.—See *Economic Harmonies* <note>original: "Harmonies Économiques"</note>, chapter 11.—EDITOR.

† In French, we do not have a noun <note>original: "substantive"</note> to express the idea opposed to... <note>Bastiat is likely referring to the lack of a single noun for "cheapness" (bon marché) to contrast with "dearness" (cherté) in the French language.</note>

<summary>Bastiat argues that while producers favor scarcity to drive up prices, consumers favor abundance; therefore, laws should prioritize the consumer's interest as it represents the general welfare of society.</summary>

<page-num>12</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS</header>

The protectionists proceed, if not intentionally then at least logically, on this premise <note>original: "datum"</note>: *a nation is rich when it is in want of everything.*

For they say: we must favor the producer by securing him a good market for his product. For this purpose, it is necessary to raise the price, and in order to raise the price, we must restrict the supply; and to restrict the supply is to create scarcity.

Let us just suppose that at the present moment, while all these laws are in full force, we take a complete inventory—not in value, but in weight, measure, volume, and quantity—of all the commodities existing in the country that are suited to satisfy the needs <note>original: "wants"</note> and tastes of its inhabitants: grain <note>original: "corn"</note>, meat, cloth, fuel, colonial products, and so on.

Suppose, again, that the next day all the barriers that prevent the introduction of foreign products are removed.

Finally, suppose that in order to test the result of this reform, a new inventory is taken three months later.

Is it not true that there will be found in France more grain, cattle, cloth, linen, iron, coal, sugar, and so on, at the time of the second inventory than at the time of the first?

This is so true that our protective tariffs have no other purpose than to hinder all these things from reaching us, to restrict the supply, and to prevent price drops <note>original: "depreciation"</note> and abundance.

Now I would ask: Are the people who live under our laws better fed because there is *less* bread, meat, and sugar in the country? Are they better clothed because there is *less* cloth and linen? Better warmed because there is *less* coal? Better assisted in their labor because there are *fewer* tools and *less* iron, copper, and machinery?

But it may be said: "If the foreigner *floods* <note>original: "inundates"</note> us with his products, he will take away our money."

And what does that matter? People do not eat money. They do not clothe themselves with gold or warm themselves with silver. What does it matter whether there is more or less money in the country, if there is more bread on our sideboards,

---

that of dearness (cheapness). It is quite remarkable that the common instinct expresses the idea with this phrase <note>original: "periphrase"</note>: *advantageous market* <note>original: "marché avantageux"</note> or *good bargain/cheap* <note>original: "bon marché"</note>. The protectionists would do well to change this wording <note>original: "locution"</note>, for it implies an economic system opposed to their own.

<summary>Bastiat argues that protective tariffs intentionally create scarcity to raise prices for producers, which harms the general public. He asserts that true national wealth is found in the abundance of actual goods like food and fuel, rather than the amount of money a country holds.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of protectionism, arguing that trade restrictions either cause harm through scarcity or are useless. He then introduces a new concept: how people often mistake obstacles (which require labor to overcome) for the actual cause of wealth.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>

<header>ABUNDANCE, SCARCITY.</header>

...more meat in our pantries <note>original: "larders"</note>, more linen in our closets <note>original: "wardrobes"</note>, and more firewood in our cellars.

Restrictive laws always lead us to this dilemma:

Either you admit that they produce scarcity, or you do not.

If you admit it, you confess <note>original: "avow"</note> by that admission that you inflict as much harm on the people as you possibly can. If you do not admit it, you are denying that you have restricted the supply and raised prices; consequently, you are denying that you have favored the producer.

What you do is either harmful or useless, injurious or ineffective. It can never result in anything useful.

---

### II.

## OBSTACLE, CAUSE.

Mistaking the obstacle for the cause—or scarcity for abundance—is the same logical fallacy <term>sophism</term> <note>original: "sophism"; a clever but false argument intended to deceive.</note> seen from another perspective. It is worth studying this in all its forms.

Man is originally born with nothing.

Between this state of poverty and the satisfaction of his needs, there are many *obstacles* that labor <note>original: "labour"</note> allows us to overcome. It is strange to ask how and why these very obstacles to our material prosperity have come to be mistaken for the *cause* of that prosperity.

Suppose I want to travel a hundred miles. Between my starting point and my destination, there are mountains, rivers, marshes, thick forests, and bandits—in a word, *obstacles*. To overcome these obstacles, I must exert a great deal of effort; or, what amounts to the same thing, others must exert a great deal of effort for me, and I must pay them for it. It is clear that I would have been in a better situation if these obstacles had not existed at all.

On his long journey through life, from the cradle to the grave, a person needs to consume an enormous quantity of food <note>original: "alimentary substances"</note>, protect himself against the harsh <note>original: "inclemency"</note> weather, and preserve himself from a...

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionist laws are either harmful by creating scarcity or useless if they don't. He begins a new section explaining that "obstacles" to our needs, like distance or terrain, require labor but are not the cause of wealth itself.</summary>
<keywords>protectionism, scarcity, abundance, producer interest, labor, obstacles, economic fallacies, Frédéric Bastiat</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s exploration of the "Obstacle vs. Cause" fallacy. He explains how the division of labor leads individuals to view the very problems they solve for others as the source of their own personal wealth.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

...a number of ailments, or cure himself of them. Hunger, thirst, disease, heat, and cold are all obstacles scattered along his path. In a state of isolation, he must overcome them all through hunting, fishing, farming <note>original: "tillage"</note>, spinning, weaving, and building. It is clear that it would be better for him if these obstacles were less numerous and daunting <note>original: "formidable"</note>—or, better still, if they did not exist at all. In society, he does not fight these obstacles personally; instead, others do it for him. In return, he spends his time removing one of the obstacles encountered by his fellow men.

It is also clear, looking at things as a whole, that it would be better for men collectively—or for society—if these obstacles were as few and weak as possible.

But when we closely examine social phenomena and how people's feelings are changed by the introduction of trade <note>original: "exchange"</note>, we soon see how they have come to confuse <note>original: "confound"</note> needs <note>original: "wants"</note> with wealth, and the obstacle with the cause.

The separation of jobs and the division of labor <note>original: "labour"</note>—which result from the ability to trade—mean that each person, instead of struggling on their own to overcome every obstacle surrounding them, fights only *one* of them. He overcomes that one obstacle not for himself, but for his fellow men, who in turn provide him the same service.

The result is that this man, in fighting the specific obstacle it is his job to overcome for others, sees that obstacle as the direct source of his own wealth. The greater, more daunting, and more painfully felt this obstacle is, the higher the payment <note>original: "remuneration"</note> his fellow men will be willing to give him. That is to say, they will be more ready to remove the obstacles that stand in *his* way.

For example, the physician does not bake his own bread, make his own tools <note>original: "instruments"</note>, or weave his own clothes. Others do these things for him, and in return, he treats the diseases that afflict his patients. The more numerous, severe, and frequent these diseases are, the more other people agree—and are forced—to do for his personal comfort. From this point of view, disease—a general obstacle to human happiness—becomes a source of material prosperity for the individual physician. The same argument applies...

<summary>Bastiat explains that while society benefits when obstacles are removed, the division of labor leads individuals to see the specific problems they solve (like disease for a doctor) as the source of their personal profit.</summary>
<keywords>division of labor, economic obstacles, exchange, wealth vs. want, Bastiat, physician analogy, social phenomena</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s exploration of the "Obstacle vs. Cause" fallacy. He explains how the division of labor leads individuals to view the very problems they solve for others as the source of their own personal wealth.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>

<header>OBSTACLE, CAUSE.</header>

...to all producers in their various fields. The shipowner derives his profits from the obstacle called *distance*; the agriculturist <note>original: "agriculturist"—a farmer or someone who studies the science of farming</note> from that called *hunger*; the manufacturer of cloth from that called *cold*; the schoolmaster lives upon *ignorance*; the lapidary <note>a person who cuts, polishes, or engraves precious stones</note> upon *vanity*; the attorney on *cupidity* <note>original: "cupidity"—excessive greed</note>; the notary upon possible *bad faith*,—just as the physician lives upon the diseases of men. It is quite true, therefore, that each profession has an immediate interest in the continuation, and even the expansion, of the special obstacle which it is its business to combat.

Observing this, theorists arrive and, building a system based on their own personal feelings, tell us: Want is wealth, labor is wealth, and obstacles to material prosperity are prosperity itself. To multiply obstacles is to support industry.

Then statesmen intervene. They have the control of the public’s power; and what could be more natural than to use it to develop and multiply obstacles, since this is seen as developing and multiplying wealth? They say, for example: "If we prevent the importation of iron from places where it is abundant, we place an obstacle in the way of it being obtained. This obstacle, keenly felt at home, will induce men to pay in order to be set free from it. A certain number of our fellow citizens will devote themselves to combating it, and this obstacle will make their fortune. The greater the obstacle is—that is, the scarcer, the more inaccessible, the more difficult to transport, the more distant the mineral becomes from the place where it is to be used—the more hands will be engaged in the various branches of this industry." Exclude, then, foreign iron; create an obstacle, for by doing so you create the labor which is to overcome it.

The same reasoning leads to the proscription <note>original: "proscription"—the act of banning or forbidding something</note> of machinery.

Here, for instance, are men who are in want of casks for the storage of their wine. This is an obstacle; and here are other men whose business it is to remove that obstacle by making the casks that are wanted. It is fortunate, then, that this obstacle should exist, since it gives employment to a branch of national industry and enriches a certain number of our fellow citizens. But then we have ingenious machinery invented for felling the oak, cutting it up into staves, and forming them into...

<summary>Bastiat explains the paradox where professionals profit from the problems they solve, leading some to wrongly conclude that creating obstacles (like trade barriers or banning machinery) creates wealth.</summary>
<keywords>economics, protectionism, labor, machinery, division of labor, trade barriers, Frédéric Bastiat</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that labor is a means to an end, not an end in itself. He concludes his discussion on how obstacles (like a lack of tools) are mistakenly viewed as sources of wealth and transitions into a new chapter comparing the value of the effort versus the value of the result.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "Sophisms"</note>.</header>

...the wine-casks that are wanted. By this means, the obstacle is lessened, and so are the earnings of the cooper <note>a person who makes or repairs wooden barrels and casks</note>. Let us maintain both at their former levels by law and ban the machinery.

To get to the root of this fallacy <note>original: "sophism"—a clever but false argument</note>, it is necessary only to reflect that human labor is not the *end*, but the *means*. *It never remains unemployed.* If one obstacle is removed, *it battles another;* and society is freed from two obstacles by the same amount of labor that was formerly required for the removal of one. If the labor of the cooper is made unnecessary in one area, it will soon take another direction. But how and from what source will it be paid <note>original: "remunerated"</note>? From exactly the same source from which it is paid at present; for when a certain amount of labor becomes available <note>original: "disposable"</note> by the removal of an obstacle, a corresponding amount of payment becomes available also. To argue that human labor will ever lack employment would be to argue that the human race will cease to encounter obstacles. In that case, labor would not only be impossible; it would be unnecessary <note>original: "superfluous"</note>. We should no longer have anything to do because we would be all-powerful <note>original: "omnipotent"</note>; we would only have to issue a decree <note>original: "fiat"—a formal authorization or command</note> in order to ensure the satisfaction of all our desires and the supply of all our wants.*

---

### III.

## EFFORT, RESULT.

We have just seen that obstacles are placed between our wants and the satisfaction of those wants. We succeed in overcoming these obstacles, or in diminishing their force, by the employment of our abilities <note>original: "faculties"</note>. We may say in a general way that industry is an effort followed by a result.

But what constitutes the measure of our prosperity or of our wealth? Is it the result of the effort? Or is it the effort itself? A relationship always exists between the effort employed—

* See later <note>original: "post"</note>, chapter 14 of the second series of *Economic Sophisms* <note>original: "Sophismes Économiques"</note>, and chapters 3 and 11 of the *Economic Harmonies* <note>original: "Harmonies Économiques"</note>.

<summary>Bastiat argues that labor-saving machinery does not create permanent unemployment because human wants are infinite and labor will always shift to address new obstacles. He introduces the distinction between the effort spent on a task and the actual result achieved as the true measure of wealth.</summary>
<keywords>labor vs. means, machinery and employment, cooper, economic fallacies, effort vs. result, Bastiat, productivity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous chapter’s conclusion regarding human labor and obstacles. It begins a new discussion comparing two opposing economic theories: one that values the end result (wealth) and one that values the effort (labor) itself.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>

# EFFORT AND RESULT

...and the result obtained. Progress consists in the relative improvement <note>original: "enhancement"</note> of the ratio <note>original: "relation"</note> between these two factors.

Both theories <note>original: "theses"</note> have been defended; and in the field of political economy, they have divided the world of opinion and thought.

According to the first system, wealth is the result of labor, increasing as the ratio of result to effort grows. Absolute perfection, of which God is the model <note>original: "type"</note>, consists in the infinite distance between the two—in this sense, effort is nothing <note>original: "nil"</note> and the result is infinite.

The second system teaches that it is the effort itself which constitutes the measure of wealth. To make progress, in this view, is to increase the ratio of *effort* compared to the *result*. The ideal of this system may be found in the pointless <note>original: "sterile"</note> and eternal efforts of Sisyphus.* <note>In Greek mythology, Sisyphus was condemned to roll a boulder up a hill for eternity, only for it to roll back down each time.</note>

The first system naturally welcomes everything that tends to diminish *hardship* <note>original: "pains"</note> and increase *production*: powerful machinery that increases human strength, trade <note>original: "exchange"</note> which allows people to gain more advantage from natural resources <note>original: "agents"</note> distributed across the earth, intelligence which discovers, experience which proves, and competition which stimulates.

Logically, the second system calls for <note>original: "invokes"</note> everything that has the effect of increasing hardship and diminishing products: privileges, monopolies, restrictions, prohibitions, the removal <note>original: "suppression"</note> of machinery, and intentional barrenness <note>original: "sterility"</note>.

It is worth noting that the universal practice of mankind always points to the principle of the first system. We have never seen, and we shall never see, any person who works in any field—be they a farmer, manufacturer, merchant, craftsman <note>original: "artificer"</note>, soldier, author, or philosopher—who does not devote all the powers of their mind to working better, working faster, and working more economically. In a word, they try to achieve *more with less*.

The opposite doctrine is favored only by theorists, legislators <note>original: "deputies"—representatives in a legislative assembly</note>, journalists, statesmen, and ministers—men, in short, born to conduct experiments on the social body.

At the same time, we may observe that in what concerns...

---

* For this reason, and for the sake of brevity <note>original: "conciseness"</note>, the reader will forgive us for calling this system in the following pages <note>original: "sequel"</note> by the name of *Sisyphism*.

<sig>B</sig>

<summary>Bastiat distinguishes between two economic schools: one that views wealth as the result of labor (valuing efficiency) and another that views labor itself as wealth (valuing the effort regardless of result). He argues that while individuals naturally seek efficiency, politicians and theorists often promote "Sisyphism," which mistakenly values the struggle over the outcome.</summary>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "sisyphism"—the economic fallacy that labor itself, rather than the result of labor, is the source of wealth. It transitions from theoretical definitions to the practical contradiction seen in how individuals act versus how they legislate.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

...themselves personally, they act as everyone else does: on the principle of obtaining the greatest possible amount of useful results from labor.

Perhaps people think I am exaggerating, and that there are no true *sisyphists* <term>sisyphists: A term coined by Bastiat for those who believe that creating more work or "effort" is better for the economy than actually achieving a result efficiently.</term>.

If it is argued that in practice they do not push their principle to its most extreme consequences, I willingly admit it. This is always the case when one starts with a false principle. Such a principle soon leads to results so absurd and so harmful that we are forced to stop short. This is the reason why practical industry never accepts *sisyphism*; punishment would follow the error too closely for it to go unnoticed. 

But in matters of theory, such as those that theorists and statesmen deal with, one may follow a false principle for a long time before discovering its falsehood through the complicated consequences that people were formerly unfamiliar with. When its falsehood is finally discovered, the authors take refuge in the opposite principle, turn around, contradict themselves, and seek their justification in a modern maxim of incomparable absurdity: *in political economy <term>political economy: The 18th and 19th-century term for the study of economics, focusing on how a country’s wealth is produced and managed.</term>, there is no inflexible rule, no absolute principle*.

Let us see, then, if these two opposite principles which I have just described do not take turns predominating—one in practical industry, and the other in industrial legislation.

I have already mentioned the saying of M. Bugeaud <note>Thomas Robert Bugeaud (1784–1849) was a French Marshal and a member of the Chamber of Deputies who often spoke on agricultural protectionism.</note> (that "when bread is dear <note>original: "dear" meaning expensive</note>, farmers become rich"). But in M. Bugeaud are embodied two separate characters: the farmer and the legislator.

As a farmer, M. Bugeaud directs all his efforts toward two goals: to save labor and to obtain cheap bread. When he prefers a good plow to a bad one; when he improves his pastures; when, in order to break up <note>original: "pulverize"</note> the soil, he substitutes the action of the atmosphere <note>The practice of letting the weather—frost, rain, and sun—break down the soil naturally.</note> for that of the harrow and the hoe as much as possible; when he calls to his aid all the processes for which science and experiment have proved the effectiveness—he has only one object in view: to diminish the *proportion of effort to result*. 

We have indeed no other test of the ability of a cultivator and the perfection of his methods than to measure to what extent they have lessened the former and added to the latter. And as all the farmers in the world act...

<summary>Bastiat argues that while individuals naturally seek efficiency in their private business, they often support "sisyphism" (valuing labor over results) in public policy. He uses the example of M. Bugeaud to show how a person can seek efficiency as a farmer while advocating for high prices as a legislator.</summary>
<keywords>sisyphism, M. Bugeaud, political economy, economic principles, labor efficiency, agricultural legislation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that while individuals naturally seek efficiency, legislators often perversely advocate for policies that create more work by making goods scarcer and more expensive. It introduces specific historical figures, like M. Bugeaud and Baron Dupin, to illustrate this contradiction.</meta>

# EFFORT, RESULT. 19

Based on this principle, we may assert that the general effort of mankind is to obtain—certainly for their own benefit—bread and all other products at a lower cost, thereby reducing the labor needed to acquire a specific amount of what they want.

Once this undeniable human tendency is established, it should, it seems, reveal the true principle to the legislator and point out how he should support industry (to the extent that it falls within his duties to do so). For it would be absurd to claim that human laws should work against the laws of Providence <note>original: "Providence." In this context, it refers to the natural order of the world or the laws of nature.</note>.

And yet we have heard M. Bugeaud <note>Thomas-Robert Bugeaud (1784–1849) was a French Marshal and a member of the Chamber of Deputies who favored protectionist trade policies to support French agriculture.</note>, in his role as a deputy, exclaim:
> “I understand nothing of this theory of cheapness; I would much rather see bread more expensive and labor more abundant.”

Following this doctrine, the deputy of the Dordogne <note>A department in southwestern France represented by Bugeaud.</note> votes for legislative measures that hinder trade, for the very reason that trade provides us indirectly with what direct production could only provide at a much higher cost.

Now, it is very evident that Mr. Bugeaud’s principles as a deputy are directly opposed to the principles by which he acts as a farmer. To act consistently, he should either vote against all legislative restrictions or apply the principle he proclaims from the speaker's podium <note>original: "tribune"</note> to his own farming operations. We would then see him sowing his grain in his most barren fields, for in this way he would succeed in *working much to obtain little*. We would see him throwing away the plow, since farming by hand would satisfy his double wish for more expensive bread and more abundant labor.

Trade restriction has as its declared goal and its acknowledged effect the increase of labor.

It also has as its declared goal and its acknowledged effect the causing of high prices <note>original: "dearness"</note>, which simply means a scarcity of products. Thus, if carried out to its extreme limits, it is pure *sisyphism* <term>sisyphism: An economic fallacy named after Sisyphus, who was condemned to roll a rock up a hill only for it to roll back down forever. It refers to the idea that labor is an end in itself, even if it produces nothing.</term>, as we have defined it—*infinite labor, zero results*.

Baron Charles Dupin <note>Charles Dupin (1784–1873) was a mathematician and economist who served in the French Chamber of Peers.</note>, who is considered the leading light of the high nobility on economic science, accuses railways of *injuring the shipping industry* <note>original: "navigation"</note>. It is certainly true that a more perfect means of transport will naturally restrict the use of a less perfect one. But railways cannot hurt shipping except by attracting traffic; and they...

<summary>This page critiques the economic fallacy that labor itself is more important than the resulting product, highlighting the contradiction between a legislator's personal efficiency and their public advocacy for protectionism. Bastiat argues that trade restrictions are a form of "sisyphism" that intentionally creates scarcity and unnecessary work.</summary>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of protectionist policies, focusing on the "sisyphism" of Baron Dupin and M. de Saint-Cricq. It argues that measuring wealth by the amount of labor expended, rather than the results achieved, leads to the logical absurdity of preferring less efficient methods of production and transport.</meta>

-> ECONOMIC SOPHISMS. <-

Railroads cannot attract traffic except by conveying goods and passengers more cheaply; and they cannot convey them more cheaply except by *diminishing the proportion of effort required to achieve a specific result*. This reduction in effort is exactly what constitutes cheapness. Therefore, when Baron Dupin <note>Charles Dupin (1784–1873) was a French mathematician and economist known for his work on statistics and industry.</note> laments this reduction of labor used to achieve a given result, he is preaching the doctrine of *sisyphism* <term>sisyphism: an economic philosophy (named after Sisyphus, who was condemned to roll a boulder up a hill forever) that values the act of working over the actual results or products of that work</term>. Logically, since he prefers the ship to the railroad, he should prefer the cart to the ship, the pack-saddle to the cart, and the pannier <note>original: "pannier," a large basket, often carried in pairs on the back of a pack animal.</note> to all other known means of transport, for it is the basket that requires the most labor for the smallest result.

“Labor constitutes the wealth of a people,” said M. de Saint-Cricq <note>Pierre Laurent Barthélemy, Comte de Saint-Cricq (1772–1854), was a French politician and Minister of Commerce who advocated for protectionist tariffs.</note>, the Minister of Commerce who has imposed so many restrictions upon trade. We must not suppose that this was a simplified expression meaning, “The results of labor constitute the wealth of a people.” No, this economist distinctly intended to claim that it is the *intensity* of labor which is the measure of wealth. The proof of this is that through a series of consequences and restrictions, he convinced France (and believed he was doing her a favor) to expend double the amount of labor in order, for example, to provide herself with the same amount of iron. In England, iron was then priced at eight francs, while in France it cost sixteen francs. Taking a day’s labor to be worth one franc, it is clear that France could, through exchange, obtain a hundredweight <note>original: "quintal," a unit of mass roughly equal to 100 kilograms or 100 pounds depending on the region.</note> of iron by setting aside eight days of work from the total national labor. Because of the restrictive measures of M. de Saint-Cricq, France was forced to expend sixteen days’ labor in order to get that same hundredweight of iron through direct production. Double the labor for the same satisfaction results in double the wealth, according to him. It follows then that wealth is not measured by the result, but by the intensity of the labor. Is this not *sisyphism* in its purest form?

And to ensure there is no mistake regarding his meaning, the Minister later takes care to explain his ideas more fully. Just as he had previously called the intensity of labor *wealth*, he goes on to call the more abundant results of that labor—or the more abundant supply of things that satisfy our needs—*poverty*. “Everywhere,” he says, “machinery has...”

<summary>Bastiat argues against the protectionist view that labor itself is wealth, showing that preferring inefficient labor over efficient results leads to the logical absurdity of favoring primitive transport and higher costs.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of protectionist arguments by examining the claims of M. de Saint-Cricq and M. d'Argout. It explores the paradoxical idea that efficiency in production—specifically in the French beet-root sugar industry—was viewed as a problem by officials because it required too little land and labor to meet the nation's needs.</meta>

# EFFORT, RESULT.

"...has taken the place of manual labor; everywhere production is overflowing; everywhere the balance between the capacity to produce and the means of consumption has been destroyed." We see, then, what the critical situation of the country was attributed to in M. de Saint-Cricq’s estimation—it was due to producing too much, and to our labor being too intelligent and too fruitful. We were too well fed, too well clothed, and too well provided with everything; production was so rapid that it surpassed all our desires. According to him, it was necessary to put a stop to this "evil," and for 그 purpose, to force us through trade restrictions to labor more in order to produce less.

I have also referred to the opinions of another Minister of Commerce, M. d'Argout <note>Antoine Maurice Apollinaire, Comte d'Argout (1782–1858), was a French statesman and Governor of the Bank of France.</note>. His views deserve to be examined for a moment. Desiring to strike a major blow against beet-root <note>The sugar beet was a major domestic alternative to imported cane sugar in 19th-century France.</note> cultivation, he says: "Undoubtedly, the cultivation of beet-root is useful, *but this utility is limited*. The growth attributed to it is exaggerated. To be convinced of this, it is enough to observe that this crop will necessarily be confined within the limits of consumption. Even if you double or triple the present consumption of France, *you will always find that a very small portion of the soil will satisfy the requirements of that consumption.*" (This is surely a rather strange thing to complain about!) 

"Do you want proof of this? How many *hectares* <term>hectare: a metric unit of area equal to 10,000 square meters, or about 2.47 acres</term> did we have dedicated to beet-root in 1828? Only 3,130, which is equivalent to 1/10,540th of our arable land. At the present time, when domestically produced sugar supplies one-third of our consumption, how much land is devoted to that crop? 16,700 *hectares*, or 1/1,978th of the arable land—that is just 45 *centiares* <term>centiare: a unit of area equal to one square meter</term> in each commune <note>commune: the smallest administrative division in France, similar to a township or parish</note>. Suppose domestic sugar already supplied our entire national consumption; we would still only have 48,000 *hectares* under beet-root, or 1/689th of the arable land." *

There are two things to notice in this citation: the facts and the doctrine. The facts tend to prove that very little land, little capital, and little labor are required to produce a large quantity of sugar, and that each commune of France would be abundantly provided for by devoting to beet-root cultivation...

---

* It is only fair to M. d'Argout to say that he put this language in the mouths of those who opposed beet-root cultivation. However, he formally adopts the argument and further endorses it by using it to justify the law he was proposing.

<summary>Bastiat critiques French ministers who argued that high productivity and efficiency were economic problems, specifically citing fears that the sugar beet industry was "too efficient" because it used so little land.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of M. d'Argout’s protectionist logic. The previous page ended mid-word with "culti-", referring to the cultivation of sugar beets.</meta>

->### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.<-

the cultivation of even one hectare of its soil. This doctrine consists of viewing this efficiency as a disadvantage, and seeing the very power and fertility of the new industry as *a limit to its usefulness*.

I do not intend to set myself up here as a defender of sugar beet <note>original: "beet-root"</note> cultivation, or as a judge of the strange facts presented by M. d'Argout;* but it is worthwhile to examine the philosophy of a statesman to whom France, for a long time, entrusted the care of her agriculture and her commerce.

I remarked at the beginning that a variable relationship exists between a productive effort and its result. Absolute imperfection consists of an infinite effort with no result; absolute perfection consists of an unlimited result with no effort; and progress <note>original: "perfectibility"</note> consists of the gradual reduction of effort compared to the result.

But M. d'Argout tells us that there is death where we think we see life, and that the importance of any branch of industry is in direct proportion to its inefficiency <note>original: "powerlessness"</note>. For instance, what should we expect from the cultivation of sugar beets? Do you not see that 48,000 hectares <note>A hectare is a metric unit of area equal to 10,000 square meters, or about 2.47 acres.</note> of land, with proportional capital and manual labor, are enough to supply all of France with sugar? Therefore, this is a branch of industry with limited usefulness—limited, of course, in terms of the amount of labor it requires, which is the only way, according to the former Minister, that any industry can be useful. This usefulness would be even more limited if, due to the fertility of the soil and the richness of the sugar beets, we could harvest from 24,000 hectares what we currently get from 48,000. Oh, if only it required twenty or a hundred times more land, capital, and labor to *produce the same result*, so much the better! We might then place some hope in this new branch of industry, and it would be worthy of state protection, for it would offer a vast field for our national labor. But to produce a lot with a little! That is a bad example, and it is time for the law to step in.

***

* Assuming that 48,000 or 50,000 hectares were enough to satisfy current consumption, it would require 150,000 for triple that consumption, which M. d'Argout admits is possible. Furthermore, if sugar beets were included in a six-year rotation of crops, they would occupy 900,000 hectares, or 1/38th of the arable land.

<summary>Frédéric Bastiat critiques the "sophism" of M. d'Argout, who argued that an industry's value is measured by how much labor it requires rather than how much it produces. Bastiat sarcastically points out that under this logic, efficiency and fertility are seen as economic evils that the law must correct.</summary>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of protectionist logic, specifically responding to M. d’Argout’s argument that efficiency in industry is a threat to labor. This text follows a discussion on sugar beet cultivation.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

# EFFORT, RESULT.

But what is true regarding sugar cannot be otherwise regarding grain <note>original: "corn"</note>. If, then, the *utility* of any branch of industry is to be estimated not by the amount of satisfaction it is fitted to provide us with a specific amount of labor, but, on the contrary, by the amount of labor which it demands in order to yield us a specific amount of satisfaction, what we ought evidently to desire is that each acre of land should yield less grain, and each grain of corn less nourishment. In other words, we should want our land to be comparatively barren; for then the quantity of land, capital, and manual labor that would be required for the maintenance of our population would be much more considerable. We could then say that the demand for human labor would be in direct proportion to this barrenness. The goals of Messrs. Bugeaud, Saint-Cricq, Dupin, and d’Argout <note>These men were prominent French politicians and advocates of protectionism during the 19th century.</note> would then be satisfied; bread would be expensive, labor would be abundant, and France would be rich—rich, at least, in the sense in which these gentlemen understand the word.

What we should also desire is that human intelligence should be weakened <note>original: "enfeebled"</note> or extinguished; for, as long as it survives, it will be continually endeavoring to increase *the proportion which the end bears to the means, and which the product bears to the labor*. It is in that precisely that intelligence consists.

Thus, it appears that *sisyphism* <note>Named after Sisyphus, the Greek mythological figure condemned to roll a boulder up a hill for eternity only for it to roll back down; here, Bastiat uses it to mean the economic policy of creating work for the sake of work, regardless of its productivity.</note> has been the doctrine of all the men who have been entrusted with our industrial destinies. It would be unfair to reproach them for it. This principle guides Ministers only because it is predominant in the Legislative Chambers; and it predominates in the Chambers only because it is sent there by the voting body; and the voting body is imbued with it only because public opinion is saturated with it.

I think it right to repeat here that I do not accuse men such as Messrs. Bugeaud, Dupin, Saint-Cricq, and d’Argout of being absolutely and under all circumstances *sisyphists*. They are certainly not so in their private transactions; for in these they always desire to obtain through exchange what would cost them more to produce directly. But I affirm they are *sisyphists* when they hinder the country from doing the same thing.*

---

* See on the same subject, *Economic Sophisms* <note>original: "Sophismes Économiques"</note>, second series, chapter 16, later in this book, and *Economic Harmonies* <note>original: "Harmonies Économiques"</note>, chapter 6.

<summary>Bastiat argues that if labor itself is the goal of industry, then inefficiency and barrenness should be desirable traits, a doctrine he calls "sisyphism." He points out that while politicians enforce this logic on the nation through protectionism, they never follow such wasteful principles in their own private lives.</summary>
