<header>ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "SOPHISMS"</note></header>

### IV.

## TO EQUALIZE THE CONDITIONS OF PRODUCTION.

It has been claimed . . . but lest I be accused of putting fallacies <note>original: "sophisms"</note> into the mouths of the protectionists, I shall allow one of their most vigorous defenders <note>original: "athletes"</note> to speak for them.

> “It has been thought that protection in our case should simply represent the difference that exists between the cost of production <note>original: "cost price"</note> of a commodity which we produce and the cost of production of the same commodity produced by our neighbors. . . . A protective duty calculated on this basis would only ensure free competition . . . ; free competition exists only when there is equality in the conditions and in the costs. In the case of a horse race, we determine the weight which each horse has to carry, and so equalize the conditions; without that, there could be no fair competition. In the case of trade, if one of the sellers can bring his commodity to market at less cost, he ceases to be a competitor and becomes a monopolist. . . . Remove this protection, which represents the difference in the cost of production, and the foreigner invades our markets and acquires a monopoly.”*
> 
> “Everyone must wish, for his own sake as well as for the sake of others, that the production of the country should be protected against foreign competition, *whenever the latter can provide products at a lower price*.”†

This argument appears constantly in the works of the protectionist school. I propose to examine it carefully, and I earnestly ask for the reader’s patience and attention. I shall consider, first of all, the inequalities that are attributable to nature, and afterwards those that are attributable to differences in taxation.

In this, as in other cases, we shall find protectionist theorists viewing their subject from the producer’s standpoint, while we advocate for the cause of the unfortunate consumers, whose interests they studiously keep out of sight. They establish a

->* Viscount de Romanet. <note>original: "M. le Vicomte de Romanet"</note><-
->† Matthieu de Dombasle. <note>A prominent 19th-century French agronomist and advocate of protectionist policies.</note><-

<summary>Bastiat introduces the protectionist argument that tariffs are necessary to "level the playing field" by offsetting the lower production costs of foreign competitors. He prepares to debunk this by distinguishing between natural advantages and those created by taxation, focusing on the consumer's perspective.</summary>
<keywords>protectionism, free competition, cost of production, monopoly, fair competition, consumers vs. producers, Viscount de Romanet, Matthieu de Dombasle</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of protectionist arguments, specifically addressing the analogy between horse racing and international trade used to justify protective tariffs.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>TO EQUALIZE THE CONDITIONS OF PRODUCTION.</header>

...comparison between the field of industry and the *turf* <note>The "turf" refers to the world of horse racing.</note>. But regarding the latter, the race is at once the *means* and the *end*. The public feels no interest in the competition beyond the competition itself. When you start your horses, your *end*, your object, is to find out which is the swiftest runner, and I see your reason for equalizing the weights each horse carries. But if your *end*, your object, were to ensure the arrival of some important and urgent news at the winning post, could you—without being inconsistent—throw obstacles in the way of anyone who offered you the best means of delivering your message quickly? 

This is what you do in commercial affairs. You forget the end, the object sought to be attained, which is material prosperity; you disregard it, you sacrifice it to a true circular argument <note>original: "*petitio principii*," a Latin logic term for the fallacy of "begging the question," where the conclusion is assumed in the premise.</note>; in plain language, you are begging the question.

But since we cannot bring our opponents to our point of view, let us place ourselves in theirs and examine the question in its relation to production.

I shall endeavor to prove:

1.  That to level and equalize the conditions of labor is to attack the very essence and principle of exchange.
2.  That it is not true that the labor of a country is canceled out <note>original: "neutralized"</note> by the competition of more favored countries.
3.  That even if that were true, protective duties would not equalize the conditions of production.
4.  That liberty—that is, freedom of trade—levels these conditions as much as they can be leveled.
5.  That the least favored countries gain the most by exchange.

I. To level and equalize the conditions of labor is not simply to cramp exchange in certain branches of trade; it is to attack the principle of exchange itself. This is because the principle of trade rests upon that very diversity—upon those very inequalities of fertility, aptitude, climate, and temperature—which you desire to erase. If Guienne <note>A historical province in southwest France, famous for its wine.</note> sends wine to Brittany, and if Brittany sends corn to Guienne, it arises because they are placed under different conditions of production. Is there a different law for international exchanges? To argue against international trade because of the inequality of conditions that gives rise to it and explains it, is to argue against its very existence. If protectionists had on their side...

<summary>Bastiat argues that the protectionist goal of "equalizing conditions" is a logical fallacy because trade is fundamentally driven by the natural differences and inequalities between regions.</summary>
<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, protectionism, free trade, exchange, production, economic logic, Guienne, Brittany, competition</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of protectionist arguments, focusing on how different conditions of production (like soil fertility) do not naturally lead to the ruin of the less-favored party in a free market.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "SOPHISMS," meaning clever but false arguments.</note></header>

sufficient logic and power, they would reduce men, like snails, to a state of absolute isolation. Moreover, there is not one of their fallacies which, when submitted to the test of rigorous deduction, does not obviously lead to destruction and annihilation.

II. It is not true, as a matter of *fact*, that inequality of conditions between two similar branches of industry necessarily entails the ruin of the one that is less favorably situated. On the turf <note>A reference to horse racing.</note>, if one horse wins the prize, the other loses it; but when two horses are employed in useful labor, each produces a beneficial result in proportion to its powers. If the stronger horse renders the greater service, it does not follow that the other horse renders no service at all. We grow wheat in all the regions <note>original: "departments," the administrative districts of France.</note> of France, even though there are enormous differences in fertility between them. If there is any one region which does not grow wheat, it is because it is not profitable to engage in that type of farming in that specific location. In the same way, analogy shows us that under a *system* <note>original: "régime"</note> of liberty, despite similar differences, wheat is produced in all the countries of Europe; and if there is one country which abandons the cultivation of that grain, it is because it finds it *more in its interest* to apply its land, labor, and capital toward something else. 

And why should the fertility of one region not paralyze the farmer of a neighboring region which is less favorably situated? It is because economic phenomena have a *flexibility*, an *elasticity*, and *balancing powers* <note>original: "levelling powers"</note> which appear to have altogether escaped the notice of the protectionist school. That school accuses us of being obsessed with a "system"; but it is the protectionists who are rigid to the highest degree, if the spirit of "system" consists in propping up arguments which rest upon a single fact instead of upon a collection of facts. 

In the example we have given, it is the difference in the value of land which compensates for the difference in its fertility. Your field produces three times more than mine? Yes, but it has cost you ten times more to buy, and therefore I can still compete with you. This is the whole mystery. And observe, that superiority in some respects leads to inferiority in others. It is just because your land is more...

<summary>Bastiat argues that differences in natural advantages, such as soil fertility, are balanced out by land costs and market flexibility, allowing different regions to compete without needing protectionist tariffs.</summary>
<keywords>economic fallacies, protectionism, free trade, agricultural competition, land value, soil fertility, Frédéric Bastiat</keywords>

<header>TO EQUALIZE THE CONDITIONS OF PRODUCTION.</header>

...fertile that it becomes more expensive <note>original: "dearer"</note>. Therefore, it is not *accidentally*, but *necessarily*, that a balance <note>original: "equilibrium"</note> is established, or tends to be established. It cannot be denied that freedom is the system <note>original: "régime"</note> which is most favorable to this tendency.

I have referred to a branch of agricultural industry; I might as well have referred to a different department of business. There are tailors in Quimper <note>A city in Brittany, France, used here as an example of a smaller, less expensive location.</note>, and that does not prevent there being tailors in Paris, even though the Parisian tailors pay a higher rent and have much higher living expenses. However, the Parisian tailors have a different set of customers, and that serves not only to restore the balance but to tip it in their favor.

When we speak, then, of equalizing the conditions of labor, we must not fail to examine whether freedom does not already give us what we seek from an arbitrary government system.

This natural balancing power of economic factors is so important to the question we are considering—and at the same time so well-suited to inspire us with admiration for the providential wisdom which presides over the fair government of society—that I must ask permission to dwell on it for a little while.

The protectionist gentlemen tell us: "Certain people have an advantage over us in the cheapness of coal, iron, machinery, and capital—we cannot compete with them."

We shall examine this proposition afterward from all its angles. At present, I limit myself to inquiring whether, when a superiority and an inferiority are both present, they do not possess within themselves—the one an ascending force and the other a descending force—which must ultimately bring them back to a just balance.

Suppose two countries, A and B. Country A possesses every kind of advantage over Country B. You infer from this that every sort of industry will concentrate itself in A, and that B is powerless. A, you say, sells much more than it buys; B buys much more than it sells. I might dispute this, but I will respect your hypothesis for now.

On this hypothesis, labor is in high demand in Country A and will soon rise in price there.

Iron, coal, land, food, and capital are also in high demand in Country A, and they will soon rise in price there.

At the same time, labor, iron, coal, land, and food...

<summary>Bastiat explains that economic advantages, such as fertile land or cheap resources, are naturally offset by rising costs in those areas, such as higher rents and wages. He argues that a free market naturally moves toward a balance (equilibrium) without the need for protective tariffs.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that economic forces naturally balance themselves out. He is explaining that if one country (A) becomes too wealthy or expensive, industry will naturally migrate to a poorer country (B) to find lower costs, creating a natural equilibrium.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

capital is in little demand in country B and will soon drop in price there.

This is not all. While Country A is constantly selling and Country B is constantly buying, money flows from B to A. It becomes plentiful in A and scarce in B.

However, an abundance of money means that people must have plenty of it to buy anything else. Therefore, in A, the *real high cost* <note>original: "dearness"</note> that arises from very high demand is joined by a *nominal high cost*, which is due to an oversupply <note>original: "redundancy"</note> of precious metals like gold and silver.

A scarcity of money means that very little is required for each purchase. Consequently, in B, a *nominal low price* <note>original: "cheapness"</note> comes to be combined with a *real low price*.

Under these circumstances, industry will have every possible motive—motives, if I may say so, of the highest intensity—to leave A and establish itself in B.

Or, to be more accurate about what would actually happen in these circumstances, we can say that because sudden shifts are so disruptive to the nature of industry, such a transfer would not have been delayed for so long. From the beginning, under a system <note>original: "régime"</note> of free trade, industry would have gradually and steadily shared and distributed itself between A and B according to the laws of supply and demand—that is to say, according to the laws of justice and utility.

When I assert that if it were possible for industry to concentrate itself in one single location, that very fact would set in motion an irresistible force to spread it out again, I am not indulging in a hollow theory.

Let us listen to what was said by a manufacturer addressing the Manchester Chamber of Commerce (I will omit the figures he used to support his demonstration):

> “In the past, we exported finished fabrics <note>original: "stuffs"</note>; then those exports were replaced by yarn, which is the raw material for fabrics; then we exported machines, which are the tools for producing yarn; afterward, we exported the capital with which we build our machines; finally, we exported our workmen and our industrial skill, which are the source of our capital. All these elements of labor, one after the other, are put to work wherever they find the most advantageous opportunity—wherever the cost of living is lower and the essentials of life..."

<summary>Bastiat argues that industry naturally redistributes itself from expensive, money-rich countries to cheaper, money-poor countries through the laws of supply and demand. He quotes a Manchester manufacturer to show how production elements like materials, machinery, and labor eventually migrate to where they are most cost-effective.</summary>

...of life are most easily obtained. Even today, in Prussia, Austria, Saxony, Switzerland, and Italy, we see massive manufacturing operations founded and supported by English capital, worked by English laborers, and managed by English engineers.”

You can see clearly, then, that nature—or rather Providence, which is wiser and more far-seeing than your narrow and rigid theory suggests—has not ordered this concentration of industry. You base your reasoning on this monopoly of advantages as if it were an unalterable and helpless fact, but it is not. Nature has provided, through means as simple as they are certain, for industry to be dispersed and shared. This creates a sense of mutual support <note>original: "solidarity"</note> and simultaneous progress among nations. Your restrictive laws <note>Protectionist policies like tariffs or trade barriers.</note> paralyze this natural process as much as they can. By isolating communities, these laws make differences in wealth and status much more extreme. They prevent equalization, hinder integration, cancel out balancing factors, and keep nations segregated, whether in their superiority or their inferiority.

III. Third, to argue that a protective duty <note>A tax on imported goods designed to protect domestic industries from foreign competition.</note> equalizes the "conditions of production" is to spread an error using deceptive language. It is simply not true that an import tax equalizes the conditions of production. Those conditions remain exactly the same after the tax is imposed as they were before. At most, all such a tax equalizes are the **conditions of sale**. My opponents might say I am playing with words, but I throw that accusation back at them. It is up to them to prove that *production* and *sale* are synonymous terms. If they cannot do this, I am justified in accusing them of—if not wordplay—at least mixing up and confusing the two.

To illustrate my point with an example: imagine some Parisian speculators <note>Investors who take risks in hopes of making a profit.</note> decide to start producing oranges. They know that Portuguese oranges can be sold in Paris for a penny each. However, because of the cold climate, the Parisians would need to use frames and greenhouses. This would mean they could not sell their oranges for less than a shilling <note>A shilling was worth twelve pence; therefore, the local oranges are twelve times more expensive than the imports.</note> to make a remunerative price <note>A price high enough to cover costs and provide a profit.</note>. They then demand that Portuguese oranges...

<summary>Bastiat argues that industry naturally spreads to where costs are lower and that protectionist tariffs only equalize the price of goods (sale), without changing the underlying geographic or climate-based difficulties of making them (production).</summary>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of the "equalization of production conditions" argument. He uses the example of Portuguese vs. French oranges to show that tariffs only equalize the price for the consumer, not the actual difficulty of growing the product.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

...should have a tax <note>original: "duty"</note> of eleven pence imposed upon them. They claim that through this tax, the *conditions of production* will be made equal. The Chamber <note>The Chamber of Deputies, the French legislative assembly at the time.</note>, following its usual logic, includes a tax of eleven pence on every imported orange in the trade regulations <note>original: "tariff"</note>.

Now, I maintain that the *conditions of production* have not changed at all. The law has made no change to the warmth of the sun in Lisbon, or to the frequency and intensity of the frosts in Paris. Oranges will continue to ripen *naturally* on the banks of the Tagus <note>A river in Portugal.</note> and *artificially* on the banks of the Seine <note>The river flowing through Paris.</note>. This means much more human labor will be required in France than in Portugal to produce the same result. It is actually the *conditions of sale* that have been equalized. 

The Portuguese must now sell us their oranges for a shilling, but eleven pence of that price goes to pay the tax. It is obvious that the French consumer will pay that tax. And look at the bizarre result: for every Portuguese orange eaten, the country as a whole loses nothing, because the extra eleven pence charged to the consumer is paid into the national treasury. This causes a shift in where the money goes <note>original: "displacement"</note>, but not a total loss. 

However, for every French orange eaten, there is a loss of nearly eleven pence. The buyer certainly loses that sum, and the seller certainly does not gain it, because—based on our starting point—the seller only receives enough to cover the high cost of production. I leave it to the protectionists <note>Those who support taxes on imports to protect domestic industries from foreign competition.</note> to draw their own conclusions from this.

IV. I have focused on this distinction between the conditions of production and the conditions of sale—a distinction the protectionists will likely call a contradiction <note>original: "paradoxical"</note>. I do this because it leads me to present an even stranger contradiction: If you truly want to equalize the conditions of production, you must allow for free trade <note>original: "leave exchange free"</note>.

"Now, really," people will say, "this is too much! You must be joking with us." Well, even if only out of curiosity, I beg the protectionists to follow my argument to its conclusion. It will not be long. Let's go back to my previous example.

Let us suppose for a moment that the average daily wage a French worker earns is one shilling. It follows that...

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that free trade, rather than tariffs, is the true equalizer of production conditions. He has just explained that while a tariff might equalize the price of a French and Portuguese orange, it does not equalize the actual labor required to produce them.</meta>

# TO EQUALIZE THE CONDITIONS OF PRODUCTION

It is undeniably true that to produce an orange directly in France, a full day’s work (or its equivalent) is required. Meanwhile, to produce the value needed to buy a Portuguese orange, only a twelfth of that day’s labor is necessary. This means exactly this: the sun does in Lisbon what human labor must do in Paris. Now, is it not very clear that if I can produce an orange—or, what amounts to the same thing, the means to buy one—with only a twelfth of a day’s labor, I am placed in exactly the same position as the Portuguese producer himself regarding this product? The only exception is the cost of shipping, which I must pay. It is certain, then, that freedom of trade equalizes the conditions of production, whether direct or indirect, as much as they can be equalized. It leaves no difference other than the inevitable cost of transport.

I will add that free trade also equalizes the conditions of enjoyment, satisfaction, and consumption. The protectionists never worry about these, yet they are the essential goal, since consumption is the end and object of all our industrial efforts. By virtue of free trade, we enjoy the sun of Portugal just like the Portuguese do. The inhabitants of Le Havre <note>A major French port city.</note> and the citizens of London are given access to all the mineral resources that nature bestowed on Newcastle <note>A city in England famous for its coal production.</note>, and on the same conditions.

V. Gentlemen of the protectionist school, you find me in a paradoxical mood, and I am inclined to go even further. I say, and I sincerely believe, that *if two countries are placed under unequal conditions of production, the one least favored by nature is the one that has the most to gain from free trade.* To prove this, I must deviate slightly from the usual style of a work like this. I will do so nonetheless, first because the entire question rests on this point, and also because it will give me a chance to explain an economic law of the highest importance. If understood correctly, this law seems capable of bringing back to the science of economics all those groups <note>Bastiat is likely referring to early socialist or utopian thinkers like Charles Fourier.</note> who, in our time, seek social harmony in a land of fantasies <note>original: "land of chimeras"</note> because they fail to discover it in nature. I refer to the law of consumption, which most economists have unfortunately neglected.

<vocab>free trade, protectionists, conditions of production, law of consumption, social harmony, Frédéric Bastiat</vocab>

<summary>Bastiat argues that free trade naturally equalizes the conditions of production and consumption between nations by allowing people to benefit from the natural advantages of other climates. He concludes by proposing the "paradox" that the countries least favored by nature actually stand to gain the most from free trade.</summary>
<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, free trade, protectionism, conditions of production, law of consumption, economic paradox, social harmony, international trade</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Bastiat’s discussion on equalizing production conditions to a fundamental principle of his economic theory: that the ultimate benefit or burden of all economic activity eventually shifts from the producer to the consumer.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

Consumption is the *end* goal and final cause of all economic phenomena; consequently, it is in consumption that we must expect to find their ultimate and definitive solution.

Nothing—whether favorable or unfavorable—remains permanently with the producer. The advantages that nature and society bestow upon them, as well as the inconveniences they may experience, glide past them, so to speak. They are absorbed and blended into the community, in so far as the community represents the consumers. This is an admirable law in both its cause and its effects; in my opinion, whoever succeeds in clearly describing it is entitled to say, "I have not passed through life without paying my debt to society."

Everything that favors the work of production is welcomed with joy by the producer, for its *immediate effect* is to place them in a position to offer greater service to the community and to demand from it greater pay <note>original: "remuneration"</note>. Conversely, every circumstance that slows or interrupts production causes the producer pain, for its *immediate effect* is to limit their services and, consequently, their pay. *Immediate* circumstances—whether fortunate or unfortunate—necessarily fall upon the producer, leaving them no choice but to accept the one and avoid <note>original: "eschew"</note> the other.

In the same way, when a worker succeeds in discovering an improved manufacturing process, the *immediate* profit from the improvement belongs to them. This was necessary to give their labor an intelligent direction, and it is just, because it is fair that an effort crowned with success should bring its reward along with it.

But I maintain that these good or bad effects, though permanent in their own nature, are not permanent as far as the producer is concerned. If they were, a principle of progressive and indefinite inequality would have been introduced among people. This is the reason why these good or evil effects are very soon absorbed into the general destiny of the human race.

How is this brought about? I shall show how it happens through some examples.

Let us go back to the thirteenth century. The people who then devoted themselves to the art of copying received for the...

<summary>Bastiat argues that while producers feel the immediate impact of economic changes, these effects are temporary and eventually pass on to the consumer. This "law" prevents permanent inequality by distributing the benefits of innovation and the burdens of hardship across the whole community.</summary>

### TO EQUALIZE THE CONDITIONS OF PRODUCTION

...service which they rendered, they received *pay <note>original: "remuneration"</note> regulated by the general rate of earnings.*\* Among them, one person emerged who discovered a way to quickly multiply copies of the same work. He invented printing.

At first, one man became wealthy, while many others were made poor. At first glance, as marvelous as the invention appears, we hesitate to decide whether it is harmful or beneficial. It seems to introduce an unlimited <note>original: "indefinite"</note> element of inequality into the world, as I have said. Gutenberg <note>original: "Guttemberg"</note> profits from his invention and expands it further using those profits until he has ruined all the scribes <note>original: "copyists"</note>. As for the public, acting as consumers, they gain little; Gutenberg takes care not to lower the price of his books any more than is necessary to undersell his rivals.

However, the same intelligence that brought harmony to the movement of the stars has also implanted it within the internal workings of society. We will see the economic advantages of the invention once it stops being private property and becomes the permanent common inheritance <note>original: "patrimony"</note> of the masses.

Eventually, the invention becomes known. Gutenberg is no longer the only printer; others imitate him. Initially, their profits are large. They are rewarded for being the first to adopt the invention; this is only right, as this higher pay <note>original: "remuneration"</note> was necessary to encourage them to contribute <note>original: "concur in"</note> to the great final result that is approaching. They earn a great deal, but less than the inventor, because *competition* now begins to work. The price of books continues to fall. 

The imitators' profits keep shrinking as the invention gets older—that is to say, as the act of imitating it becomes less praiseworthy <note>original: "meritorious"</note>. . . . Eventually, this new branch of industry reaches its normal state. In other words, the printers' pay stops being exceptionally high and, like that of the scribes before them, becomes *regulated by the ordinary rate of earnings.* Here we have production, as

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the financial benefits of an invention (like the printing press) eventually shift from the individual inventor to the public at large, resulting in lower prices and a shared "inheritance" of progress.</meta>

### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

Eventually, the price of books is brought back to the point from which it started <note>This refers to the previous page's argument that competition eventually forces prices down to the cost of labor alone.</note>. And yet, the invention is no less of an achievement; the savings in time, labor, and effort required to produce a specific result—that is, to produce a set number of copies—are still achieved. But how does this benefit show itself? It shows in the cheapness of books. And to whose profit? To the profit of the consumer, of society, and of the human race. The printers, who from then on have no exceptional merit, no longer receive exceptional pay <note>original: "remuneration"</note>. As men, and as consumers, they undoubtedly participate in the advantages which the invention has bestowed upon the community. But that is all. As printers, and as producers, they have returned to the ordinary condition of all other producers in the country. Society pays them for their labor, and not for the usefulness of the invention. The invention itself has become the common and free inheritance <note>original: "gratuitous heritage"</note> of mankind at large.

I admit that the wisdom and the beauty of these laws call forth my admiration and respect. I see in them **Saint-Simonianism** <note>A movement founded by Henri de Saint-Simon (1760–1825) that argued for a society organized by merit and productive work.</note>: *To each according to his capacity; to each capacity according to its works.* I see in them **communism** <note>In this 19th-century context, the author is referring to the "communal" sharing of benefits through lower prices, rather than the political ideology of state-controlled property.</note>; that is, the tendency of products to become the *common heritage* of men. But this is a Saint-Simonianism and a communism regulated by infinite foresight <note>original: "prescience"</note>, and not abandoned to the frailties, the passions, and the unpredictable whims <note>original: "arbitrary will"</note> of men.

What I have said of the art of printing may be affirmed of all the instruments of labor, from the nail and the hammer to the locomotive and the electric telegraph. Society gains possession of all through its more abundant consumption, and *it enjoys all for free* <note>original: "gratuitously"</note>. This is because the effect of inventions and discoveries is to reduce the price of commodities; and all that part of the price which has been eliminated <note>original: "annihilated"</note>, and which represents the share the invention has in production, clearly renders the product free to that extent. All that remains to be paid for is the human labor—the immediate labor—and it is paid for without reference to the result of the invention, at least once that invention has passed through the cycle I have just described—the cycle which it is designed to go through. For example, I send for a workman to come to my house; he comes and brings his saw with him. I pay him two shillings <note>A British coin worth 1/10th of a pound; in the 1840s, this was a standard daily wage for a laborer.</note> for his day's work, and he saws me twenty-five boards. Had the saw not been invented, he...

# TO EQUALIZE THE CONDITIONS OF PRODUCTION

...would probably not have been able to provide me with one, and I would have had to pay him the same wages for his day’s work. The *utility* <note>In economics, "utility" refers to the usefulness or the capacity of a good or service to satisfy a human want.</note> produced by the saw is then, as far as I am concerned, a free gift of nature; or rather, it is a part of that inheritance which, *along with all my fellow humans*, I have received from my ancestors. I have two workmen in my field. One handles the plow, the other the spade. The result of their labor is very different, but their day’s wages are the same, because the pay <note>original: "remuneration"</note> is not proportioned to the utility produced, but to the effort—the labor—which is required.

I ask for the reader’s patience, and beg him to believe that I have not lost sight of free trade. Let him only have the goodness to remember the conclusion I have reached: *Pay is not in proportion to the utility which the producer brings to market, but to his labor.**

I have drawn my illustrations so far from human inventions. Let us now turn our attention to natural advantages.

In every branch of production, nature and man work together. But the portion of utility which nature contributes is always free. It is only the portion of utility which human labor contributes that is subject to exchange and, consequently, to payment. This payment varies, no doubt, very much in proportion to the intensity of the labor, its skill, its speed, its suitability, the demand for it, the temporary absence of competition <note>original: "rivalry"</note>, and so on. But it is nonetheless true, in principle, that the cooperation of natural laws, which are common to everyone, counts for nothing in the price of the product.

We do not pay for the air we breathe, although it is so *useful* to us that without it we could not live for two minutes. Nevertheless, we do not pay for it because nature provides it to us without the help of human labor. But if, for example, we should desire to separate one of the gases of which it is composed to conduct an experiment, we must make an effort; or if we wish someone else to make that effort for us, we must trade an equivalent amount of effort for them, even though we...

---

* It is true that labor does not receive a uniform payment. It may be more or less intense, dangerous, skilled, etc. Competition settles the usual or current price in each field—and this is the fluctuating price of which I speak.

<summary>Bastiat argues that market prices are determined by human effort (labor) rather than the total usefulness (utility) of a product, because the contributions of nature and past inventions are free "gifts" to society.</summary>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that we do not pay for the "utility" or usefulness provided by nature, but only for the human effort required to harness it. He uses examples like oxygen, coal, and water to show that market prices reflect labor, not the inherent value of natural gifts.</meta>

-> ECONOMIC SOPHISMS <-

...might have embodied it in another product. From this, we see that the pain, effort, and exertion involved are what we actually trade <note>original: "subjects of exchange"</note>. I do not pay for oxygen gas itself, since it is available to me everywhere for free. Instead, I pay for the work required to isolate <note>original: "disengage"</note> it—work that was saved for me and must be paid for <note>original: "recompensed"</note>. Will someone say that there are other things to be paid for, such as materials and equipment? Even then, in paying for those, I am paying for labor. The price of the coal used, for example, represents the work needed to mine it and transport it to the place where it will be used.

We do not pay for the light of the sun because it is a gift of nature. But we pay for gas, candles <note>original: "tallow"</note>, oil, and wax because human work must be paid for <note>original: "remunerated"</note>. It should be noted that in these cases, the payment is based not on how useful the product is, but on the amount of work required. This is why one type of artificial light might be much brighter yet cost us less: it simply takes less human labor to produce it.

If the porter who carries water to my house were paid based on the *total usefulness* <note>original: "absolute utility"</note> of water, all my wealth would not be enough to pay him. But I pay him based on the effort he makes. If he charges too much, others will do the work, or if necessary, I will do it myself. In truth, we are not bargaining over the water, but over the labor of carrying it. This perspective is so important, and the conclusions I will draw from it shed so much light on the question of free trade <note>original: "freedom of international exchanges"</note>, that I find it necessary to explain it with further examples.

The nutrition <note>original: "alimentary substance"</note> found in potatoes is not very expensive because we can produce a large amount of it with relatively little effort. We pay more for wheat because growing it requires more human labor. It is obvious that if nature did as much for wheat as it does for potatoes, the price of both would be nearly the same. It is impossible for a wheat farmer to permanently earn much more than a potato farmer because the law of competition would prevent it.

If, by some lucky miracle, the fertility of all farmland increased, it would not be the farmer <note>original: "agriculturist"</note>, but the

<summary>Bastiat explains that economic value and prices are based on human labor rather than the inherent utility of natural resources. Using examples like water and coal, he argues that consumers pay for the effort of extraction and delivery, while nature's contributions remain free.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that market prices are determined by the amount of human labor required to produce a good, rather than the inherent usefulness provided by nature. He explains that when nature makes production easier, the benefit is passed to the consumer through lower prices due to competition.</meta>

### TO EQUALIZE THE CONDITIONS OF PRODUCTION.

...consumer, who would benefit from that phenomenon, as it would result in abundance and cheapness. There would be less labor incorporated in each quarter <note>original: "quarter" — a traditional English unit of volume for grain, equal to 8 bushels</note> of corn, and the farmer <note>original: "cultivator"</note> could exchange it only for a smaller amount of labor worked up in some other product. If, on the other hand, the fertility of the soil suddenly diminished, nature's part in the process of production would be less, while the role of human labor would be greater, and the product would become more expensive. I am, then, justified in saying that it is in consumption—the human element—that all economic phenomena eventually find their resolution. A person who fails to see them in this light, who does not follow them to their ultimate effects but stops short at *immediate* results and views them only from the *producer's* standpoint, can no more be considered an economist than a man who prescribes a medicinal drink <note>original: "draught"</note> and, instead of watching its effect on the patient’s entire system, only asks how it affected the mouth and throat, could be considered a physician.

Tropical regions are very favorably situated for the production of sugar and coffee. This means that nature does a great part of the work and leaves little for human labor to do. But who reaps the advantage of this generosity of nature? It is not the producing countries, for competition causes the price to barely cover the cost of labor. It is the human race that reaps the benefit, for the result of nature's generosity is cheapness, and cheapness benefits everyone.

Suppose there is a temperate region where coal and iron ore are found on the surface of the ground, where one only has to stoop down to get them. I allow that, in the first instance, the inhabitants would profit by this happy circumstance. But competition would soon intervene, and the price of coal and iron ore would continue falling until the gift of nature became free to all, and then only the human labor employed would be paid for according to the general rate of earnings.

Thus the generosity of nature, like improvements in the processes of production, is—or continually tends to become—under the law of competition, the common and free inheritance <note>original: "gratuitous patrimony"</note> of consumers, of the masses, and of mankind in general. Therefore, the countries which do not possess these advantages have everything...

<summary>Bastiat argues that the benefits of natural resources and fertile land are passed to the consumer in the form of lower prices. He concludes that true economic analysis must focus on the ultimate effect on the consumer rather than the immediate interests of the producer.</summary>
<keywords>consumption, production, competition, labor theory of value, natural resources, economic theory, Bastiat, consumer benefits</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that when a country has natural advantages (like a better climate), the benefit of those advantages is passed on to the consumer in the form of lower prices, rather than staying with the producer as profit.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

...to gain by exchanging their products with those countries which possess them <note>The author refers to countries with natural advantages like superior soil or climate.</note>, because the subject of exchange is *labor*, regardless of the natural benefits <note>original: "natural utilities" — the useful properties provided for free by nature, such as heat from the sun or fertility of the soil</note> worked into that labor. The countries that have incorporated the greatest amount of these *natural benefits* into a given amount of their labor are evidently the most favored countries. Their products, which represent the least amount of human labor, are the least profitable for the producer; in other words, they are *cheaper*. If the entire generosity of nature results in *cheapness*, it is clearly not the producing country, but the consuming country, which reaps the benefit.

Hence, we see the enormous absurdity of consuming countries that reject products for the very reason that they are cheap. It is as if they said, "We want nothing that nature gives us for free. You ask me for an effort equal to two in exchange for a product which I cannot create without an effort equal to four; you can make that effort because, in your case, nature does half the work. So be it; I reject your offer, and I shall wait until your climate, having become more harsh <note>original: "inclement"</note>, forces you to demand from me an effort equal to four, so that I may trade with you *on equal terms*."

Country A is a favored country. Country B is a country to which nature has been less bountiful. I maintain that exchange benefits both, but benefits Country B especially; because exchange is not an exchange of *utility for utility*, but of *value for value*. Now, Country A includes a *greater amount of utility within the same value*, seeing that the utility <note>In this context, "utility" refers to the total usefulness of a product, while "value" refers to the human labor required to produce it.</note> of a product includes what nature has put there as well as what labor has put there, while value includes only what labor has put there. Therefore, Country B makes quite an advantageous bargain. In paying the producer of Country A for his labor only, it receives a greater amount of natural utility as a bonus than it has given in return.

This enables us to lay down the general rule: Exchange is a barter of *values*. Under the action of competition, value is made to represent labor; therefore, exchange becomes a barter of equal labor. What nature has contributed to the products exchanged is, on both sides, given *freely and as a bonus*. From this, it follows necessarily that exchanges made with the countries most favored by nature are the most advantageous.

<summary>Bastiat argues that international trade is an exchange of human labor (value) rather than natural resources (utility). Consequently, consumers in less-favored nations benefit most when they trade with nations where nature does more of the work, as they receive nature's "gifts" for free through lower prices.</summary>
<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, free trade, labor theory of value, natural utility, competition, economic advantage, protectionism</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page concludes Bastiat’s discussion on how competition levels the playing field for consumers and introduces a new argument regarding how taxes are used to justify protectionism.</meta>

# TO EQUALIZE THE CONDITIONS OF PRODUCTION

The theory I have tried to outline in this chapter requires much more development. I have only touched upon it as far as it relates to my subject of free trade. But perhaps the attentive reader has noticed in it the fertile seed which, when fully grown, will not only smother protectionism but also *Fourierism* <note>original: "Fourierisme" — a system of social reform proposed by Charles Fourier (1772–1837) involving self-sufficient cooperatives</note>, *Saint-Simonianism* <note>original: "Saint-Simonianisme" — a social movement that advocated for a society led by experts and industrialists for the benefit of the poor</note>, *communism*, and all those schools of thought that aim to exclude the law of COMPETITION from the government of the world. Regarded from the producer’s point of view, competition no doubt frequently clashes with our *immediate* and individual interests. However, if we change our perspective and look at industry in general—at universal prosperity, or in a word, at *consumption*—we shall find that competition in the moral world plays the same part that equilibrium <note>balance</note> does in the material world. It is the foundation of true communism and true socialism—of that equality of status and happiness so much desired today. If so many sincere writers and well-meaning reformers seek *arbitrary* <note>government-mandated or forced</note> solutions, it is because they do not understand *liberty*.*

---

### V.

## OUR PRODUCTS ARE BURDENED WITH TAXES.

We have here again the same sophism <note>a clever but false argument</note>. We demand that foreign products should be taxed to neutralize the effect of the taxes that weigh down our own national products. The goal, then, is still to equalize the conditions of production. We—

* The theory sketched in this chapter is the same one that, four years later, was developed in the *Harmonies Économiques* <note>original: "Economic Harmonies"</note>. This includes the idea that payment is reserved exclusively for human labor; the free nature of natural forces; the progressive mastery of these forces for the profit of mankind (making them common property); the increase of general well-being; and the tendency toward the relative equalization of conditions. We recognize here the essential elements of the most important of all of Bastiat’s works.—EDITOR.

<summary>Bastiat argues that while competition may seem harmful to individual producers, it is essential for consumer prosperity and social equality. He then introduces the common "sophism" that domestic taxes justify imposing tariffs on foreign goods to keep competition "fair."</summary>

<keywords>competition, consumption, free trade, socialism, Fourierism, Saint-Simonianism, Bastiat, Economic Harmonies, taxes, protectionism, production costs</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of protectionism, specifically addressing the argument that national industries need tariffs to compensate for high domestic taxes.</meta>

->### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.<-

I have only a word to say, and it is this: that a tax is an artificial obstacle which produces exactly the same result as a natural obstacle; its effect is to increase prices. If this increase reaches a point that makes it a greater loss to produce the product for ourselves than to obtain it from abroad by producing something of equal value to trade for it, then *leave things alone* <note>original: "laissez faire"</note>. Of two evils, private interest will do well to choose the lesser. I might, then, simply refer the reader to the previous demonstration; but the fallacy <note>original: "sophism"</note> which we must combat here occurs so frequently in the complaints and demands—I might even say the challenges—of the protectionist school, that it deserves a special discussion.

If the question relates to one of those exceptional taxes which are imposed on specific products, I grant readily that it is reasonable to impose the same duty on the foreign product. For example, it would be absurd to exempt foreign salt from a tax; not that France would lose anything economically by doing so, but rather the opposite. Let them say what they will, principles are always the same; and France would gain by the exemption just as she must always gain by removing a natural or artificial obstacle. But in this instance, the obstacle has been put in place for the purpose of raising revenue. These revenue goals must be met; and were foreign salt sold in our market tax-free, the Treasury would lose its hundred million francs (four million pounds sterling) and would have to raise that sum from some other source. There would be an obvious inconsistency in creating an obstacle to raise money and then failing to achieve that objective. It might have been better to have used another tax from the start instead of the tax on French salt. But I admit that there are certain circumstances in which a tax may be laid on foreign commodities, provided it is not *protective*, but intended for the Treasury <note>original: "fiscal"</note>.

But to claim that a nation—simply because she is subjected to heavier taxes than her neighbors—should protect herself with tariffs against the competition of her rivals, is a fallacy, and it is this fallacy which I intend to attack.

I have said more than once that I propose only to explain the theory and reveal, as far as possible, the sources of protectionist errors. Had I intended to raise a controversy, I

<summary>Bastiat argues that while specific taxes for government revenue (fiscal taxes) on foreign goods may be necessary, using high national taxes as an excuse for protective tariffs is an economic fallacy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of the "taxation argument" for protectionism, focusing on how public spending on infrastructure and security can actually benefit national industry rather than hindering it.</meta>

# OUR PRODUCTS ARE BURDENED WITH TAXES.

One should have asked the protectionists why they direct their tariffs chiefly against England and Belgium, the most heavily taxed countries in the world. Am I not justified in viewing their argument as nothing more than a pretext? But I am not one of those who believe that people are protectionists out of pure self-interest rather than conviction. The doctrine of protection is too popular not to be sincere. If the majority had faith in liberty, we would be free. Undoubtedly, self-interest makes our tariffs so heavy, but conviction is at the root of it. "The will," says Pascal, "is one of the principal organs of belief." Nevertheless, the belief exists, even though it is rooted in the will and in the subtle suggestions of selfishness <note>original: "egotism"</note>.

Let us return to the fallacy <note>original: "sophism"</note> based on taxation.

The State may make a good or a bad use of the taxes it collects <note>original: "levies"</note>. When it provides the public with services that are equal in value to the money it receives, it makes good use of them. When it wastes its revenue without providing any service in return, it makes a bad use of them.

In the first case, to claim that taxes place the country paying them at a disadvantage in production compared to a country that is exempt is a fallacy. We pay twenty million francs for the justice system and law enforcement <note>original: "justice and police"</note>; but in return, we have the security they provide and the time they save us. It is very likely that production is neither easier nor more active in those countries—if any such exist—where the people must take the business of justice and policing into their own hands. We pay many hundreds of millions of francs for roads, bridges, harbors, and railways. This is true; but then we enjoy the benefits of these roads, bridges, harbors, and railways. Whether we made a good or a bad deal in building them, it cannot be said that they make us inferior to other nations who do not, it is true, support a budget for public works, but who also have no public works. And this explains why, while accusing taxation of being a cause of industrial inferiority, we direct our tariffs especially against those countries which are the most heavily taxed. Their taxes, when well-employed, have improved <note>original: "ameliorated"</note> rather than worsened the conditions of production in those countries. Thus, we are continually arriving at the conclusion that pro-

<summary>Bastiat argues that taxes spent on essential public services like infrastructure and the legal system actually improve a nation's productive capacity, debunking the protectionist claim that high taxes necessitate tariffs.</summary>
<keywords>taxation, protectionism, tariffs, public works, infrastructure, economic fallacies, Frédéric Bastiat, Blaise Pascal</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of the "taxation argument" for protectionism, moving from the benefits of public services to the specific fallacy that high taxes justify high tariffs.</meta>

# ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "sophisms"</note>

Protectionist fallacies are not only untrue, but are the very reverse of the truth.*

If taxes are unproductive, get rid of them if you can; but surely the strangest way to cancel out their effect is to add private taxes to public ones. What a fine trade-off! You tell us that the State taxes are too high, and you give that as a reason why we should tax each other!

A protective duty <term>protective duty: a tax on imported goods designed to protect domestic industries from foreign competition</term> is a tax aimed at a foreign product; but we must never forget that it ultimately falls back on the domestic consumer. Now, the consumer is the taxpayer. The pleasant message you address to him is this: “Because your taxes are heavy, we are raising the price of everything you buy; because the State takes one part of your income, we are handing over another part to the monopolist <term>monopolist: here, a domestic producer granted an unfair advantage through trade restrictions</term>.”

But let us look a little deeper into this fallacy <note>original: "sophism"</note>, which is so popular with our lawmakers. The extraordinary thing is that it is the very same people who support unproductive taxes who also blame those taxes for our industrial weakness—and then use that weakness as an excuse for imposing further taxes and restrictions.

It seems clear to me that the nature and effects of protectionism would not change even if the State were to collect a direct tax and then distribute that money afterward as subsidies and compensation <note>original: "premiums and indemnities"</note> to the favored branches of industry.

Suppose that while foreign iron cannot be sold in our market for less than eight francs, French iron cannot be sold for less than twelve francs.

In this scenario, there are two ways in which the State can guarantee the home market for the domestic producer.

The first method is to place a duty of five francs on foreign iron. It is clear that this duty would exclude it, since it could no longer be sold for less than thirteen francs (eight francs for the cost price, plus five francs for the tax). At that price, it would be driven out of the market by French iron, the price of which we assume to be only twelve francs. In this case, the purchaser—the consumer—would bear the entire cost of the protection.

Alternatively, the State might collect a tax of five francs from...

<summary>Bastiat argues that using high national taxes as a reason to implement protective tariffs is illogical, as it effectively forces consumers to pay twice: once to the state and once to domestic monopolies. He uses the example of the iron trade to show how tariffs function as a hidden subsidy paid for by the public.</summary>

# OUR PRODUCTS ARE BURDENED WITH TAXES

the public, and give the proceeds as a subsidy <note>original: "premium"</note> to the iron-master. The protective effect would be the same. Foreign iron would in this case be equally excluded; for our iron-master can now sell his iron at seven francs, which, with the five-franc subsidy, would make up to him the profitable <note>original: "remunerative"</note> price of twelve francs. But with home iron at seven francs, the foreigner could not sell his for eight, which by our assumption is his lowest profitable price.

Between these two modes of going to work, I can see only one difference. The principle is the same; the effect is the same; but in the one, certain individuals pay the price of protection; in the other, it is paid for by the nation at large.

I frankly admit my preference for the second method. It appears to me more just, more economical, and more honorable:
* **More just**, because if society desires to give gifts <note>original: "largesses"</note> to some of its members, everyone should contribute; 
* **More economical**, because it would save much expense in collecting taxes and get us rid of many trade restrictions; 
* **More honorable**, because the public would then see clearly the nature of the operation and act accordingly.

But if the protectionist system had taken this form, it would have been laughable to hear men say:

> “We pay heavy taxes for the army, for the navy, for the administration of justice, for public works, for the university, the public debt, and so on—in all exceeding a billion <note>original: "milliard" (at the time, approximately £40,000,000 sterling)</note>. For this reason, the State should take another billion from us to relieve these poor iron-masters, these poor shareholders in the coal mines of Anzin <note>The Anzin Mining Company was one of the first major industrial coal operations in France</note>, these unfortunate owners of forests, and these useful men who supply us with codfish.”

Look at the subject closely, and you will be satisfied that this is the true meaning and effect of the fallacy <note>original: "sophism"</note> we are combating. It is all in vain; you cannot *give money* to some members of the community without taking it from others. If you desire to ruin the taxpayer, you may do so. But at least do not mock <note>original: "banter"</note> him by saying:

> “In order to compensate your losses, I am taking from you again as much as I have taken from you already.”

To expose fully all that is false in this fallacy would be an endless work. I shall confine myself to three observations.

You assert that the country is overburdened with taxes,

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of the "taxation argument" for protectionism. He argues that using tariffs to "offset" the high taxes paid by domestic industries does not actually lower the tax burden; it merely shifts it onto the public while adding the extra cost of higher prices.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

And on this fact, you base an argument for the protection <term>protection: the use of tariffs or duties to favor domestic industries over foreign competitors</term> of certain branches of industry. But we must pay these taxes despite protection. If, then, a particular industry comes forward and says, "I share in the payment of taxes; that raises the production cost of my products, and I demand that a protective duty should also raise their selling price," what does such a demand actually mean? It simply means that the tax should be shifted onto the rest of the community. The goal is to be reimbursed for the tax through a rise in prices. 

However, since the Treasury requires the full amount of all taxes, and since the public has to pay the higher prices, it follows that the people must bear not only their own share of taxation but also the share of the specific industry being protected. But you will say, "We intend to protect everybody." I answer, first, that this is impossible; and second, that even if it were possible, there would be no relief. I would pay for you, and you would pay for me, but the tax would still have to be paid just the same.

You are thus the victims of an illusion. First, you choose to pay taxes so that you may have an army, a navy, a church, a university, judges, highways, and so on. Then, you wish to free first one industry from taxation, then a second, then a third, always throwing the burden back onto the masses. You do nothing more than create endless complications, with no result other than the complications themselves. If you could show me that a price increase caused by protectionism falls upon the foreigner, I might find something plausible <note>original: "specious"</note> in your argument. But if it is true that the public pays the tax before your law, and that after the law is passed it pays for both the protection and the tax into the bargain, I truly cannot see what is gained by it.

In fact, I go further: I maintain that the heavier our taxes are, the more we should hurry to open our ports and our borders to foreigners who are less heavily taxed than ourselves. Why? In order to shift a greater share of our burden onto them. Is it not an undisputed rule in economics <note>original: "political economy"</note> that taxes ultimately fall on the consumer? Therefore, the more our trade <note>original: "exchanges"</note> increases, the more foreign consumers will reimburse us for the taxes built into and worked...

<summary>Bastiat argues that protective tariffs intended to offset domestic taxes merely shift the tax burden onto the general public, creating a cycle of rising prices with no net benefit. He suggests that high taxes are actually a reason to increase foreign trade to shift the tax burden onto external consumers.</summary>

In short, have you never asked yourselves whether these heavy burdens, on which you base your argument for a system of trade bans <note>original: "prohibitory régime"</note>, are not caused by that very system? If trade were free, what use would you have for your great standing armies and powerful navies? . . . . But this belongs to the field of politics.

### VI.

## BALANCE OF TRADE.

Our opponents have adopted tactics that are rather embarrassing. When we establish our doctrine, they admit it with the greatest possible respect. When we attack their principle, they abandon it with the best grace in the world. They demand only one thing—that our doctrine, which they hold to be true, should remain confined to books, and that their principle, which they acknowledge to be flawed <note>original: "vicious"</note>, should reign supreme in practical legislation. Leave the management of tariffs <term>tariffs: taxes imposed by a government on imported goods</term> to them, and they will give up all dispute with you in the realm of theory.

“Assuredly,” said Monsieur Gauthier de Rumilly on a recent occasion, “no one wishes to revive the outdated theories of the balance of trade.” You are very right, Monsieur Gauthier, but please remember that it is not enough to give a passing insult to an error and then immediately afterward, for two hours straight, reason as if that error were the truth.

Let me speak of Monsieur Lestiboudois. Here we have a consistent reasoner, a logical debater. There is nothing in his conclusions that cannot be found in his premises. He asks—

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionism creates the very military costs used to justify it and begins a critique of the "balance of trade" theory, noting that his opponents often concede the theory but ignore it in practice.</summary>
<keywords>standing armies, free trade, balance of trade, Gauthier de Rumilly, Lestiboudois, protectionism, economic theory vs practice</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the "Balance of Trade" theory. Bastiat contrasts the inconsistent arguments of Monsieur Gauthier de Rumilly (who admits theory is one thing and practice another) with the consistent, though flawed, logic of Monsieur Lestiboudois.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

He does nothing in practice that he does not justify in theory. His principle may be false; that is open to question. But, at any rate, he has a principle. He believes, and he proclaims it aloud, that if France gives ten in order to receive fifteen, she loses five. It follows, of course, that he supports laws which are in keeping with this view of the subject.

> "The important thing to watch," he says, "is that the amount of our imports goes on increasing and exceeds the amount of our exports—that is to say, France every year purchases more foreign products and sells less of her own. Figures prove this. What do we see? In 1842, imports exceeded exports by 200 million francs. These facts appear to prove in the clearest manner that national industry is *not sufficiently protected*, that we depend upon foreign labor for our supplies, and that the competition of our rivals *oppresses our industry*. The current law seems to acknowledge a fact—though economists claim it is false—that when we buy, we must necessarily sell a matching amount of goods. It is evident that we can purchase not only with our usual products, not just with our revenue, and not only with the results of regular labor, but with our capital—with products which have been accumulated and stored up, specifically those intended for reproduction. That is to say, we may spend or waste the proceeds of earlier savings <note>original: "anterior economies"</note>; we may impoverish ourselves and proceed on the road to ruin, entirely consuming the national capital. *This is exactly what we are doing. Every year we give away 200 million francs to foreigners.*"

Well, here is a man with whom we can reach an understanding. There is no hypocrisy in this language. The doctrine of the **balance of trade** <term>balance of trade: the difference in value between a country's imports and exports; traditionally, a "favorable" balance meant exporting more than importing</term> is openly declared. He believes France imports 200 million more than she exports, and therefore we lose 200 million a year. And what is the remedy? To place restrictions on imports. The conclusion is logically sound based on his premise.

It is with Monsieur Lestiboudois, then, that we must argue, for how can we argue with Monsieur Gauthier? If you tell Gauthier that the balance of trade theory is an error, he replies that that was what he said at the beginning. If you say that the balance of trade is a truth, he will reply that that is what he proves in his conclusions.

<summary>Bastiat examines the arguments of M. Lestiboudois, who consistently (if incorrectly) argues that a trade deficit—where imports exceed exports—represents a direct loss of national wealth and capital.</summary>
<keywords>Balance of Trade, imports, exports, M. Lestiboudois, M. Gauthier de Rumilly, protectionism, national capital, trade deficit</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the "Balance of Trade" theory. Bastiat moves from discussing the theory's presence in the legislature to testing it against the practical reality of commercial accounting, using a specific example of a merchant's shipping venture.</meta>

### BALANCE OF TRADE

The school of economists will no doubt blame me for arguing with Monsieur Lestiboudois. They will say that attacking the "balance of trade" is like fighting a windmill <note>The author refers to "tilting at windmills," a reference to Don Quixote attacking imaginary enemies.</note>.

But be careful. The doctrine of the balance of trade is not as outdated, sick, or dead as Monsieur Gauthier would suggest. After all, the entire Chamber—including Monsieur Gauthier himself—has supported Monsieur Lestiboudois's theory through their votes.

I will not bore the reader by trying to dissect that theory; instead, I will simply test it against the facts.

We are constantly told that our principles are only valid in theory. But tell me, gentlemen, do you consider the ledgers of merchants to be valid in practice? It seems to me that if anything in the world should carry practical weight regarding profit and loss, it is commercial accounts. Have all the merchants in the world agreed for centuries to keep their books in a way that shows profits as losses and losses as profits? It might be so, but I would much rather conclude that Monsieur Lestiboudois is a bad economist.

Now, a merchant of my acquaintance had two transactions with very different results. I was curious to compare the accounting books of his office with the records of the Customs office, as interpreted by Monsieur Lestiboudois to the satisfaction of our six hundred lawmakers.

Monsieur T. sent a ship from Le Havre to the United States with a cargo of French goods—mostly those known as *luxury items from Paris* <note>original: "articles de Paris," referring to high-end fashion, jewelry, and decorative items for which Paris was famous</note>—worth 200,000 francs. This was the value declared at the Customs office. When the cargo arrived in New Orleans, it was charged 10 percent for freight and 30 percent for duty, totaling 280,000 francs. It was sold at a 20 percent profit (40,000 francs), bringing the total to 320,000 francs. The agent then invested this amount in cotton. This cotton still had to cover costs for freight, insurance, and commissions, etc., adding another 10 percent. So, when the new cargo arrived back at Le Havre, it had cost 352,000 francs, which was the figure entered in the Customs office books. Finally, Monsieur T. made a 20 percent profit on this return shipment, or 70,400 francs; in other words, the cotton was sold for 422,400 francs.

<summary>Bastiat argues that the "Balance of Trade" theory—which views an excess of imports over exports as a national loss—is still influential among lawmakers despite being logically flawed. He introduces a practical example of a merchant's profitable trade to show how the Customs office would mistakenly record a successful venture as a loss for the country.</summary>

<keywords>balance of trade, protectionism, Monsieur Lestiboudois, commercial accounting, imports and exports, Le Havre, New Orleans, trade deficit, profit and loss</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of Monsieur Lestiboudois and the "balance of trade" theory. Having established the setup of a merchant’s shipping venture on the previous page, Bastiat now demonstrates how the same transaction appears as a "loss" to the government but a "profit" to the merchant, and vice versa in the event of a shipwreck.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

If Monsieur <note>original: "M.", the French abbreviation for Monsieur (Mister).</note> Lestiboudois desires it, I shall send him an extract from the books of Monsieur T. He will see there, credited to the *profit and loss account*—that is to say, as profits—two entries: one of 40,000 francs and another of 70,400 francs. Monsieur T. is very sure that his accounts are accurate.

And yet, what do the Customs <note>original: "Customhouse"</note> books tell Monsieur Lestiboudois regarding this transaction? They tell him simply that France exported 200,000 francs’ worth of goods and imported 352,000 francs’ worth. From this, the honorable deputy concludes, "*that she had expended and dissipated the profits of her previous savings <note>original: "anterior economies"</note>, that she is impoverishing herself, that she is on the high road to ruin, and has given away 152,000 francs of her capital to foreigners.*"

Some time afterward, Monsieur T. dispatched another vessel with a cargo also valued at 200,000 francs, composed of the products of our native industry. This unfortunate ship was lost in a storm after leaving the harbor, and all Monsieur T. had to do was make two short entries in his books to this effect:

> "*Miscellaneous goods owed to X, 200,000 francs, for purchases of different commodities dispatched by the ship N.*"
>
> "*Profit and loss owed for miscellaneous goods, 200,000 francs, in consequence of the definitive and total loss of the cargo.*"

At the same time, the Customs books recorded an entry of 200,000 francs in the list of *exports*; and as there was no corresponding entry to make in the list of *imports*, it follows that Monsieur Lestiboudois and the Chamber will see in this shipwreck a *clear and net profit* for France of 200,000 francs.

There is still another inference to be drawn from this: according to the theory of the balance of trade, France has a very simple means of doubling her capital at any moment. It is enough to pass goods through the Customs office and then throw them into the sea. In this case, the exports will represent the amount of her capital, the imports will be *nothing* <note>original: "nil"</note>—even impossible—and we shall gain everything that the sea swallows up.

"This is a joke," the protectionists <note>Economic thinkers who support high tariffs and restrictions on foreign trade to protect domestic industries.</note> will say. "It is impossible we could say such absurdities." You do say them, however, and, what is more, you act upon them.

<summary>Bastiat uses a hypothetical merchant's ledger to show that the "balance of trade" theory absurdly views a profitable trade as a national loss and a total shipwreck as a national gain. He argues that if the theory were true, a nation could grow wealthy simply by exporting goods and sinking them at sea.</summary>
<keywords>balance of trade, protectionism, exports, imports, capital, accounting, Monsieur Lestiboudois, trade deficit, economic logic</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Bastiat’s critique of the "balance of trade" theory, demonstrating that an excess of imports over exports actually represents national profit. It then begins the "Candlemakers' Petition," one of the most famous satirical arguments in economic history, which mocks protectionism by asking the government to block out the sun to protect the lighting industry.</meta>

# BALANCE OF TRADE.

them, and impose them on your fellow-citizens to the utmost of your power.

The truth is, it would be necessary to take the balance of trade in reverse <note>original: "au rebours"</note> and calculate the national profits from foreign trade by the excess of imports over exports. This excess, after deducting costs, constitutes the real profit. But this theory, which is true, leads directly to free trade. I offer this to you, gentlemen, as I do all the theories in the preceding chapters. Exaggerate it as much as you please—it has nothing to fear from that test. Suppose, if it amuses you, that the foreigner floods us with all kinds of useful goods <note>original: "commodities"</note> without asking anything in return, that our imports are *infinite* and exports *zero* <note>original: "nil"</note>, I challenge <note>original: "defy"</note> you to prove to me that we should be poorer because of it.

## VII.

# PETITION

### OF THE MANUFACTURERS OF CANDLES, WAX-LIGHTS, LAMPS, CANDLESTICKS, STREET LAMPS, SNUFFERS, EXTINGUISHERS, AND OF THE PRODUCERS OF OIL, TALLOW, RESIN <note>original: "rosin"</note>, ALCOHOL, AND, GENERALLY, OF EVERYTHING CONNECTED WITH LIGHTING.

->To THE MEMBERS <note>original: "Messieurs the Members"</note> OF THE CHAMBER OF DEPUTIES.<-

**GENTLEMEN**, — You are on the right track. You reject abstract theories, and have little regard for cheapness and abundance. Your chief concern is the interest of the producer. You desire to protect <note>original: "emancipate"</note> him from foreign competition, and reserve the *national market for national industry*.

We are about to offer you an admirable opportunity for applying your—what shall we call it? Your theory? No; nothing is more deceptive than theory. Your doctrine? Your system? Your principle? But you dislike doctrines, you abhor systems, and as for principles, you deny that there are any in social economics <note>original: "social economy"</note>.

<summary>Bastiat concludes that national wealth is increased by importing more than is exported and introduces a satirical petition from candlemakers asking for protection against the sun's competition.</summary>
