--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Candlemakers' Petition," a satirical letter addressed to the French Chamber of Deputies. Having established that the government favors producers over consumers, the petitioners now name their greatest foreign competitor: the sun.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

...economy: we shall say, then, your practice—your practice without theory and without principle.

We are suffering from the intolerable competition of a foreign rival who is placed, it seems, in a condition so far superior to ours for the production of light that he absolutely *floods* <note>original: "inundates"</note> our *national market* with it at a fabulously reduced price. The moment he shows himself, our trade leaves us—all consumers go to him; and a branch of native industry, having countless branches <note>original: "ramifications"</note>, is all at once rendered completely stagnant. This rival, who is none other than the Sun, wages total war <note>original: "war to the knife"</note> against us, and we suspect that he has been encouraged <note>original: "raised up"</note> by *treacherous England* <note>original: "perfidious Albion," a common 19th-century French catchphrase for Great Britain</note> (good policy as times go), especially since he treats that haughty island with a caution that he ignores in our case.

What we pray for is that it may please you to pass a law ordering the shutting of all windows, skylights, dormer windows, outside and inside shutters, curtains, blinds, bull's-eyes <note>small, circular windows or thick panes of glass</note>; in a word, of all openings, holes, chinks, cracks <note>original: "clefts"</note>, and fissures through which the light of the sun has been accustomed to enter houses, to the disadvantage of the deserving <note>original: "meritorious"</note> manufacturers with which we pride ourselves we have supplied our country—a country which, in gratitude, ought not to abandon us now to a struggle so unequal.

We trust, Gentlemen, that you will not regard this request of ours as a satire, or refuse it without at least first hearing the reasons which we have to offer in its support.

And, first, if you shut out as much as possible all access to natural light, and create a demand for artificial light, which of our French industries will not be encouraged by it?

If more tallow is consumed, then there must be more oxen and sheep; and, consequently, we shall see the increase of artificial meadows, meat, wool, hides, and, above all, manure, which is the basis and foundation of all agricultural wealth.

If more oil is consumed, then we shall have an expanded cultivation of the poppy, of the olive, and of rapeseed <note>original: "rape"</note>. These rich and soil-exhausting plants will come at the right time to enable us to take advantage of the increased fertility which the raising of additional cattle will give to our lands.

Our heaths will be covered with resin-producing <note>original: "resinous"</note> trees. Numerous...

# PETITION, ETC.

Swarms of bees will gather perfumed treasures on the mountains—fragrance that is currently wasted on the desert air, much like the flowers from which they come. There is no branch of agriculture that will not then show an encouraging development.

The same remark applies to navigation. Thousands of ships will set out for the whale fisheries; and in a short time, we shall possess a navy capable of maintaining the honor of France and satisfying the patriotic aspirations of your petitioners, the undersigned candlemakers and others.

But what shall we say of the manufacture of *articles de Paris* <note>original: "articles de Paris"; refers to a specific category of Parisian luxury goods, jewelry, and fashion accessories</note>? From now on, you will see gilding, bronzes, and crystals in candlesticks, lamps, chandeliers <note>original: "lustres"</note>, and candelabras, shining in spacious showrooms compared to which those of the present day can be regarded as mere shops.

No poor <term>resin collector</term> <note>original: "resinier"; a worker who harvests resin from pine trees, used to make turpentine and rosin for candles and lamps</note> on the coastal heights, and no coal miner in the depths of his dark <note>original: "sable"</note> tunnels, will fail to rejoice in higher wages and increased prosperity.

Only have the goodness to reflect, Gentlemen, and you will be convinced that there is perhaps no Frenchman—from the wealthy coal owner to the humblest seller of matches <note>original: "vender of lucifer matches," an early type of friction match</note>—whose life will not be improved <note>original: "ameliorated"</note> by the success of our petition.

We foresee your objections, Gentlemen, but we know that you can only oppose us with arguments you have picked up from the worn-out <note>original: "effete"</note> works of the supporters of free trade. We defy you to utter a single word against us which will not instantly rebound against yourselves and your entire policy.

You will tell us that, even if we gain from the protection we seek, the country will lose by it, because the consumer must bear the cost.

We answer:

You no longer have any right to invoke the interest of the consumer; for whenever his interest is found to be opposed to that of the producer, you sacrifice the consumer. You have done so for the purpose of *encouraging labor and increasing employment*. For the same reason, you should do so again.

You have already addressed <note>original: "obviated"</note> this objection yourselves. When you are told that the consumer is interested in the free importation of iron, coal, grain, or textile fabrics—yes, you reply, but the producer...

<summary>The petitioners argue that blocking out the sun would create a massive economic boom for the candle, whaling, shipping, and luxury goods industries. They challenge the government's consistency, noting that the state already prioritizes producers over consumers in other industries.</summary>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the satirical "Candlemakers' Petition" by Frédéric Bastiat. The previous page argued that blocking out sunlight would create massive demand for domestic products like tallow, wax, and coal, thereby boosting the entire French economy through a ripple effect of increased consumption.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

...is interested in excluding them. Well, so be it; if consumers are interested in the free entry of natural light, the producers of artificial light are just as interested in its prohibition.

But, again, you might say that the producer and consumer are one and the same. If the manufacturer gains through protectionism, he will make the farmer <note>original: "agriculturist"</note> a gainer as well; and if agriculture prospers, it will provide a market <note>original: "open a vent"</note> for manufactured goods. Very well; if you grant us the monopoly of providing light during the day, first of all, we will purchase huge quantities of tallow, coal, oil, resinous substances, wax, and alcohol—as well as silver, iron, bronze, and crystal—to carry on our manufacturing. Then, we and those who supply us with these commodities, having become wealthy, will consume a great deal and bring prosperity to every other branch of our national industry.

If you argue that sunlight is a free <term>gratuitous</term> gift of nature, and that to reject such gifts is to reject wealth itself under the pretense of encouraging the means of acquiring it, we would warn you not to deal a fatal blow to your own policy. Remember that until now, you have always rejected foreign products *because* they are closer to being free gifts than domestic products are. To satisfy the demands of other <term>monopolists</term> <note>here referring to domestic producers who want to exclude foreign competition</note>, you have only *half a motive*; but to reject us simply because we stand on stronger ground than others would be to adopt the equation + plus + equals —; in other words, it would be to heap *absurdity upon absurdity*.

Nature and human labor work together in various proportions (depending on countries and climates) to produce commodities. The part that nature performs is always free <note>original: "gratuitous"</note>; it is the part performed by human labor that creates value and is paid for.

If an orange from Lisbon <note>Lisbon, Portugal, has a much warmer climate than Paris, France, allowing oranges to grow naturally</note> sells for half the price of a Paris orange, it is because natural—and therefore free—heat does for the one what artificial—and therefore expensive—heat must do for the other.

When an orange comes to us from Portugal, we may conclude that it is provided partly for free and partly for a financial cost <note>original: "onerous consideration"</note>; in other words, it comes to us at *half-price* compared to those grown in Paris.

<summary>Bastiat satirically argues that if the government rejects free foreign goods to protect domestic industry, they must logically reject free sunlight to protect the lighting industry. He explains that the price of a good is determined only by the human labor required, while nature’s contribution is always free.</summary>
<keywords>protectionism, free trade, labor theory of value, competition, monopoly, Frédéric Bastiat, Candlemakers' Petition, economic logic</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat's satirical "Candlemakers' Petition," concluding the argument that if protectionism seeks to block cheap foreign goods to protect domestic industry, it must logically block the sun, which provides light for free. It then transitions into a new section titled "Differential Duties," illustrating economic principles through the story of a French wine-grower.</meta>

Now, it is precisely the *free half* (pardon the term) that we argue should be excluded. You ask: "How can domestic labor compete with foreign labor when the domestic worker has to do all the work, while the foreigner only does half, with the sun doing the rest?" But if this *half-free* portion leads you to exclude competition, why would the *entire* portion being free induce you to accept it? If you were consistent, you would exclude things that are half-free as harmful to native industry, and with even more zeal—and for even stronger reasons <note>original: "a fortiori"</note>—exclude that which is entirely free.

Furthermore, when products like coal, iron, grain <note>original: "corn," which in this period generally referred to the primary cereal crop of a region, such as wheat</note>, or textiles are sent to us from abroad, and we can get them with less labor than if we made them ourselves, the difference is a free gift given to us. This gift is larger or smaller depending on how great that difference is. It equals a quarter, a half, or three-quarters of the product's value when the foreigner only asks us for three-fourths, a half, or a quarter of the price we would otherwise pay. It is as perfect and complete as possible when the donor (like the sun providing light) asks for nothing. The question we formally ask is this: Do you want our country to have the benefit of free consumption, or the supposed advantages of costly <term>onerous</term> production? Make your choice, but be logical; for as long as you exclude things like coal, iron, grain, and foreign fabrics in proportion as their price gets closer to zero, what an inconsistency it would be to allow in the light of the sun, which is already priced at zero all day long!

His success made him forget that every drop of this fine wine <note>original: "precious nectar"</note> had cost...

The Belgian said to him: “Give me your cask of wine, and I will give you fifteen parcels of fabric in exchange.” The Englishman said: “Give me your wine, and I will give you twenty parcels of fabric; for we English can manufacture cloth more cheaply than the Belgians.” 

But a Customs officer <note>original: "Customhouse officer"</note> who was present stepped in and said: “My good friend, trade with the Belgian if you wish, but my orders are to prevent you from trading with the Englishman.” “What!” exclaimed the countryman; “you want me to be satisfied with fifteen parcels of fabric from Brussels when I can get twenty parcels from Manchester <note>Manchester was the world's leading center for textile manufacturing during the 19th-century Industrial Revolution</note>?” “Certainly; don’t you see that France would lose if you received twenty parcels instead of fifteen?” “I don't understand you,” said the vine-dresser. “And I am at a loss to explain it,” replied the Customs official; “but it is certain, for all our representatives <note>original: "deputies"</note>, ministers, and journalists agree on this: the more a nation receives in exchange for a specific amount of its products, the more it is made poor.” The peasant found it necessary to make the deal with the Belgian. The peasant’s daughter received only three-quarters of her wedding outfit; and these simple people are still asking themselves how it can be that a person is ruined by receiving four items instead of three, and why someone is wealthier with three dozen towels than with four dozen.

### IX.
## AN IMMENSE DISCOVERY.

At a time when everyone is intent on reducing the cost of transportation—and when, to achieve this saving, we are building roads and canals, improving our steamships,

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat's satirical critique of economic policy. While the previous page ended with a story about a vine-dresser being forced into an unfair trade, this page begins mid-sentence, transitioning into a new "discovery" regarding transport costs and infrastructure.</meta>

->## IMMENSE DISCOVERY<-

...and connecting Paris with all our borders <note>original: "frontiers"</note> by a network of railways—at a time, too, when I believe we are passionately and sincerely seeking a solution to the problem: *how to bring the prices of goods, in the place where they are to be consumed, as close as possible to their prices in the place where they were produced*. I would consider myself failing my country, my era, and myself if I kept secret any longer the marvelous discovery I have just made.

The illusions of inventors are well-known <note>original: "proverbial"</note>, but I am absolutely certain that I have discovered an infallible way of bringing products from every part of the world to France, and the other way around <note>original: "vice versa"</note>, at a significantly lower cost.

Infallible, did I say? Being infallible is only one of the advantages of my invention.

It requires no plans, cost estimates, preliminary studies, engineers, mechanics, contractors, capital, shareholders, or government aid!

It poses no risk of shipwreck, explosion, fire, or collision!

It can be put into use at any time!

Furthermore—and this will certainly recommend it to the public—it will not add a single penny to the national budget, but rather the opposite. It will not increase the number of government officials <term>functionaries</term>, but rather the opposite. It will not interfere with any person's liberty, but rather the opposite.

It was observation, not luck, that gave me this discovery, and I will tell you what suggested it.

I had to solve this question:
"Why does an item manufactured in Brussels, for example, cost more when it arrives in Paris?"

I soon realized that it happens because of this: between Paris and Brussels, many *obstacles* exist. First, there is distance, which leads to a loss of time; we must either endure this ourselves or pay someone else to do it. Then come rivers, marshes, accidents, and bad roads, which are all *difficulties* that must be overcome. We succeed in building bridges, building roads, and making them smoother with pavement, iron rails, and so on. But all of this is expensive, and the goods must carry that cost. Finally, there are robbers who haunt the roads, and a police force <note>original: "body of police"</note> must be maintained, which also adds to the expense.

<summary>Bastiat introduces a satirical "infallible discovery" that promises to reduce transport costs and trade barriers without requiring government spending or infrastructure. He identifies the various physical and social obstacles, such as distance and crime, that increase the price of goods between cities like Brussels and Paris.</summary>

<keywords>free trade, transport costs, infrastructure, Bastiat, economic obstacles, railways, protectionism, Brussels, Paris</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat's satirical argument regarding the absurdity of government-imposed trade barriers. Having just "discovered" that high prices are caused by distance and obstacles, he now points out that the government intentionally creates these obstacles while simultaneously spending money to remove natural ones.</meta>

## ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

Now, among these *obstacles*, there is one that we have created ourselves—and at quite a high price—between Brussels and Paris. There are men lying in ambush along the border, armed to the teeth, whose job it is to throw *difficulties* in the way of transporting goods from one country to the other. They are called customs officers, and they act in exactly the same way as potholes and bad roads. They slow down and hinder <term>trammel</term> commerce; they increase the gap we have noticed between the price paid by the consumer and the price received by the producer—that very gap which we are trying to reduce as much as possible.

The solution to that problem can be expressed in three words: Reduce your tariff.

By doing this, you will have achieved the equivalent of building the Northern Railway at no cost, and you will immediately begin putting money back into your own pocket.

In truth, I often seriously ask myself how such an absurd <note>original: "whimsical"</note> idea could ever have entered the human mind: first, to spend many millions to remove the *natural obstacles* that exist between France and other countries, and then to spend many more millions to create *artificial obstacles* that have exactly the same effect. In fact, the obstacle created and the obstacle removed cancel each other out, leaving things exactly as they were before—except the whole operation has resulted in a double expense.

A Belgian product is worth 20 francs in Brussels, and the cost of shipping <note>original: "carriage"</note> would raise the price in Paris to 30 francs. The same item made in Paris costs 40 francs. How do we proceed?

First, we impose a tax <note>original: "duty"</note> of 10 francs on the Belgian product to raise its cost in Paris to 40 francs. We pay many officials to ensure this tax is strictly collected, so that along the way, the product is charged 10 francs for shipping and 10 francs for the tax.

After doing this, we reason like this: "Shipping from Brussels to Paris, which costs 10 francs, is very expensive. Let us spend two or three hundred million francs on railways, and we will reduce that cost by half." Clearly, all we gain from this is that the Belgian product would then sell in Paris for 35 francs—namely:

<summary>Bastiat argues that tariffs act as artificial obstacles to trade, functioning exactly like bad roads or distance to increase prices. He points out the irony of governments spending millions on infrastructure to reduce transport costs while simultaneously spending millions on customs to increase them.</summary>
<keywords>tariffs, customs officers, trade barriers, transport costs, railways, economic irony, Frédéric Bastiat, protectionism</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat's satirical mathematical proof of the absurdity of protectionist policies. He demonstrates that the money spent on infrastructure to lower costs is rendered useless by tariffs designed to keep prices high.</meta>

# IMMENSE DISCOVERY

->20 francs, its price at Brussels.<-
->10 francs, duty <term>import tax</term>.<-
->5 francs, reduced carriage by railway.<-
->---<-
->Total, 35 francs, representing cost price at Paris.<-

Now, I ask, would we not have attained the same result by lowering the tariff <note>original: "tariff"</note> by 5 francs? We should then have—

->20 francs, the price at Brussels.<-
->5 francs, reduced duty.<-
->10 francs, carriage by ordinary roads.<-
->---<-
->Total, 35 francs, representing cost price at Paris.<-

And by this process, we would have saved the 200 million which the railway cost, *plus* the expense of customhouse monitoring <note>original: "surveillance"</note>, for this monitoring would be reduced in proportion to the diminished encouragement held out to smuggling.

But it will be said that the duty is necessary to protect Parisian industry. Be it so; but then you destroy the effect of your railway.

For, if you persist in desiring that the Belgian product should cost 40 francs at Paris, you must raise your duty to 15 francs, and then you have—

->20 francs, the price at Brussels.<-
->15 francs, protecting duty.<-
->5 francs, railway carriage.<-
->---<-
->Total, 40 francs, being the equalized price.<-

Then, I venture to ask, what, under such circumstances, is the good of your railway?

In sober earnestness, let me ask: is it not humiliating that the nineteenth century should make itself a laughingstock to future ages by such childish absurdities <term>puerilities</term>, practiced with such unshakable gravity? To be the dupe of other people is not very pleasant, but to employ a vast representative apparatus <note>referring to the government and legislature</note> in order to dupe—and double dupe—ourselves, and that, too, in a simple matter of arithmetic, should surely humble the pride of this *age of enlightenment.*

<summary>Bastiat uses a simple arithmetical comparison to show that lowering a tariff achieves the same economic result as building an expensive railway, and argues that using tariffs to "protect" industry makes infrastructure investments pointless.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of trade barriers. Having established that tariffs act as artificial obstacles to transport, he now addresses the argument of "reciprocity"—the idea that one country should only lower its trade barriers if another country does the same.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS <note>Sophisms are fallacious arguments, usually used to deceive.</note></header>

### X.

## RECIPROCITY.

We have just seen that whatever increases the expense of moving goods <note>original: "conveying commodities"</note> from one country to another—in other words, whatever makes transport more difficult <note>original: "onerous"</note>—acts in the same way as a protective duty. Or, if you prefer to put it another way, a protective duty acts in the same way as more difficult transport.

A tariff <term>a tax on imported goods</term>, then, may be regarded in the same light as a swamp, a rut, an obstruction, or a steep slope—in a word, it is an *obstacle*. Its effect is to increase <note>original: "augment"</note> the difference between the price the producer receives and the price the consumer pays. In the same way, it is undoubtedly true that swamps and bogs <note>original: "quagmires"</note> are to be regarded in the same light as protective tariffs.

There are people (few in number, it is true) who are beginning to understand that obstacles are no less obstructive just because they are artificial, and that our trade prospects have more to gain from freedom than from protection—for the exact same reason that a canal is more favorable to traffic than a steep, winding, and inconvenient road.

But they maintain that this freedom must be mutual <note>original: "reciprocal"</note>. If we remove the barriers we have built against the admission of Spanish goods, for example, they argue that Spain must remove the barriers she has built against the admission of ours. They are, therefore, the advocates of *commercial treaties* based on exact reciprocity, exchanging concession for concession. "Let us make the *sacrifice* of buying," they say, "to obtain the advantage of selling."

I am sorry to say that people who reason in this way are protectionists in principle, whether they know it or not. They are only a little more inconsistent than pure protectionists, just as pure protectionists are more inconsistent than those who wish to ban imports entirely <note>original: "absolute prohibitionists"</note>.

The following fable <note>original: "apologue"</note> will demonstrate this:

->STULTA AND PUERA<-
<note>In Latin, "Stulta" means foolish and "Puera" means childish.</note>

There were once, it does not matter where, two towns called Stulta and

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegory of two neighboring towns, Puera and Stulta, illustrating the absurdity of trade barriers that act as artificial obstacles to the very roads built to facilitate commerce.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>59</page-num>

<header>RECIPROCITY.</header>

Puera <note>original: "Puera" (from the Latin for "child" or "girl," implying a youthful or perhaps naive state)</note>. They completed a highway from one town to the other at great cost. When this was done, Stulta <note>original: "Stulta" (from the Latin for "foolish" or "stupid")</note> said to herself, “See how Puera floods us with her products; we must do something about it.” Consequently, they created and paid a body of ***obstructives***—so called because their business was to place ***obstacles*** in the way of traffic coming from Puera. Soon afterward, Puera did the same.

At the end of some centuries, during which knowledge had made great progress, the common sense of Puera enabled her to see that such reciprocal obstacles could only be mutually harmful. She therefore sent a diplomat to Stulta, who, putting aside official jargon, spoke to this effect: “We have built a highway, and now we throw obstacles in the way of using it. This is absurd. It would have been better to have left things as they were. In that case, we would not have had to pay to build the road in the first place, nor would we have later incurred the expense of maintaining ***obstructives***. In the name of Puera, I come to propose to you—not that we give up opposing each other all at once, for that would be to act upon a fixed principle, and we despise principles as much as you do—but to slightly lessen the current obstacles, taking care to fairly estimate the respective ***sacrifices*** we each make for this purpose.”

So spoke the diplomat. Stulta asked for time to consider the proposal and proceeded to consult, one by one, her manufacturers and farmers <note>original: "agriculturists"</note>. At length, after several years had passed, she declared that the negotiations were broken off.

On receiving this announcement <note>original: "intimation"</note>, the inhabitants of Puera held a meeting. An old gentleman (who was always suspected of having been secretly bribed by Stulta) rose and said: "The obstacles created by Stulta injure our sales, which is a misfortune. Those which we have created ourselves injure our purchases, which is another misfortune. Regarding the first, we are powerless; but the second rests with us. Let us, at least, get rid of one of these evils, since we cannot rid ourselves of both. Let us abolish our ***obstructives*** without requiring Stulta to do the same. Someday, no doubt, she will come to understand her own interests better."

A second counselor, a practical, matter-of-fact man, who knew nothing of abstract principles and was raised in the ways...

<summary>Through an allegory of two towns, the author argues that trade barriers are self-inflicted wounds, suggesting that a country should remove its own tariffs even if its neighbors refuse to do the same.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the allegory of the towns of Stulta and Puera, illustrating the contradictory nature of trade protectionism. It then introduces a new chapter on the distinction between nominal prices and actual abundance.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>60</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "Sophisms"</note>.</header>

of his ancestors, replied: “Don’t listen to that Utopian dreamer, that theorist, that innovator, that economist, that *Stultomaniac* <note>meaning: one obsessed with the foolishness represented by the town of Stulta</note>. We shall all be ruined if the roadblocks are not equalized, weighed, and balanced between Stulta and Puera. There would be greater difficulty in *going* than in *coming*, in *exporting* than in *importing*. We would find ourselves in the same state of inferiority compared to Stulta as Havre, Nantes, Bordeaux, Lisbon, London, Hamburg, and New Orleans are in relation to the towns situated at the headwaters of the Seine, the Loire, the Garonne, the Tagus, the Thames, the Elbe, and the Mississippi; for it is more difficult for a ship to sail upriver than to sail downriver. (*A Voice*: Towns at the *mouths* <note>original: "embouchures"</note> of rivers prosper more than towns at their source.) This is impossible. (*Same Voice*: But it is so.) Well, if it be so, they have prospered *against the rules*.” 

Reasoning so “conclusive” convinced the assembly, and the orator followed up his victory by talking grandly of national independence, national honor, national dignity, national labor, the flood of products, tributes, and cutthroat competition. In short, he won the vote in favor of maintaining obstacles; and if you are at all curious about it, I can point out to you countries where you will see with your own eyes Road-makers and Obstructives working together on the friendliest terms possible. They work under the orders of the same legislature and at the expense of the same taxpayers—the first group trying to clear the road, and the second group doing their utmost to make it impassable.

### XI.

## NOMINAL PRICES.

Do you want to be in a position to decide between liberty and protection? Do you want to appreciate the impact of an economic phenomenon? Look into its effects *upon the abundance or scarcity of goods*, and not *upon the rise or fall of*

<summary>This page concludes an allegory mocking the irony of governments simultaneously funding infrastructure and trade barriers. It begins a new discussion on why the abundance of goods is a better economic measure than price levels.</summary>
<keywords>protectionism, free trade, trade barriers, infrastructure, economic theory, nominal prices, Bastiat, Stulta, Puera</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded an allegory about trade barriers between two fictional towns, illustrating the logical fallacies of protectionism. This page transitions into a specific critique of "nominal prices" and how they obscure the reality of wealth and labor.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>NOMINAL PRICES <note>meaning: the face-value price of goods in money, which does not account for purchasing power</note>.</header>

Distrust *nominal prices*; they will only lead you into an inextricable maze.

M. Matthieu de Dombasle <note>Mathieu de Dombasle (1777–1843) was a famous French agronomist and proponent of protected agriculture</note>, after showing that protectionist policies raise prices, adds:

"Increasing prices raises the cost of living and, *consequently*, the price of labor. Every person receives compensation through the higher prices of their own products for the higher costs they are forced to pay for the things they need to buy. Thus, if everyone pays more as a consumer, everyone receives more as a producer."

It is evident that we could reverse this argument and say:

"If everyone receives more as a producer, everyone pays more as a consumer."

Now, what does this prove? Nothing except that protectionism *displaces* wealth uselessly and unjustly. In doing so, it simply commits legal plunder <note>original: "spoliation"; Bastiat's term for the unfair seizure of property through government policy</note>.

Furthermore, to conclude that this vast system leads to simple compensation, we must rely on M. de Dombasle’s "consequently" and be certain that the price of labor will definitely rise along with the price of protected products. This is a question of fact that I leave to M. Moreau de Jonnés <note>Alexandre Moreau de Jonnés (1778–1870) was a prominent French statistician</note>, so that he may take the trouble to find out whether the rate of wages increases alongside the price of shares in the Anzin coal mines <note>The Anzin Company was one of the first major industrial coal mining operations in France and a beneficiary of high tariffs</note>. For my own part, I do not believe that it does; because, in my opinion, the price of labor—like the price of everything else—is governed by the relationship of supply to demand. 

Now, I am convinced that *restriction* <note>meaning: trade barriers or tariffs</note> diminishes the supply of coal and consequently increases its price; but I do not see so clearly that it increases the demand for labor so as to raise the rate of wages. That this effect should be produced is even less likely because the quantity of labor demanded depends on the available capital. Protectionism may indeed displace capital and cause it to be transferred from one employment to another, but it can never increase it by a single cent.

---

\* I have translated the expression *des prix absolus* <note>original Latin/French: "absolute prices"</note> as *nominal prices*, or *actual money prices*, because English economists do not, as far as I remember, make use of the term *absolute price*. See *later* <note>original: "post"</note>, chapter 5 of the *second series*, where the author uses the expression in this sense.—**TRANSLATOR.**

<summary>Bastiat refutes the idea that high prices caused by protectionism are harmlessly offset by higher wages. He argues that while tariffs raise the cost of goods, they do not necessarily increase the demand for labor or the total amount of capital in an economy.</summary>

<meta>The previous page introduced a critique of the protectionist argument that rising prices are compensated by rising wages. This page continues that critique by exposing the fallacy of "nominal prices"—the idea that money value alone determines a nation’s wealth.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>

# ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "sophisms"; a sophism is a clever but false argument intended to deceive</note>

But this question, which is of the greatest interest and importance, will be examined in another section.* I return to the subject of *nominal price* <note>meaning: the price of a good in money, as opposed to its real value in terms of labor or what it can be traded for</note>, and I maintain that it is not one of those absurdities that can be made to look plausible by arguments like those of M. de Dombasle.

Consider the case of a nation that is isolated and possesses a specific amount of money in gold and silver coins <note>original: "specie"</note>. Suppose this nation chooses to entertain itself by burning half of all the goods it possesses every year. I aim to prove that, according to M. de Dombasle’s theory, it would not be any less rich.

In fact, as a result of the fire, the price of everything would double. Records of property made before and after the destruction would show exactly the same *nominal* value. But then, what would the country in question have lost? If John buys his cloth more expensively, he also sells his grain <note>original: "corn," which in 19th-century British English referred to any cereal crop like wheat or barley</note> at a higher price; and if Peter loses money on his purchase of grain, he recovers his losses by selling his cloth at a higher price.

> "Everyone recovers, through the higher price of their products, the extra cost of living they have incurred. If everyone pays more as a consumer, everyone also receives a corresponding amount more as a producer."

All of this is clever wordplay <note>original: "jingling quibble"</note> and not science. The truth, in plain terms, is this: people consume cloth and grain whether they are destroyed by fire or by being used. The effect on *price* is the same, but the effect on *wealth* is not. This is because wealth or material prosperity consists precisely in the *use* of goods.

In the same way, trade restrictions <note>original: "restriction"</note>, while reducing the abundance of things, may raise their price to such an extent that everyone is, *financially speaking*, as rich as they were before. But would recording three measures of grain at 20 shillings, or four measures at 15 shillings, result in the same thing for satisfying human needs—simply because the total is still 60 shillings?

I shall never stop bringing the protectionists <note>advocates for government policies that restrict international trade to help domestic industries</note> back to this consumer’s point of view. This is the ultimate goal of all our efforts and the solution to all our problems.† I shall—

---

* See later, chapter 5, second series.—TRANSLATOR.

† The author frequently returns to this view of the subject. In his eyes, it was all-important; four days before his death, he dictated this recommendation: "Tell M. de Fontenay <note>original: "M. de F."; referring to Roger de Fontenay, a friend and editor of Bastiat's work</note> to always treat economic questions from the consumer's point of view, for the interest of the consumer is the interest of the human race."

<summary>This page argues that monetary "nominal" prices do not reflect true wealth; destroying goods to raise prices might keep money values the same but reduces the actual satisfaction of human needs.</summary>

<meta>The previous page introduced a critique of the protectionist argument that rising prices are compensated by rising wages. This page continues that critique by exposing the fallacy of "nominal prices"—the idea that money value alone determines a nation’s wealth.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>

# NOMINAL PRICES

I never cease saying to them: Is it, or is it not true that restriction—by blocking exchanges, limiting the division of labor, and forcing labor to contend with difficult climates and locations—ultimately reduces the quantity of goods produced by a fixed amount of effort? And what does this matter, it will be said, if the smaller quantity produced under a system <note>original: "régime"</note> of protection has the same *money value* <note>original: "nominal value"; the price of a good in money, which may fluctuate regardless of the good's actual utility</note> as that produced under a system of liberty? The answer is obvious. Man does not live upon *money values*, but upon real products; and the more products there are, whatever their price may be, the richer he is.

In writing the preceding pages, I never expected to encounter an anti-economist who was enough of a logician to admit, in so many words, that the wealth of nations depends on the value of things, separate from the consideration of their abundance. But here is what I find in the work of M. de Saint-Chamans <note>Auguste, Viscount de Saint-Chamans (1777–1860), a French deputy and advocate for protectionism</note> (p. 210):

> “If fifteen millions’ worth of commodities, sold to foreigners, are taken from the total production (estimated at fifty millions), the remaining thirty-five millions’ worth of commodities will not be sufficient to meet the ordinary demand. They will increase in price and rise to a value of fifty millions. In that case, the revenue of the country will represent a value of fifteen millions more than before. . . . There would then be an increase of the wealth of the country to the extent of fifteen millions, exactly the amount of hard currency <note>original: "specie"; gold or silver coins</note> imported.”

This is a delightful view of the matter! If a nation produces a value of fifty million in one year from its agriculture and commerce, it only has to sell a quarter of it to foreigners to become a quarter richer! Then, if it sells half, it will be one-half richer! And if it should sell everything—to its last tuft of wool and its last grain of wheat—it would bring its revenue up to 100 million. What a strange way of getting rich: by producing infinite high prices <note>original: "dearness"</note> through absolute scarcity!

Again, would you judge these two doctrines? Submit them to the test of exaggeration.

According to the doctrine of M. de Saint-Chamans, the—

<margin>“From the consumer’s point of view, for the interest of the consumer is identical with that of the human race.”—Editor.</margin>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS</header>

The French would be just as rich—that is to say, just as well supplied with all things—if they had only a thousandth part of their annual products, because those products would be worth a thousand times more.

According to our doctrine, the French would be infinitely rich if their annual products were infinitely abundant, and, consequently, had no market value at all.*

### XII.

## DOES PROTECTION RAISE THE RATE OF WAGES?

An atheist was ranting one day against religion and the clergy <note>original: "priestcraft"</note>. He became so outrageous in his abuse that one of his listeners, who was not very religious himself, exclaimed, “If you go on much longer in this tone, you will make me a convert.”

In the same way, we see our "beardless scribblers," novelists, reformers, and fops <term>men who are excessively concerned with their clothes and appearance</term>. These amateur newspaper contributors, smelling of musk and soaked in champagne, fill their portfolios with radical pamphlets. They issue tirades under fancy, gilded covers against the selfishness <note>original: "egotism"</note> and individualism of the age. When we hear such people speak against the harshness of our institutions, groan over the working class <term>proletariat</term> and the wages system, raise their eyes to Heaven, and weep over the poverty of the working classes—a poverty which they never see except when they are paid to paint it—we are likewise tempted to exclaim, “If you go on much longer in this tone, we shall lose all interest in the working classes.”

Pretentiousness <note>original: "affectation"</note> is the defining sin of our times. When a serious writer, in a spirit of genuine concern for humanity <note>original: "philanthropy"</note>, refers to the sufferings of the working classes, his words are caught up by these sentimentalists. They are twisted, distorted, and exaggerated to the point of nausea <note>original: "usque ad nauseam"</note>. The great, the only remedy, it would seem, lies in the high-sounding phrases "association" and "organization." The working—

<summary>Bastiat argues that true wealth consists of the abundance of goods rather than their high monetary value. He then begins a critique of sentimental reformers who use the plight of the working class to promote "organization" and "association" without understanding economic realities.</summary>

<page-num>65</page-num>

<header>DOES PROTECTION RAISE THE RATE OF WAGES?</header>

...classes are flattered—excessively and submissively flattered. They are portrayed as being in the condition of slaves, and people of common sense will soon be ashamed to publicly support their cause. For how can common sense make itself heard in the midst of all this dull and empty talk?

Let us reject this cowardly indifference, which would not be justified even by the sentimental posturing <note>original: "affectation"</note> that inspires it.

Workmen! Your situation is unique! They treat you like merchandise, as I shall show you immediately. . . . But no; I withdraw that expression. Let us avoid aggressive language that might be misused; for plunder <note>original: "spoliation"—a key term for Bastiat referring to the unfair or legal seizure of property or value</note>, wrapped up in the complex fallacies <term>sophistry</term> that conceal it, may be in full operation without the plunderer even knowing it, and with the blind consent of his victim. Still, you are deprived of the fair payment <term>remuneration</term> for your labor, and no one is concerned with doing you *justice*. 

If all that was needed to comfort you were passionate appeals to philanthropy, to useless <note>original: "impotent"</note> charity, or to degrading handouts; or if the grand words "organization," "communism," and "*phalanstère*" <note>A reference to the "Phalanstère," a type of utopian community building advocated by socialist Charles Fourier. original: "phalanstère"</note> were enough for you, truly they would not be withheld. But *justice*—simple *justice*—no one thinks of offering you. And yet, would it not be *just* that when, after a long day's toil, you have received your modest wages, you should have the power to exchange them for the greatest amount of satisfaction and enjoyment you could possibly get for them from anyone, in any part of the world?

Someday I may also have the opportunity to talk to you about "association" and "organization," and we shall then see what you can expect from those fantasies <term>chimeras</term> which currently mislead you.

In the meantime, let us ask whether *injustice* is not done to you by the government legally deciding the people from whom you must purchase the things you need—bread, meat, linens, or cloth—and by dictating, if I may say so, the artificial scale of prices which you are forced to use in your dealings.

Is it true that protectionism, which admittedly makes you pay more for everything and causes you a loss in that respect, raises your wages proportionally?

On what does the rate of wages depend?

One of your own class has put it forcefully, like this: "When two..."

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page transitions from a general appeal to justice for the working classes to a specific economic argument. The author begins to demonstrate that protective tariffs do not increase the overall demand for labor, but merely shift capital from one industry to another.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>

<header>### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS</header>

When many workmen compete for one master, wages fall; they rise when two masters compete for one workman.

For the sake of brevity, allow me to use a more scientific formula, though it may not be quite as clear: The rate of wages depends on the proportion of the supply of labor to the demand for it.

Now, on what does the *supply* of labor depend?

It depends on the number of men waiting for employment; and on this first element, protection <note>original: "protection"—the policy of using tariffs or restrictions to favor domestic industries over foreign imports</note> can have no effect.

On what does the *demand* for labor depend?

It depends on the available capital of the nation. But does a law which says, "We shall no longer receive such or such a product from abroad; we shall make it at home," increase that capital? Not in the least degree. It may force capital from one employment to another, but it does not increase it by a single penny. Therefore, it does not increase the demand for labor.

We point with pride to a certain factory. Was it established or maintained with capital that fell from the moon? No; that capital was withdrawn from agriculture, from shipping, or from the production of wine. And this is the reason why, under a system <note>original: "régime"</note> of protective tariffs, there are more workmen in our mines and in our manufacturing towns, but fewer sailors in our ports and fewer laborers in our fields and vineyards.

I could speak at length on this subject, but I prefer to explain what I mean with an example.

A farmer <note>original: "countryman"</note> owned twenty acres of land, which he worked with a capital of £400. He divided his land into four parts and established the following rotation of crops: 1st, corn <note>original: "maize"</note>; 2nd, wheat; 3rd, clover; 4th, rye. He required for his own family only a moderate portion of the grain, meat, and milk which his farm produced, and he sold the surplus to buy oil, flax, wine, and other necessities. His entire capital was spent each year on wages, hiring costs, and small payments to the working classes in his neighborhood. This capital returned to him through his sales and even increased year by year. Our farmer, knowing very well that capital produces nothing when it is unemployed, benefited the working classes by devoting his annual surplus to enclosing and clearing his land, and

<summary>Bastiat argues that wages are determined by the supply and demand of labor, and that protectionist laws do not create new capital or demand for labor but merely redirect it from one industry to another. He illustrates this by showing how a farmer's capital is distributed among the local workforce regardless of specific government trade interventions.</summary>

<keywords>wages, supply and demand, labor, capital, protectionism, tariffs, agriculture, crop rotation, Frédéric Bastiat</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegory of a farmer to illustrate how "self-sufficiency" (protectionism) actually reduces wealth and the ability to pay wages. It transitions from the father's successful specialized farming to the son's failed attempt to produce everything locally.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>

<header>DOES PROTECTION RAISE THE RATE OF WAGES?</header>

...to improving his agricultural implements and farm buildings. He even had some savings in the neighboring town with his banker, who, of course, did not let the money lie idle in his till, but lent it to shipowners and contractors for public works. In this way, these savings were always turning into wages <note>The author argues that saved capital doesn't disappear; it is reinvested into the economy to pay workers in other sectors.</note>.

At length the farmer died, and his son, who succeeded him, said to himself, “My father was a fool all his life. He purchased oil, and so paid *tribute* <note>original: "tribute"—the son views buying goods from other regions as a form of losing money or paying a tax to strangers.</note> to Provence, while our own land, with some effort, can be made to grow olives. He bought cloth, wine, and oranges, and thus paid tribute to Brittany, Medoc, and Hyères, while we can cultivate hemp, the vine, and the orange tree with more or less success. He paid *tribute* to the miller and the weaver, while our own servants can weave our linen and grind our wheat.” In this way, he believed his father had ruined himself and spent money among strangers that he might have kept at home.

Misled by such reasoning, the impulsive youth changed his rotation of crops. He divided his land into twenty sections. In one he planted olives, in another mulberry trees, in a third he sowed flax, in a fourth he had vines, in a fifth wheat, and so on. By this means, he succeeded in supplying his family with what they required and felt himself independent. He no longer drew anything from the general circulation <note>The "general circulation" refers to the broader economy where goods and money are exchanged.</note>, nor did he add anything to it. 

Was he any richer for this? No; for the soil was not adapted for the cultivation of the vine, and the climate was not suited for the successful cultivation of the olive. He was not long in finding out that his family was less plentifully provided with all the things they wanted than in the time of his father, who procured them by exchanging his surplus produce.

As for his workmen, they had no more employment than before. There were five times more fields, but each field was five times smaller; they produced oil, but they produced less wheat. He no longer purchased linens, but he no longer sold rye. Moreover, the farmer could pay in wages only the amount of his capital, and his capital went on constantly diminishing. A great part of it was spent on buildings and the various tools needed for the more varied cultivation in which he had engaged. In short, the supply of labor re—

<summary>Through the story of a son who tries to make his farm self-sufficient, the author illustrates that protectionism leads to inefficiency, lower yields, and a depletion of capital. This results in the family having fewer goods and the farmer having less money to pay his workers.</summary>

<keywords>protectionism, self-sufficiency, capital, wages, labor supply, specialization, trade, agriculture, economic allegory</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy of the farmer's son who, in an attempt at self-sufficiency, has divided his land into many inefficient plots. It explains how this mirrors a nation's move toward protectionism, which ultimately reduces the total pool of wealth available for wages.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>

-> # ECONOMIC SOPHISMS. <-

remained the same, but as the means of paying for <note>original: "remunerating"</note> that labor decreased, the final result was a forced reduction in wages.

On a larger scale, this is exactly what happens when a nation isolates itself by adopting a protectionist system <note>original: "prohibitive régime"</note>. I admit that it increases its various types of industries, but they become less efficient; it adopts, so to speak, a more complicated *industrial rotation* <note>A metaphor comparing a diverse but inefficient set of national industries to a farmer trying to grow too many different crops at once.</note>, but it is not as productive, because its capital and labor must now struggle against natural difficulties. A larger portion of its circulating capital <note>The liquid assets used for immediate business costs, such as payroll.</note>, which forms the wages fund, must be converted into fixed capital <note>Long-term assets like buildings and machinery.</note>. What remains may have more varied uses, but the total amount is not increased. It is like spreading the water of a pond into a multitude of shallow reservoirs—it covers more ground and exposes a larger surface to the rays of the sun, and it is for this very reason that the water is absorbed, evaporated, and lost all the sooner.

Given a set amount of capital and labor, they create a smaller amount of goods <note>original: "commodities"</note> in proportion to the obstacles they encounter. It is beyond doubt that when international trade barriers force capital and labor into channels and locations where they face greater difficulties of soil and climate, the general result must be fewer products created—that is to say, fewer benefits for consumers. Now, when there are fewer benefits overall, will the worker’s share of them be increased? If it were increased, as some claim, then the rich—the men who make the laws—would find their own share not only subject to the general decrease, but that already diminished share would be further reduced by whatever was added to the laborers' portion. Is this possible? Is it believable? I advise you, workers, to reject such suspicious generosity.

\* See *Economic Harmonies* <note>original: "Harmonies Économiques"</note>, chapter 14.

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionism forces capital and labor into inefficient uses, spreading a nation's resources too thin and ultimately reducing the total wealth available to pay workers. He warns workers that laws made by the wealthy are unlikely to increase the laborers' share of an already shrinking economic pie.</summary>

<header>THEORY AND PRACTICE.</header>

# XIII.

As advocates of free trade, we are accused of being theorists and of not taking practical experience sufficiently into account.

“What terrible prejudices were held against Jean-Baptiste Say,” <note>original: "M. Say." Jean-Baptiste Say (1767–1832) was a leading French economist who popularized free-market ideas.</note> says François Ferrier, <note>original: "M. Ferrier." François Ferrier (1777–1861) was a French customs official and a vocal supporter of protectionist trade barriers.</note> “by that long line of distinguished administrators and that impressive group of authors who disagreed with his opinions. Say was not unaware of this. Hear what he says:

‘To support long-standing errors, it has been claimed that there must be some foundation for ideas that have been adopted by all nations. Should we not be skeptical of observations and reasoning that go against opinions that have been constantly held down to our own time, and which have been regarded as sound by so many men remarkable for their enlightenment and good intentions? This argument, I admit, is designed to make a profound impression. It might have cast doubt upon points we consider most certain, if we had not seen—one after another—the most false opinions (now generally acknowledged to be false) accepted and practiced by everyone for many centuries. Not very long ago, all nations, from the most primitive to the most enlightened, and all men, from the street porter to the scholar <note>original: "savant"</note>, believed in the existence of the four elements. No one thought of questioning that doctrine, which is nevertheless false; so much so, that even the newest assistant in a science classroom would be ashamed to say that he regarded earth, water, and fire as elements.’ ”

On this, Ferrier remarks:

“If Say thinks he is answering the very strong objection he brings forward, he is significantly mistaken. It is easy to understand why men who were otherwise well-informed could be mistaken for centuries on certain points of natural history, but that proves nothing. Water, air, earth, and fire, whether they are elements or not, are no less useful to man. . . . Such errors are unimportant; they lead to no popular unrest <note>original: "commotions"</note>, no un—”

---

\* *On Commercial Administration Opposed to Political Economy* <note>original: "De l’Administration Commerciale opposée à l’Economie Politique"</note>, p. 5.

...rested easy in the public mind; they run counter to no financial interest; and this is the reason why, without any felt inconvenience, they may endure for a thousand years. The physical world goes on as if they did not exist. But can the same be said of errors in the moral world <note>In this context, "moral world" refers to the social and political sciences, which involve human behavior and ethics.</note>? Can we conceive that a system of administration, found to be absolutely false and therefore hurtful, should be followed by many nations for centuries, with the general approval of all well-informed men? Can it be explained how such a system could coexist with the constantly increasing prosperity of nations? Jean-Baptiste Say <note>original: "M. Say."</note> admits that the argument he is fighting against is designed to make a profound impression. Yes, indeed; and the impression remains; for Say has actually deepened it rather than done away with it.”

Let us hear what Auguste de Saint-Chamans <note>original: "M. de Saint-Chamans." He was a prominent French official and an advocate of protectionism.</note> says on the same subject:

> “It was only in the middle of the last century—that eighteenth century which subjected all subjects and all principles without exception to free discussion—that these *speculative* dealers in ideas, applying them to all things without being really applicable to anything, began to write upon political economy. Previously, there existed a system of political economy that was not to be found in books, but which had been put into *practical* operation by governments. Jean-Baptiste Colbert <note>original: "Colbert." Colbert (1619–1683) was the French Minister of Finance under Louis XIV and a famous proponent of state-controlled trade.</note>, it is said, was the inventor of it, and it was adopted as a rule by all the nations of Europe. The singular thing is that, despite the contempt and condemnations <note>original: "maledictions"</note> and all the discoveries of the modern school, it still remains in practical operation. This system, which our authors have called the *mercantile system* <note>The mercantile system, or Mercantilism, was an economic theory that favored exports and restricted imports to accumulate gold and silver.</note>, was designed to... impede, by prohibitions or import duties, the entry of foreign products, which might ruin our own manufactures by their competition. Economic writers of all schools\* have declared this system untenable, absurd, and calculated to impoverish any country. It has been banished from all their books...”

\* Might we not say that it is a “fearful prejudice” against Messrs. <note>original: "MM."</note> Ferrier and Saint-Chamans that “*economists of all schools*—that is to say, everyone who has studied the question—should have arrived at the conclusion that, after all, liberty is better than constraint, and the laws of God wiser than those of Colbert?”

<header>THEORY, PRACTICE.</header>

books, and forced to take refuge in the *practical* laws of all nations. They cannot conceive why, in measures relating to national wealth, governments should not follow the advice and opinions of learned authors, rather than trust to their *experience* of the well-tested working of a system which has been long in operation. Above all, they cannot conceive why the French government should, in economic questions, obstinately set itself to resist the progress of enlightenment and maintain in its *practice* those ancient errors which all our economic writers have exposed. But enough of this mercantile system <note>The mercantile system, or mercantilism, was an economic theory that favored government regulation of the economy to increase state power, often through trade barriers and the accumulation of gold.</note>, which has nothing in its favor but *facts*, and is not defended by any speculative writer.”*

Such language as this would lead one to suppose that in demanding for everyone *the free disposal of their property*, economists were proposing some new system—some new, strange, and imaginary social order, a sort of *phalanstery* <note>original: "phalanstère." This refers to a type of communal building designed for a self-contained utopian community, a concept developed by the socialist Charles Fourier.</note>, created in the minds of theorists and without precedent in the history of the human race. To me, it would seem that if we have here anything artificial or accidental, it is to be found not in liberty, but in protection; not in the free power of exchanging goods, but in customs duties used to artificially overturn the natural course of payment for labor.

But our business at present is not to compare or choose between the two systems, but to inquire which of the two is founded on experience.

The advocates of monopoly maintain that *the facts* are on their side, and that we have on our side only *theory*.

They flatter themselves that this long series of public acts—this *old experience* of Europe which they invoke—seemed very formidable to Jean-Baptiste Say <note>original: "M. Say."</note>. I admit that he has not refuted it with his usual wisdom. For my own part, I am not willing to give up the ground of *facts* to the monopolists; for they have only in their favor facts which are forced and exceptional. In contrast, we oppose these with facts which are universal: the free and voluntary acts of mankind at large.

What do we say, and what do they say?

We say,

<meta>This page continues the author's rebuttal against the protectionist argument that "theory" and "practice" are at odds. Having established that the protectionists claim their system is based on practical experience while free trade is merely abstract theory, the author now begins to test which principle people actually follow in their daily lives.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS <note>An "economic sophism" is a clever but false argument used to deceive people about how the economy works.</note>

"You should buy from others what you cannot make for yourself except at a greater expense."

And they <note>Referring to the protectionists mentioned on the previous page.</note> say,

"It is better to make things for yourself, even if they cost you more than the price at which you could buy them from others."

Now, gentlemen, setting aside theory, argument, and demonstration—all of which seem to nauseate you—which of these two assertions is supported by *universal practice*?

Visit your fields, your workshops, your forges, and your warehouses; look above, below, and around you; look at what happens in your own houses; observe your own everyday actions; and say what principle guides these laborers, artisans, and merchants. Tell me what your own personal *practice* is.

Does the farmer make his own clothes? Does the tailor grow the corn he eats? Does your housekeeper continue to have your bread made at home once she finds she can buy it cheaper from the baker? Do you put down the pen to pick up the brush just to avoid paying a small fee <note>original: "tribute"</note> to the shoeblack? Does the entire economy of society not rest upon the separation of professions and the division of labor—in a word, upon *exchange*? And what is exchange but a calculation we make so we can stop producing something directly whenever we find that acquiring it indirectly enables us to save time and effort?

Therefore, it is not you who are the "men of *practice*," since you cannot point to a single human being who actually acts according to your principle.

But you will say, "We never intended to make our principle a rule for individual relationships. We perfectly understand that this would break the bonds of society and force men to live like snails, each in his own shell. All we contend is that our principle regulates, in practice <note>original: "de facto"</note>, the laws that exist between the different groups <note>original: "agglomerations"</note> of the human family."

Well, I maintain that this principle is still wrong. The family, the community, the district, the department, the province—these are all groups <note>original: "agglomerations"</note> which, without any exception,

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that free trade is based on actual human behavior ("practice"), whereas protectionism is an artificial "theory" imposed by force. The author has just pointed out that in their private lives, even protectionists act like free traders by buying things wherever they are cheapest.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>

### THEORY AND PRACTICE

...reject your principle *in practice*, and have never even dreamed of acting on it. Everyone obtains for themselves, through exchange, those things that would cost them more to produce for themselves. And nations would do the same if you did not stop them *by force*.

We, therefore, are the men of practice and experience. We contrast the restrictions which you have placed specifically on certain international exchanges with the practice and experience of all individuals, and of all groups <note>original: "agglomerations"</note> of individuals, whose acts are voluntary and can therefore be used as evidence. But you begin by *forcing* and *hindering* people, and then you point to these *forced* or *prohibited* acts as evidence to claim, "We have practice and experience on our side!"

You attack <note>original: "inveigh against"</note> our *theory*, and even *theories* in general. But when you establish a principle in opposition to ours, do you perhaps imagine you are not also acting on a *theory*? Get that idea out of your heads. In fact, you form a theory just as we do; but between your theory and ours, there is this difference:

Our theory consists merely of observing universal *facts* and universal opinions—the calculations and ways of acting that prevail everywhere—and then classifying and organizing them so they can be more easily understood.

Our theory is so far from being opposed to practice that it is nothing else but *practice explained*. We observe people acting as they are moved by the instinct of self-preservation and a desire for progress; what they do freely and voluntarily, we call <note>original: "denominate"</note> political or social economy. We must keep repeating that every individual man is *practically* an excellent economist, producing or exchanging according to whether he finds it more in his interest to produce or to exchange. Through experience, everyone educates themselves in this science; or rather, the science itself is only this same experience accurately observed and systematically explained.

But on your side, you construct a *theory* in the worst sense of the word. You imagine and invent a way of proceeding that is not supported by the practice of any living person on earth <note>original: "under the canopy of heaven"</note>; and then you call for the help of force and prohibition. It is quite necessary that you should—

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of protectionist logic. He argues that protectionists rely on "force" because their theories contradict how people naturally act in their own best interest. He dismisses the idea that what is good for an individual (buying cheap) could somehow be bad for a nation.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>

->ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.<-

...have recourse to *force*, because you desire that men should be made to produce those things which they find it *more advantageous* to buy. You desire that they should renounce this *advantage* and act upon a doctrine that is a contradiction in terms.

The doctrine you advocate would be absurd in the relationships between individuals; I challenge you to extend it, even in theory, to transactions between families, communities, or provinces. By your own admission, it is only applicable to international relations.

This is the reason why you are forced to keep repeating:
> "There are no absolute principles, no inflexible rules. What is *good* for an individual, a family, or a province is *bad* for a nation. What is *good* in detail—namely, to purchase rather than produce when purchasing is more advantageous than producing—is *bad* for the whole nation. The political economy of individuals is not the same as that of nations;" and other nonsense of the same sort <note>original: "ejusdem farinæ"</note>.

And where does all this lead? Look at it a little closer. The intention is to prove that we, the consumers, are your property! That we belong to you, body and soul! That you have an exclusive right over our stomachs and our limbs! That it is up to you to feed and clothe us on your own terms, regardless of your ignorance, incompetence, or greed!

No, you are not men of practical experience; you are men of abstract theory—and of extortion.

## XIV.

### CONFLICT OF PRINCIPLES.

There is one thing that confuses me, and it is this:

Sincere writers on public policy <note>original: "publicists"</note>, studying the economy of society from the producer's point of view, have established this double formula:
> "Governments should regulate the interests of consumers who are subject to their laws in such a way as to be favorable to national industry.
> "They should bring distant consumers under subjection to..."

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionism is an abstract theory imposed by force, which falsely claims that national economic health requires individuals to act against their own private interests. He begins a new section examining the "Conflict of Principles" found in the writings of pro-industry theorists.</summary>

<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, Economic Sophisms, protectionism, consumer rights, national industry, political economy, trade theory, conflict of principles</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of protectionism, specifically focusing on how the "Balance of Trade" theory leads to both trade restrictions at home and colonial aggression abroad.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>

<header>CONFLICT OF PRINCIPLES.</header>

...their laws, for the purpose of organizing their interests in a way favorable to national industry."

The first of these formulas is called "protection"; the second we call *market outlets* <note>original: "débouchés"</note>, or the creation of markets—outlets for our produce.

Both are founded on the assumption <note>original: "datum"</note> which we call the *Balance of Trade*:

> "A nation is impoverished when it imports; enriched when it exports."

For if every purchase from a foreign country is a *tribute paid* and a national loss, it follows, of course, that it is right to restrain, and even prohibit, importations.

And if every sale to a foreign country is a *tribute received*, and a national profit, it is quite right and natural to create markets for our products even by force.

The *system of protection* and the *colonial system* are, then, only two aspects of one and the same theory. To hinder our fellow citizens from buying from foreigners, and to force foreigners to buy from our fellow citizens, are only two consequences of one and the same principle.

Now, it is impossible not to admit that this doctrine, if true, makes the general public good depend on *monopoly* (internal plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note>) and on *conquest* (external plunder).

I enter a cottage on the French side of the Pyrenees <note>The mountain range forming the border between France and Spain.</note>.

The father of the family has received only meager wages. His half-naked children shiver in the icy north wind; the fire is out, and there is nothing on the table. There are wool, firewood, and corn on the other side of the mountain; but these good things are forbidden to the poor day-laborer, for the other side of the mountain is not in France. Foreign firewood is not allowed to warm the cottage hearth; the shepherd's children can never know the taste of Biscayan corn <note>original: "méture," a type of mixed-grain bread</note>, and the wool of Navarre <note>A region in northern Spain.</note> can never warm their numbed limbs. "General utility" has ordered it so. Be it so; but let us agree that all this is in direct opposition to the first principles of justice. To legally manipulate the interests of consumers and postpone them to the supposed interests of national...

---

<note>The French word used is *méture*, likely a French version of the Spanish word *mestura*—meaning meslin, or mixed corn, such as wheat and rye. —TRANSLATOR.</note>

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionism and colonialism both stem from the "Balance of Trade" fallacy, which views imports as a loss and exports as a gain. He illustrates the human cost of these policies through the image of a poor French family barred by law from buying cheap Spanish goods.</summary>
