--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of protectionist theory, arguing that if the "Balance of Trade" theory were true, it would mean that economic prosperity is fundamentally at odds with justice and international peace.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS <note>A "sophism" is a fallacious argument, especially one used deliberately to deceive.</note></header>

...industry, is to encroach upon their liberty—it is to prohibit an action, namely the act of exchange, which contains nothing contrary to good morals. In a word, it is to commit an act of injustice against them.

And yet we are told this is necessary, unless we wish to see national labor come to a standstill and public prosperity suffer a fatal shock.

Writers of the protectionist school, then, have arrived at the melancholy conclusion that there is a fundamental incompatibility between justice and utility <note>In this context, "utility" refers to the material well-being or economic benefit of a nation.</note>.

On the other hand, if it is in the interest of each nation to *sell* and not to *buy*, the natural state of their relations must consist of violent action and reaction; for each will seek to impose its products on everyone else, and all will endeavor to reject the products of each.

A sale, in fact, implies a purchase. Since according to this doctrine selling is beneficial and buying is the opposite, every international transaction would imply the improvement of one people and the deterioration of another.

But if men are, on the one hand, irresistibly drawn toward what is for their profit, and if, on the other, they instinctively resist what is hurtful, we are forced to conclude that each nation carries within itself a natural force of expansion and an equally natural force of resistance. These forces are equally injurious to all other nations; or, in other words, antagonism and war are the *natural* state of human society.

Thus, the theory we are discussing may be summed up in these two axioms:

> Economic utility is incompatible with justice at home.
> Economic utility is incompatible with peace abroad.

Now, what astonishes and confounds me is that any political writer <note>original: "publicist"</note> or statesman who sincerely holds an economic doctrine that runs so violently counter to other undeniable principles should be able to enjoy even one moment of calm or peace of mind.

For my own part, it seems to me that if I had entered the fields of this science through the same gate—if I had failed to perceive clearly that liberty, utility, justice, and peace are things...

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionist logic leads to the bleak conclusion that a nation's economic success requires domestic injustice and international warfare.</summary>

<keywords>protectionism, free trade, economic utility, justice, international relations, Bastiat, trade theory, conflict of principles</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that if economic "laws" suggest that prosperity requires injustice, then those laws must be fundamentally flawed and contrary to the nature of God’s world.</meta>

# CONFLICT OF PRINCIPLES. 77

...not only compatible but closely allied with each other—and, so to speak, identical—I would have tried to forget what I had learned, and I would have asked:

> "How could God have willed that men should only achieve prosperity through injustice and war? How could He have willed that they should be unable to avoid injustice and war without giving up the possibility of achieving prosperity?
> 
> "Dare I adopt as the foundation for a great nation's laws a science that misleads me with false lights <note>original: "false lights"; here meaning deceptive or misleading ideas</note>, leads me to this horrible blasphemy, and lands me in such a terrible dilemma? Many famous philosophers have spent their lives studying this science and have reached more comforting conclusions. They claim that Liberty and Utility <note>Utility here refers to material well-being or economic benefit.</note> are perfectly compatible with Justice and Peace. They argue that all these great principles run in parallel forever without ever clashing. 
> 
> "Don't these philosophers have in their favor the assumption that comes from everything we know about God's goodness and wisdom, as seen in the magnificent harmony of the physical world? Given such an assumption and so many reliable authorities, should I easily believe that God chose to plant conflict and discord in the laws of the moral world? No. Before I would dare conclude that the principles of social order clash, cancel each other out, and are in eternal, irreconcilable opposition—and before I would dare impose on my fellow citizens a system as unholy <note>original: "impious"</note> as my reasoning seems to suggest—I would re-examine every step of that reasoning. I would make sure that I hadn't lost my way at some point in the journey."

But if, after a sincere and thorough examination repeated twenty times, I always reached this frightening conclusion—that we must choose between what is Right and what is Good—I would be so discouraged that I would reject the science and hide myself in voluntary ignorance. Above all, I would refuse to participate in public affairs, leaving the heavy responsibility of such a painful choice to people with a different temperament and character.

<summary>Bastiat argues that economic principles must align with moral truths; if a theory suggests that prosperity requires injustice, the theory itself must be flawed rather than the moral order of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Bastiat, economic harmony, justice vs. utility, social order, moral world, divine wisdom, protectionism critique, philosophy of economics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of protectionist arguments. Having just argued that the laws of economics must align with justice and the divine order, he now turns to the specific "sophism" or logical fallacy regarding "reciprocity" in trade—the idea that we should only buy from those who buy directly from us.</meta>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

## XV.
# RECIPROCITY AGAIN.

Mr. de Saint-Cricq <note>Pierre de Saint-Cricq (1772–1854) was a French politician and proponent of protectionism.</note> asks, "Is it certain that the foreigner will buy from us as much as he sells to us?"

Mr. de Dombasle <note>Mathieu de Dombasle (1777–1843) was a prominent French agriculturalist and supporter of trade restrictions.</note> asks, "What reason do we have to believe that English producers will take from us, rather than from some other country in the world, the goods they need—and in an amount equal in value to their exports to France?"

I wonder how so many men who call themselves *practical* men could have reasoned so completely without regard for actual practice!

In practice, does a single exchange take place—out of a hundred, a thousand, or perhaps ten thousand—that represents the direct barter of one product for another? Never since the invention of money has any farmer said: "I want to buy shoes, hats, advice, and lessons, but only from the shoemaker, the hat-maker, the lawyer, or the professor who will buy grain from me of exactly equal value." Why, then, should nations impose such a burden on each other?

How are such matters actually transacted?

Let us imagine a people isolated from foreign trade. A man, let us suppose, produces wheat. He sends it to the *domestic* market and offers it for the highest price he can get. He receives in exchange—what? Coins, which are essentially just "vouchers" or orders <note>original: "drafts or orders"; here meaning money acts as a claim on future goods</note>, varying in amount. By means of these, he can, in his turn, draw from the nation's stock of goods whenever he thinks it proper—subject to fair competition—everything he may want or desire. Ultimately, at the end of the process, he will have taken from the total supply the exact equivalent of what he contributed to it. In terms of value, *his consumption will exactly equal his production.*

If the trades of this nation with foreigners are left free, it is no longer to the *national* market, but to the *global* <note>original: "general"</note> market, that each person sends his contributions and, in turn, gets the goods he consumes. He has no need to worry whether...

<summary>Bastiat refutes the idea that international trade must be a direct barter between two nations, explaining that money allows for multilateral exchange where production and consumption naturally balance out.</summary>
<keywords>reciprocity, balance of trade, money vs barter, Saint-Cricq, Dombasle, free trade, production and consumption</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s argument that international trade functions exactly like domestic trade. He concludes Chapter XV by showing that "isolation" is the ultimate goal of protectionism, then begins Chapter XVI with a satirical anecdote about how improving infrastructure (like rivers) is often viewed as a threat by those who favor trade restrictions.</meta>

<header>->RECIPROCITY AGAIN.<-</header>

...it does not matter whether what he sends into the market of the world is purchased by a fellow countryman or by a foreigner; whether the payment orders <note>original: "drafts or orders"; here referring to money or bills of exchange acting as claims on goods</note> he receives come from a Frenchman or an Englishman; or whether the commodities for which he later exchanges these orders are produced on this or on the other side of the Rhine or the Pyrenees. There is always, in every individual case, an exact balance between what is contributed and what is received—between what is poured into and what is drawn out of the great common reservoir of the market. And if this is true for each individual, it is true for the nation as a whole.

The only difference between the two cases is that in the latter, everyone has access to a much larger market for both sales and purchases. Consequently, everyone has a better chance of conducting both types of transactions to their advantage.

This objection may perhaps be raised: "If everyone joins a league to refuse the products of a specific individual, that individual cannot, in turn, obtain from the general mass the things he needs." The same could be said of nations.

The reply to this is that if a nation cannot obtain what it needs in the global market, it will no longer contribute anything to that market. It will work only for itself. In that case, it will be forced to submit to exactly what you want to impose on it beforehand—*isolation*.

And this would realize the ideal of the protectionist system <note>original: "prohibitive régime"</note>. Is it not amusing to think that you inflict this very system upon the nation now, out of a fear that it might otherwise run the risk of reaching that state on its own?

---

## BLOCKED NAVIGATION ARGUING FOR PROTECTIONISM.

Some years ago, I happened to be in Madrid and attended a session of the Spanish Parliament <note>original: "Cortes"</note>. The subject of debate was a proposed treaty with Portugal for improving the navigation of the Douro River. One of the...

It then introduces Chapter XVII, "A Negative Railway," where Bastiat continues to explore the absurdity of prioritizing producers over consumers.</meta>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

The deputies rose and said: “If the navigation of the Douro River is improved in the way now proposed, shipping will be carried out at less expense. As a result, the grain of Portugal will be sold in the markets of Castile <note>A historical region in central Spain.</note> at a lower price and will become a formidable rival to our *national industry*. I oppose the project, unless, of course, our ministers will undertake to raise the customs tariffs to the level required to re-establish the balance.” The Assembly found the argument unanswerable.

Three months later I was in Lisbon. The same question was discussed in the Senate. A noble hidalgo <term>A member of the Spanish or Portuguese nobility.</term> made a speech: “Mr. President,” he said, “this project is absurd. You place guards, at great expense, along the banks of the Douro to prevent Portugal from being invaded by Castilian grain; and at the same time you propose, also at great expense, to facilitate that invasion. This is an inconsistency to which I cannot agree. Let us leave the Douro to our children just as it has come to us from our fathers.”

Afterward, when the subject of improving the navigation of the Garonne <note>A major river in southwest France.</note> was discussed, I remembered the arguments of the Iberian orators, and I said to myself: If the Toulouse deputies were as good at economics as the Spanish deputies, and the representatives of Bordeaux as sharp at logic as those of Oporto, they would surely leave the Garonne

->“To sleep to the flattering sound of its rising wave;” <note>original: “Dormir au bruit flatteur de son onde naissante;” a line from Jean de La Fontaine’s poem, “The Oak and the Reed.”</note><-

for the canalization <note>original: canalisation; the process of making a river navigable or turning it into a canal.</note> of the Garonne would favor the *invasion* of products from Toulouse to the disadvantage of Bordeaux, and the flood of products from Bordeaux would do the same thing to the detriment of Toulouse.

### XVII.

## A NEGATIVE RAILWAY.

I HAVE said that when, unfortunately, one considers the interest of the producer and not that of the consumer, it is impossible to avoid working against the general interest.

<summary>Bastiat uses a satirical story about Spanish and Portuguese politicians rejecting river improvements to show that protectionists view efficient transportation as a threat to local industry. He introduces the concept of the "Negative Railway" to further illustrate the absurdity of creating obstacles to trade.</summary>
<keywords>Douro River, Garonne River, Castile, Lisbon, protectionism, national industry, tariffs, infrastructure, consumer interest, producer interest</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page begins Chapter XVII, "A Negative Railway," where Bastiat applies the logic of protectionism—prioritizing the work of producers over the needs of consumers—to the infrastructure of a railway line.</meta>

<header>A NEGATIVE RAILWAY.</header>
<sig>F</sig>

...because the demand of the producer, as such, is only for effort, wants, and obstacles.

I find a remarkable illustration of this in a Bordeaux newspaper.

M. Simiot proposes this question:

->Should the proposed railway from Paris to Madrid offer a break in the line <note>original: "solution of continuity," a phrase meaning a gap or interruption in a continuous path.</note> at Bordeaux?<-

He answers the question in the affirmative, and gives a multiplicity of reasons, which I shall not stop to examine, except for this one:

The railway from Paris to Bayonne should have a break at Bordeaux; for if goods and passengers are forced to stop at that town, profits will benefit bargemen, peddlers, commission agents <note>original: "commissionaires," agents who handle the shipment and sale of goods for others.</note>, hotel-keepers, etc.

Here we have clearly the interest of labor put before the interest of consumers.

But if Bordeaux has a right to profit by a gap in the line of railway, and if such profit is consistent with the public interest, then Angoulême, Poitiers, Tours, Orleans—nay, more, all the intermediate places, such as Ruffec, Châtellerault, etc.—should also demand gaps, as being for the general interest, and, of course, for the interest of national industry. For the more these breaks in the line are multiplied, the greater will be the increase of consignments, commissions, transshipments, etc., along the whole extent of the railway. In this way, we shall succeed in having a line of railway composed of successive gaps, which may be called a *Negative Railway*.

Let the protectionists <term>Advocates for government policies that restrict international trade to help domestic industries.</term> say what they will, it is no less certain that *the principle of restriction* is the very same as the *principle of gaps*: the sacrifice of the consumer’s interest to that of the producer—in other words, the sacrifice of the end to the means.

<summary>Bastiat satirizes a proposal to intentionally create a gap in a railway line at Bordeaux to generate work for local porters and businesses, arguing that this "Negative Railway" logic is the same as protectionism.</summary>
<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, protectionism, free trade, railway, infrastructure, Bordeaux, producer vs consumer, economic logic, Negative Railway</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific example of the "negative railway" to a broader philosophical critique. Bastiat addresses the common argument used by politicians to avoid taking a firm stance on trade: the claim that there are no "absolute principles" in economics.</meta>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

### XVIII.

## THERE ARE NO ABSOLUTE PRINCIPLES.

WE cannot wonder enough at the ease with which men resign themselves to remaining ignorant of what is most important for them to know; and we may be certain that such ignorance is incurable in those who dare to proclaim this axiom: "There are no absolute principles."

Imagine you enter the legislative chambers. The subject of debate is whether the law should prohibit international trade <note>original: "exchanges"</note> or proclaim its freedom.

A representative <note>original: "deputy," referring to a member of the French Chamber of Deputies</note> stands and says:

"If you allow these trades, the foreigner will flood you with his products: England with her textiles, Belgium with coal, Spain with wool, Italy with silk, Switzerland with cattle, Sweden with iron, and Prussia with grain <note>original: "corn," which in 19th-century European contexts refers to cereal grains like wheat rather than maize.</note>; as a result, domestic industry will no longer be possible."

Another replies:

"If you prohibit international trade, the various gifts that nature has showered upon different climates will, for you, be as if they did not exist. You cannot share in the mechanical skill of the English, in the wealth of the Belgian mines, in the fertility of the Polish soil, in the richness of the Swiss pastures, in the cheapness of Spanish labor, or in the warmth of the Italian climate. You will be forced to obtain through difficult <note>original: "refractory," meaning stubborn or unyielding</note> and misguided production those goods which, through trade, would have been provided to you by easy production."

Certainly, one of these representatives must be wrong. But which one? We must take care to make no mistake on this subject, for this is not merely a matter of abstract opinion. You have to choose between two paths, and one of them leads inevitably to *poverty*.

To escape this dilemma, we are told that "there are no absolute principles."

This axiom, which is so fashionable nowadays, not only encourages laziness but also serves personal ambition.

If the theory of prohibition <term>A policy of restricting or banning foreign trade to protect domestic industries from competition.</term> comes to prevail, or if the doctrine...

<summary>Frédéric Bastiat critiques the dismissive claim that there are no absolute principles in economics, arguing that this mindset is used to avoid choosing between free trade and protectionism. He illustrates that while politicians vacillate, the choice between these two systems is actually a choice between national wealth and poverty.</summary>
<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, Economic Sophisms, free trade, protectionism, absolute principles, international trade, legislative debate, domestic industry, economic theory</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that economic laws are simple when based on absolute principles. It contrasts the complexity of government regulation with the spontaneous order of a free market, using the famous "Provisioning of Paris" example.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>

# THERE ARE NO ABSOLUTE PRINCIPLES.

If the idea of free trade triumphs, one brief law will make up our entire economic code. In the first case <note>referring to a system of protectionism</note>, the law will proclaim that *all exchanges with foreign countries are prohibited*; in the second <note>referring to free trade</note>, that *all exchanges with foreign countries are free*. If this happens, many high-ranking and distinguished officials will lose their positions of importance.

But suppose trade does not have its own unique character—that it is not governed by any natural law. Suppose it is randomly useful at some times and harmful at others. Suppose its driving force is not the good it accomplishes, and its limit is not the point where it ceases to be beneficial. If its consequences cannot be judged by those who actually engage in the trades—in a word, if there are no absolute principles—then we must step in to weigh, balance, and regulate every transaction. We would have to equalize the conditions of labor and try to find the average rate of profits. This would be a monumental task, well-deserving the high salaries and powerful influence granted to those who undertake it.

Upon entering Paris for a visit, I said to myself: "Here are a million human beings who would all die in a short time if provisions of every kind stopped flowing toward this great metropolis." The imagination struggles to grasp the vast variety of goods that must enter through the city gates <note>original: "barriers," referring to the tax and customs stations located at the entrances to Paris</note> tomorrow to save the inhabitants from famine, rebellion, and looting. And yet, everyone is sleeping at this moment. Their peaceful rest is not disturbed for a single second by the prospect of such a terrible catastrophe. 

On the other hand, eighty different districts <note>original: "departments," the administrative regions of France</note> have been working today to provide for Paris, without any central plan or mutual agreement. How does each succeeding day bring exactly what is wanted—nothing more and nothing less—to such a gigantic market? What, then, is the ingenious and secret power that governs the amazing regularity of such complicated movements? It is a regularity in which everyone has absolute faith, even though their happiness and life itself are at stake. That power is an *absolute principle*: the principle of freedom in transactions. We have faith in that inner light which Providence <note>God or a guiding spiritual force</note> has placed in the hearts of all men, and to which He has entrusted the preservation and endless improvement of our species: namely, a regard for...

<summary>Bastiat argues that economic systems are either simple and free or complex and regulated. He uses the daily feeding of Paris as an example of "spontaneous order," where free individuals meet society's needs without central planning.</summary>

<keywords>free trade, absolute principles, spontaneous order, Paris, economic regulation, Frédéric Bastiat, Providence, market coordination</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat's argument regarding the "Provisioning of Paris," illustrating how the complex needs of a city are met through individual self-interest rather than government planning. It transitions into a critique of protectionism based on the concept of "national independence."</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

Self-interest—since we must call it by its right name—is a principle so active, so vigilant, and so forward-thinking when it is free to act. In what situation, I ask, would the inhabitants of Paris find themselves if a government minister took it into his head to replace this natural power with the schemes of his own genius, however superior we might imagine them to be? What if he thought to subject this massive mechanism to his supreme direction, to hold its controls in his hands, and to decide by whom, in what manner, or on what conditions everything needed should be produced, transported, exchanged, and consumed? 

Truly, there may be much suffering within the walls of Paris—poverty, despair, and perhaps starvation, causing more tears to flow than even the most passionate charity is able to dry up. But I maintain that it is probable—nay, it is certain—that the arbitrary intervention of government would multiply those sufferings infinitely. It would spread over all our fellow citizens those evils which currently affect only a small number of them.

If we place this much faith in a principle when it relates to our domestic transactions, why should we not maintain it when the same principle is applied to our international transactions? International trade is undoubtedly less frequent, less delicate, and less complicated than local trade. And if it is not necessary for the local government <note>original: "préfecture"; the administrative office of a French department or city</note> to regulate our Parisian industries, weigh our chances, balance our profits and losses, ensure that our money supply is not exhausted, or equalize the conditions of our domestic labor, why should it be necessary for the Customs office, moving beyond its duty to collect taxes, to claim to exercise a protective control over our foreign commerce?

## NATIONAL INDEPENDENCE.

Among the arguments we hear brought forward in favor of a protectionist system <note>original: "restrictive régime"</note>, we must not forget the one founded on **national independence**.

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat's critique of protectionism, moving from the internal logistics of a city like Paris to international relations. He challenges the common argument that "national independence" requires a country to be self-sufficient through trade barriers.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>NATIONAL INDEPENDENCE.</header>

“What would we do in a time of war,” people ask, “if we were left at the mercy of England for our iron and coal?”

English monopolists, in their turn, do not fail to cry out:

“What would become of Great Britain during a war if she were dependent on France for her food supplies?”

People overlook one thing: the kind of dependence that results from exchange—from commercial transactions—is a *reciprocal dependence*. We cannot be dependent on a foreigner without that foreigner being equally dependent on us. This, in fact, is the very essence of *society*. To break up these natural relationships is not to place ourselves in a state of independence, but rather in a state of isolation.

Note this: a nation isolates itself in anticipation of the possibility of war; but is not this very act of isolation the beginning of war? It makes war easier to start, less of a financial burden, and perhaps less unpopular. If countries were permanent markets for each other’s products—if their mutual relations were so tight that they could not be broken without inflicting the double suffering of scarcity and a surplus of unsold goods—then they would no longer need the naval fleets that ruin them or the massive armies that crush them. The peace of the world would no longer be threatened by the whims of a Thiers or a Palmerston <note>Adolphe Thiers was a French statesman and historian; Lord Palmerston was a dominant British Foreign Secretary and Prime Minister. Both were known for assertive nationalist policies.</note>. War would disappear for lack of what sustains it: a lack of resources, incentives, excuses, and popular support.

I am well aware that I will be criticized (as is the fashion today) for basing the brotherhood of nations on men’s personal interest—on "vile, dull self-interest." Some might think it would be far better if this brotherhood were based on charity, love, or even a little self-denial, and that it should have the merit of a generous sacrifice by interfering somewhat with men’s material comforts.

When will we be done with these childish speeches? When will <term>hypocrisy</term> <note>original: "tartuferie"; a term derived from Molière’s character Tartuffe, symbolizing religious or moral hypocrisy</note> finally be banished from the study of science? When will we stop showing this sickening contradiction between our public claims and our private actions? We boo and curse *personal interest*; in other words, we denounce what is useful and good (for to say that all men have an interest in something is to say that the thing is good in itself), as if personal interest were—

<summary>Bastiat argues that international trade creates a mutual dependence that discourages war, whereas protectionist isolation actually makes conflict more likely. He also defends "self-interest" as a practical and honest foundation for peace between nations.</summary>

<keywords>free trade, national independence, reciprocal dependence, isolationism, peace, Adolphe Thiers, Lord Palmerston, self-interest, hypocrisy</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of the moral arguments used to justify trade restrictions. He specifically challenges the idea that "self-interest" is a dirty word, arguing instead that the pursuit of well-being is a natural and divine mechanism for human progress.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

...not the necessary, eternal, and indestructible mainspring to which God <note>original: "Providence"</note> has entrusted the improvement of humanity. Are we not depicted as being all angels of total selflessness? And does the thought never occur to those who say so, that the public is beginning to see with disgust that this fake, pretentious language stains the pages of those very writers who are most successful in filling their own pockets at the public expense? Oh, pretension! Pretension! You are truly the defining sin of our age!

What! Because material prosperity and peace are interconnected, and because it has pleased God to establish this beautiful harmony in the moral world, am I not to admire, am I not to adore His decrees? Am I not to gratefully accept the laws that make justice a requirement for happiness? You desire peace only as long as it works against material prosperity, and liberty is rejected because it does not demand heavy sacrifices. If self-denial <note>original: "abnegation"</note> is indeed so appealing to you, why do you fail to practice it in your private life? Society would be grateful to you, for someone, at least, would reap the benefit. But to want to impose it upon all of mankind as a core principle is the very height of absurdity; for the self-sacrifice of everyone is the sacrifice of everything, which is simply evil turned into a theory.

But, thank Heaven, one can write or read many of these dramatic speeches without the world stopping, for that reason, to obey the social driving force which leads us to avoid evil and seek out good—a force which, whether they like it or not, we must call *personal interest*.

After all, it is strange enough to see claims of the most noble self-denial used to support outright plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note>. Look at what this boasted selflessness actually leads to! These men, who are so strangely sensitive that they do not want peace itself if it is founded on the "vile" *interest* of mankind, are the same ones who reach into the pockets of others, and especially the poor. For what part of the trade taxes <note>original: "tariff"</note> actually protects the poor? Please, gentlemen, do whatever you like with what belongs to you, but leave us the right to handle the fruits of our own hard work—to use it or exchange it as we see fit. Lecture us on *self-sacrifice* as much as you choose; it is all very fine and very beautiful, but at least be consistent.

<summary>Bastiat argues that self-interest is a natural, God-given engine for human progress and critiques the hypocrisy of protectionists who preach "self-sacrifice" while using tariffs to profit at the expense of the poor.</summary>
<keywords>personal interest, self-sacrifice, hypocrisy, protectionism, tariffs, economic justice, human perfectibility, spoliation</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Bastiat’s philosophical defense of self-interest and divine harmony into a specific economic argument regarding the logical inconsistency of protectionists who embrace technology while fearing foreign trade.</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>HUMAN LABOR, NATIONAL LABOR.</header>

### XX

# HUMAN LABOR, NATIONAL LABOR.

Machine-breaking <note>This refers to "Luddism," the practice of workers destroying labor-saving machinery to protect their jobs.</note> and the prohibition of foreign goods are two actions based on the same underlying theory.

We see people who applaud when a great invention is introduced, yet still support a protectionist system <note>original: "régime"</note>. These people are completely inconsistent!

What is their complaint against free trade? They claim it encourages foreigners—who are more skilled or better situated than we are—to produce goods that would otherwise be made at home. In short, they accuse free trade of harming *national labor*.

For that same reason, shouldn't they also criticize machinery for using natural forces to accomplish what would otherwise require manual effort, and therefore for harming *human labor*?

A foreign worker who is in a better position than a domestic worker to produce certain goods is, compared to the domestic worker, a literal *economic machine* that crushes him through competition. Similarly, a machine that performs a task more cheaply than a group of men can do by hand is, compared to those manual laborers, a *foreign competitor* who paralyzes them through rivalry.

If it is considered wise policy to protect *national labor* from the competition of *foreign labor*, it is no less wise to protect *human labor* from the rivalry of *mechanical labor*.

Therefore, any supporter of protectionism, if they are being logical, should not stop at banning foreign products; they should also ban the products of the weaving shuttle and the plow <note>The author uses the shuttle and the plow to represent the technological advancements of the Industrial and Agricultural Revolutions.</note>.

This is why I prefer the logic of those who, while speaking out against the *invasion* of foreign goods, also speak out against the *excess of production* caused by the inventive power of the human mind.

<vocab>Machine-breaking, free trade, protectionism, national labor, human labor, mechanical labor, economic machine, competition, system of protection</vocab>

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionism and the opposition to labor-saving machinery are logically identical, as both attempt to protect jobs by banning more efficient competition. He points out the inconsistency of those who welcome technology but fear foreign trade.</summary>
<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, protectionism, free trade, automation, Luddism, economic logic, industrial revolution, labor competition</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between foreign trade and mechanical invention, specifically citing the protectionist writer M. de Saint-Chamans to illustrate the logical conclusion of the anti-machinery argument.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

Monsieur de Saint-Chamans <note>Auguste, vicomte de Saint-Chamans (1777–1861) was a French politician and advocate for protectionism.</note> is such a man. He says, “One of the strongest arguments against free trade is the excessive use of machinery; for many workmen lose their jobs, either because of foreign competition, which reduces the price of our manufactured goods, or because of mechanical instruments which take the place of men in our workshops.”*

M. de Saint-Chamans has clearly seen the analogy—or rather, the identity—between *imports* and *machinery*. For this reason, he condemns <note>original: "proscribes"</note> both. It is truly refreshing to deal with such bold thinkers who, even when they are wrong, carry their arguments to their logical conclusion.

But here is the predicament <note>original: "mess"</note> in which they find themselves:

If it were theoretically true <note>original: "a priori"</note> that the realm of *invention* and the realm of *labor* cannot grow at the same time except at each other's expense, then we should expect to find the fewest *workers* in those countries where machinery is most abundant—in Lancashire <note>A county in Northwest England that was the heart of the industrial revolution and textile manufacturing.</note>, for example. And if, on the other hand, we establish the *fact* that mechanical power and manual labor exist together to a greater extent among wealthy nations than among primitive societies, the conclusion is unavoidable: these two powers do not exclude each other.

I cannot understand how any thinking person can have a moment’s peace when faced with the following dilemma:

Either the inventions of man are not harmful to manual labor—as general facts prove, since there are more of both in England and France than among the Hurons and Cherokees—and if that is the case, I am on the wrong track, though I do not know where or when I lost my way. At any rate, I see I am wrong, and I would be committing a crime against humanity <note>original: "lese-humanity," a play on the term "lèse-majesté" or treason.</note> if I were to introduce my error into the laws of my country.

Or else, the discoveries of the human mind limit the amount of manual labor, as specific instances seem to suggest; for every day I see some machine or other replacing twenty or a hundred workers. If this is true, I am forced to admit a glaring, permanent, and hopeless contradiction <note>original: "antithesis"</note> between the intellectual and physical powers of man—between his progress and his current well-being. In these circumstances, I am forced to conclude...

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the perceived conflict between human invention (machinery) and human labor, specifically responding to the claim that economic science has no fixed principles.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>HUMAN LABOR, NATIONAL LABOR.</header>

...to say that the Creator of man might have endowed him with reason, or with physical strength; with moral force, or with brute force; but that He mocked him by conferring on him, at the same time, faculties which are destructive of each other.

The difficulty is pressing and puzzling; but you contrive to find your way out of it by adopting this strange maxim <note>original: "apophthegm"—a concise saying or pointed maxim</note>:

*In political economy, there are no absolute principles.*

In plain language, this means:

> "I do not know whether it is true or false; I am ignorant of what constitutes the general good or evil. I give myself no trouble about that. The immediate effect of each measure upon my own personal interest is the only law which I can consent to recognize."

There are no principles! You might as well say there are no facts; for principles are merely formulas which classify such facts as are well established.

Machinery and the importation of foreign commodities certainly produce effects. These effects may be good or bad; on that, there may be a difference of opinion. But whatever view we take of them, it is reduced to a formula by one of these two *principles*: machinery is a good, or machinery is an evil; imports of foreign produce are beneficial, or such importations are hurtful. But to assert that *there are no principles* certainly exhibits the lowest degree of degradation <note>original: "abasement"</note> to which the human mind can descend. I confess that I blush for my country when I hear such a monstrous error <note>original: "heresy"</note> proclaimed in the French Chambers <note>The French legislative houses</note>, and with their assent—that is to say, in the face of and with the consent of the most prominent <note>original: "élite"</note> of our fellow citizens. This is done in order to justify their imposing laws upon us in total ignorance of the real state of the case.

But then I am told to debunk the fallacy <note>original: "destroy the sophism"</note> by proving that machinery is not hurtful to *human labor*, nor the importation of foreign products to *national labor*.

A work like this cannot easily include very full or complete demonstrations. My design is rather to state difficulties than to resolve them; to encourage reflection rather than to satisfy doubts. No conviction makes so lasting an impression on the mind as that which it works out for itself. But I shall endeavor, nevertheless, to put the reader on the right road.

<summary>The author critiques the idea that there are no "absolute principles" in economics, arguing that this claim is merely an excuse for ignorance or self-interest. He maintains that one must determine if machinery and imports are inherently good or bad rather than dismissing economic principles altogether.</summary>
<keywords>political economy, economic principles, machinery, foreign imports, French Chambers, human labor, national labor, economic sophisms</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical breakdown of why technological progress and imports do not decrease the total amount of work available in a nation, but rather increase the standard of living.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS
<note>original: "Sophisms"—fallacious arguments, especially those used deliberately to deceive.</note>

What misleads the opponents of machinery and foreign imports is that they judge them by their immediate and temporary effects, instead of following them through to their general and final consequences.

The immediate effect of inventing and using an ingenious machine is to make a certain amount of manual labor unnecessary for achieving a specific result. But the process does not stop there. Because the desired result is obtained with less effort, the product is sold to the public at a lower price. The total savings realized by all buyers allows them to fulfill other needs; that is to say, they can support manual labor in general to the exact extent that labor was saved in the specific industry that was recently improved. Consequently, the overall level of labor has not fallen, while the level of enjoyment has risen.

Let us make this clear with an example.

Suppose ten million hats are sold annually in this country at 15 shillings <note>A shilling was a unit of British currency; 20 shillings made one pound sterling (£).</note> each. This means £7,500,000 is spent to support this branch of industry. A machine is then invented that allows these hats to be manufactured and sold for 10 shillings. The money now needed to support this industry is reduced to £5,000,000, assuming the demand does not increase because of the change. 

However, the remaining sum of £2,500,000 is not withdrawn from the support of **human labor** by this change. That money, saved by the buyers of hats, will enable them to satisfy other wants. Consequently, that amount will go toward paying for the total industry of the country. With the five shillings saved, John will purchase a pair of shoes, James a book, and Jerome a piece of furniture, and so on. 

Human labor, taken as a whole, will continue to be supported and encouraged to the level of £7,500,000. However, this same sum will now provide the same number of hats, plus all the extra satisfactions and enjoyments worth £2,500,000 that the use of the machine has allowed the consumers of hats to save. These additional enjoyments represent the clear profit the country has gained from the invention. This is a free gift—a tribute that human genius has extracted from nature. We do not dispute at all...

<summary>Bastiat argues that while machinery may reduce labor in one specific industry, the money saved by consumers is spent elsewhere, maintaining the total demand for labor while increasing the nation's overall wealth.</summary>

<keywords>machinery, automation, manual labor, consumer savings, economic efficiency, Frédéric Bastiat, industrial progress</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that while technological or trade changes may shift where people work, they do not reduce the total amount of work available in a nation.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>

# HUMAN LABOR, NATIONAL LABOR.

...that in the course of the transformation, a certain amount of labor will have been *displaced*; but we cannot accept that it has been destroyed or diminished.

The same principle applies to the importation of foreign goods. Let us return to our previous example <note>original: "hypothesis"</note>.

Suppose the country manufactures ten million hats, which cost 15 shillings <note>A shilling was a unit of British currency; 20 shillings made one pound sterling (£). In the 1800s, 15 shillings was a significant amount, roughly equivalent to several days' wages for a laborer.</note> each. Now, a foreigner sends similar hats to our market and provides them at 10 shillings each. I maintain that *national labor* will not be reduced by this.

This is because the nation must still produce £5,000,000 worth of goods to pay for the ten million hats at 10 shillings each.

Furthermore, each purchaser is left with a saving of five shillings per hat, totaling £2,500,000. This money will be spent on other comforts—which is to say, it will go toward supporting labor in other branches of industry.

Therefore, the total labor of the country remains exactly what it was. The additional goods and services represented by the £2,500,000 saved on hats form the clear profit gained from imports under a system of free trade.

It is useless to try to frighten us with descriptions of the suffering that the displacement of labor would cause in this scenario.

If the prohibition <term>prohibition: A government trade policy that completely bans the import of certain foreign goods to protect domestic industries from competition.</term> had never been imposed, labor would have found its natural place under the normal laws of exchange, and no displacement would have occurred.

If, on the other hand, the prohibition led to labor being used in an artificial and unproductive way, then it is the prohibition—not liberty—that is to blame for the displacement. This shift is an inevitable part of the transition from a harmful system to a beneficial one.

In any case, no one should claim that just because an abuse cannot be ended without inconveniencing those who profit from it, something that has been suffered to exist for a time should be allowed to exist forever.

<summary>This page argues that importing cheaper foreign goods does not decrease total national labor; instead, it reallocates labor while leaving consumers with extra money to spend elsewhere. The author concludes that any temporary hardship caused by this shift is the fault of previous protectionist laws rather than free trade itself.</summary>

<keywords>free trade, labor displacement, national labor, protectionism, consumer savings, economic transition, imports</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that importing goods does not decrease total national labor, but rather shifts it toward more productive sectors while saving consumers money.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "Sophisms"</note></header>

## XXI.
# RAW MATERIALS.

It is said that the most beneficial of all branches of trade is that which provides manufactured goods in exchange for raw materials. This is because these raw materials are the fuel and support of *national labor*.

From this, the following conclusion is drawn:

> That the best customs law is that which makes it as easy as possible to import raw materials, while placing the most obstacles in the way of importing finished goods.

There is no fallacy <term>sophism: a clever but false argument, often used to deceive.</term> <note>original: "sophism"</note> in political economy more widely spread than this. It is cherished not only by the protectionist school, but also, and especially, by the school that calls itself "liberal." This is unfortunate, for what can be more harmful to a good cause than for it to be simultaneously attacked with vigor and defended with weakness?

Commercial freedom will likely share the fate of liberty in general; it will only find a place in the law books after it has taken possession of people’s minds and convictions. But if it is true that a reform must be generally understood to be firmly established, it follows that nothing can delay reform as much as that which misleads public opinion. And what is more calculated to mislead public opinion than works that, while advocating for freedom, call for help from the doctrines of monopoly?

Some years ago, three of the great cities of France—Lyons, Bordeaux, and Le Havre—joined together in a movement against the restrictive system <note>original: "restrictive régime"</note>. All of Europe was stirred by seeing what they took to be the banner of liberty raised. Alas! It proved to be also the banner of monopoly—a monopoly a little more stingy and much more absurd than the one they seemed to want to overthrow. By using the fallacy <note>original: "sophism"</note> which I have just tried to expose, the petitioners did nothing more than repeat the doctrine of *protection for national industry*, adding to it a further inconsistency.

<summary>Bastiat critiques the popular but flawed idea that trade policy should favor importing raw materials over finished goods, arguing that even self-proclaimed "liberals" mistakenly support this protectionist logic.</summary>
<keywords>raw materials, national labor, protectionism, free trade, political economy, France, trade policy, economic fallacies</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the "liberal" opposition to trade restrictions, specifically examining how even supposedly "free trade" groups like the Bordeaux petitioners still fall into the trap of protectionist logic.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>

<header>RAW MATERIALS.</header>

It was, in fact, nothing else than the system <note>original: "régime"</note> of prohibition. Just listen to Monsieur <note>original: "M."</note> de Saint-Cricq:

> "Labor constitutes the wealth of a nation, because labor alone creates those material objects which our needs demand; and universal prosperity consists in the abundance of these things." So much for the principle.

> "But this abundance must be produced by *domestic labor*. If it were the result of foreign labor, domestic labor would be immediately brought to a standstill." Here lies the error. (See the previous fallacy <term>sophism: a clever but false argument, often used to deceive.</term> <note>original: "sophism"</note>.)

> "What course should an agricultural and manufacturing country take under such circumstances? Reserve its markets for the products of its own soil and of its own industry." Such is the end and design.

> "And for that purpose, restrain imports by taxes <note>original: "duties"</note>, and, if necessary, prohibit the importation of products from the soil and industry of other nations." Such are the means.

Let us compare this system with the one that the Bordeaux petition advocates.

Commodities are there divided into three classes:

> "*The first includes food supplies <note>original: "provisions"</note> and raw materials upon which no human labor has been bestowed. In principle, a wise economy would demand that this class should be free of taxes.*" Here we have no labor, so there is no protection.

> "*The second consists of products which have, to some extent, been prepared. This preparation warrants such products being charged with a certain amount of tax.*" Here protection begins, because here, according to the petitioners, begins *domestic labor*.

> "*The third comprises goods and products in their finished and perfect state. These contribute nothing more to domestic labor, and we regard this class as the most taxable.*" Here labor, and production along with it, reach their highest point.

We thus see that the petitioners profess their belief in the doctrine that foreign labor is harmful <note>original: "injurious"</note> to domestic labor; and this is the *error* of the prohibitive system.

They demand that the home market should be reserved for home industry. That is the *design* of the system of prohibition.

<summary>This page analyzes the arguments of Monsieur de Saint-Cricq and the Bordeaux petition, showing how both rely on the mistaken belief that foreign labor harms domestic industry. The author points out that even "liberal" groups often mistakenly advocate for protectionist taxes based on how much a product has been processed.</summary>

<keywords>protectionism, domestic labor, foreign labor, raw materials, Bordeaux petition, Saint-Cricq, trade taxes, economic fallacies</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the protectionist policies of Monsieur de Saint-Cricq and the seemingly more moderate "Bordeaux petitioners," arguing that Saint-Cricq’s logic is more consistent, even if his conclusions are wrong.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS</header>

They demand that foreign labor should be subjected to restrictions and taxes. These are the *means* employed by the system of prohibition.

What difference, then, can we possibly discover between the Bordeaux petitioners and the leader <term>corypheus: the leader of a chorus in ancient Greek drama; here used to mean a chief spokesperson.</term> <note>original: "Corypheus"</note> of restriction? One difference, and one only—the greater or lesser scope given to the word *labor*.

Monsieur <note>original: "M."</note> de Saint-Cricq extends it to everything, and so he wishes to protect *all*.

"Labor constitutes *all* the wealth of a people," he says; "to protect agricultural industry, and *all* agricultural industry; to protect manufacturing industry, and *all* manufacturing industry, is the cry which should never cease to be heard in this Chamber."

The Bordeaux petitioners take no labor into account but that of the manufacturers; and for that reason they would admit them alone to the benefits of protection.

"*Raw materials are commodities upon which no human labor has been bestowed.* In principle, we should not tax them. Manufactured products can no longer serve the cause of national industry, and we regard them as the best subjects for taxation."

It is not our business here to inquire whether protection to national industry is reasonable. Monsieur <note>original: "M."</note> de Saint-Cricq and the Bordeaux gentlemen are in agreement on this point, and, as we have shown in the preceding chapters, we differ from both of them on this subject.

Our present business is to discover whether it is Monsieur <note>original: "M."</note> de Saint-Cricq or the Bordeaux petitioners who use the word "labor" in its correct sense.

Now, from this perspective, we think that Monsieur <note>original: "M."</note> de Saint-Cricq has much the better of the argument; and to prove this, we may imagine them holding a dialogue such as the following:

MONSIEUR <note>original: "M."</note> DE SAINT-CRICQ: You grant that national labor should be protected. You grant that no products of foreign labor can be introduced into our market without replacing a corresponding amount of our national labor. However, you contend that there are a great many products possessed of *value* (for they sell), but upon which *no human labor has been bestowed* <note>original: *vierges de tout travail humain* — literally "virgin of all human labor"</note>. And you include among these—

...other things: grain <note>original: "corn"; in this context, it refers to cereal grains like wheat or barley rather than maize.</note>, flour, meat, cattle, tallow <term>tallow: rendered animal fat, used historically to make candles and soap.</term>, salt, iron, copper, lead, coal, wool, hides, seeds, and so on.

If you can only prove to me that the *value* of these things is not caused by labor, I will admit that it is useless to protect them with tariffs.

But, on the other hand, if I demonstrate to you that there is as much labor worked into 100 francs <note>original: "fr."</note> worth of wool as in 100 francs worth of fabric, you must allow that the one is as worthy of protection as the other.

Now, why is this sack of wool *worth* 100 francs? Is it not because that is its cost price? And what does its cost price represent, but the total wages of all the labor and the profits of all the capital which have contributed to the production of the commodity?

**THE BORDEAUX PETITIONERS:** Well, perhaps regarding wool you may be right. But take the case of a sack of grain, a bar of iron, or a hundredweight <term>hundredweight: a unit of weight; 112 pounds in the British system or 100 pounds in the American system.</term> of coal—are these commodities produced by labor? Are they not *created* by nature?

**MONSIEUR <note>original: "M."</note> DE SAINT-CRICQ:** Undoubtedly nature creates the elements of all these things, but it is labor which produces their *value*. I was wrong myself in saying that labor *created* material objects, and that flawed phrasing <note>original: "vicious form of expression"</note> has led me into other errors. It does not belong to man to create—to make anything out of nothing—whether he is a farmer or a manufacturer. If by *production* we mean *creation*, then all our labor must be considered unproductive; and yours, as merchants, would be more unproductive than all others, except perhaps my own as a government official.

The farmer, then, cannot claim to have *created* grain, but he has created *value*. I mean to say that he has—by his own labor and that of his servants, laborers, and reapers—transformed substances which had no resemblance to grain into the crop itself. The miller who converts the grain into flour, and the baker who converts the flour into bread, do the same thing.

In order for a person to be able to clothe themselves, a multitude of operations are necessary. Prior to any intervention of human labor, the true *raw materials* of cloth are the air, the water, the heat, the gases, the light, and the salts which enter into its composition. These are the *raw materials* upon which, strictly speaking, no human labor has been employed because they are...

<summary>Monsieur de Saint-Cricq argues that "raw materials" like grain and coal possess value only because of the human labor required to extract or transform them, making them just as deserving of protectionist tariffs as manufactured goods.</summary>

...untouched by any human labor <note>original: "vierges de tout travail humain"</note>; and since they have no value, I would never dream of protecting them. Who can claim that all of these operations—from the first furrow opened by the plow to the last stitch of the tailor’s needle—do not boil down to **labor**?

And is it because these operations are spread over several different branches of industry—to speed up and simplify the final goal of providing clothing to those who need it—that you want to use an arbitrary distinction to rank the importance of these tasks based on the order in which they happen? Do you mean to say that the first step in the series doesn't even deserve the name "labor," while the last, being labor *above all else* <note>original: "par excellence"</note>, is the only one worthy of the favors of protection?

**THE PETITIONERS:** Yes; we are beginning to see that grain <note>original: "corn"; as established on the previous page, this refers to cereal crops like wheat, not maize.</note>, like wool, is not exactly a product that has had *no human labor spent on it*. However, the farmer has not, at least like the manufacturer, done everything himself or through his workmen. Nature has assisted him, and if there is labor worked into the grain, it is not the simple product of labor alone.

**M. DE SAINT-CRICQ:** But its **value** is derived exclusively from labor. I am happy that nature helps in the physical formation of the grain. I could even wish that it were entirely her work; but you must admit that I have harnessed this assistance from nature through my own labor. When I sell you my grain, you will notice this: I am not asking you to pay for nature's labor, but for **my own**.

But, as you describe the case, manufactured goods are no longer the exclusive products of labor either. Is the manufacturer not also indebted to nature in his processes? Does he not use the help of the steam engine or the pressure of the atmosphere, just as I use the soil's moisture with the help of the plow? Did he create the laws of gravity, the transmission of force, or chemical attraction <term>affinity: in 19th-century science, the force that causes certain chemical elements to bind together; the text uses "affinity."</term>?

**THE PETITIONERS:** Well, this is the same point we had with the wool; but coal is certainly the work—the exclusive work—of—

<sig>G</sig>

nature. It is indeed a product upon which *no human labor has ever been spent.* <note>original: "bestowed"</note>

**M. DE SAINT-CRICQ:** Yes; nature has undoubtedly created the coal, but *labor has given value to it.* For the millions of years during which it was buried 600 feet <note>original: "100 fathoms"; a fathom is six feet, so 100 fathoms is 600 feet.</note> underground, unknown to everyone, it had no *value*. It was necessary to search for it—that *is labor*; it was necessary to send it to market—that *is* additional *labor*. Then the price you pay for it in the market is nothing more than the payment for the labor of mining and transport.*

So far, we see that M. de Saint-Cricq has the better end of the argument; that the *value* of raw materials, like that of manufactured goods, represents the cost of production—that is to say, the *labor* invested in them. It is not possible to imagine a product possessing *value* which *has had no human labor spent on it*. The distinction made by the petitioners is useless in theory; as the basis for an unfair distribution of *favors* <term>favors: government-granted advantages, such as protective tariffs or subsidies</term>, it would be unjust in practice. This is because the result would be that one-third of our countrymen, who happen to be engaged in manufacturing, would obtain the advantages of a monopoly on the alleged ground that they produce by *labor*, while the other two-thirds—namely, the agricultural population—would be abandoned to competition under the excuse that they produce *without labor*.

I am quite sure the response to this will be that a nation derives more advantages from importing what are called *raw* materials—whether produced by labor or not—and exporting manufactured goods. This will be repeated and insisted upon, and it is a very widely held opinion.

\* I am not specifying the portions of the payment <note>original: "remuneration"</note> going to the leaseholder, the capitalist, etc., for several reasons: 1st, Because, on looking at the matter more closely, you will see that the payment always comes down to the reimbursement of expenses or the payment of previous *labor* <note>original: "anterior labour"</note>. 2ndly, Because, under the term *labor*, I include not only the wages of the workmen, but the fair compensation for everything that cooperates in the work of production. 3rdly (and above all), Because the production of manufactured products is, like that of raw materials, burdened with additional costs other than the mere expense of *manual labor*; and, moreover, this objection, trivial in itself, would apply just as much to the most delicate processes of manufacture as to the simplest operations of agriculture.

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the "value" of raw materials. <page-num>98</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

> "The more abundant raw materials are," says the Bordeaux petition, "the more manufacturing <note>original: "manufactures"</note> is promoted and increased."

> "Raw materials," says the same document in another place, "create an unlimited field of work for the inhabitants of the countries into which they are imported."

> "Raw materials," says the Le Havre <note>original: "Havre"</note> petition, "since they are the basic components of labor, must be treated *differently* and gradually admitted at the *lowest possible tariff rate* <note>original: "duty"</note>."

The same petition expresses a desire for manufactured products to be admitted, not gradually, but after an indefinite period of time, and not at the lowest tariff rate, but at a rate of 20 percent.

> "Among other articles for which low prices and abundance are necessary," says the Lyon <note>original: "Lyons"</note> petition, "manufacturers include *all raw materials*."

This entire argument is based on a misconception.

We have seen that all *value* represents *labor*. Now, it is quite true that manufacturing labor increases the *value* of raw materials tenfold, or even a hundredfold; that is to say, it creates ten or a hundred times more profit for the nation. Therefore, people are led to argue this way: "Producing a hundredweight <term>hundredweight: a unit of weight equal to 112 pounds in the British system</term> of iron earns only fifteen shillings for workers of all classes. But converting this same hundredweight of iron into watch springs increases their earnings to £500. Would anyone dare to say that a nation does not have a greater interest in securing £500 for its labor than only fifteen shillings?"

However, we do not trade a hundredweight of raw iron for a hundredweight of watch springs, nor a hundredweight of raw wool for a hundredweight of cashmere shawls. Instead, we trade a certain value of one of these materials *for an equal value of another*. Now, to trade equal value for equal value is to trade equal labor for equal labor. Therefore, it is not true that a nation which sells five pounds' worth of manufactured fabrics or watch springs gains more than a nation which sells five pounds' worth of wool or iron.

In a country where no law can be passed and no tax can be imposed without the consent of those whose business dealings the law [affects...]

...is to regulate, and whose pockets the tax is to affect, the public cannot be robbed without first being deceived and misled. Our ignorance is the *raw material* of every extortion we suffer, and we can be certain beforehand that every *sophism* <term>sophism: a clever but false argument, especially one used to deceive</term> is the precursor to an act of plunder. My good friends! When you detect a sophism in a petition, button up your pockets <note>original: "breeches-pocket"</note>, for you can be sure that this is the target they are aiming for.

Let us see, then, what the real object is that the shipowners of Bordeaux and Le Havre <note>original: "Havre"</note> and the manufacturers of Lyon <note>original: "Lyons"</note> are secretly aiming for, and which is hidden under the distinction they try to make between agricultural and manufactured goods.

"It is principally this first class (which includes raw materials *upon which no human labor has been bestowed*) that provides," say the Bordeaux petitioners, "*the main support for our merchant shipping.* . . . . In principle, a wise economy would not tax this class. . . . . The second (goods partly processed) may be taxed *to a certain extent*. The third (goods which require no further labor) we regard as *the most appropriate subjects for taxation*."

The Le Havre petitioners "consider it essential to gradually reduce the tax <note>original: "duty"</note> on raw materials to the *lowest rate*, so that our manufacturers may gradually find work for the shipping industry, which provides them with the primary and essential materials for labor."

The manufacturers could not lag behind in politeness toward the shipowners. So the Lyon petition asks for the duty-free import <note>original: "free introduction"</note> of raw materials, "in order to prove," as they put it, "that the interests of the manufacturing towns are not always opposed to those of the maritime towns."

No; but the interests of both, as understood by the petitioners, are in direct opposition to the interests of agriculture and consumers.

Well, gentlemen, we have finally come to see what you are aiming for and the purpose of your subtle economic distinctions. You want the law to restrict the transport of *finished goods* across the ocean so that the more expensive shipping of raw and rough materials—bulky and mixed with waste—should provide more opportunities for your merchant

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionist distinctions between "raw materials" and "finished goods" are deceptive tactics used by shipowners and manufacturers to profit at the expense of the public. He reveals that their goal is to force the transport of bulky, unrefined goods to increase shipping profits and manufacturing control.</summary>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues Frédéric Bastiat’s satirical critique of protectionist arguments. He is mocking the idea that "creating work" for transporters by importing bulky, unprocessed goods is a benefit to society, arguing instead that the goal of all labor is consumption, not the labor itself.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

...shipping, and more largely employ your shipping resources. This is what you call *a wise economy*.

On the same principle, why do you not ask that the pines of Russia should be brought to you with their branches, bark, and roots; the silver of Mexico in its raw mineral state; and the hides of Buenos Aires <note>original: "Buenos Ayres"</note> still sticking to the bones of the diseased carcasses from which they have been torn?

I expect that railway shareholders, the moment they are in a majority in the legislative chambers, will proceed to make a law forbidding the manufacture of the brandy which is consumed in Paris. And why not? Would not a law enforcing the transportation <note>original: "conveyance"</note> of ten casks of wine for every one cask of brandy provide Parisian industry with the *indispensable materials for its labor*, and give employment to our *locomotive resources*? <note>Bastiat is pointing out that because brandy is distilled wine, it is much more concentrated. If the goal were simply to give railroads more work, it would be "logical" to ban brandy and force the transport of the much bulkier wine instead.</note>

How long will men shut their eyes to this simple truth?

Manufacturing, shipping, and labor—all have as their end goal the general public good. To create useless industries, to favor unnecessary transportation, and to support a greater amount of labor than is necessary—not for the good of the public, but at the expense of the public—is to perform a true *circular argument* <note>original: "petitio principii"; a logical fallacy where the premise of an argument assumes the truth of the conclusion it is supposed to prove.</note>. It is not labor which is desirable for its own sake; it is consumption. All labor without a matching result is a loss. You may as well pay sailors for throwing stones into the sea as pay them for transporting useless refuse. Thus, we arrive at the result to which all *economic sophisms* <term>sophism: a clever but false argument used to deceive</term>, numerous as they are, lead us: namely, confusing the *means* with the *end*, and developing one at the expense of the other.

---

### XXII.

## METAPHORS.

A sophism sometimes expands and runs through the whole fabric of a long and elaborate theory. More frequently, it shrinks and contracts, assumes the guise of a principle, and lurks in a single word or phrase.

<summary>Bastiat uses satirical examples, like transporting whole trees or raw ore, to argue that the goal of the economy is consumption (the end) rather than labor and transport (the means). He concludes by introducing how economic fallacies often hide within simple metaphors.</summary>

<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, Free Trade, economic sophisms, labor vs. consumption, transport industry, protectionism, circular reasoning, metaphors</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues Frédéric Bastiat’s satirical critique of protectionist arguments. Having previously mocked the idea that increasing labor and transportation for its own sake is beneficial, he now turns his attention to the linguistic tricks—specifically metaphors—that protectionists use to deceive the public.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>METAPHORS. 101</header>

"May God protect us from the devil and from metaphors!" was the exclamation of Paul-Louis <note>original: "Paul-Louis"; referring to Paul-Louis Courier (1772–1825), a French political writer and polemicist known for his sharp wit.</note>. And it is difficult to say which of them has done more mischief in this world of ours. The devil, you will say; for he has put the spirit of plunder into all our hearts. True, but he has left us the means of resisting abuses through the opposition of those who suffer from them. It is the *sophism* <note>sophism: a clever but false argument, often used to deceive.</note> which paralyzes this resistance. The sword which *malice* puts into the hands of attackers would be powerless if *sophistry* did not break the shield that should protect the party being attacked. It was with good reason, therefore, that Malebranche <note>Nicolas Malebranche (1638–1715) was a prominent French rationalist philosopher and priest.</note> inscribed on the title-page of his work this sentence: *Error is the cause of human misery.* <note>original French: "L'erreur est la cause de la misère des hommes."</note>

Let us see how this happens. Ambitious men are often motivated by sinister and wicked intentions; their design, for example, may be to plant the seeds of international hatred in the public mind. This fatal seed may grow, ignite a general conflict, halt civilization, cause torrents of blood to be shed, and bring upon the country the most terrible of all disasters: *invasion*. At any rate, even aside from this, such feelings of hatred lower us in the eyes of other nations and force Frenchmen who retain any sense of justice to blush for their country. These are undoubtedly most serious evils; and to guard the public against the secret practices of those who would expose the country to such risks, it is only necessary to see clearly into their designs. How do they manage to hide them? By the use of *metaphors*. They twist, distort, and pervert the meaning of three or four words, and the job is done.

The word *invasion* itself is a good illustration of this.

A French ironmaster <note>ironmaster: a manufacturer or manager of an ironworks.</note> exclaims: "Protect us from the *invasion* of English iron!" An English landowner exclaims in return: "Protect us from the *invasion* of French corn!" And then they proceed to place barriers between the two countries. These barriers create isolation; isolation gives rise to hatred; hatred leads to war; and war leads to a real *invasion*. "What does it matter?" cry the two *sophists*; "is it not better to expose ourselves to an eventual military *invasion* than accept a commercial *invasion* which is certain?" And the people believe them, and the barriers are kept up.

And yet, what similarity is there between a peaceful exchange and an...

<meta>This page continues Frédéric Bastiat’s critique of the deceptive metaphors used by protectionists to advocate for trade restrictions. He moves from discussing general sophisms to specific loaded terms like "invasion," "inundation," and "tribute."</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
->### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.<-

*invasion*? What possible similarity can be imagined between a ship of war which comes to vomit fire and devastation on our towns, and a merchant ship which comes to offer a free, voluntary exchange of commodities for commodities?

The same thing holds true for the use of the word *inundation* <note>original: "inundation"; here used as a metaphor for an "overwhelming" amount of foreign goods entering a market.</note>. This word is usually used in a negative sense, for we often see our fields damaged and our harvests swept away by floods. If, however, these floods leave something of greater value on our soil than what they carry away—like the flooding of the Nile—we should be thankful for them, just as the Egyptians are. Before we protest, then, against the *inundations* of foreign products—and before proceeding to restrain them with annoying and costly obstacles—we should inquire into what class they belong, and whether they ravage or fertilize. 

What should we think of Mehemet Ali <note>Muhammad Ali Pasha (1769–1849), the Ottoman governor and de facto ruler of Egypt who modernized the nation.</note> if, instead of raising expensive dams across the Nile to spread its *inundations* further, he were to spend his money digging a deeper channel to prevent Egypt from being "soiled" by the *foreign* silt which descends upon her from the Mountains of the Moon <note>A legendary mountain range in East Africa, historically thought to be the source of the Nile.</note>? We display exactly the same degree of wisdom and sense when we desire, at the cost of millions, to defend our country . . . . From what? From the benefits which nature has bestowed on other climates.

Among the *metaphors* which conceal a destructive theory, there is none more commonly used than that presented by the words *tribute* and *tributary*.

These words have now become so common that they are used as synonyms for *purchase* and *purchaser*, and are employed interchangeably.

And yet, a *tribute* is as different from a *purchase* as *theft* is from an *exchange*; and I would just as soon hear it said, "Cartouche <note>Louis Dominique Bourguignon (1693–1721), known as Cartouche, was a famous French highwayman and outlaw who became a folk figure.</note> has broken into my safe and *purchased* a thousand pounds," as to hear one of our representatives repeat, "We have paid Germany *tribute* for a thousand horses which she has sold us."

For what distinguishes the act of Cartouche from a *purchase* is that he has not put into my safe, with my consent, a value equal to what he has taken out of it.

And what distinguishes our payment of £20,000 to Germany from a *tribute* paid to her is this: that Germany has not received the money without giving us in exchange, and with our consent, a value in horses equal to the money received.

<summary>Bastiat deconstructs the negative metaphors of "invasion," "inundation," and "tribute," arguing that protectionists use these violent terms to mischaracterize mutually beneficial trade as an act of war or theft.</summary>
