# METAPHORS.

that she has not received the money for free, but has given us in exchange a thousand horses, which we have judged to be worth the £20,000.

Is it worth seriously exposing such an abuse of language? Yes; for these terms are used seriously both in newspapers and in books.

Do not let it be supposed that these are instances of a mere slip of the tongue <note>original: "lapsus linguae"</note> on the part of certain ignorant writers! For every one writer who abstains from using them this way, I will point out ten who do, and among them are the D’Argouts, the Dupins, and the Villèles <note>Count d’Argout (1782–1858), André Dupin (1783–1865), and Joseph de Villèle (1773–1854) were prominent French statesmen, ministers, and financiers during the Restoration and July Monarchy.</note>—peers, deputies, and ministers of state—men, in short, whose words are laws, and whose fallacies <note>original: "sophisms"</note>, even the most obvious, serve as a basis for the government of the country.

A celebrated modern philosopher has added to the categories of Aristotle the fallacy which consists in employing a phrase that begs the question <note>original: "petitio principii"; a logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premises.</note>. He gives many examples of it, and he should have added the word *tributary* to his list. The business, in fact, is to discover whether purchases made from foreigners are useful or hurtful. They are hurtful, you say. And why? Because they render us *tributaries* to the foreigner. This is simply using a word which implies the very thing that still needs to be proved.

It may be asked how this abuse of words first came to be introduced into the rhetoric of the protectionists <note>original: "monopolists"; Bastiat often uses this term to refer to those who benefit from trade tariffs.</note>?

Money *leaves the country* to satisfy the greed of a victorious enemy. Money *also leaves the country* to pay for goods. An analogy is established between the two cases by taking into account only the points in which they resemble each other, while ignoring the points in which they differ.

Yet this circumstance—that is to say, the lack of repayment in the first case, and the repayment voluntarily agreed upon in the second—establishes such a difference between them that it is really impossible to put them in the same category. To hand over a hundred pounds *by force* to a man who has caught you by the throat, or to hand them over *voluntarily* to a man who provides you with what you want, are things as different as light and darkness. You might as well assert that it is a matter of indifference whether you throw your bread...

Whether bread is thrown into the river or eaten, in both cases the bread is *destroyed*. The flaw <note>original: "vice"</note> in this reasoning, like that applied to the word *tribute*, consists in asserting a total similarity between two cases by looking only at their points of resemblance while ignoring the points in which they differ.

# CONCLUSION.

**ALL** the fallacies <note>original: "sophisms"</note> which I have so far exposed refer to a single question—the system of trade restriction. There are other tempting subjects, such as *vested interests, poor timing <note>original: "inopportuneness"</note>, the draining away of our money*, and so on, which I will not trouble the reader with at this time.

Nor does social economics <note>original: "Social Economy"</note> confine itself to this narrow circle. *Fourierism, Saint-Simonism* <note>Followers of Charles Fourier and Henri de Saint-Simon, influential early 19th-century French socialist thinkers.</note>, communism, mysticism, sentimentalism, false philanthropy, affected aspirations after an impossible <note>original: "chimerical"</note> equality and brotherhood; questions relating to luxury, wages, machinery, the supposed tyranny of capital, colonies, markets and outlets for produce, conquests, population, social organization <note>original: "association"</note>, emigration, taxes, and loans—all these have cluttered the field of science with a multitude of parasitic arguments and fallacies <note>original: "sophisms"</note>. These provide plenty of work for the hoe and the grubber <note>A tool used for digging up roots; here used metaphorically for a researcher clearing away errors.</note> of the diligent economist.

I am quite aware of the inconvenience of this approach—or rather, this lack of a plan. To attack so many disconnected fallacies one by one—which sometimes clash with each other and more often overlap—is to engage in an unpredictable struggle and involve ourselves in constant repetitions.

How much I would prefer to explain simply the way things *actually are*, without occupying myself with the thousand different ways that ignorance *views* them! To explain the laws under which societies either thrive or decay is to effectively demolish *virtually all these fallacies at once*. When Laplace <note>Pierre-Simon Laplace (1749–1827) was a French scholar whose work was essential to the development of engineering, mathematics, statistics, physics, and astronomy.</note> described all that was then known of the movements of the heavenly bodies, he cleared away—without even naming them—all the 

<summary>Bastiat concludes his first series of critiques by arguing that individual economic fallacies are best defeated by a systematic understanding of economic laws, rather than by addressing every incoherent argument one by one.</summary>

...the dreams <note>original: "reveries"</note> of the Egyptian, Greek, and Hindu <note>original: "Hindoo"</note> astrologers far more effectively than he could have done by refuting them directly in countless volumes. Truth is one, and the work which explains it is a structure at once durable and imposing:

->It defies greedy tyrants,<-
->Bolder than the Pyramids<-
->And more durable than bronze.<-
<note>original: "Il brave les tyrans avides, / Plus hardi que les Pyramides / Et plus durable que l’airain." A quote from the French poet Lebrun-Pindare, echoing the Roman poet Horace.</note>

Error is diverse and of a temporary <note>original: "ephemeral"</note> nature; and the work which fights it does not carry within itself a principle of greatness and longevity.

But if the power, and perhaps the opportunity, have been lacking to enable me to proceed in the manner of <term>Laplace</term> <note>Pierre-Simon Laplace (1749–1827), a world-renowned French mathematician and astronomer whose work "System of the World" summarized the laws of the universe.</note> and of <term>Say</term> <note>Jean-Baptiste Say (1767–1832), the premier French economist of the era who organized economic thought into a systematic science.</note>, I cannot help thinking that the form I have adopted has also its modest utility. It seems to me well suited to the needs of our day, and the occasional moments which are set aside for study.

A treatise undoubtedly has unquestionable superiority, but on one condition—namely, that it is read and carefully weighed and thought over. It is addressed to a select class of readers. Its mission is to fix first of all, and afterwards enlarge, the circle of our acquired knowledge.

A refutation of common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> errors and prejudices cannot occupy this high position. It aspires merely to clear the road before the march of truth, to prepare men’s minds for its reception, to rectify public opinion, and disarm dangerous ignorance.

It is, above all, in the department of <term>Social Economy</term> <note>This term refers to what we now call economics, specifically the study of how society manages its resources and trade.</note> that this hand-to-hand struggle, these constantly-recurring battles with popular errors, are of true practical utility.

The sciences may be divided into two classes.

One of these classes may be known only to scholars <note>original: "savans"</note>. It includes those sciences the application of which constitutes the business of special professions. The common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> reap the fruit, in spite of their ignorance. A man may find use for a watch, though ignorant of mechanics and astronomy, and he may be carried along by a locomotive or a steamer, trusting to the skill of the engineer and the pilot. We walk according to the laws of balance <note>original: "equilibrium"</note>, although unacquainted with these laws, just as <term>M. Jourdain</term> <note>A character from Molière’s 1670 play *The Bourgeois Gentleman* who is surprised to learn he has been speaking "prose" for forty years without knowing it.</note> had talked prose all his life without knowing it.

But there are sciences which exercise on the public mind an...

### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

...influence that is only proportional to public enlightenment, and they derive all their effectiveness, not from knowledge accumulated in a few gifted minds, but from knowledge spread among the general public. Among these, we include morals, medicine, social economy, and, in countries where men are their own masters, politics. It is to such sciences that the saying of <term>Bentham</term> <note>Jeremy Bentham (1748–1832), the English philosopher and founder of modern utilitarianism, who believed that the goal of society should be the "greatest happiness for the greatest number."</note> especially applies: "To spread these sciences is better than to advance them." What does it matter if some great man, or even God himself, should have proclaimed the laws of morality, as long as men, filled with false ideas, confuse virtues with vices and vices with virtues? What does it matter that <term>Smith</term> <note>Adam Smith (1723–1790), the Scottish philosopher often called the "father of modern economics."</note>, <term>Say</term> <note>Jean-Baptiste Say (1767–1832), a leading French economist who popularized Smith's ideas in Europe.</note>, and, according to <term>M. de Saint-Chamans</term> <note>Auguste, Vicomte de Saint-Chamans, a French official and protectionist who often argued against free trade.</note>, *economists of all schools*, have proclaimed, in reference to commercial transactions, the superiority of *liberty* over *restriction*, if the men who make our laws, and for whom our laws are made, think differently?

Those sciences, which have been correctly named *social*, also have this peculiarity: because they are applied universally and daily, no one will admit to being ignorant of them. When the task is to solve a question in chemistry or geometry, no one claims to have mastered these sciences by intuition; no one is ashamed to consult <term>M. Thénard</term> <note>Louis Jacques Thénard (1777–1857), a prominent French chemist known for discovering hydrogen peroxide.</note>, or feels any difficulty about referring to the works of <term>Legendre</term> or <term>Bezout</term> <note>Adrien-Marie Legendre and Étienne Bézout were influential French mathematicians whose textbooks were standard for students in the 18th and 19th centuries.</note>. But in the social sciences, authority is hardly recognized. As each person daily takes charge of their own morals (whether good or bad), their health, their wallet, and their politics (whether sound or absurd), so each person believes themselves qualified to discuss, comment on, and pass judgment on social questions. Are you ill? There is no old woman who will not at once tell you the cause of your ailment and the remedy for it. "*It is the humours*," <note>original: "Humours." A reference to the ancient and now-discredited medical theory that the body was controlled by four fluids: blood, yellow bile, black bile, and phlegm.</note> she will say; "*you must take medicine.*" But what are humours? And is there any such disease? About this, she does not concern herself. I cannot help thinking of this old woman when I hear social ills explained by these hackneyed phrases: "the overabundance of products," "the tyranny of capital," "an industrial glut," and other such clichés. We cannot even say of these phrases that they are "words and voices, and nothing more," <note>original: "Verba et voces, prætereaque nihil"</note> for they are actually many dangerous errors.

From what I have said, two things result—1st, That the...

<meta>This page concludes the author's discussion on the nature of social sciences, arguing that because they rely on public opinion, the most important task is to dismantle common economic fallacies (sophisms) that prioritize producers over consumers.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>

-> # CONCLUSION. <-

Social sciences inevitably contain more *sophisms* <note>original: "sophisms"; a sophism is a clever but false argument, often intended to deceive.</note> than other fields for two reasons: first, because in these matters every person relies on their own judgment and instincts; and second, because it is in these specific sciences that *sophisms* are especially harmful. They mislead public opinion, and in social matters, public opinion is the driving force that becomes law.

In these sciences, we need two types of books: those that explain the subjects to further and advance them—establishing truth—and those that actively combat error.

It seems to me that the natural flaw of this short work—*repetition*—is precisely what will make it effective.

In the questions I have addressed, every sophism certainly has its own phrasing and specific impact, but all of them can be traced back to a single common root: *forgetting the interests of people as consumers*. To demonstrate that a thousand errors can be traced back to this widespread fallacy is to teach the public how to detect it, to see it for what it truly is, and to distrust it in all circumstances.

After all, the purpose of my current work is not exactly to instill firm convictions, but rather to awaken doubts.

I do not expect that the reader, upon finishing this book, will exclaim, "*I know everything*!" I would much prefer that they say candidly, "*I am ignorant!*"

> “I am ignorant, because I am beginning to fear there is something deceptive in the flattering promises of *scarcity*.” (Sophism I.)
> 
> “I am not as charmed by *obstacles* as I once was.” (Sophism II.)
> 
> “*Effort without result* no longer seems as desirable to me as *result without effort*.” (Sophism III.)
> 
> “It is very possible that the secret of trade does not consist—as the fencing master in *The Middle-Class Gentleman* <note>original: "Bourgeois Gentilhomme"; a famous 1670 comedy by the French playwright Molière. The character mentioned is a fencing master who defines the "secret of arms" as the art of giving hits without receiving any.</note> defines the secret of swordplay—in *giving and not receiving*.” (Sophism VI.)
> 
> “I can understand that a commodity is *worth* more in proportion to the amount of labor bestowed upon it; but in an exchange, will two *equal* values cease to be equal because one comes from the plough and the other from the loom?” (Sophism XXI.)

<page-num>108</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "Sophisms"</note>.</header>

> "I admit that I am beginning to find it strange that the human race should be improved by restrictions <note>original: "shackles"</note> and enriched by taxes; and, truth be told, I would be relieved of a heavy burden and feel pure satisfaction if it were proven to me—as the author of the *Fallacies* <note>original: "Sophismes"</note> asserts—that there is no conflict between prosperity and justice, between peace and liberty, or between the growth of labor and the progress of intelligence." (Fallacies XIV. and XX.)

> "Then, even without being entirely convinced by his arguments—which I am not sure whether to call logical reasoning or paradoxes—I shall turn my attention to the recognized masters of the science."

Let us conclude this detailed study <note>original: "monography"</note> of fallacies with a final and important observation.

The world is not sufficiently aware of the influence exerted over it by fallacies.

To speak my mind: once the *law of the strongest* <note>original: "right of the strongest"; the idea that might makes right</note> was set aside, fallacies established the *right of the most cunning* in its place. It is difficult to say which of these two tyrants has been more disastrous for humanity.

People have an excessive love of pleasure, influence, status, and power—in a word, wealth.

At the same time, they are driven by a powerful, overwhelming urge to obtain the things they desire at other people's expense.

But these *other people*—to be blunt, the public—have an equally strong desire to keep what they have earned, if they *can*, and if they *realize* what is happening.

Plunder <note>original: "Spoliation"; the act of taking goods or property by force or unethical means</note>, which plays such a large role in world affairs, has only two tools at its command: *force and deception*. It also has two limits: *courage and intelligence*.

The use of force to carry out plunder forms the basis of human history <note>original: "annals"</note>. To trace back its history would be to recount the history of almost every nation—Assyrians, Babylonians, Medes, Persians, Egyptians, Greeks, Romans, Goths, Franks, Huns, Turks, Arabs, Mongols, Tartars; not to mention Spaniards in America, Englishmen in India, Frenchmen in Africa, Russians in Asia, and so on.

But civilized nations, at least, are composed of men who produce—

<summary>The author concludes his study of economic fallacies by explaining that when societies move away from raw physical force, they often fall prey to deception and cunning. He argues that plunder—the act of taking from others—is a primary driver of human history, limited only by the public's courage and intelligence.</summary>

<keywords>economic fallacies, plunder, force and cunning, wealth, human history, Bastiat, social science, justice</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's introduction to the first series of his work, transitioning from a discussion on how the public has learned to resist physical force to how they must now learn to recognize intellectual deception.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>

<header>CONCLUSION.</header>

wealth, have become sufficiently numerous, and sufficiently strong to defend themselves. Does this mean that they are no longer plundered? Not at all; they are plundered as much as ever, and, what is more, they plunder one another.

Only, the agent employed has been changed; it is no longer by *force*, but by *cunning*, that they seize upon the public wealth.

To rob the public, we must first deceive it. The trick consists in persuading the public that the theft is for its own advantage, and by this means inducing it to accept, in exchange for its property, services which are fictitious, and often worse. Hence comes the *Fallacy* <note>original: "Sophism"; an argument that seems correct but is actually false, used to deceive others</note>—religious fallacies, economic fallacies, political fallacies, and financial fallacies. Since, then, physical force is held in check, the *fallacy* is not only an evil, but the very genius of evil. It must in its turn be held in check also. And for that end, we must render the public more *cunning* than the cunning, just as it has already become *stronger* than the strong.

Good Public! It is under the influence of this conviction that I dedicate to you this first essay—although the preface is strangely out of place <note>original: "transposed"</note>, and the dedication is somewhat late.

<meta>This page begins the Second Series of Frédéric Bastiat's "Economic Sophisms," transitioning from basic theoretical rebuttals to a deeper analysis of how wealth is redirected through social and political systems.</meta>

# ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "Sophisms" — clever but false arguments often used to deceive or mislead.</note>

## SECOND SERIES.

---

### I.

### THE PHYSIOLOGY OF PLUNDER <note>original: "Spoliation" — the act of taking property or resources by force or through legal/political maneuvers rather than earning them through production.</note>

Why should I continue to torment myself with this dry and dreary science of *Political Economy*? <note>Political Economy was the 18th and 19th-century term for the study of how nations manage resources, trade, and wealth; it is the precursor to modern Economics.</note>

Why? The question is reasonable. Every kind of labor is, in itself, unpleasant enough to justify asking what result it leads to.

Let us see, then, how things stand.

I am not addressing those philosophers who claim to adore poverty—if not for themselves, then at least for the rest of the human race.

I speak to those who consider *wealth* to be of some importance. By that word, we do not mean the excessive riches of a few classes, but rather the comfort, material prosperity, security, independence, education, and dignity of everyone.

There are only two ways to obtain the necessities, conveniences, and pleasures of life: PRODUCTION and PLUNDER.

There are some people who portray PLUNDER as a mere accident—a local and temporary abuse, condemned by moralists, denounced by the law, and unworthy of an economist’s attention.

Yet, despite our goodwill and optimism, we are forced to acknowledge that PLUNDER plays too prominent a role in the world and is too deeply intertwined with important human affairs for it to be ignored.

<summary>Bastiat introduces the second series of his work by questioning the value of studying economics and identifies only two ways to acquire wealth: through productive labor or through plunder.</summary>
<keywords>Political Economy, Plunder, Spoliation, Production, Wealth, Labor, Economics, Frédéric Bastiat</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Physiology of Plunder," where Bastiat argues that the illicit taking of property—which he calls "spoliation"—is not a rare accident but a major driver of social and economic history.</meta>

# ECONOMIC FALLACIES <note>original: "Sophisms" — clever but false arguments used to deceive.</note>

...is enough to justify social science—and especially *political economy*—in treating it as a matter of no importance.

I would go even further. What prevents the social order from reaching its potential perfection is the constant effort of its members to live and enjoy themselves at one another's expense. Therefore, if PLUNDER <note>original: "Spoliation" — the act of taking property or resources by force or through legal maneuvers rather than earning them through production.</note> did not exist, social science would have no purpose, for society would then be perfect.

I go further still. Once PLUNDER has become the recognized way of life for a group of men united by social ties, they soon move to create a law that authorizes it and a code of morality that makes it sacred.

We only need to list some of the most obvious forms that *plunder* takes to show how much it influences human interactions.

First of all, there is WAR. Among primitive peoples, the winner kills the loser to gain a right—which, if not undeniable, is at least *unchallenged*—to his enemy’s hunting grounds.

Next is SLAVERY. When man discovers that the land can be made productive through labor, he says to his fellow man, “The labor shall be yours, and the product shall be mine.”

Then we have PRIESTCRAFT <note>original: "Priestcraft" — here referring to the abuse of religious authority for financial gain rather than sincere faith.</note>. “Depending on whether you give or refuse me a portion of your property, I will open the gates of Heaven or Hell to you.”

Finally, there is MONOPOLY. Its defining trait is that it maintains the great social law of *service for service*, but uses force to influence the deal, thereby destroying the fair balance between the *service received* and the *service performed*.

Plunder always carries within itself the seed of its own destruction. It is rarely the many who plunder the few. If that were the case, the few would soon be so impoverished that they could no longer satisfy the greed of the many, and plunder would die out for lack of resources.

It is almost always the majority who are oppressed, yet plunder is still subject to an inevitable limit.

For if the method used is force—as with war and slavery—it is natural that, in the long run, force will end up on the side of the majority.

<summary>Bastiat argues that plunder (spoliation) is a central force in human history that corrupts laws and morals, and he identifies its four primary forms: war, slavery, priestcraft, and monopoly.</summary>
<keywords>spoliation, plunder, political economy, war, slavery, priestcraft, monopoly, social order, justice</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Physiology of Plunder," explaining the natural laws that eventually cause systems of exploitation to collapse.</meta>

<header>PHYSIOLOGY OF PLUNDER <note>original: "Spoliation" — the act of taking property or resources by force or legal manipulation rather than through production.</note></header>

And if the method is **cunning**—as in the cases of religious abuse and monopoly—it is natural that the majority will eventually see through it; otherwise, intelligence would cease to be intelligence.

Another natural law plants a second seed of death in the heart of plunder, which is this:

Plunder not only *displaces* wealth, but always partially *destroys* it.

**War** destroys many things of value.

**Slavery** paralyzes, to a great extent, the natural abilities of men.

**Religious abuse** <note>original: "Priestcraft" — the use of religious influence for financial or political gain.</note> redirects human effort toward objects that are childish or harmful.

**Monopoly** transfers wealth from one pocket to another, but much of it is lost during the transfer.

This is a remarkable law. Without it—assuming there was a balance between the forces of the oppressors and the oppressed—plunder would never end. Because of this law, that balance always tends to be disrupted; either because the plunderers fear such a massive loss of wealth, or because (if they don't fear it) the evil constantly grows until it is in the very nature of things that get worse and worse to eventually perish and be destroyed.

A time finally comes when, in its rapid acceleration, this loss of wealth is so great that the plunderer finds himself poorer than he would have been if there had been no plunder at all.

Take, for example:
*   A nation where the cost of war is more than the value of the loot.
*   A master who pays more for slave labor than he would for free labor.
*   A system of religious abuse that makes people so dull and stupid, and destroys their energy to such an extent, that there is no longer anything to be taken from them.
*   A monopoly that increases its efforts to absorb wealth even as there is less to take—much like someone trying to milk a cow more forcefully the less milk it has to give.

Monopoly, it will be seen, is a species of the category of plun—

<sig>H</sig>

<vocab>plunder, religious abuse, monopoly, balance, plunderers, slavery, wealth, natural law</vocab>

<summary>Bastiat explains that plunder is inherently self-destructive because it destroys wealth rather than just redistributing it, eventually leaving the plunderer worse off than if they had traded fairly.</summary>
<keywords>economic destruction, inefficiency of slavery, costs of war, religious exploitation, monopoly, diminishing returns, natural law</keywords>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS <note>Sophisms: clever but false arguments, especially those used to deceive.</note></header>

stition. There are many varieties <note>of plunder</note>; among others, sinecures <term>Sinecure: a high-paying job that requires little or no actual work.</term>, privileges, and restrictions.

Among the forms which it assumes, there are some which are very simple and primitive. Of this kind are feudal rights. Under this system <note>original: "régime"</note>, the masses are exploited, and they know it. It implies an abuse of force and collapses when that force is lacking.

Other forms are very complicated. The masses are frequently exploited without knowing it. They may even imagine that they owe everything to this plunder—not only what is left to them, but also what is taken from them and what is lost in the process. Furthermore, I argue that, over time and owing to the clever mechanisms to which they become accustomed, many men become plunderers <note>original: "spoliators"</note> without knowing that they are so, or desiring to be so. Monopolies of this kind are created by trickery and fed by error. They disappear only as people become more enlightened.

I have said enough to show that political economy has a clear practical use. It is the torch which, by exposing trickery and clearing up error, puts an end to this social disorder of plunder. Someone—I believe it was a lady—has rightly described our science as “the safety lock of the people’s savings” <note>original: "la serrure de sureté du pécule populaire"</note>.

### COMMENTARY.

If this little book were destined to last for three or four thousand years and, like a new Koran, were to be read, reread, pondered over, and studied sentence by sentence, word by word, and letter by letter; if it were destined for a place in all the libraries of the world and to be explained by massive amounts of notes and explanations; I might leave the previous observations to their fate, even though they are somewhat unclear because they are so brief. But since they require an explanation <note>original: "gloss"</note>, I think it is best to be my own commentator.

The true and fair law of human transactions is the *exchange of service for service, freely bargained for*. Plunder consists of using force or trickery to take away this freedom of bargaining for the purpose of enabling a man or a class to receive a service without providing an equal service in return.

<summary>This page explains that plunder ranges from simple force (feudalism) to complex trickery (monopolies) and defines political economy as the "safety lock" that protects the public's wealth by exposing these errors.</summary>
<keywords>plunder, political economy, sinecures, monopolies, feudalism, free exchange, economic education</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Physiology of Plunder" (originally titled "Physiology of Spoliation"), contrasting the social reception of individual theft versus national conquest.</meta>

### PHYSIOLOGY OF PLUNDER <note>original: "Spoliation"</note>.

Plunder by force consists of waiting until a person has produced a good and then taking it from them by force.

This kind of plunder is officially forbidden by the Decalogue <term>Decalogue: The Ten Commandments.</term>—*You shall not steal.*

When this happens between individuals, it is called theft and leads to the prison ships <term>Hulks: decommissioned ships used as floating prisons.</term>; when it happens between nations, it is called *conquest* and leads to glory.

Why is there this difference? It is worth investigating the cause, for it will reveal to us the existence of an irresistible power: public opinion. Like the atmosphere, it surrounds and envelops us so thoroughly that we no longer notice it. Rousseau <note>Jean-Jacques Rousseau (1712–1778), a philosopher whose ideas influenced the Enlightenment and the French Revolution.</note> never said anything truer than this: "It takes much philosophy to observe the facts that are too close to us" <note>original French: "Il faut beaucoup de philosophie pour observer les faits qui sont trop près de nous"</note>—"You need much philosophy to observe accurately things which are under your nose."

A *thief*, precisely because he works in secret, always has public opinion against him. He frightens everyone within his reach. Yet, if he has accomplices, he takes pride in showing off his skill and bravery to them. Here we begin to see the power of opinion; for the applause of his partners-in-crime removes his sense of guilt and even encourages him to take pride in his shameful acts.

The *warrior* exists in a different environment. The public opinion that condemns him is elsewhere—among the nations he has conquered—and he does not feel its pressure. The public opinion at home applauds and supports him. He and his fellow soldiers feel a strong bond of unity. A country that has made enemies and brought danger upon itself feels it is necessary to praise the bravery of its sons. It awards honors, fame, and glory to the boldest men who have expanded the nation's borders or brought home the most loot <note>original: "booty"</note>. Poets sing of their deeds, and women weave wreaths and garlands for them. Such is the power of public opinion that it removes all sense of injustice from plunder and all sense of wrongdoing from the plunderer.

The public opinion that reacts against military plunder is felt not in the conquering country, but in the conquered one, where it has little influence. And yet it is not

<summary>Bastiat explores how public opinion distinguishes between individual theft and national conquest, noting that social approval can strip the sense of guilt from acts of plunder.</summary>
<keywords>plunder, spoliation, theft, conquest, public opinion, Rousseau, ethics of war, social pressure</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Physiology of Plunder," analyzing how public opinion and social institutions like religion react to national conquest and war.</meta>

-># ECONOMIC SOPHISMS<-

...altogether ineffective, and it becomes more noticeable as nations interact more frequently and understand each other better. As a result, we see that the study of languages and more open communication between nations tends to create and make widespread a stronger feeling against this kind of plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note>.

Unfortunately, it often happens that the nations surrounding an aggressive and warlike people are also prone to plunder when they can manage it, and thus they become filled with the same prejudices.

In that case, there is only one remedy: time. Nations must be taught through painful experience the enormous evils of mutual plunder.

We might identify another check—a superior and growing sense of morality. But the goal of morality is to increase virtuous actions. How, then, can morality restrain acts of plunder when public opinion ranks such acts as the most exalted form of virtue? What more powerful way is there to make a people moral than religion? And what religion is more favorable to peace than Christianity? Yet, what have we witnessed for eighteen hundred years? Throughout all these ages, we have seen men fight, not only in spite of their religion, but in the very name of religion itself.

The wars fought by a conquering nation are not always offensive and aggressive. Sometimes, such a nation is so unfortunate that it is forced to send its soldiers into the field to defend their homes <note>original: "domestic hearth"</note> and to protect their families, property, independence, and liberty. In those cases, war takes on a character of greatness and sacredness. The national banner—blessed by the ministers of the God of peace—represents everything most sacred in the land. It is followed as the living image of patriotism and honor, and military <note>original: "warlike"</note> virtues are praised above all others. But once the danger has passed, public opinion still holds sway; and through a natural reaction driven by a spirit of revenge—which is mistaken for patriotism—the banner is paraded from capital to capital. In this way, nature seems to prepare a punishment for the aggressor.

It is the fear of this punishment, and not the progress of philosophy, that keeps weapons in the armories <note>original: "arsenals"</note>; for we cannot deny that even the nations most advanced in civilization still go to war...

<keywords>plunder, spoliation, public opinion, morality, religion, Christianity, defensive war, patriotism, revenge, military virtue</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Physiology of Plunder," analyzing how public opinion and economic reality interact with systems of violence, specifically focusing on the transition from the plunder of war to the plunder of slavery.</meta>

# PHYSIOLOGY OF PLUNDER <note>original: "spoliation"</note>. 117

...and think little of justice when they have no fear of retaliation, as the history of the Himalayas, the Atlas Mountains, and the Caucasus Mountains bears witness.

If religion is powerless, and if philosophy is equally powerless, how then can wars be ended?

Political economy demonstrates that, even for the nation that proves victorious, war is always waged in the interest of a few individuals and at the expense of the masses. When the masses see this clearly, the weight of public opinion—which is now divided—will come to be entirely on the side of peace.

Plunder by force <note>original: "spoliation"</note> assumes yet another form. No one will engage voluntarily in the business of production only to be robbed of what they produce. Therefore, the person themselves is seized, robbed of their freedom and personality, and forced to labor. The language used toward them is not, "*If you do this for me, I will do that for you;*" but rather, "*Yours is the fatigue, and mine is the enjoyment.*" This is slavery, which always implies the abuse of force.

It is important to ask whether it is not in the very nature of an unquestionably dominant force to commit abuses. For my part, I would be reluctant to trust it; I would as soon expect a stone thrown from a height to stop mid-air of its own accord as expect absolute power to set limits for itself.

I would like someone to point out at least one country and one era in which slavery was abolished by the free, graceful, and voluntary act of the masters.

Slavery provides a second and striking example of how insufficient religious and philanthropic sentiments are when they are set in opposition to the powerful and energetic drive of self-interest. This may appear to be a gloomy view of the subject to certain modern schools of thought that seek the renewing principle of society in self-sacrifice. Let them begin, then, by reforming human nature itself.

In the West Indies, ever since the introduction of slavery, the masters have professed the Christian religion from generation to generation. Many times a day they repeat these words: "All men are brothers; to love your neighbor is to fulfill the whole law."

<summary>The author argues that only political economy, by showing that war harms the masses, can end conflict, and he identifies slavery as a form of plunder driven by self-interest that persists despite religious teachings.</summary>
<keywords>political economy, war, public opinion, slavery, self-interest, West Indies, Christianity, plunder, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat's "Physiology of Plunder," moving from the historical inevitability of slavery's end due to economic forces to the role of public opinion in dismantling systems of deception.</meta>

### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

And they continue to have slaves. Nothing appears more natural and legitimate to them. Do modern reformers expect that their system of ethics will ever be as universally accepted, as popular, hold as much authority, and be as frequently spoken of as the Gospel? And if the Gospel has not been able to reach from the lips to the heart—by piercing or climbing over the formidable barrier of self-interest—how can they expect their system of ethics to perform this miracle?

What! Is slavery then invulnerable? No; what introduced it will destroy it—I mean *self-interest*—provided that, by favoring the special interests that created this scourge, we do not oppose the general interests from which we seek the remedy.

It is one of the truths that political economy has demonstrated: that free labor is essentially progressive, while slave labor is necessarily stagnant <note>original: "stationary"</note>. Therefore, the triumph of the former over the latter is inevitable. What has become of the cultivation of indigo by slave labor? <note>Indigo production, once a massive industry reliant on enslaved labor, was eventually undermined by the development of synthetic dyes and more efficient production methods.</note>

Free labor directed toward the production of sugar will lower its price further and further, and slave property will become less and less valuable to its owners. Slavery would have collapsed of its own accord in America long ago if our laws in Europe had not artificially raised the price of sugar <note>Bastiat refers to protectionist tariffs and colonial monopolies that kept sugar prices high, effectively subsidizing slave-based economies.</note>. It is for this reason that we see the masters, their creditors, and their representatives working actively to maintain these laws, which are currently the pillars of the system <note>original: "edifice"</note>.

Unfortunately, they still carry with them the sympathies of the very populations from which slavery has already disappeared; this shows once again how powerful an agent public opinion is.

If public opinion is sovereign even in the realm of Force, it is much more so in the realm of Cunning <note>original: *Ruse*</note>. Truly, this is its proper domain. Cunning is the abuse of intelligence, and public opinion is the progress of intelligence. These two powers are of the same nature. Deception <note>original: "Imposture"</note> on the part of the plunderer <note>original: "spoliator"</note> implies credulity on the part of those being plundered, and the natural antidote to credulity is truth. Therefore, it follows that to enlighten people's minds is to take away from this kind of plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note> the very thing that supports and feeds it.

<summary>Bastiat argues that while morality alone has failed to end slavery, economic self-interest and free labor will inevitably make it obsolete, provided that protectionist laws do not artificially sustain it. He identifies public opinion and education as the primary weapons against the "cunning" of those who use law to plunder others.</summary>

<keywords>slavery, self-interest, political economy, free labor, public opinion, spoliation, sugar duties, protectionism, indigo, deception</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s analysis of "The Physiology of Plunder." Having previously discussed how self-interest eventually dismantles slavery through economic pressure, he now shifts to analyzing how deception—specifically religious deception—is used to exploit others.</meta>

-># THE PHYSIOLOGY OF PLUNDER <note>original: "spoliation"</note><-

->### 119<-

I will briefly review some examples of plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note> that result from deception practiced on a very large scale.

The first example that presents itself is plunder through religious deception <note>original: "ruse théocratique"</note>.

What is the goal? The goal is to obtain food, clothing, luxury, respect, influence, and power by trading fake services for real ones.

If I tell a man, "I am going to provide you with great and immediate benefits," I must keep my word. Otherwise, he will soon be able to detect the fraud, and my trickery will be immediately exposed.

But what if I say to him: "In exchange for your services, I will provide you an immense service—not in this world, but in the next. After this life ends, whether you are eternally happy or miserable depends on me. I am a mediator between God and His creatures, and I can open the gates of heaven or hell at my will." If this man only believes me, I have him in my power.

This type of deception has been practiced on a massive scale since the beginning of the world, and we know how much absolute power the Egyptian priests once held.

It is easy to see how these deceivers operate. We only have to ask ourselves what we would do if we were in their position.

If I arrived among an uneducated tribe with these intentions and succeeded—through some extraordinary or miraculous act—in passing myself off as a supernatural being, I would claim to be a messenger of God with absolute control over the future destiny of humanity.

Then, I would strictly forbid any investigation into the truth of my claims or titles. I would go even further. Since logic would be my most dangerous enemy, I would forbid the use of reason itself, except when applied to this terrifying subject. In the language of the islanders <note>Bastiat uses the word "savages" to refer to non-Western cultures, specifically referencing the Polynesian concept of "taboo," which was a relatively new word in Europe at the time.</note>, I would ***taboo*** this question and everything related to it. To discuss it, or even to think about it, would be declared an unforgivable sin.

The ultimate success of my scheme would be to use this ***taboo*** barrier to block every intellectual path that might lead to the discovery of my fraud. What better security is there than to declare that even having a doubt is a sacrilege <note>A "sacrilege" is the act of treating something sacred with a lack of respect.</note>?

<vocab>spoliation, priestcraft, ruse théocratique, imposture, Egyptian priests, taboo, sacrilege</vocab>

<summary>Bastiat explores how "priestcraft" or religious deception serves as a form of economic plunder by trading imaginary future rewards for real, material wealth and power. He explains that this system relies on suppressing human reason and declaring any doubt to be a sin.</summary>

<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, plunder, spoliation, religion, priestcraft, Egyptian priests, taboo, social control, economic deception</keywords>

-># ECONOMIC SOPHISMS<-

->### 120<-

And to this fundamental security, I would add others. For example, to effectively prevent enlightenment from ever reaching the masses, I would take for myself and my accomplices a monopoly on all knowledge. I would hide this knowledge under the veil of a dead language and mysterious symbols <note>original: "hieroglyphic characters"; Bastiat is likely referring to the use of Latin and complex theological jargon to keep the laity from understanding religious texts</note>. Furthermore, in order to ensure I am never exposed to any danger, I would take care to establish an institution that would enable me, day after day, to look into the secrets of everyone’s conscience <note>a reference to the practice of auricular confession</note>.

It would also be useful to satisfy some of the real needs of my people at the same time, especially if doing so could increase my influence and authority. Thus, as men have a great need for instruction and moral teaching, I would appoint myself as the distributor of these. By this means, I would direct the minds and hearts of my people as I saw fit. I would establish an unbreakable connection between morality and my own authority. I would represent them as being incapable of existing separately, so that if some bold man were to attempt to raise a *forbidden* <note>original: "tabooed"</note> question, society at large—which cannot function without moral teaching—would feel the earth tremble under its feet and would turn with rage against this crazed reformer.

When things had reached this point, it is obvious that the people would become my property in a more absolute sense than if they were my slaves. While a slave curses his chains, these people would embrace theirs. I would thus succeed in branding the mark of servitude not on their foreheads, but on their innermost consciences.

Public opinion alone can overturn such a structure of injustice; but where can it even begin when every stone of the structure is *forbidden* <note>original: "tabooed"</note>? It is clearly a matter of time and the power of the printing press.

God forbid that I should wish to undermine the comforting religious beliefs that connect this life of trial with a life of happiness. But no one—not even the head of the Church himself—can deny that our natural religious yearnings have been exploited. It seems to me that there is a sure test by which a people can discover whether they are being duped or not. Examine religion and the clergy <note>original: "Priest"</note> to discover whether...

<summary>Bastiat describes how a ruling class can use a monopoly on knowledge, the secrets of confession, and the control of moral education to create a "servitude of the conscience" that is harder to break than physical slavery.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Frédéric Bastiat’s exploration of "The Physiology of Plunder" (or Spoliation). Bastiat is examining the two ways religious institutions can function: either as a genuine service to morality or as a tool for the clergy to gain power and wealth at the expense of the people.</meta>

-># PHYSIOLOGY OF SPOLIATION<-
<note>original: "spoliation"; this term refers to the act of plundering, looting, or taking property by force or deception—a central theme in Bastiat's critique of institutional corruption.</note>

->### 121<-

...whether the priest is the instrument of religion, or whether religion is rather the instrument of the priest.

*If the priest is the instrument of religion*—if his only concern is to spread morality and blessings throughout the country—he will be gentle, tolerant, humble, charitable, and full of zeal. His life will be a reflection of his Divine Model <note>A reference to Jesus Christ as the ultimate example for the clergy.</note>. He will preach liberty and equality among men, and peace and brotherhood between nations. He will reject the temptations of worldly power <note>original: "temporal power"; referring to political or secular authority as opposed to spiritual authority.</note>, wanting no alliance with the very thing that, above all else, needs to be kept in check. He will be a man of the people, a man of wise advice, a man of comfort, a man of public opinion, and a man of the Gospel.

If, on the contrary, *religion is the instrument of the priest*, he will treat it as we treat any tool, which we alter, bend, and twist in every direction to make it serve our own purposes. He will increase the number of topics that are *forbidden* <note>original: "tabooed"</note>. His morals will change with the times, the people, and the circumstances. He will try to impress people with grand gestures and practiced poses; he will mumble words a hundred times a day whose meaning has evaporated, becoming nothing more than empty *formality* <note>original: "conventionalism"</note>. He will profit from sacred things, but in a way that does not destroy people’s faith in their sanctity. When he encounters sharp, clear-sighted people, he will be careful not to carry out this trade as openly or actively as he might in other circumstances. He will involve himself in worldly plots and take the side of those in power, provided they take his side. In short, in all his actions, we will see that his goal is not to advance religion through the clergy, but to advance the clergy through religion. Because every effort must have a goal—and because in this scenario that goal can be nothing but wealth and power—the most undeniable sign that the people have been fooled is when the priest has become rich and powerful.

It is quite clear that a true religion can be abused just as easily as a false one. The more respectable its authority is, the more we should fear that the evidence of that respectability will be pushed too far. But the results will be very different. Abuses tend to provoke the sound, enlightened, and...

He argues that the enrichment of the clergy is the primary indicator that religion is being exploited for plunder.</summary>

Plunder <note>original: "spoliation"; the act of taking property or value from others through force or deception.</note> through such means and the intelligence of a people are always in an inverse ratio to each other; for it is the nature of abuses to be carried only as far as safety permits. This does not mean that pure and devoted priests are never to be found even among the most ignorant people; but the question is, how can we prevent a scoundrel from putting on the priest's robe <note>original: "cassock"</note>, and prevent ambition from crowning his head with a bishop’s hat <note>original: "mitre"</note>? Plunderers obey the Malthusian law <note>Thomas Malthus’s theory that populations grow as long as there are resources to sustain them. Bastiat applies this to social "parasites" who multiply as long as there are gullible people to exploit.</note>: they multiply as the means of existence increase; and a scoundrel’s means of existence is the gullibility of those he tricks. Public opinion must be enlightened. There is no other remedy.

Another variety of plunder by craft and artifice is found in what are called *commercial frauds*. This expression, it appears to me, is not broad enough; for not only is the merchant who thins out his goods <note>original: "adulterates his commodities"</note> or uses a false measure guilty of fraud, but also the physician who gets paid for bad advice, and the lawyer <note>original: "advocate"</note> who fans the flames of and encourages lawsuits. In an exchange between two services, one of them may be of bad quality; but here, as the services received are agreed upon beforehand, plunder must clearly retreat as public awareness increases.

Next in order come abuses of *public services*—a vast field of plunder, so vast that we can only glance at it.

If man had been created as a solitary animal, each man would work for himself. Individual wealth would, in that case, be in proportion to the services each man performed for himself.

*But, man being a social animal, services are exchanged for other services*; a proposition which you may, if you choose, interpret in reverse <note>original: "à rebours"</note>.

There exist in society needs so general and universal that its members provide for them by organizing *public services*. Such, for example, is the need for security. We arrange, we join together, to pay—through *services* of various kinds—those who perform for us the *service* of watching over our general security.

<summary>Bastiat explains that institutional plunder is limited only by the intelligence of the public, and he identifies commercial fraud and the abuse of public services as major methods of exploitation.</summary>

<keywords>spoliation, Malthusian law, commercial fraud, public services, social contract, security, public opinion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of "spoliation" (plunder). He transitions from discussing religious exploitation to the economic nature of government services and how they can become a tool for taking wealth from the public.</meta>

# PHYSIOLOGY OF PLUNDER <note>original: "spoliation"</note>.

### 123

There is nothing which does not come within the domain of political economy. *Do this for me, and I will do that for you.* The essence of the transaction is the same; only the method of payment is different. However, this difference is a matter of great importance.

In ordinary transactions, each person is the judge of both the service they receive and the service they provide. One can always refuse an exchange or go elsewhere; therefore, it is necessary to bring services to market that people will willingly accept.

This is not the case with government affairs, especially before the introduction of representative government. Whether we need the services the government provides or not, and whether they are good or bad, we are always forced to accept them exactly as they are and at the price the government sets.

Now, it is a human tendency to view the services we give through the small end of a telescope and the services we receive through the large end <note>The author uses this metaphor to show that people tend to undervalue what they do for others and overvalue what others should do for them.</note>. In private transactions, we would be led into a trap if we lacked the security provided by *a price freely and openly negotiated.*

We have this guarantee either not at all, or only to a very limited extent, in public transactions. Yet the government is composed of men—although today some would try to convince us that legislators are something more than human—and it obeys the same universal tendencies. The government wants to provide us with great service—more than we actually need—and force us to accept, as true *services*, things that are often far from being so, and then demand other services or taxes <note>original: "contributions"</note> from us in return.

In this way, the state is also subject to the Malthusian law <note>Thomas Malthus’s theory that populations grow as long as there are resources to sustain them. Bastiat applies this to government, arguing it will expand as long as there is taxpayer wealth to consume.</note>. It tends to grow beyond its means of existence; it expands in proportion to those means, and those means consist of the people’s wealth. Woe, then, to those nations that cannot set limits on the government’s actions! Liberty, private enterprise, wealth, thrift, and independence will all be lost in such circumstances.

For there is one circumstance in particular that must be noted: among the services we demand from the government, the most important is *security*. To

<summary>Bastiat explains that unlike private markets where prices are negotiated freely, government services are compulsory and their costs are set by the state, leading to a natural tendency for the government to over-expand and consume the public's wealth.</summary>
<keywords>political economy, government services, taxation, Malthusian law, security, plunder, spoliation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s analysis of how governments, once granted the power to provide "public services," often use that power to exploit the citizens they are meant to protect. He transition from the general tendency of men to live at each other's expense to the specific mechanisms of state-sanctioned plunder.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

### 124

To ensure this, a force is needed that is capable of overcoming all other forces—whether individual or collective, internal or external—that could be brought against it. When combined with the unfortunate human tendency to live at the expense of others, a danger emerges that is self-evident.

Just consider the immense scale on which plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note> has been practiced throughout history through the abuse and excess of government powers. Consider what meager services have been rendered to the people, and what massive services the public authorities have exacted from them, among the Assyrians, Babylonians, Egyptians, Romans, Persians, Turks, Chinese, Russians, English, Spaniards, and French. The imagination is startled at the enormous disproportion.

At length, representative government has been established, and we might have thought, in theory <note>original: "a priori"</note>, that these disorders would have disappeared as if by magic.

In fact, the principle of representative government is this:

> “The people themselves, through their representatives, are to decide on the nature and extent of the functions which they judge it right to regard as *public services*, and the amount of payment to be attached to such *services*.”

Since the tendency to seize the property of others and the tendency to defend one's own property were thus placed in opposite scales, we should have thought that the second would have outweighed the first.

I am convinced that this is what must ultimately happen, but it has not happened so far.

Why? For two very simple reasons: governments have had too much, and the people too little, wisdom <note>original: "sagacity"</note>.

Governments are very skillful. They act with method and consistency, following a well-arranged plan that is constantly improved by tradition and experience. They study men and their passions. If they discover, for example, that people are driven by warlike impulses, they stimulate this fatal tendency and add fuel to the flame. They surround the nation with dangers through the actions of diplomacy, and then they quite naturally demand more soldiers, more sailors, more arsenals, and more fortifications. Sometimes they do not even have to ask for these, but have them offered; and then they have rank, pensions,

<summary>Bastiat explains how governments use their monopoly on force to engage in large-scale plunder, even within representative systems, by exploiting human passions like the impulse for war.</summary>
<keywords>plunder, spoliation, representative government, public services, human nature, state power, military spending</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of how governments expand their power. The previous page ended by explaining that even in representative governments, the people often fail to curb state expansion because the government possesses too much influence and too many positions to distribute.</meta>

# THE PHYSIOLOGY OF PLUNDER <note>original: "SPOLIATION"</note>

### 123

...and positions to distribute. To fund all of this, enormous sums of money are required, and the government turns to taxes and loans.

If the nation is generous, the government promises to cure every human problem: to jumpstart trade, help agriculture thrive, develop industry, support the arts and education, wipe out poverty, and so on. To do all this, they only need to create new government jobs <note>original: "offices"</note> and pay new officials <note>original: "functionaries"</note>.

In short, the strategy is to frame regulations and restrictions as helpful services. Consequently, the nation pays not for actual help, but for harm <note>original: "disservices"</note>. As governments grow to massive proportions, they end up consuming half of the tax revenue they collect. Meanwhile, the people—wondering why they must work so hard despite hearing about new inventions that should multiply products indefinitely <note>original: "ad infinitum"</note>—remain as naive as overgrown children.

While the government shows great skill and cleverness, the public shows very little. When it comes time to elect the people who decide the scope and cost of government actions, whom do they pick? The very agents of that government! In doing so, they give the executive branch the power to set the limits of its own work and its own taxes. They act like the *The Middle-Class Gentleman* <note>original: "Bourgeois Gentilhomme," a character from a play by Molière who is easily tricked by those seeking his money</note> who, instead of choosing the number and style of his own coats, left the decision entirely—to his tailor.

When things have gone from bad to worse, the people finally open their eyes—not to the solution (they haven’t reached that point yet), but to the problem.

Governing is such a pleasant business that everyone wants a part of it. The people's advisors never stop telling them: "We see your suffering and we pity you. Things would be very different if we were the ones governing you."

In the meantime, and often for a long time, there are rebellions and riots <note>original: "émeutes"</note>. When the people are defeated, the cost of the war only adds to their tax burden. When they are victorious, the government's staff changes, but the same abuses remain unreformed.

This state of affairs will continue until the people learn to recognize and defend their true interests. Thus, we always return to the same conclusion: our only hope lies in the progress of public awareness <note>original: "public intelligence"</note>.

<vocab>spoliation: the act of plundering or taking property by force or authority; functionaries: government officials or bureaucrats; disservices: actions that are harmful rather than helpful; ad infinitum: Latin for "forever" or "without limit"; Bourgeois Gentilhomme: a reference to a satirical play by Molière about a man with more money than sense; émeutes: French for riots or civil disturbances; public intelligence: the general level of education and awareness among the citizenry</vocab>

<summary>Bastiat explains how governments maintain power by promising to solve all social ills through the creation of expensive bureaucracies, even when those actions harm the public. He concludes that political revolutions are often useless unless the public becomes educated enough to recognize and resist these economic abuses.</summary>
<keywords>bureaucracy, taxation, government expansion, public awareness, political reform, Frederic Bastiat, Molière, civil unrest</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how governments justify their expansion by framing their interventions as necessary "services." It builds on the previous page's example of a public that mistakenly grants the government the power to set its own limits and tax levels.</meta>

-> ECONOMIC SOPHISMS <-

Certain nations seem remarkably prone to becoming the prey of government plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note>; this is especially true in places where the people, losing sight of their own dignity and initiative, feel they are lost unless they are *governed and controlled* in every aspect of life. Even without much travel, I have seen countries where it is believed that agriculture cannot progress unless the government maintains experimental farms; that horses would soon disappear if not for state-run stud farms <note>original: "haras"</note>; and that parents would either fail to educate their children or allow them to be taught immorality if the state did not dictate the curriculum, and so on. In such countries, revolutions happen one after another, and the governing powers are replaced in rapid succession. But the people continue, nevertheless, to be governed on the principle of paternalistic "mercy and compassion"—for the tendency I am exposing here is the very food upon which such governments live. This continues until the people finally realize that it is better to leave the greatest possible number of *services* in the category of those which the parties involved exchange at a *price set by free and open bargaining*.

We have seen that an *exchange of services* is what makes a society; and it must be an exchange of good and honest services. But we have also shown that men have a strong interest, and consequently an irresistible urge, to exaggerate the relative value of the services they provide. In truth, I can see no other cure for this evil than the freedom of those to whom these services are offered to either accept or refuse them.

This is why some people turn to the law to control this freedom in certain branches of industry. This kind of plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note> is called Privilege or Monopoly. Take careful note of its origin and character.

Everyone knows that the services one brings to the general market are valued and paid for in proportion to their rarity. The law is then called upon to intervene and drive out of the market all those who offer similar <note>original: "analogous"</note> services. Or—which amounts to the same thing—if a specific tool or machine is required to provide those services, the law steps in to give one person exclusive possession of it.

This variety of plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note>, being the principal subject of the...

<summary>Bastiat argues that people often mistakenly look to the government to manage every aspect of life, which leads to a cycle of revolutions and state-sanctioned monopolies that stifle free market exchange.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the government justifies its expansion through "services." It shifts focus to the "Physiology of Plunder," explaining how systematic monopoly and protectionism create a cycle of mutual impoverishment and social instability. The author also defends his use of the harsh term "spoliation" (plunder) to describe these economic systems.</meta>

# PHYSIOLOGY OF PLUNDER <note>original: "spoliation"</note>.

Since I have already discussed this in the current volume, I shall not expand on it here, but instead content myself with one remark.

When a monopoly is an isolated occurrence, it never fails to enrich the person who holds it. It may happen, then, that other classes of producers, instead of waiting for the downfall of this monopoly, demand similar monopolies for themselves. This type of plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note>, when built into a system, becomes the most ridiculous of delusions for everyone; and the final result is that every man believes himself to be deriving greater profit from a market which is *impoverished by everyone*.

It is unnecessary to add that this strange system <note>original: "régime"</note> introduces a universal hostility among all classes, all professions, and all nations. It calls for the constant but always uncertain intervention of government action; it gives rise to all the abuses we have listed; it places all branches of industry in a state of hopeless insecurity; and it accustoms men to rely upon the law, rather than themselves, for their means of survival. It would be difficult to imagine a more active cause of social instability <note>original: "perturbation"</note>.

### JUSTIFICATION.

But it may be said, "Why use this harsh term, Plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note>? It is coarse; it wounds, irritates, and turns all calm and moderate men against you—it poisons the controversy."

To speak plainly, I respect the people involved, and I believe in the sincerity of nearly all the supporters of protectionism. I claim no right to question the personal integrity, the refinement, or the philanthropy of anyone whatsoever. I repeat again that protectionism is the fruit—the fatal fruit—of a common error, of which everyone, or at least the majority of men, are at once the victims and the accomplices. But despite all this, I cannot prevent things from being what they are.

Imagine Diogenes <note>A Greek philosopher known for his radical honesty and for living in a ceramic jar or "tub."</note> putting his head out of his tub and saying, "Athenians, you are served by slaves. Has it never occurred to you that you are thereby exercising over your brothers the most wicked form of plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note>?"

Or, again, imagine a tribune <note>An elected official in ancient Rome.</note> speaking thus in the forum: "Romans, you derive all your means of survival from the looting of all nations in succession."

<summary>The author argues that widespread protectionism and monopolies create a system of mutual plunder that impoverishes society and breeds instability. He defends his use of the term "spoliation" (plunder) as a necessary, if harsh, description of these economic errors.</summary>

<keywords>monopoly, spoliation, plunder, protectionism, social instability, Diogenes, economic systems, government intervention</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s defense of using the term "plunder" (spoliation) to describe economic protectionism. He argues that he is not attacking the morality of individuals, but rather a flawed social system that blinds people to injustice, drawing parallels to the historical acceptance of slavery.</meta>

In saying so, they would only be speaking the unquestionable truth. But should we conclude from this that Athens and Rome were inhabited only by bad and dishonest people? Should we hold Socrates, Plato, Cato, and Cincinnatus <note>Cato the Elder and Cincinnatus were famous Roman statesmen known for their personal virtue and integrity.</note> in contempt?

Who could entertain such a thought for even a moment? But these great men lived in a social environment <note>original: "medium"</note> that stripped away all awareness of injustice. We know that Aristotle could not even conceive of a society existing without slavery.

In modern times, slavery existed until very recently without causing many pangs of conscience in the minds of plantation owners. Armies serve as the instruments of great conquests—which is to say, great acts of plunder <note>original: "spoliations"</note>. But that does not mean they do not contain many soldiers and officers with sensibilities as refined as those found in business careers—perhaps even more so. These are men who would blush at the very thought of anything dishonest and would face a thousand deaths rather than stoop to any mean or shameful act.

We must not blame individuals, but rather the general movement that carries them along and blinds them to the reality of the situation—a movement for which society as a whole is responsible.

The same holds true for monopoly. I blame the system and not individuals—society at large, and not its individual members. If the greatest philosophers were unable to see the wickedness <note>original: "iniquity"</note> of slavery, how much more easily might farmers and manufacturers be led to a mistaken view of the nature and effects of a protectionist system <note>original: "system of restriction"</note>!

## TWO PRINCIPLES OF MORALITY.

> "Well, are we wrong to accuse economists of being dry and cold? What a bleak picture of human nature! What! Is...
