<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s discussion on the systemic nature of "spoliation" (plunder). He argues that while critics of political economy view society as inherently chaotic and unjust, they simultaneously believe that a single "reformer" or "organizer" can rewrite human nature to achieve a utopia.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
# TWO PRINCIPLES OF MORALITY.
<sig>I</sig>

> "...is plunder <note>original: "spoliation"</note>, then, to be seen as an inevitable, almost normal force? Does it take every form, working under every excuse—both with and without the law—misusing even the most sacred things, preyed upon the weak and the gullible, and advancing exactly as much as those weaknesses allow? Is there a more depressing picture in the world than this?"

The question is not whether the picture is depressing, but whether it is true. History will tell us.

It is strange enough that those who criticize political economy (or *economism* <note>original: "economisme"</note>, as they like to call it) because that science studies man and the world as they actually are, are themselves much further advanced in pessimism—at least regarding the past and the present—than the economists they look down upon. Open their books and their journals; what do you find? Bitterness and a hatred of society carried to such a pitch that the very word *civilization* is, in their eyes, a synonym for injustice, disorder, and anarchy. They even go as far as denouncing *liberty*, so little confidence do they have in the development of the human race as the natural result of its own organization. Liberty! They believe it is liberty that is driving us closer and closer to ruin.

Granted, these writers are optimists regarding the future. For if the human race, left to its own devices, has followed a wrong path for six thousand years, a "discoverer" has supposedly appeared to point out the true way to safety. No matter how little the flock respects the shepherd's staff, they will be led without fail toward the promised land. There, happiness awaits them without any effort on their part, and order, security, and harmony are the easy rewards of their lack of foresight <note>original: "improvidence"</note>.

The human race only has to consent to these reformers changing—to use Rousseau’s <note>Jean-Jacques Rousseau (1712–1778), a philosopher whose work *The Social Contract* influenced the French Revolution.</note> expression—*its physical and moral constitution*.

It is not the job of political economy to ask what society might have become if God had made man differently than He chose to. Perhaps it is a matter of regret that, in the beginning, Providence forgot to consult some of our modern *organizers* <note>original: "organisateurs"</note>. Just as the mechanisms of the heavens would have been built very differently if the Creator had consulted Alfonso the Wise <note>Alfonso X of Castile (1221–1284), known as "the Wise," famously joked that if God had consulted him during Creation, he would have recommended a simpler design for the universe.</note>, in

<summary>Bastiat contrasts the realism of political economy with the pessimism of social reformers, who view society as inherently broken but believe it can be "fixed" by re-engineering human nature.</summary>
<keywords>spoliation, plunder, political economy, economism, civilization, liberty, Rousseau, organizers, Alfonso the Wise, social reform</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s critique of utopian social reformers. He argues that rather than trying to redesign humanity from scratch—as thinkers like Charles Fourier suggested—we must instead study the inherent laws of human nature and the existing social order to achieve progress.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

...in the same way, had God only taken the advice of Fourier <note>Charles Fourier (1772–1837) was a French philosopher and influential early socialist thinker who proposed reorganizing society into self-sufficient communities called "phalansteries."</note>, the social order would have had no resemblance to the one in which we are forced to breathe, live, and move. But since we are here—since **in Him we live, move, and have our being** <note>original: *in eo vivimus, movemur, et sumus*; a Latin reference to Acts 17:28</note>—all we have to do is study and familiarize ourselves with the laws of the social order in which we find ourselves, especially if its improvement depends essentially on our knowledge of these laws.

We cannot prevent the human heart from being the seat of insatiable desires.

We cannot arrange it so that these desires can be satisfied without labor.

We cannot arrange it so that man does not have as much dislike for labor as he has desire for enjoyment.

We cannot arrange it so that, from this human nature, there does not result a perpetual effort on the part of certain men to increase their own share of enjoyments at the expense of others—pushing onto them, by force or cunning, the labor and exertion which are the necessary conditions for obtaining such enjoyments.

It is not for us to ignore universal history or stifle the voice of the past, which tells us that such has been the state of affairs from the beginning. We cannot deny that war, slavery, <term>bondage</term> <note>original: "thraldom"</note>, <term>religious manipulation</term> <note>original: "priestcraft"</note>, government abuses, privileges, frauds of every kind, and monopolies have been the undeniable and terrible expressions of these two sentiments combined in the heart of man: *the desire for enjoyment and a dislike of fatigue.*

**"By the sweat of your brow you shall eat bread."** <note>original: "In the sweat of thy face shalt thou eat bread," a reference to the curse in Genesis 3:19</note> Yes, but everyone desires to have the greatest possible amount of bread with the least possible amount of sweat. Such is the testimony of history.

But let us be thankful that history also shows us that the distribution of enjoyments and efforts has a tendency to become more and more equal among men.

Unless we shut our eyes to the light of the sun, we must admit that society has made progress in this respect.

If this is so, there must be in society a natural and divine force, a law that increasingly rejects the principle of dishonesty and increasingly realizes the principle of justice.

<summary>Bastiat argues that human nature is defined by a desire for enjoyment and an aversion to labor, leading historically to systemic plunder. However, he maintains that society possesses a natural, providential law that drives humanity toward greater justice and equality.</summary>
<keywords>Charles Fourier, human nature, labor, social order, plunder, history of injustice, social progress, economic laws</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that human nature contains inherent forces for progress. Having established that some men naturally seek to live at the expense of others through "spoliation" (plunder), Bastiat now introduces two distinct moral systems that work to counteract this tendency.</meta>

## TWO PRINCIPLES OF MORALITY

We maintain that this force exists in society, and that God has placed it there. If it did not exist, we would be reduced, like Utopian dreamers, to searching for it in artificial social schemes—arrangements which would require a total change in the *physical and moral nature of man*. Or rather, we would conclude that the search was useless and vain, for the simple reason that we cannot understand how a lever can work without a fulcrum.

Let us try, then, to describe the beneficent force which tends gradually to overcome the mischievous and injurious force to which we have given the name of <term>spoliation</term> <note>original: "spoliation"; this refers to the act of plunder, or the unfair seizure of property and labor</note>, the presence of which is only too well explained by logic and confirmed by experience.

Every injurious or hurtful act necessarily has two parts: the point where it begins and the point where it ends—the **point of origin** <note>original: *terminus a quo*</note> and the **point of impact** <note>original: *terminus ad quem*</note>—the man who acts, and the man acted upon; or, in the language of the <term>schoolmen</term> <note>original: "schoolmen"; referring to the Scholastic philosophers of the Middle Ages</note>, the *agent* and the *patient* <note>In this context, the "patient" is the person who receives or suffers the action of the agent</note>.

We may be protected, then, from an injurious act in two ways: by the voluntary restraint of the *agent*, or by the resistance of the *patient*.

These two moral principles, far from opposing each other, work together: namely, the religious or philosophical moral principle, and the moral principle which I shall venture to call *economic*.

The religious moral principle, in order to ensure the suppression of an injurious act, addresses the person responsible; it addresses man in his capacity as the *agent* and says to him:

> "Amend your life; purify your conduct; cease to do evil; learn to do well; subdue your passions; sacrifice self-interest; do not oppress your neighbor, whom it is your duty to love and assist; first of all be just, and be charitable afterward."

This type of moral principle will always be considered the most beautiful and touching—the one that best displays the human race in its natural majesty, which will be most praised by the eloquent, and call forth the greatest amount of admiration and sympathy.

The economic moral principle aims at achieving the same result, but it addresses man more especially in his capacity as the *patient*. It points out to him the effects of human actions, and...

<summary>Bastiat introduces two ways to combat social "spoliation" (plunder): the religious moral principle, which urges the aggressor to be virtuous, and the economic moral principle, which empowers the victim to resist.</summary>
<keywords>morality, spoliation, agent and patient, religious ethics, economic ethics, social order, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that there are two ways to stop social harm (spoliation): the voluntary restraint of the person causing harm, or the resistance of the person being harmed. Bastiat is now explaining how the "economic principle of morality" encourages that resistance by appealing to the intellect.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

By that simple explanation, this principle encourages the victim to react against those who injure him and to honor those who are useful to him. It strives to spread enough common sense, information, and justified skepticism among the oppressed masses to make oppression increasingly difficult and dangerous.

We must also note that the economic principle of morality also affects the oppressor. A harmful act produces both good and evil: evil for the person subjected to it, and good for the person who benefits from it—otherwise, the act would never have been considered in the first place. But the good and the evil do not cancel each other out. The total amount of evil always and necessarily outweighs the good. This is because the very presence of oppression causes a loss of productivity, creates dangers, provokes retaliation, and makes expensive security measures necessary. Simply explaining these effects not only triggers a reaction from the oppressed but also brings everyone with an honest heart to the side of justice and undermines the security of the oppressors themselves.

It is easy to understand why this economic principle of morality—which is more potential than formal, being essentially a scientific demonstration that would lose its effectiveness if it changed its character—is accused of being cold and uninspired <note>original: "dry and prosaic"</note>. This principle speaks to the intellect rather than the heart. It aims to convince rather than persuade. It does not give advice, but provides proofs. Its mission is not to touch the feelings but to enlighten the judgment. It achieves victory over vice only by depriving it of support. Because of this, it is easy to see why it is criticized.

The criticism is accurate in its description but unfair in its application. It is the same as saying that <term>political economy</term> <note>The study of how wealth is produced, distributed, and consumed within a society</note> does not discuss everything and does not understand everything—that it is not, in short, a universal science. But who ever claimed it was, or made such an outrageous demand on its behalf?

This accusation would be fair only if political economy presented its methods as the only ones available, and had the arrogance, so to speak, to deny to philosophy and

<summary>Bastiat explains how "economic morality" uses logic to show that oppression is inefficient and costly for society. He argues that while this approach may seem cold or "dry," it is a necessary scientific tool that complements rather than replaces traditional philosophy.</summary>

<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, economic morality, political economy, oppression, social justice, logic vs. emotion, incentives</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that while traditional morality seeks to change the heart of the oppressor, the economic principle of morality seeks to enlighten the judgment of the victim, making oppression harder to sustain.</meta>

# TWO PRINCIPLES OF MORALITY.

...religion has its own specific and unique ways of working toward the personal development and improvement of humanity.

Let us acknowledge, then, that morality (in the traditional sense) and political economy act together. One condemns the harmful act based on its motive and exposes its indecency; the other discredits it in our judgment by showing its negative consequences.

We might even admit that the victory of the religious moralist, when successful, is more beautiful, more comforting, and more profound. However, we must also recognize that the victory of the economist is easier to achieve and more certain.

In just a few lines—worth more than many thick volumes—Jean-Baptiste Say <note>J.B. Say (1767–1832) was a leading French economist who popularized Adam Smith's ideas in Europe.</note> remarked that to end the chaos a hypocrite brings to an honorable family, there are only two choices: reform *Tartuffe* (the hypocrite) or *sharpen the wits of Orgon* (the man being fooled). Molière, that great observer of human nature, seems to have consistently viewed the second method as more effective.

It is exactly the same in real life and on the world stage.

Tell me what Caesar did, and I will tell you the character of the Romans during his era.

Tell me what modern diplomacy achieves, and I will tell you the moral state of the nations where it is practiced.

We would not be paying nearly two billion <note>original: "milliards"</note> [approximately £80,000,000] in taxes if we did not give the power to vote for those taxes to the very people who live off of them.

We would not have been stuck with all the difficulties and expenses caused by the "African question" <note>This refers to the French colonization of Algeria, which was a subject of intense economic debate in the 1840s.</note> if we had realized that *two plus two equals four* in political economy just as it does in math.

Mr. Guizot <note>François Guizot (1787–1874) was a prominent French statesman and historian serving under King Louis Philippe.</note> would never have felt entitled to say that *France is wealthy enough to pay for her glory* if the French people had not been infatuated with a false sense of glory.

That same statesman would never have dared to say that *liberty is too precious a thing for France to haggle about its price*, if the public had only considered that *a massive government budget and true liberty are incompatible*.

<summary>Bastiat argues that while religious morality addresses the motives of the oppressor, economic morality protects society by enlightening the victims. He suggests that political corruption and expensive wars only persist because the public does not yet understand the economic consequences of "false glory."</summary>

<keywords>J.B. Say, Molière, Guizot, political economy, morality, taxes, French history, colonialism, government spending, liberty</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's argument that social progress is better achieved by educating victims to recognize exploitation rather than waiting for exploiters to have a change of heart.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

It is not the monopolists, but their victims, who maintain monopolies.

Regarding elections, it is not because there are parties offering bribes that there are people ready to receive them, but the other way around. The proof of this is that it is the people who receive the bribes who, in the long run, pay the cost of that corruption. Is it not their responsibility to put an end to the practice?

Let the religious principles of morality try, if they can, to touch the hearts of the "Tartuffes" <note>original: "Tartuffes"; a reference to the hypocritical protagonist of Molière's play, representing religious pretension.</note>, the "Caesars" <note>original: "Cæsars"; used here to represent absolute rulers or dictators.</note>, the colonialists, the sinecurists <term>sinecurist: a person who holds a position that provides a salary but requires little or no actual work</term>, the monopolists, and others like them. However, the clear duty of political economy is to enlighten the people they have fooled.

Which of these two processes has a more effective <note>original: "efficacious"</note> influence on social progress? I feel it is almost unnecessary to say that I believe it is the second. I fear we can never free mankind from the necessity of first learning *defensive morality*.

In all I have heard, read, and observed, I have never yet seen a single instance where a widespread abuse was ended by the voluntary choice of those who profited from it.

On the other hand, I have seen many abuses stopped by the determined resistance of those who suffered from them.

Therefore, exposing the effects of abuses is the surest way to end them. This is especially true of policies like trade restriction <term>policy of restriction: protectionist trade policies like tariffs and quotas intended to limit foreign competition</term>. While these policies cause real harm to the general public, they provide nothing but illusion and deception even to those who imagine they are profiting from them!

After all, can this kind of morality we are advocating achieve by itself all the social perfection that the sympathetic nature of the human soul and its noble abilities lead us to hope for? I am far from saying so. Even if we assume the complete spread of *defensive morality*, it only results in the conviction that human interests, when rightly understood, are always in harmony with justice and the public good <note>original: "general utility"</note>. Such a society, although certainly well-ordered, would not be very inspiring. There would be fewer cheats simply because there would be fewer victims to fool. Vice would always be lurking in the background, starving for lack of support, but it would return the moment that support was restored to it.

<summary>The author argues that social abuses and monopolies are ended by educating the victims rather than waiting for the perpetrators to reform. While "defensive morality" creates an orderly society by aligning interest with justice, it is only the first step toward true social perfection.</summary>

<keywords>monopoly, corruption, defensive morality, political economy, social progress, trade restriction, justice, general utility</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's comparison between the roles of the economist and the religious moralist in improving society.</meta>

## TWO PRINCIPLES OF MORALITY.

The caution of each person would be reinforced by the alertness of everyone else. However, a reform that limits itself to regulating outward actions, never going deeper than the surface, would fail to reach people's hearts and consciences. Such a society would remind us of those precise, strict, and fair men who are ready to resent the slightest violation of their rights and defend themselves from attacks on all sides. You might respect them; you might even admire them; you might elect them as your representatives; but *you would never make them your friends.*

But the *two principles of morality* I have described, instead of working against each other, work together, attacking vice from opposite sides. While the economists do their part—sharpening the wits of the Orgons <note>original: "Orgons"; a reference to the gullible character in Molière's play *Tartuffe*, representing those who are easily deceived by hypocrites.</note>, removing prejudices, encouraging a healthy and necessary skepticism, and explaining the true nature of things and actions—let the religious moralist carry out their more appealing, though more difficult, work on their side. Let them confront dishonesty directly; let them pursue it into the most secret corners of the heart; let them vividly describe the beauty of charity, self-sacrifice, and devotion; let them open the fountains of virtue, while we can only dry up the fountains of vice. This is their duty, and it is a noble one. But why should they question the usefulness of the duty that has fallen to us?

In a society that is not necessarily made of personally virtuous individuals, but is nonetheless well-ordered because of the *economic principle of morality* (which means an understanding of the *economy of the social body*), would there not be a better opportunity for the work of the religious moralist?

As the saying goes, habit is second nature.

A country might still be unhappy even if its people have long been unaccustomed to injustice because of the constant resistance of an informed public. But it seems to me that such a country would be well-prepared to receive a more pure and elevated moral teaching. We make significant progress toward what is good once we are no longer used to what is evil. Humans can never remain stationary. Once they are turned away from the path of vice, feeling that it leads only to disgrace, they will feel the attraction of virtue even more strongly.

<summary>The author argues that while economics creates a well-ordered society by preventing external abuses, religious morality is needed to transform the human heart, and both must work together for social progress.</summary>
<keywords>economic morality, religious morality, virtue, vice, Orgon, social order, ethics, reform</keywords>

ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

Society must perhaps pass through this practical state of transition, in which people practice virtue for reasons of prudence, in order to rise later to that more beautiful and poetic state where such calculating motives are no longer needed.

### III.

## THE TWO HATCHETS.

->Petition of Jacques Bonhomme <note>The name "Jacques Bonhomme" is a traditional French nickname for a peasant or commoner, similar to "Average Joe."</note>, Carpenter, to Mr. Cunin-Gridaine, Minister of Commerce.<-

->MR. MANUFACTURER-MINISTER,<- <note>original: "Monsieur le Fabricant-Ministre." This title highlights the conflict of interest in Laurent Cunin-Gridaine being both a businessman and the Minister of Commerce who sets trade policy.</note>

I am a carpenter by trade, as was Saint Joseph of old; and I use the hatchet and adze for your benefit.

Now, while I am busy chopping and hewing from morning to night on the lands of our Lord the King, the idea has struck me that my labor may be considered *national* <note>Bastiat uses "national" here to mock the protectionist argument that certain industries deserve state support because they are vital to the nation.</note>, just like yours.

And in these circumstances, I cannot see why "protection" should not visit my woodyard just as it does your workshop.

For, truth be told, if you make cloth, I make roofs; and both, in their own way, shelter our customers from the cold and the rain.

And yet I have to chase after customers, while customers chase after you. You have found a way to secure them by preventing them from buying from anyone else, while my customers apply to whoever they please.

What is so surprising about this? Mr. Cunin, the Minister of State, has not forgotten Mr. Cunin, the manufacturer—which is all quite natural. But alas! My humble trade has not given a Minister to France, even though it was practiced in biblical times by far more distinguished people.

And in the eternal laws which I find in the Bible, I cannot find the slightest phrase that could be quoted by carpenters to justify enriching themselves at the expense of others.

You see, then, how I am situated. I earn fifteen pence a

# THE TWO HATCHETS.

...any day that is not a Sunday or a holiday. I offer you my services at the same time as a Flemish carpenter offers you his, and because he lowers his price by a halfpenny, you give him the preference.

But I also need to clothe myself; and if a Belgian weaver presents his cloth alongside yours, you drive him and his cloth out of the country.

Consequently, because I am forced to shop at your store—even though it is the most expensive—my poor fifteen pence go no further in reality than fourteen.

In fact, they are not worth more than thirteen! For instead of expelling the Belgian weaver *at your own expense* (which was the least you could do), you make me pay for the people you set at his heels for your own purposes.

And as a great number of your fellow lawmakers—with whom you are on remarkably good terms—each take a halfpenny or a penny under the pretext of "protecting" iron, coal, oil, or corn, I find, when everything is taken into account, that I have only been able to save seven or eight pence of my fifteen from being plundered.

You will no doubt tell me that these small halfpennies, which pass in this way from my pocket to yours, support the workers who live around your estate and enable you to live in a style of magnificence. To this I will only reply that if the money had been left with me—the person who earned it—it would have supported the workers in my own neighborhood.

Be this as it may, Mr. Manufacturer-Minister <note>original: "Monsieur le Ministre-fabricant"</note>, knowing that I would be poorly received by you, I have not come to require you to withdraw the restriction which you impose on your customers, even though I have every right to do so. I prefer following the ordinary course, and I approach you to solicit a little bit of *protection* for myself.

Here, of course, you will raise an objection. "My good friend," you will say, "I would protect you and your fellow workmen with all my heart; but how can I confer custom-house favors <note>A "custom-house favor" refers to a protective tariff or trade restriction that benefits a specific industry.</note> on carpentry? What use would it be to prohibit the importation of houses by sea or by land?"

That would be a good joke, to be sure; but by dint of thinking, I have discovered another way of favoring the children of St. Joseph <note>Saint Joseph is the patron saint of carpenters; the phrase refers to all workers in that trade.</note>, which you will welcome the more willingly, I...

...hope, as it is no different from the privilege that you vote for every year in your own favor.

The method of helping us, which I have so wonderfully discovered, is to ban the use of sharp axes in this country.

I claim that such a *restriction* would be no more illogical or arbitrary than the one you impose on us regarding your cloth.

Why do you drive away the Belgians? Because they sell more cheaply than you do. And why do they sell more cheaply? Because they are more efficient manufacturers than you <note>original: "Because they have a certain degree of superiority over you as manufacturers."</note>.

Therefore, the difference between you and a Belgian is exactly the same as the difference in my trade between a blunt axe and a sharp one.

And do you force me, as a craftsman, to buy from you the product of a blunt hatchet?

Think of the country as a whole as a worker who wants to use his labor to get everything he needs—including cloth.

There are two ways to do this.

The first is to spin and weave the wool ourselves.

The second is to make other items—for example, French clocks, wallpaper <note>original: "paper-hangings"</note>, or wine—and trade them with the Belgians for the cloth we want.

Of these two methods, the one that gives the best result is represented by the sharp axe, and the other by the blunt one.

You do not deny that right now in France, we obtain a piece of fabric <note>original: "stuff"</note> through our own looms (the blunt axe) with *more labor* than by producing and trading wine (the sharp axe). You don’t even try to deny it; in fact, it is precisely because of this *extra labor* (which you claim creates wealth) that you recommend—no, you *force*—the use of the worse tool.

Now, just be consistent and fair. If you want to be just, treat poor carpenters the same way you treat yourselves.

Pass a law to this effect:

> "*From now on, no one is allowed to use any beams or rafters unless they are made and shaped with blunt hatchets.*"

And see what will happen immediately.

While we currently strike a hundred blows with an axe, we will then strike three hundred. The work that now takes us an hour will then take three. What a powerful boost this will give to labor! And if the academic supporters of *free trade* ever dare to question the usefulness of this policy, we know exactly where to find the arguments to prove them wrong. Your *Inquiry of 1834* <note>The Inquiry of 1834 was a French government investigation into commercial policy where various industries argued for continued protectionist tariffs.</note> is still available. We will win using that weapon; for in those documents, you have brilliantly defended the cause of trade restrictions—and of blunt axes, which are actually the same thing.

### IV.

## THE LABORERS' LOWER COUNCIL

> "What! You have the nerve to demand for all citizens the right to sell, buy, barter, and exchange; to provide and receive service for service, and to judge for themselves—on the sole condition that they act honestly and pay their taxes <note>original: "comply with the demands of the public treasury"</note>? Then you simply want to rob our workers of their jobs, their wages, and their bread!"

This is what people say to us. I know very well what to think of it, but what I want to know is what the workers themselves think.

I have an excellent method for investigating this. I will not look to those *Upper Councils of Industry*, where big landowners call themselves "laborers," rich shipowners call themselves "sailors," and wealthy shareholders pretend to be "workers"—using their supposed charity <note>original: "philanthropy"</note> for their own profit in ways we all recognize.

No; we must deal with actual workers—joiners, carpenters, stonemasons, tailors, and shoemakers...

...dyers, blacksmiths, innkeepers, grocers, and others who have founded a *friendly society* <term>original: "friendly society"; a mutual aid organization where members contribute small amounts of money to a fund to support one another during illness, injury, or old age</term> in my village.

On my own initiative, I transformed this friendly society into a *Lower Council of Labor*. I then started an investigation that will prove to be very important, even though it isn't stuffed with statistics or swollen into a massive, expensive book printed at taxpayer expense.

My goal was to ask these ordinary people how they are—or believe themselves to be—affected by the policy of protection <note>original: "policy of protection"; protectionism, or the use of high taxes (tariffs) on imported goods to encourage people to buy domestic products</note>. The president pointed out that this might violate the basic rules of the *Association*. In France—supposedly the land of liberty—people who form an *association* often have to give up their right to discuss *politics*; in other words, they lose the right to discuss their own shared interests. However, after some hesitation, he agreed to put the question on the schedule for the day.

The assembly was divided into committees representing different trades. Each committee was given a form to fill out after fifteen days of discussion.

On the appointed day, the worthy president (using formal bureaucratic language) took the chair, and fifteen reports were laid upon the table (again, in the official style) and read one after another.

| **Inconveniences** | **Advantages** |
| :--- | :--- |
| **1st:** Because of protectionist policies, we pay more for bread, meat, sugar, firewood, thread, and needles. For us, this is the same as a significant pay cut. | <br> <br> <br> *None.* |
| **2nd:** Because of protectionist policies, our customers also pay more for everything. This leaves them with less money to spend on clothing. | **3rd:** Because of protectionist policies, the fabrics <note>original: "stuffs"</note> we use are expensive. Because of this, people wear their clothes for a longer time or buy fewer items. This again reduces our work and forces us to offer our services for lower pay. | |

LOWER COUNCIL OF LABOR.

## Here is another report:—

### EFFECTS OF PROTECTION—REPORT OF THE BLACKSMITHS.

| Disadvantages | Advantages |
| :--- | :--- |
| 1st. The policy of protectionism imposes a tax upon us every time we eat, drink, keep ourselves warm, or clothe ourselves, and this tax does not go to the national treasury. | |
| 2nd. It imposes a similar tax upon all our fellow citizens who are not in our trade. Because they are poorer as a result, they turn to cheap substitutes for our work, which robs us of the employment we would have otherwise had. | |
| 3rd. It keeps the price of iron so high that it is no longer used in this country for plows, grates, gates, balconies, and so on. Our trade, which could provide jobs for so many people who need them, no longer even provides enough work for ourselves. | None. |
| 4th. The revenue that the treasury fails to collect from goods that are *not imported* <note>Because high taxes (tariffs) stop people from buying foreign goods, the government doesn't collect any import duty on them</note> is instead collected through taxes on the salt we use, postage costs, and other similar items. | |

All the other reports (which I will not trouble the reader with) follow the same pattern. Gardeners, carpenters, shoemakers, clogmakers <term>original: "clogmakers"; makers of wooden shoes, common among the working class at the time</term>, boatmen, and millers all voice the same complaints.

I regret that there are no agricultural laborers in our association. Their report would certainly have been very educational.

But, alas! In our region of the Landes <note>The Landes is a department in southwestern France, which at the time was characterized by vast, marshy plains and a struggling rural population</note>, the poor laborers—"protected" though they are told they are—do not have the money to join an association. Even after insuring their cattle, they find they cannot afford to join a *friendly society* <term>original: "friendly society"; a mutual aid organization where members contribute to a fund to support each other during illness or old age</term>. The supposed "gift" of protectionism does not stop them from being the pariahs <note>original: "parias"; social outcasts or people at the very bottom of the social hierarchy</note> of our social order. What can I even say of the wine-growers?

What I notice especially is the common sense shown by our villagers. They recognize not only the direct harm protectionism does to them, but also the indirect harm which—though it first hits their customers—falls back on them as a ricochet effect <note>original: "par ricochet"; the author uses this to describe how hurting a customer's wealth eventually hurts the person who sells to them</note>.

This is exactly what the economists who write for the *Moniteur Industriel* <note>A prominent 19th-century French newspaper that supported protectionist policies</note> do not seem to understand.

In that case, they might say to themselves, "It is far better to be self-supported among customers who are financially stable than to be *protected* in the midst of an impoverished *client base* <note>original: "clientèle"; the group of people who buy a person's goods or services</note>. ### V.
## DEARNESS—CHEAPNESS.

I think it is necessary to present some theoretical remarks to the reader regarding the illusions created by the words *dearness* <term>high prices</term> and *cheapness* <term>low prices</term>. At first glance, these remarks may be seen as overly subtle. However, the question is not whether they are subtle or the reverse, but whether they are true. I not only believe them to be perfectly true, but also well-suited to provoke thought in people—of whom there are many—who have sincere faith in the effectiveness <note>original: "efficacy"</note> of a protectionist policy.

The advocates of Free Trade <note>original: "Liberty"</note> and the defenders of Trade Restriction are both forced to use the terms *dearness* and *cheapness*. The former group declares themselves in favor of low prices for the benefit of the consumer; the latter group argues in favor of high prices, focusing specifically on the interest of the producer. Others content themselves with saying, "*The producer and consumer are one and the same person*," which leaves the question undecided as to whether the law should promote low or high prices.

In the middle of this conflict, it would seem that the law has only one path to follow: to allow prices to settle and adjust themselves naturally. But then we are attacked by—

<summary>The author argues that protectionism ultimately harms producers by impoverishing their customers and introduces a theoretical analysis of how the concepts of high and low prices are used in economic debates.</summary>
<keywords>protectionism, dearness, cheapness, consumer interest, producer interest, free trade, market prices, economic theory</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the economic concepts of "dearness" and "cheapness," specifically how protectionist policies and free trade impact market prices in ways that often surprise advocates on both sides.</meta>

### DEARNESS—CHEAPNESS.

...the bitter enemies of **free-market policy** <note>original: "laissez faire"</note>. At all costs, they want the law to interfere, without knowing or caring in what direction. And yet, it is up to those who wish to create an artificial dearness <term>high prices</term> or an unnatural cheapness <term>low prices</term> through legal intervention to explain the reasons for their preference. The **burden of proof** <note>original: "onus probandi"</note> rests exclusively on them. Liberty is always considered good until the opposite is proven; and to allow prices to settle and adjust themselves naturally is the very essence of liberty.

But the parties in this dispute have swapped positions. The advocates of high prices have seen their system triumph, and it is now up to the defenders of natural prices to prove the merits of their cause. On both sides, the argument centers on two words; it is therefore essential to determine what these two words really mean.

But first, we must note a series of facts that are likely to confuse the champions of both camps.

To create **high prices** <note>original: "dearness"</note>, the restrictionists <term>those who favor trade restrictions</term> have secured protective taxes <note>original: "duties"</note>. Yet, a **low price** <note>original: "cheapness"</note> that they cannot explain has arrived to disappoint their hopes.

To create **low prices**, free-traders have occasionally succeeded in securing economic liberty; yet, to their astonishment, a rise in prices has been the result.

For instance, in France, to favor agriculture, a tax of 22 percent was placed on foreign wool. It turned out that French wool sold at a lower price after the measure than before it.

In England, to satisfy the consumer, they lowered and eventually removed the tax on foreign wool; and it has resulted in the price of wool being higher than ever in that country.

And these are not isolated cases; the price of wool is governed by the exact same laws that govern the price of everything else. The same result occurs in all similar situations. Contrary to expectations, protection has, to some extent, caused prices to fall, and competition has, to some extent, caused them to rise.

When the resulting confusion reached its peak, the protectionists began saying to their opponents: "It is our system that brings about the low prices of which you..."

<summary>The author argues that the burden of proof lies with those who wish to interfere with natural market prices and provides historical examples where protectionism unexpectedly lowered prices while free trade raised them.</summary>
<keywords>laissez-faire, dearness, cheapness, protectionism, free trade, wool trade, market prices, burden of proof</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how market prices can behave counter-intuitively under different trade systems, leading to the core argument that wealth is measured by abundance rather than by price tags.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

boast so much." To this, the reply was: "It is liberty that has caused the high prices <term>dearness</term> you find so useful." *

At this rate, wouldn't it be amusing to see **low prices** <note>original: "cheapness"</note> become the slogan of the protectionists <note>original: "Rue Hauteville," the headquarters of the pro-tariff Committee of Manufacturers</note>, and **high prices** <note>original: "dearness"</note> become the slogan of the free-traders <note>original: "Rue Choiseul," the headquarters of the Free Trade Association</note>?

Clearly, there is a misunderstanding or an illusion here that needs to be cleared up; that is what I will now try to do.

Suppose there are two isolated nations, each with a million inhabitants. Assume that, **all other things being equal** <note>original: "cæteris paribus"</note>, one nation possesses double the amount of everything—grain, meat, iron, furniture, fuel, books, clothing, and so on—compared to the other.

It would be agreed that the first nation is twice as wealthy as the second.

And yet, there is no reason to assume that a difference in **actual money prices** <note>original: "prix absolus"</note> † exists between the two countries. Nominal prices <term>the price of a good in terms of money, regardless of its purchasing power</term> might even be higher in the wealthier country. For example, everything might be nominally **more expensive** in the United States than in Poland, yet the people in the U.S. are better provided with everything they need. From this, we can conclude that wealth is not found in the nominal price of products, but in their relative abundance. Therefore, when we want to judge the merits of trade restrictions versus free trade, we should not ask which system causes high or low prices. Instead, we should ask which system brings abundance or scarcity.

Note this: because products are traded for one another, a general scarcity of everything or a general abundance of everything leaves the nominal prices of goods at roughly the same level. However, the actual condition of the people in those two countries will be very different.

Let us look even more closely at this subject.

->* * *<-

\* Recently, M. Duchâtel <note>Charles Marie Tanneguy Duchâtel (1803–1867), a French Minister of the Interior who shifted his stance from free trade to protectionism</note>, who had formerly advocated for free trade to achieve low prices, told the Chamber of Deputies: "It would not be difficult for me to prove that protection leads to low prices."

† The expression **absolute prices** <note>original: "prix absolus"</note>, which the author uses here and in chapter 11 of the *First Series*, is not commonly used by English economists. From the context in both cases, I take it to mean actual money prices, or what Adam Smith calls "nominal prices." — **TRANSLATOR.**

<summary>Bastiat argues that the true measure of a nation's wealth is the abundance of goods available to its citizens, not the nominal money price of those goods. He illustrates this by showing that a country can have high prices but still be wealthier than a low-price country if it possesses more physical resources and products.</summary>
<keywords>abundance, scarcity, nominal prices, absolute prices, free trade, protectionism, Duchâtel, economic wealth, purchasing power</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page explains why price changes often defy expectations, introducing the idea that "dearness" (high prices) and "cheapness" (low prices) can each result from two very different causes.</meta>

-> ### DEARNESS—CHEAPNESS ### <-

When we see that increasing or reducing duties <term>taxes on imported goods</term> produces results so different from what we expected—where prices sometimes drop after taxation and rise after trade is freed—it becomes the essential job of economics <note>original: "political economy"</note> to find an explanation for these facts that seem to contradict our ideas. After all, if a science is to be worthy of its name, it must be nothing more than an honest report and a logical explanation of the facts.

The phenomenon we are looking at here is explained very clearly by one factor we must never forget.

**High prices** <note>original: "dearness"</note> are caused by **two things**, not just one.

The same is true for **low prices** <note>original: "cheapness"</note>.

It is one of the most widely accepted points in economics that price is decided by the relative state of supply and demand.

Therefore, there are two factors that affect price: supply and demand. These factors are fundamentally changing. They can move in the same direction, in opposite directions, and in countless different combinations. Because of this, the combinations that result in a specific price are endless.

A high price can be the result of either a diminished supply or an increased demand.

A low price can be the result of either an increased supply or a diminished demand.

Consequently, there are two kinds of high prices and two kinds of low prices.

There is a **high price** of a harmful kind: the kind that comes from a decrease in supply. This implies **scarcity** and **hardship** <note>original: "privation"</note> (such as has been felt this year* from the shortage of grain <note>original: "corn"</note>). And there is a **high price** of a beneficial kind: the kind that results from an increase in demand, because the latter suggests the growth of general wealth.

In the same way, there is a **low price** that is desirable: the kind that comes from abundance. There is also a harmful **low price**: the kind caused by the failure of demand and the impoverishment of consumers.

Now, please notice this: trade restriction tends to cause, at the same time, both the harmful cause of high prices,

<note>* This was written in 1847.—TRANSLATOR.</note>

<sig>K</sig>

<summary>Bastiat explains that market prices are determined by the balance of supply and demand, meaning that high or low prices can be either beneficial or harmful depending on whether they are caused by abundance or scarcity.</summary>
<keywords>supply and demand, price theory, dearness, cheapness, scarcity, abundance, taxation, free trade, Frédéric Bastiat</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how high prices (dearness) and low prices (cheapness) are affected by supply and demand, specifically comparing the effects of trade restrictions versus free trade.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS #

...and the harmful cause of low prices <note>original: "cheapness"</note>: harmful high prices <note>original: "dearness"</note> come from reducing the supply—which is the stated goal of trade restriction—and harmful low prices come from also reducing the demand. This happens because restriction misdirects labor and capital and burdens consumers with taxes and obstacles <note>original: "trammels"</note>.

So, as far as *price* is concerned, these two tendencies cancel each other out. This is why the restrictive system—by stifling both demand and supply at the same time—does not even achieve the high prices it aims for in the long run.

But, regarding the *well-being of the people*, these causes do not cancel each other out at all; on the contrary, they work together to make things worse.

The effect of free trade <note>original: "freedom of trade"</note> is exactly the opposite. In its overall result, it might not actually achieve the low prices it promises. This is because it has two tendencies: one toward beneficial low prices through increased supply (abundance), and the other toward noticeable high prices through the growth of demand (general wealth). These two tendencies cancel each other out regarding the ***nominal price*** <term>the price of a good in currency, without adjusting for purchasing power or income levels</term>, but they combine to improve the material prosperity of the population.

In short, under the restrictive system, to the extent that it is effective, people move backward toward a state where both demand and supply are weakened. Under a system of freedom, they progress toward a state where both grow together, without necessarily changing nominal prices. Such prices are not a good measure of wealth. They may stay the same while society is falling into extreme poverty, or while it is advancing toward great prosperity.

We shall now, in a few words, show the practical application of this theory.

A farmer <note>original: "cultivator"</note> in the south of France might believe himself to be very rich because he is protected from foreign competition by import duties. He may be as poor as Job, yet he still imagines that he will eventually get rich through protectionism. In this situation, if we ask him the question posed by the Odier Committee <note>A French parliamentary committee in the 1840s that investigated commercial and industrial policy.</note> in these words—

“Do you desire—yes or no—to be subject to foreign competition?”

<summary>Bastiat argues that while trade restrictions and free trade might both result in similar nominal prices, restrictions impoverish society by stifling both supply and demand, whereas free trade enriches it by expanding both.</summary>
<keywords>Free Trade, protectionism, supply and demand, nominal price, prosperity, Odier Committee, economic theory, France</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how trade restrictions (protectionism) affect prices by manipulating supply and demand, specifically addressing the arguments of the Odier Committee—a 19th-century French protectionist group.</meta>

### DEARNESS AND CHEAPNESS ###

...competition?" <note>Completing the word "competition" from the previous page.</note> His first impulse is to answer "No," and the Odier Committee <note>A group of French manufacturers in the mid-19th century who advocated for protectionist trade policies.</note> proudly welcomes his response.

However, we must go a little deeper into the matter. Unquestionably, foreign competition—indeed, competition in general—is always troublesome; and if one branch of trade alone could get rid of it, that branch of trade would profit greatly for some time.

But protection is not an isolated favor; it is a system. If, to the profit of the farmer <note>original: "agriculturist"</note>, protection tends to create a scarcity of grain <note>original: "corn"</note> and of meat, it tends likewise to create—to the profit of other industries—a scarcity of iron, of cloth, of fuel, tools, etc.; a scarcity, in short, of everything.

Now, if a scarcity of grain tends to raise its price through a reduction <note>original: "diminution"</note> of supply, the scarcity of all other commodities for which grain is exchanged tends to reduce the price of grain by a reduction of demand. Therefore, it is not at all certain that grain will ultimately be a penny more expensive than it would have been under a system of free trade. There is nothing certain in the whole process but this: as there is overall less of every commodity in the country, each man will be less plentifully provided with everything he has occasion to buy.

The farmer should ask himself whether it would not be more for his interest that a certain quantity of grain and cattle should be imported from abroad, and that he should at the same time find himself surrounded by a population in comfortable circumstances, able and willing to consume and pay for all sorts of agricultural produce.

Suppose a region <note>original: "department"</note> in which the people are clothed in rags, fed upon chestnuts, and housed in hovels. How can agriculture flourish in such a locality? What can the soil be made to produce with a well-founded expectation of fair payment <note>original: "remuneration"</note>? Meat? The people do not eat it. Milk? They must content themselves with water. Butter? It is regarded as a luxury. Wool? The use of it is avoided as much as possible. Does anyone imagine that all the ordinary objects of consumption can thus be put beyond the reach of the masses without tending to lower prices as much as protection is tending to raise them?

What has been said of the farmer holds equally true...

<summary>The author argues that while trade protection aims to help producers by raising prices through scarcity, it ultimately impoverishes the entire population—including the producers themselves—by destroying the demand for their goods.</summary>
<keywords>protectionism, free trade, supply and demand, scarcity, agricultural economics, Odier Committee, purchasing power</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that while trade restrictions might seem to help specific producers by limiting supply, they ultimately harm those same producers by stifling general wealth and reducing overall demand.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS #

...of the manufacturer. Our cloth manufacturers assure us that foreign competition will lower prices by increasing the supply. Granted; but will these prices not be raised again by an increased demand? Is the consumption of cloth a fixed and unchanging quantity? Does every man have as much of it as he would like? And if general wealth is improved and developed by the abolition of all these taxes and restrictions, will the population's first use of this newfound freedom <note>original: "emancipation"</note> not be to dress better?

The question—the constantly recurring question—then, is not to find out whether protection is favorable to any one special branch of industry, but whether, when everything is weighed, balanced, and taken into account, restriction is, *in its own nature*, more productive than liberty.

Now, no one will venture to maintain this. On the contrary, we are perpetually met with the admission, "You are right in principle."

If this is so—if restriction confers no benefit on individual branches of industry without doing a greater amount of injury to general wealth—we are forced to conclude that actual money prices, considered by themselves, only express a relationship between each special branch of industry and industry in general, and between supply and demand. On this account, a *remunerative price* <note>original: "remunerative price"; a price high enough to cover production costs and ensure a profit for the producer.</note>, which is the stated goal of protectionism, is actually harmed rather than helped by the system.

The article we published under the title of *Dearness and Cheapness* has brought us several letters. We present them here, along with our replies:

I went everywhere saying, "When free trade is granted, the prices of bread, meat, cloth, linen, iron, and fuel will continue to fall." This displeased the sellers but gave great pleasure to the buyers of these commodities. And now you are casting doubt on whether free trade will—

### DEARNESS—CHEAPNESS ###

...bring us *cheapness* or not. What, then, is to be gained by it? What gain will it be to the people if foreign competition, which may damage their sales, does not benefit them in their purchases?

**Mr. Free-Trader**,---Allow us to tell you that you must have read only half the article that prompted your letter. We stated that free trade acts in exactly the same way as roads, canals, railways, and everything else that makes communication easier by removing obstacles. Its first tendency is to increase the supply of the commodity freed from taxes <note>original: "duty"</note>, and consequently to lower its price. But by simultaneously increasing the supply of all other commodities for which this article is exchanged, it increases the *demand*, and the price rises again because of this. You ask what gain this would be to the people? 

Imagine a balance with several scales, in each of which is placed a certain quantity of the articles you have listed. If you add more corn to one scale, it will tend to drop; but if you also add a little cloth, a little iron, and a little fuel to what the other scales contain, you will restore the balance. If you look only at the crossbar <note>original: "beam"</note>, you will find nothing has changed. But if you look at the people for whose use these articles are produced, you will find them better fed, clothed, and warmed.

**Mr. Editor**,---I am a manufacturer of cloth and a protectionist. I confess that your article on *dearness* and *cheapness* has made me think. It contains some arguments that seem plausible <note>original: "specious"</note>, but they would need to be well-proven before we declare ourselves converted.

**Mr. Protectionist**,---We say that your restrictive measures have an unfair <note>original: "iniquitous"</note> object in view: namely, *artificial dearness*. However, we do not claim that they always achieve the hopes of those who promote them. It is certain that they inflict all the harmful consequences of scarcity on the consumer. It is not certain, however, that they always grant a matching advantage to the producer. Why? Because if they diminish the *supply*, they also diminish the *demand*.

This proves that there is a moral force in the economic arrangement of this world—a healing power <note>original: "vis medicatrix"; a Latin phrase referring to the "healing power of nature," used here to describe how the economy naturally corrects for human interference.</note>—which causes unjust ambition to eventually fall victim to its own self-deception.

Would you be so good, Sir, as to notice that one of the elements of prosperity for every individual branch of industry...

He argues that protectionism fails because lowering supply also destroys demand, showing that the economy has a natural tendency to correct for unfair restrictions.</summary>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

...is the general wealth of the community. The value of a house is not always in proportion to what it cost to build, but also in proportion to the number and fortune of the tenants. Are two houses that are exactly similar necessarily of the same value? By no means, if one is situated in Paris and the other in Lower Brittany <note>original: "Basse-Bretagne"; a historically rural and remote region of France compared to the capital</note>. Never speak of price without taking into account surrounding circumstances, and let it be remembered that no attempt is so futile <note>original: "bootless"</note> as trying to base the prosperity of one part of society on the ruin of the whole. And yet, this is exactly what the policy of restriction <note>protectionist trade policies like tariffs</note> claims to do.

Consider what would have happened in Paris, for example, if this conflict of interests had been successful.

Suppose that the first shoemaker who established himself in that city had succeeded in banning all others; that the first tailor, the first mason, the first printer, the first watchmaker, the first physician, the first baker, had been equally successful in keeping out competitors. Paris would at this moment still be a village of 1,200 or 1,500 inhabitants. Instead, it has turned out very differently. The market of Paris has been open to all (excepting those whom you still keep out), and it is this freedom which has enlarged and increased it. The struggles of competition have been bitter and long-lasting, and this is what has made Paris a city of a million inhabitants. The general wealth has increased, no doubt; but has the individual wealth of the shoemakers and tailors been diminished? This is the question you have to ask. You may say that as the number of competitors increased, the price of their products would continue to fall. Has it done so? No; for if the *supply* has been increased, the *demand* has been enlarged even more.

The same principle will hold true for your own product, cloth; let it enter the market freely. You will have more competitors in the trade, it is true; but you will also have more customers, and, above all, richer customers. Is it possible you have never thought of this, when you see nine-tenths of your fellow citizens poorly clothed in winter for lack of the very product you manufacture?

If you wish to prosper, allow your customers to thrive. This is a lesson which you have been very slow to learn. When it is thoroughly learned, each man will seek his own...

### VI.
## TO ARTISANS AND WORKMEN.

Many newspapers have attacked me in your presence and hearing. Perhaps you will not mind reading my defense?

I am not a suspicious person. When a man writes or speaks, I take for granted that he believes what he says.

And yet, after reading and re-reading the newspapers to which I am now responding, I find myself unable to see anything in them but depressing tendencies.

Our current task is to investigate which is more favorable to your interests—liberty or restriction <note>original: "restriction"; referring to protectionist trade policies such as tariffs and import bans</note>.

I believe it is liberty; they believe it is restriction. It is up to each side to prove its own case.

Was it necessary to suggest that we free-traders <note>original: "libres-échangistes"; those advocating for the removal of international trade barriers</note> are agents of England, of the south of France, or of the government?

On this point, you can see how easy it would be to trade insults.

They say we are agents of England because some of us use the words *meeting* and *free-trader*!

And do they not use the words *drawback* <note>original: "drawback"; a refund of a tariff or tax paid on goods that are subsequently exported</note> and *budget*?

It seems we are imitating Richard Cobden <note>Richard Cobden (1804–1865) was a British politician and leader of the Anti-Corn Law League who campaigned for free trade</note> and English democracy!

And do they not imitate Lord George Bentinck <note>Lord George Bentinck (1802–1848) was an English politician who led the protectionist opposition to the repeal of the Corn Laws</note> and the British aristocracy?

We borrow the doctrine of liberty from "perfidious Albion" <note>original: "la perfide Albion"; a common derogatory nickname for Great Britain, implying it is untrustworthy in international affairs</note>!

And do they not borrow the petty arguments for protection from that same source?

We follow the lead of Bordeaux and the south! <note>Bordeaux and southern France were major wine-exporting regions that benefited from free trade</note>

And do they not take advantage of the greed <note>original: "cupidity"</note> of Lille and the north? <note>Lille and northern France were centers of heavy industry and textiles that lobbied for protectionist tariffs</note>

We allegedly favor the secret plans of the government <note>original: "le ministère"; the executive cabinet or ministry</note>, whose goal is to distract the public from their true policies!

You see, then, very clearly, that if we did not despise this war of insults, we would not lack the weapons to carry it on.

But this is beside the point.

The question—and we must never lose sight of it—is this: **Is it better for the working classes to be free, or not to be free, to purchase foreign goods?**

Workers! They tell you that, "If you are free to purchase from foreigners those things which you now produce yourselves, you will stop producing them; you will be without employment, without wages, and without bread. It is therefore for your own good to limit your liberty."

This objection returns to us in two forms: They say, for example, "If we clothe ourselves with English cloth; if we make our plows of English iron; if we cut our bread with English knives; if we wipe our hands with English towels—what will become of French workers? What will become of **national labor** <note>original: "national labour"; refers to the total domestic industry and employment within a country</note>?"

Tell me, workers! If a man should stand on the dock at Boulogne and say to every Englishman who landed, "If you will give me these English boots, I will give you this French hat"; or, "If you will give me that English horse, I will give you this French **tilbury** <note>A tilbury is a light, two-wheeled open carriage without a top</note>"; or ask him, "Will you exchange that machine made in Birmingham for this clock made in Paris?"; or, again, "Can you arrange to barter this Newcastle coal for this champagne wine?" Tell me whether—assuming this man makes his proposals with good judgment—anyone would be justified in saying that our national labor, taken as a whole, would suffer as a result?

Nor would it make the slightest difference in this respect if we were to suppose twenty such offers were made instead of one, or a million such barters were carried out instead of four; nor would it in any respect alter the case if we were to assume the use of merchants and money, by which such transactions would be greatly simplified and multiplied.

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s address to the working class, refuting the idea that buying foreign goods harms domestic workers. He begins to analyze the specific rhetorical strategies used by those who defend trade restrictions.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>

# TO ARTISANS AND WORKMEN.

We find that commerce essentially consists of barter: *products for products, services for services.* If, then, barter does no injury to **national labor** <note>original: "national labour"; this refers to the total amount of work and employment within one's own country</note>—since it implies that as much national labor is given as foreign labor is received—it follows that a hundred billion such acts of barter would do as little injury as one.

But who would profit? you will ask. The profit consists in making the best use of the resources of each country, so that the same amount of labor shall yield a greater amount of satisfaction and enjoyment everywhere.

There are some who use a specific strategy when addressing you. They begin by admitting that a free trade system is superior to a **prohibitive system** <note>A "prohibitive system" uses high taxes or bans on imports to prevent foreign competition</note>, likely because they do not want to fight the battle on that ground.

Then they remark that the transition from one system to another is always accompanied by some *displacement* of labor.

Lastly, they dwell on the hardships which, in their opinion, such displacements must always cause. They exaggerate these sufferings, they multiply them, they make them the main subject of discussion, and they present them as the final and only result of reform. In this way, they endeavor to recruit you to the cause of **monopoly** <note>In this context, "monopoly" refers to domestic industries that are "protected" from competition, allowing them to control the market and keep prices high</note>.

This is the exact strategy that has been used to defend every kind of systemic abuse. One thing I must plainly admit: it is this strategy which constantly frustrates those who advocate for reforms, even those most beneficial to the people. You will soon see the reason for this.

When an abuse has once taken root, everything is organized around the assumption that it will continue. Some men depend on it for their livelihood, others depend on them, and so on, until a massive structure is built.

If you venture to pull it down, everyone protests. And notice this—the people who shout the loudest always seem, at first glance, to be in the right. This is because it is far easier to point out the disruptions that must accompany a reform than the improvements that will follow it.

The supporters of these abuses point to specific instances of suffering; they highlight particular employers who, along with their workmen and the people who supply them with materials, are about to be harmed. Meanwhile, the poor reformer can only refer to—

<summary>Bastiat explains that trade is simply an exchange of labor for labor and argues that opponents of free trade focus on the temporary "displacement" of workers to protect entrenched monopolies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s address to the working class, illustrating how those in power use fear of change to maintain systems that actually exploit the people they claim to protect.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

...the **general good** <note>The "general good" refers to the overall economic health of the whole country, which is harder for individuals to notice than a specific tax or job</note> which must gradually spread itself over the masses. That, however, does not produce the same immediate sensation.

For example, consider the question of the **abolition of slavery** <note>Bastiat is writing this in the mid-1840s, a time of intense global debate over the ending of slavery</note>. 
"Poor men!" is the language addressed to the enslaved <note>original: "negroes"</note>, "who will support you from now on? The manager handles the whip, but he also distributes the **cassava** <note>original: "cassava"; a starchy root vegetable that was a staple food for enslaved populations in the colonies</note>."

The enslaved people regret losing their chains, for they ask themselves, "Where will the cassava come from?" 

They fail to see that it is not the manager who feeds them, but their own labor—which feeds both themselves and the manager.

When the government began reforming the **monasteries** <note>original: "convents"; in this context, referring to religious houses in Spain that provided charity</note> in Spain, people asked the beggars, "Where will you find food and clothing now? The **prior** <note>The head of a religious house</note> is your best friend. Is it not very convenient to be able to go to him for help?"

And the beggars replied, "True; if the prior goes away, we see very clearly that we will be the losers, and we do not see clearly at all who is to take his place."

They did not take into account that while the monasteries gave out alms, they lived off the nation's wealth first; so the nation actually had more to give away than it received back in charity.

In the same way, workmen! **Monopoly** <note>Protectionist policies that favor domestic industries by blocking foreign competition</note> quite invisibly burdens you with taxes, and then, with the money from those taxes, it "finds" you employment.

And your fake friends exclaim, "If not for monopolies, where would you find work?"

And you, like the Spanish beggars, reply, "True, true; the employment the monopolists provide us is certain. The promises of freedom are uncertain to be fulfilled."

For you do not see that they first take money from you, and then use only a portion of it to give you a job.

You ask, "Who is to provide you with employment?" The answer is that you will give employment to one another! With the money that is no longer taken from him by taxation, the shoemaker will dress better and give employment to the tailor. The tailor will more frequently replace his **footwear** <note>original: "chaussure"</note> and provide employment to the shoemaker; and the same thing will happen in every other branch of trade.

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionist monopolies are like slavery or state-run charity: they take wealth from the workers and then return a small portion of it as "employment," tricking the workers into fearing the loss of their own exploiters.</summary>
<keywords>abolition of slavery, Spanish monasteries, monopoly, taxation, labor displacement, free trade benefits, economic dependency</keywords>

<page-num>155</page-num>

<header>TO ARTISANS AND WORKERS.</header>

It has been argued that under a system of free trade, we would have fewer workers in our mines and textile mills <note>original: "spinning-mills"</note>.

I do not believe this is true. But even if it did happen, we would *necessarily* have a greater number of people working freely and independently, either in their own homes or in outdoor jobs.

For if our mines and textile factories <note>original: "spinning-factories"</note> are not capable of supporting themselves—as is claimed—without the help of taxes collected from the *general public*, then the moment these taxes are repealed, *everyone* will be that much better off. It is this improvement in the general circumstances of the community that provides the support for individual branches of industry.

Please forgive me for dwelling a little longer on this view of the subject; my great desire is to see you all supporting the cause of freedom.

Suppose that the capital <note>The money or assets used to start and run a business</note> used in manufacturing yields a 5 percent profit. Now, imagine a man named Mondor <note>A fictional name used here to represent a typical factory owner or "ironmaster"</note> has a business in which he employs £100,000, but he runs it at a loss of 5 percent instead of making a profit. Between the expected gain and the actual loss, there is a difference of £10,000. What happens then? A small tax of £10,000 is simply taken from the public and handed over to Mondor. You don't see it happen because the thing is skillfully disguised. It is not a tax collector who comes to your door to demand your share of this burden; instead, you pay it to Mondor, the ironworks owner, every time you purchase your trowels, hatchets, and planes. 

Then they tell you that unless you pay this tax, Mondor will not be able to provide jobs, and his workers, James and John, will be unemployed. And yet, if they stopped this tax, it would enable you to find work for one another, independent of Mondor.

And then, with a little patience, once this "smooth pillow" of protection has been pulled from under his head, you can bet that Mondor will use his ingenuity and find a way to turn his loss into a profit. James and John will not be sent away, and in that case, there will be a profit for *everyone*.

You may still respond: "We admit that after the reform <note>original: "reform"; here referring to the movement to abolish trade tariffs and protections</note> there will be more employment overall than before; but in the meantime, James and John are starving."

<summary>Bastiat argues that protecting inefficient industries through tariffs is actually a hidden tax on the tools and goods workers buy, and that ending these protections would lead to a more prosperous, independent workforce.</summary>

<header># ECONOMIC SOPHISMS</header>

To this, I reply:

**1st**, When labor is simply displaced in order to be increased <note>original: "augmented"</note>, a man who has both a head and hands is rarely left in a state of destitution for long.

**2nd**, There is nothing to stop the State from setting aside a fund to address any temporary lack of employment during the transition—though, personally, I do not believe such a fund would be needed.

**3rd**, If I do not misunderstand the workers, they are quite prepared to endure any temporary suffering that naturally comes with a transfer of labor from one sector to another, provided the community as a whole benefits and justice is served. I only wish I could say the same for their employers!

What! Will it be said that because you are workers, you are therefore unintelligent and immoral? Your so-called friends seem to think so. Is it not surprising that they should discuss such a question in your presence, talking exclusively of wages and profits without ever once allowing the word *justice* to pass their lips? And yet they know that trade restriction <note>Protectionist policies like tariffs that limit imports</note> is *unjust*. Why do they not have the courage to admit it and say to you: "Workers! An injustice prevails in this country; it is profitable to you, and so we must maintain it." Why? Because they know you would reject it.

It is not true that this injustice is profitable to you. Give me your attention for a few moments longer, and then judge for yourselves.

What is it that we "protect" in France? Things produced on a large scale by rich capitalists in massive factories, such as iron, coal, cloth, and textiles; and they tell you that this is done for your sake, to secure your employment, rather than for the benefit of the owners.

And yet, whenever *foreign labor* presents itself in our markets in a way that might be harmful to you but advantageous for your employers, it is allowed to enter without any restriction being imposed.

Are there not thirty thousand Germans in Paris who make clothes and shoes? Why are they permitted to set themselves up alongside you, while the importation of cloth is restricted? Because cloth is manufactured in large-scale <note>The text ends mid-sentence as the original document continues onto the next page.</note>
