<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s address to workers, where he exposes the double standards of industrialist legislators who demand protection for their own manufacturing but allow open competition for the goods and services produced by artisans.</meta>

<page-num>157</page-num>

<header>TO ARTISANS AND WORKMEN.</header>

...elements which belong to manufacturing legislators. But clothes are made by workmen in their own houses. In converting wool into cloth, these gentlemen desire to have no competition, because that is their business; but in converting cloth into coats, they allow competition, because that is your business.

In making our railways, a ban <note>original: "embargo"</note> was placed on English rails, but English workmen were brought over. Why was this? Simply because English rails came into competition with the iron produced in our great factories, while the English laborers were only rivals to you.

We have no wish that German tailors and English laborers <note>original: "navvies"; manual laborers who worked on civil engineering projects like railways</note> should be kept out of France. What we ask is that the entry of cloth and rails should be left free of restrictions. We simply demand justice and equality before the law for all.

It is a mockery to tell us that trade restrictions <note>original: "customs restrictions"</note> are imposed for your benefit. Tailors, shoemakers, carpenters, masons, blacksmiths, shopkeepers, grocers, watchmakers, butchers, bakers, dressmakers! I challenge you all to point out a single way in which restriction is profitable to you, and I shall point out, whenever you desire it, four ways in which it is hurtful to you.

And, after all, see how little evidence your journalists have for claiming that the monopolists are acting selflessly <note>original: "self-abnegation"</note>.

I may venture to call the *rate of wages* which establishes itself *naturally* under a system <note>original: "régime"</note> of freedom the *natural rate of wages*. When you affirm, therefore, that restriction is profitable to you, it is the same as saying that it adds a *surplus* <note>original: "overplus"</note> to your *natural wages*. Now, a surplus of wages beyond the natural rate must come from somewhere or other; it does not fall from the skies, but comes from those who pay it.

You are led to this conclusion, then, by your so-called friends: that the policy of protection has been introduced so that the interests of capitalists should be sacrificed to those of the workmen.

Do you think this is probable?

Where is your place, then, in the Chamber of Peers <note>The upper house of the French parliament at the time</note>? When did you take your seat in the Palais Bourbon <note>The meeting place of the French National Assembly</note>? Who has consulted you? And where did this idea of establishing a policy of protection take its rise?

<summary>Bastiat argues that protectionist laws are designed by wealthy industrialists to shield their own profits while forcing workers and artisans to face unfair competition and higher costs. He highlights the hypocrisy of the ruling class, noting that workers have no say in the legislative bodies that create these economic policies.</summary>

<keywords>protectionism, free trade, natural rate of wages, Chamber of Peers, Palais Bourbon, labor competition, industrial monopolies, artisans, French economy</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s address to workers, where he challenges the claim that protective trade laws are designed for their benefit. He uses irony to point out that if the wealthy ruling class truly intended these laws to help workers at their own expense, they would have no reason to deny workers the right to vote.</meta>

<page-num>158</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

I think I hear you answer, "It is not we who have established it. Alas! we are neither Peers, nor Deputies, nor State Councilors <note>original: "Councillors of State"; high-ranking government officials who advise on legislation</note>. The capitalists have done it all."

Truly, they must have been in a good mood that day! What! These capitalists have made the law; they have established a policy of prohibition <term>prohibition</term> <note>In this context, the legal banning or extreme restriction of imported foreign goods to protect domestic industries</note> for the express purpose of enabling you to profit at their expense!

But here is something stranger still.

How does it come to pass that your pretended friends, who lecture you on the goodness, the generosity, and the self-sacrifice <note>original: "self-abnegation"</note> of capitalists, never cease expressing sympathy for you because you are deprived of your political rights? From their point of view, I would ask what you could make of such rights if you had them? The capitalists have a monopoly on legislation—granted. By means of this monopoly, they have awarded themselves a monopoly on iron, cloth, textiles, coal, wood, and meat—granted likewise. But here are your "pretended friends," who tell you that by acting this way, capitalists have impoverished themselves without being under any obligation to do so, in order to enrich you—who have no right to be enriched! Assuredly, if you were voters <note>original: "electors"</note> and deputies tomorrow, you could not manage your affairs better than they are being managed for you; you could not manage them nearly as well.

If the industrial legislation under which you live is intended for your profit, it is an act of treachery <note>original: "perfidy"</note> to demand political rights for you. These new-fashioned democrats can never escape this dilemma: the law made by the *bourgeoisie* <note>original French term for the middle and upper classes who own the means of production</note> either gives you *more* or it gives you *less* than your natural wages. If that law gives you *less*, they deceive you by urging you to support it. If it gives you *more*, they still deceive you by inviting you to demand political rights at the very time when the *bourgeoisie* are making sacrifices for you—sacrifices which, in common honesty, you could not demand by your votes even if you had the power to do so.

Workmen! I should be sorry indeed if this address were to excite feelings of anger in your minds against the rich. If *self-interest*, misunderstood or too easily alarmed, still maintains monopoly, let us not forget that monopoly has its root in *errors* which are common to both capitalists and laborers.

<summary>Bastiat argues that if trade protections were actually intended to help workers, it would imply the ruling capitalist class is acting against its own self-interest, making the push for workers' voting rights unnecessary. He concludes that trade monopolies are based on shared economic errors rather than intentional malice.</summary>
<keywords>Bastiat, Economic Sophisms, protectionism, labor rights, class interests, political rights, bourgeoisie, monopoly, wages</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s address to workers, where he argues that natural social forces, if left alone, tend toward equality. He begins a new section titled "A Chinese Story," using irony to mock the "social reformers" of his day who claim that even protectionist taxes like the Customs House are designed for the benefit of the poor.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>

<header>-># TO ARTISANS AND WORKMEN.<-</header>

Instead of exciting one class against the other, let us try to bring them together. And for that end, what ought we to do? If it is true that natural social tendencies work together to level inequalities among men, we only have to allow these tendencies to act, remove the artificial obstructions that slow their operation, and allow the relations of the various classes of society to be established on principles of JUSTICE—principles always mixed up, in my mind at least, with the principle of LIBERTY.

---

### VII.

## A CHINESE STORY.

We hear a great outcry against the greed <note>original: "cupidity"</note> and the selfishness <note>original: "egotism"</note> of the age!

For my own part, I see the world, especially Paris, filled with people acting like Decius <note>original: "Deciuses"; referring to the Roman family of Publius Decius Mus, famous for sacrificing their lives in battle for the state</note>.

Open the thousand volumes, the thousand newspapers of all sorts and sizes, which the Parisian press pours out <note>original: "vomits forth"</note> every day on the country—are they not all the work of "minor saints"?

How vividly they depict the vices of the times! How touching is the tenderness they display for the masses! How liberally they invite the rich to share with the poor, or even for the poor to share with the rich! How many plans for social reforms, social improvements <note>original: "ameliorations"</note>, and social organizations! What superficial <note>original: "shallow"</note> writer fails to devote himself to the well-being of the working classes? We have only to contribute a few pennies <note>original: "shillings"</note> to give them the leisure to produce their humanitarian writings <note>original: "humane lucubrations"</note>.

And then they declare against the selfishness <note>original: "egotism"</note> and individualism of our age!

There is nothing which they do not pretend to recruit into the service of the working classes—there is positively no exception, not even the *Customs House* <note>original: "Customhouse"</note>. You fancy, perhaps, that the *Customs House* is merely an instrument of taxation, like the *octroi* <term>octroi</term> <note>A local tax traditionally collected on goods brought into a town or city</note> or the toll-bar? Nothing of the kind. It is essentially

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues Bastiat’s address to workers, where he argues that natural social forces, if left alone, tend toward equality. He here critiques the Customs House, portraying it not as a source of revenue but as a deliberate obstruction to trade that creates "make-work" jobs at the expense of general prosperity.</meta>

<page-num>160</page-num>

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

...an institution for promoting the progress <note>original: "march"</note> of civilization, brotherhood <note>original: "fraternity"</note>, and equality. What are you aiming for? It is the fashion to introduce, or pretend to introduce, sentiment and emotion everywhere—even into the customs collector's booth.

We must admit that the Customhouse has a very strange way of achieving philanthropic goals.

It employs an army of directors, sub-directors, inspectors, sub-inspectors, controllers <note>original: "comptrollers"</note>, examiners, department heads, clerks, and trainees <note>original: "supernumeraries, aspirant-supernumeraries"</note>, not to mention the officers of the *active service* <note>original: "service actif"; the field agents or guards who patrolled the borders</note>. The purpose of all this complicated machinery is to exert a negative influence over the people's industry, which can be summed up in one word: *obstruct*.

Notice that I do not say the goal is to *tax*, but to *obstruct*. The goal is not to prevent acts that are harmful to good morals or public order, but to block transactions that are inherently harmless and even suited to maintain peace and unity among nations.

And yet, the human race is so flexible and resilient <note>original: "elastic"</note> that it always overcomes these *obstacles*. Then we hear complaints about the labor market being oversupplied <note>original: "glutted"</note>.

If you prevent a people from getting their necessities <note>original: "subsistence"</note> from abroad, they will produce them at home. The labor is harder and more painful, but they must have what they need to live. If you stop a man from crossing the valley, he must climb over the hills. The road is longer and more difficult, but he must reach his journey's end.

This is tragic, but now we come to what is ridiculous. Once the law has created these obstacles, and once society has shifted a large amount of labor from other jobs to overcome them, you are no longer allowed to ask for reform. If you point out the *obstacle*, you are told about the amount of labor it "employs." If you reply that this labor was not created but merely displaced, you are answered in the words of the *Public Spirit* <note>original: "Esprit Public"; a contemporary French newspaper</note>: "The impoverishment alone is certain and immediate; as for our enrichment, that is more than doubtful <note>original: "problematical"</note>."

This reminds me of a Chinese story, which I will tell you.

There were in China two large towns, called *Tchin* and *Tchan*.

# A CHINESE STORY

A magnificent canal connected two cities <note>original: "united them"</note>. The Emperor decided to order enormous blocks of stone to be thrown into it for the purpose of making it useless.

Upon seeing this, Kouang, his primary advisor <term>original: "mandarin"; a high-ranking official in the Chinese imperial government</term>, said to him:
“Son of Heaven! This is a mistake.”
To which the Emperor replied:
“Kouang! You are talking nonsense.”
I am giving you only the basic summary <note>original: "substance"</note> of their conversation.

At the end of three months, the Celestial Emperor <note>A traditional name for the Chinese Empire</note> sent for the advisor again and said to him:
“Kouang, look!”
And Kouang opened his eyes and looked.
He saw a multitude of men **at work** a short distance from the canal. Some were digging, others were filling in holes, leveling the ground, and paving. The advisor, who was very clever, said to himself, "They are building a highway."

When another three months had passed, the Emperor sent for Kouang again and said to him:
“Look!”
And Kouang looked.
He saw that the road was finished, and from one end to the other, he saw inns for travelers built here and there. Crowds of people on foot, carts, and palanquins <term>a covered litter for one passenger, carried on poles by two or more men</term> came and went. Innumerable Chinese people, exhausted with fatigue, carried heavy burdens back and forth from *Tchin* to *Tchan*, and from *Tchan* to *Tchin*. Kouang said to himself, "It is the destruction of the canal that gives jobs to these poor people." But it never occurred to him that their labor was simply **diverted** from other useful work.

Three more months passed, and the Emperor said to Kouang:
“Look!”
And Kouang looked.
He saw that the inns <note>original: "hostelries"</note> were full of travelers, and to meet their needs, shops were grouped around them: butchers, bakers, and stalls selling edible birds' nests <note>A traditional Chinese delicacy made from the saliva of swiftlets</note>, and so on. He also saw that because the workers needed clothes, tailors, shoemakers, and people selling umbrellas and fans had settled among them. Since they could not sleep outside, even in the Celestial Empire, there were also masons,

He forgot that men would have eaten and drunk while passing along the canal just as they did while passing along the highway.

*The Emperor*: "I believe you are repeating a lesson by rote."

*Kouang*: "True, Sire; it would be better if I simply read what I have to say."

-> # A CHINESE STORY. <-

"...abundance <note>OCR error: "dance"; the context implies "abundance"</note> of labor constitutes the wealth of nations; and the best economic system is that which supplies the people with the greatest amount of employment. The question is not whether it is better to pay four or eight *cash* <note>a low-denomination Chinese coin</note> for a cup of tea, or five or ten *taels* <note>original: "tales"; a unit of currency formerly used in China</note> for a shirt. These are trivialities <note>original: "puerilities"</note> unworthy of a thinking mind. Nobody disputes your proposition. The question is whether it is better to pay more for a commodity you want to buy, but have—through the abundance of employment and the higher price of labor—the means of acquiring it; or whether it is better to limit the sources of employment, and with them the total national production, in order to transport goods more cheaply by *improved means of transit*. This might make objects of consumption cheaper, it is true, but it also takes away from our population the means of purchasing these objects even at their reduced price."

Seeing the Emperor still unconvinced, Kouang added, “Sire, please give me your attention. I have still another quotation from the *Industrial Monitor* <note>original: "Moniteur Industriel," a real-life protectionist newspaper in 19th-century France that Bastiat is satirizing</note> to bring to your notice."

But the Emperor said:

“I do not require your Chinese journals to help me find out that to create *obstacles* is to divert and misapply labor. But that is not my mission. Go and clear out the canal; and we shall reform the Customhouse afterwards.”

And Kouang went away tearing his beard and appealing to his God, “O Buddha! <note>original: "O Fo!"</note> take pity on your people; for we have now got an Emperor of the *English school* <note>referring to the British "Manchester School" of free-trade economic thought</note>, and I see clearly that in a short time we shall be in want of everything, for we shall no longer need to do anything.”

### VIII.

## AFTER THIS, THEREFORE BECAUSE OF THIS.
<note>original: "POST HOC, ERGO PROPTER HOC"</note>

THIS is the greatest and most common fallacy in reasoning.

Real sufferings, for example, have manifested themselves in England.*

<margin>\* This was written in January 1848.—TRANSLATOR.</margin>

<summary>Kouang fails to convince the Emperor that creating obstacles to trade is beneficial for labor, leading to the restoration of the canal. The author then introduces the "Post hoc, ergo propter hoc" fallacy, using England's economic state in 1848 as a starting point.</summary>

<keywords>free trade vs protectionism, make-work fallacy, efficiency, English school, logical fallacies, Kouang, Moniteur Industriel</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of economic fallacies, specifically the fallacy of "after this, therefore because of this," by examining the economic situation in England during the mid-1840s following tariff reforms and agricultural failures.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

These hardships follow in the wake of two other occurrences:
1st, The reformed tariff;
2nd, Two bad harvests in a row.
To which of these two circumstances are we to attribute the first?
The protectionists exclaim:
> It is this accursed free trade which does all the harm. It promised us wonderful things; we accepted it; and here our manufactures are at a standstill, and the people are suffering: With this, therefore because of this <note>original: "*Cum hoc, ergo propter hoc*"; a logical fallacy where one assumes that because two things happen together, one must have caused the other</note>.

Free trade distributes the fruits that Providence grants to human labor in the most uniform and equitable manner. If we are deprived of part of these fruits by natural causes, such as a series of bad seasons, free trade still distributes what remains in that same equitable manner. Men are, no doubt, not as well provided with what they want; but should we blame this on free trade, or on the bad harvests?

Liberty operates on the same principle as insurance. When an accident, like a fire, occurs, insurance spreads the losses over a great number of men and many years—losses which, without insurance, would have fallen all at once upon one individual. But would anyone try to claim that fire has become a greater evil since the introduction of insurance?

In 1842, 1843, and 1844, the reduction of taxes began in England. At the same time, the harvests were very abundant; and we are led to conclude that these two circumstances worked together to produce the unparalleled prosperity which England enjoyed during that period.

In 1845, the harvest was bad; and in 1846, it was even worse.

Food prices rose; and the people were forced to spend their resources on basic necessities and limit their consumption of other goods. Clothing was less in demand, factories had less work, and wages tended to fall.

Fortunately, in that same year, the barriers of trade restriction were even more effectively removed, and an enormous quantity of food reached the English market. Had this not happened, it is nearly certain that a violent revolution would have taken place.

<summary>This page analyzes the economic situation in 1840s England, arguing that the hardships blamed on free trade were actually caused by bad harvests, and that trade liberalization prevented a total social collapse.</summary>
<keywords>free trade, protectionism, logical fallacy, England, harvests, tariff reform, insurance principle, economic history, 1846</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the "after this, therefore because of this" fallacy by using an analogy of a cured leper who suffers a new, unrelated injury. It then transitions into a more direct, satirical attack on protectionist policies.</meta>

# AFTER THIS, THEREFORE BECAUSE OF THIS <note>original: "*Post hoc, ergo propter hoc*"; a logical fallacy where one assumes that because event B followed event A, event A must have caused event B</note>.

And yet free trade is blamed for disasters which it actually tended to prevent and, at least in part, to repair!

A poor leper lived in isolation. Anything he happened to touch, no one else would touch. Forced to waste away in solitude, he led a miserable existence. A famous physician cured him, and now our poor hermit was admitted to all the benefits of *free trade* and had full liberty to trade goods. What brilliant prospects opened up for him! He delighted in calculating the advantages which, through his restored connection with his fellow men, he was able to gain from his own hard work. He then happened to break both his arms and fell into poverty and misery. The journalists who witnessed that misery said, “See what this freedom of trade has reduced him to! Truly, he was less to be pitied when he lived alone.” “What!” said the physician, “do you make no allowance for his broken arms? Does that accident have nothing to do with his current unhappy state? His misfortune comes from losing the use of his hands, not from being cured of his leprosy. He would have been a more deserving subject for your pity had he been both crippled and leprous into the bargain.”

*After this, therefore because of this.* <note>original: "*Post hoc, ergo propter hoc*"</note> Beware of that logical fallacy <note>original: "sophism"; a clever but false argument intended to deceive</note>.

---

### IX.

## THE PREMIUM THEFT <note>original: "Le Vol à la Prime"; this refers to government subsidies or "premiums" given to protected industries, which the author argues is a form of legal theft from the public</note>.

This little book of logical fallacies is found to be too theoretical, scientific, and metaphysical. So be it. Let us try the effect of a more casual and common—or, if you prefer, a cruder—style. Convinced that the public is being cheated in this matter of protectionism <note>original: "protection"; the policy of taxing imports to "protect" domestic producers from competition</note>, I have tried to prove it. But if shouting is preferred to argument, then let us shout:

> “King Midas has a snout and the ears of a donkey.” *

* “King Midas has the ears of a donkey.” <note>original: "*Auriculas asini Mida rex habet.*"</note>—Persius, Satire 1. The line as given in the text is from John Dryden’s translation.—TRANSLATOR.

<vocab>Post hoc ergo propter hoc, sophism, free trade, protection, King Midas, Persius, Dryden</vocab>

<summary>The author uses a parable of a cured leper who breaks his arms to show that free trade is often wrongly blamed for unrelated economic disasters. He then shifts to a more blunt, satirical tone to attack the 'theft' of government subsidies.</summary>
<keywords>logical fallacies, causality, free trade vs protectionism, economic parables, subsidies, King Midas metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's transition from exposing logical fallacies to a more direct, satirical critique of protectionist policies, which he characterizes as a form of legalized theft.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS <note>original: "sophisms"; clever but false arguments intended to deceive</note>.

A burst of plain speaking frequently has more effect than the most polished indirectness. You remember Oronte, and the difficulty which the *Misanthrope* had in convincing him of his folly. <note>The author refers to Molière's 1666 play *The Misanthrope*, where the character Alceste tries to tell Oronte that his poetry is terrible without being too rude.</note>

**ALCESTE.** One risks playing a foolish part.
**ORONTE.** Do you mean to tell me by that
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That I am wrong to want to. . . .
**ALCESTE.** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I am not saying that.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But . . . .
**ORONTE.** Do I write poorly?
**ALCESTE.** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I am not saying that.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But finally . . . .
**ORONTE.** But can I not know what is in my sonnet? . . .
**ALCESTE.** Frankly, it is only good enough to be put in the cabinet. <note>original French: "Cabinet"; in this context, it refers to a "privy" or toilet, suggesting the writing is only fit for use as toilet paper.</note>

To speak plainly, Dear Public! **you are being robbed**. This is speaking bluntly, but the truth is very evident. It is blunt, but it is clear <note>original: "*C'est cru, mais c'est clair*"</note>.

The words *theft*, *to steal*, and *robbery* may appear as ugly words to many people. I ask such people, as Harpagon <note>The protagonist of Molière's play *The Miser* (*L’Avare*).</note> asks Elise, "Is it the word or the thing itself which frightens you?"

> "Whoever has fraudulently taken possession of a thing which does not belong to him is guilty of theft." (*Penal Code, article 379.*)

> *To steal*: To take by stealth or by force. (*Dictionary of the Academy.*)

> *Thief*: One who exacts more than is due to him. (*Dictionary of the Academy.*)

Now, does the monopolist—who, by a law of his own making, obliges me to pay him 20 francs for what I could get elsewhere for 15—not fraudulently take 5 francs from me which belonged to me?

Does he not take them by stealth or by force?

Does he not exact more than is due to him?

He takes, purloins, and exacts, it may be said; but not *by stealth or by force*, which are the defining characteristics of theft.

When our **tax bills** <note>original: "*bulletins de contributions*"</note> have included in them 5 francs for the premium <note>In this context, a "premium" is a government subsidy or bounty paid to a protected domestic industry.</note> which the monopolist takes, exacts, or abstracts, what can be more stealthy for the unsuspecting? And for those who are not fools, and who do suspect the truth, what 

---

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that protectionist tariffs are essentially a form of legalized theft. He transitions from abstract theory to a satirical dramatization of how different industries—represented here by a shipowner and an iron manufacturer—negotiate these "legalized robberies" in government councils.</meta>

# THE PREMIUM THEFT <note>original: "vol à la prime"; here referring to government subsidies or "bounties" paid to domestic producers</note>.

...savors more of *force*, seeing that at the first refusal the tax collector's bailiff is at the door?

But let monopolists take heart. *Premium thefts* and *tariff thefts*, even if they violate fairness as much as theft "in the American style" <note>original: "à l'Americaine"; likely referring to bold or highway robbery</note>, do not violate the law. On the contrary, they are carried out according to the law; and if they are worse than common thefts, they do not fall under the jurisdiction of the *criminal courts* <note>original: "la correctionnelle"; the French court for handling misdemeanors and minor crimes</note>.

Besides, right or wrong, we are all either victims or perpetrators of robbery in this business. The author of this book might very well cry "Stop, thief!" when he buys anything; and with equal reason, that same cry might be directed at him when he sells;* and if his situation differs from many of his fellow citizens, the difference is simply this: he knows he loses more than he gains by this game, while they do not. If they knew it, the game would soon be over.

I do not claim to be the first to call this by its right name. Sixty years ago, Adam Smith said that "when manufacturers meet together, we can be sure a plot is being hatched against the public's wallet." Should we be surprised by this, when the public turns a blind eye?

Well then, imagine a formal meeting of manufacturers under the title of *General Councils* <note>original: "conseils generaux"; official advisory bodies in France</note>. What happens there, and what is decided?

Here is a shortened report of one of their meetings:

> "SHIPOWNER: Our merchant shipping is at an all-time low. (*Dissent.*) This is hardly surprising. I cannot build ships without iron. I could buy it on the *global market* for 10 francs; but by law, the French iron manufacturer forces me to pay him 15 francs, which takes 5 francs out of my pocket. I demand the freedom to buy iron wherever I see fit.
>
> "IRONMASTER: In the *global market*, I find shipping rates <note>original: "freights"</note> at 20 francs. By law, I am forced to pay the French shipowner 30; he takes 10 francs out of my pocket. He robs me, and I rob him; everything is perfectly fair.
> 
> "STATESMAN: The shipowner has reached a hasty conclu—"

\* Because he owns some land where he lives, he belongs to the protected class. This fact should deflect criticism. It shows that even if he uses harsh words, they are directed against the system itself, and not against the intentions or motives of individuals.

### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

...sion. Let us maintain our unity regarding what constitutes our strength. If we abandon even a single point of the theory of protection, the entire theory collapses.

**SHIPOWNER**: For us shipowners, protection has been a failure. I repeat that the merchant fleet is at its lowest point.

**SHIPMASTER**: Well, let us raise the *surcharge* <note>original: "surtaxe"; an additional tax or customs duty</note>, and let the shipowner who currently charges the public 30 francs for freight instead charge 40.

**A MINISTER**: The government will make as much use as possible of the marvelous mechanism of the surcharge; but I fear that will not be sufficient.

**A GOVERNMENT OFFICIAL**: You are all too easily frightened. Does the tariff alone protect you? And do you not take taxation into account? If the consumer is kind and generous, the taxpayer is no less so. Let us heap taxes upon him, and the shipowner will be satisfied. I propose a subsidy <note>original: "premium" or "prime"; a government grant or bounty paid to a domestic industry</note> of five francs to be taken from the public taxpayers and handed over to the shipbuilder for every ton of iron he uses.

**Confused voices**: Agreed! Agreed! 
**AN AGRICULTURIST**: A three-franc *subsidy* on every hundred liters <note>original: "hectolitre"; a metric unit equal to 100 liters</note> of grain for me! 
**A MANUFACTURER**: A two-franc subsidy on every yard of cloth for me! etc., etc.

**THE PRESIDENT**: This, then, is what we have agreed upon. Our session has established a system of *subsidies*, and it will be to our eternal honor. What branch of industry could possibly lose money from now on, since we have two methods—both so very simple—of turning our losses into gains: the tariff and the subsidy? The meeting is adjourned.

I truly believe some supernatural vision must have foreshadowed the coming of the *subsidy* system to me in a dream (who knows if I might have even first suggested the idea to Mr. Dupin <note>Charles Dupin (1784–1873), a French mathematician and economist who often advocated for protectionist policies</note>?) when six months ago I wrote these words:

> "It seems evident to me that protection, without changing its nature or the effects it produces, could take the form of a direct tax collected by the state and distributed as compensatory subsidies among privileged branches of industry."

And after comparing a protective duty to a subsidy, I added,

"I candidly admit my preference for the latter system. It seems to me more just, more economical, and more fair. More just,

<summary>In this satirical dialogue, Bastiat portrays various industries and government officials conspiring to replace failing tariffs with direct taxpayer-funded subsidies (premiums). The author notes that he had predicted this shift toward direct taxation to support privileged industries in his earlier writings.</summary>

<header>THE THEFT BY SUBSIDY.</header>
<page-num>169</page-num>

...because if society desires to give gifts to some of its members, everyone ought to bear the expense. This would be more economical because it would save a great deal in the cost of collection and do away with many of the obstacles <note>original: "trammels"</note> with which trade is hampered. It would also be more fair because the public would see clearly the nature of the operation and act accordingly."*

Since the occasion presents itself to us so opportunely, let us study this *system of plunder by subsidy* <note>original: "premium"</note>; for all we say of it applies equally to the *system of plunder by tariff*. As the latter is a little better concealed, the direct system may help us to detect and expose the indirect system of cheating. The mind will thus be led from what is simple to what is more complicated.

But it may be asked, is there not a species of theft which is more simple still? Undoubtedly; there is *highway robbery*, which only needs to be legalized and made a monopoly of, or—in the language of the present day—*organized*.

I have been reading what follows in a book of travels:—

> "When we reached the kingdom of A., all branches of industry declared themselves to be in a state of suffering. Agriculture groaned, manufactures complained, trade murmured, the shipping interest grumbled, and the government was at a loss for what to do. First of all, the idea was to lay a fairly heavy tax on all the dissatisfied parties, and afterwards to divide the proceeds among them after the government retained its own portion; this would have been based on the principle of the Spanish lottery. There are a thousand of you, and the State takes a piastre <note>a silver coin used in Spain and its colonies</note> from each; then by sleight of hand, it carries away 250 piastres and divides the remaining 750 in larger and smaller proportions among the ticket-holders. The gallant hidalgo <note>a member of the Spanish lower nobility</note> who gets three-fourths of a piastre, forgetting that he had contributed a whole piastre, cannot conceal his delight and rushes off to spend his fifteen reals <note>Spanish currency; eight reals typically equaled one piastre</note> at the alehouse. This is very much the same thing as we see taking place in France. But the government had overrated the stupidity of the population when it endeavored to make them accept such a species of protection, and at length it hit upon the following expedient.
> "The country was covered with a network of highroads. The

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the satirical story of the "Kingdom of A.," where the government legalizes highway robbery as a metaphor for protectionism and subsidies.</meta>

<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

The government had these roads accurately measured; and then it announced to the farmer <note>original: "agriculturist"</note>, "All that you can steal from travellers between these two points is yours; let that serve as a subsidy <note>original: "premium"</note> for your protection and encouragement." Afterwards, it assigned to each manufacturer and each shipowner a certain portion of the road to be used for their profit, according to this formula:—

->I give and grant to you<-
->The power and authority<-
->To steal,<-
->To pillage,<-
->To rob,<-
->To swindle,<-
->And to cheat,<-
->With impunity throughout this whole<-
->Road.<-
<note>original: "Dono tibi et concedo / Virtutem et puissantiam / Volandi, / Pillandi, / Derobandi, / Filoutandi, / Et escroquandi, / Impunè per totam istam / Viam." Bastiat is parodying the mock-Latin used by Molière in his plays.</note>

Now it has come to pass that the people of the kingdom of A. have become so used to this system that they take into account only what they are allowed to steal, not what is stolen from them. They are so determined to view pillage only from the standpoint of the thief that they look upon the sum total of individual thefts as a *national gain*, and refuse to abandon a system of *protection*, without which they say no branch of industry could support itself.

You object to this. "It is not possible," you exclaim, "that a whole people should be led to *attribute an abundance of wealth to mutual robbery*."

And why not? We see that this conviction spreads throughout France, and that we are constantly organizing and improving the system of reciprocal robbery under the respectable names of subsidies <note>original: "premiums"</note> and protective tariffs.

We must not, however, be guilty of exaggeration. Regarding the *method of collection* <note>original: "mode of levying"</note> and other related details, the system adopted in the kingdom of A. may be worse than ours; but we must at the same time admit that, regarding the principle and its necessary consequences, there is not a shred of difference between these two types of theft. Both are organized by law for the purpose of adding to the profits of particular branches of industry.

<summary>Bastiat uses a satirical fable about a kingdom that legalizes highway robbery to argue that protective tariffs and subsidies are essentially "reciprocal robbery" organized by the government.</summary>
<keywords>Bastiat, protectionism, subsidies, tariffs, satire, legalized robbery, economic sophisms, national gain</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the satirical comparison between highway robbery and government-sponsored protectionism in the "Kingdom of A." Bastiat is illustrating how government subsidies (premiums) for exports effectively rob domestic taxpayers to benefit foreign consumers.</meta>

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>THE SUBSIDY THEFT <note>original: "THE PREMIUM THEFT"; in this context, a "premium" refers to a government bounty or subsidy paid to exporters.</note></header>

Note also that if *highway robbery* presents some inconveniences in its actual execution, it also has certain advantages that we do not find in *robbery by tariff* <note>original: "robbery by tariff" refers to protective import duties that raise prices for domestic consumers.</note>.

For example, it is possible to distribute the proceeds fairly among all producers. This is not the case with customs duties. The latter are incapable of protecting certain classes of society, such as artisans, shopkeepers, writers <note>original: "men of letters"</note>, lawyers, soldiers, laborers, and so on.

It is true that *robbery by subsidy* <note>original: "robbery by premium"</note> takes an infinite number of forms, and in this regard, it is not inferior to *highway robbery*; but, on the other hand, it often leads to results so bizarre and awkward that the citizens of the kingdom of A. might well laugh at us.

What the victim of a highway robbery loses, the thief gains, and the stolen goods stay in the country. But under the system of *robbery by subsidy*, what the tax exacts from the French citizen is frequently granted to the Chinese, to the Hottentots <note>A historical term for the Khoekhoe people of Southern Africa.</note>, to the Xhosa <note>original: "Caffres," a historical term for Southern African ethnic groups; Bastiat uses these names to represent distant foreign nations.</note>, and so on. Here is the way in which this takes place:

Suppose a piece of cloth is worth 100 francs in Bordeaux. It cannot be sold below that price without a loss. It is impossible to sell it above that price because competition among merchants prevents the price from rising. In these circumstances, if a Frenchman wants to have the cloth, he must pay 100 francs or go without it. But if it is an Englishman who wants the cloth, the government steps in and says to the merchant, "Sell your cloth, and we will get you 20 francs from the taxpayers." The merchant, who could not get more than 100 francs for his cloth, sells it to the Englishman for 80. This sum, added to the 20 francs produced by the *subsidy theft* <note>original: "premium theft"</note>, makes the accounts balance. This is exactly the same as if the taxpayers had given 20 francs to the Englishman, on the condition that he buy French cloth at a 20-franc discount—at 20 francs below the cost of production, or 20 francs below what it has cost us to make it. The *robbery by subsidy*, then, has this peculiarity: the *people being robbed* live in the country that tolerates the practice, while the *people who profit* by the robbery are scattered all over the world.

Truly, it is marvelous that people should persist in maintain—

<summary>Bastiat explains that export subsidies ("premiums") are a form of "theft" where domestic taxpayers fund discounts for foreign buyers, making the nation poorer to benefit other countries.</summary>
<keywords>subsidies, export bounties, protectionism, international trade, taxation, Claude-Frédéric Bastiat, economic satire</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues the satirical argument from the Kingdom of A, where Bastiat compares government subsidies and tariffs to a "civilized" form of highway robbery, mocking those who call for "moderate" protectionism.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>

-> ECONOMIC SOPHISMS. <-

...claiming that everything an individual steals from the masses is a *general gain*. Perpetual motion, the philosopher’s stone, and the squaring of the circle <note>original: "quadrature of the circle"; a famously impossible mathematical problem used here to represent dead-end pursuits.</note> are antiquated problems; but the theory of *progress by plunder* is still held in high regard. Initially <note>original: *A priori*; reasoning based on theory rather than experience.</note>, we would have thought that, of all imaginable foolishness <note>original: "puerilities"</note>, this was the least likely to survive.

Some people will say, "Are you supporters, then, of *non-interference* <note>original: *laissez passer*; the economic policy of allowing the market to operate without government intervention.</note>—economists of the school of Adam Smith and Jean-Baptiste Say? You do not desire the *organization of labor*." Yes, gentlemen, organize labor as much as you choose, but please have the goodness not to organize theft.

Another, and more numerous, group keeps repeating, "Subsidies <note>original: "premiums"</note>, tariffs—all that has been exaggerated. We should use them without abusing them. A sensible freedom, combined with a moderate protectionism—that is what *discreet and practical* men desire. Let us steer clear of *fixed principles and inflexible rules*."

This is precisely what the traveler tells us takes place in the Kingdom of A. "Highway robbery," say the wise men, "is neither good nor bad in itself; that depends upon circumstances. All we are concerned with is to *weigh the results*, and see our officials <note>original: "functionaries"</note> well paid for the work of weighing. It may be that we have allowed too much room for pillage; perhaps we have not allowed enough. Let us examine and balance the accounts of each man employed in the work of pillage. To those who do not earn enough, let us assign a larger portion of the road. To those who gain too much, we must limit the days or months of pillage."

Those who talk in this way gain a great reputation for moderation, prudence, and good sense. They never aspire to the highest offices in the state. <note>Bastiat is likely being sarcastic here, as "moderates" who compromised on principles often held power in 19th-century France.</note>

Those who say, "Suppress all injustice, whether on a large or small scale, and permit no dishonesty, no matter how minor," are labeled as *ideologues* <note>original: *idéologues*; a term used dismissively by practical politicians for those who prioritize abstract principles over political compromise.</note>, idle dreamers who keep repeating the same thing over and over again. The people, moreover, find their arguments too clear; and why should they be expected to believe what is so easily understood?

<summary>Bastiat satirizes "moderate" protectionists by comparing their policies to a government-regulated system of highway robbery. He mocks the idea that theft becomes acceptable when it is balanced by officials, and notes how proponents of clear, principled justice are dismissed as impractical dreamers.</summary>

<keywords>Frédéric Bastiat, protectionism, free trade, subsidies, tariffs, Adam Smith, Jean-Baptiste Say, organization of labor, economic satire, Kingdom of A</keywords>

<header>THE TAXGATHERER.</header>

## X.

# THE TAXGATHERER.

->JACQUES BONHOMME, a Vinedresser.<-
->M. LASOUCHE, Taxgatherer.<-

**LASOUCHE:** You have produced twenty large casks <note>original: "tuns"; a tun is a large barrel of wine, traditionally holding about 252 gallons.</note> of wine?
**JACQUES:** Yes; through my own skill and hard work.
**LASOUCHE:** Have the goodness to hand over six of the best to me.
**JACQUES:** Six casks out of twenty! Good Heaven! You are going to ruin me. And, please, Sir, for what purpose do you intend to use them?
**LASOUCHE:** The first will be handed over to the creditors of the State. When people have debts, the least thing they can do is to pay interest on them.
**JACQUES:** And what becomes of the original debt <note>original: "capital"</note>?
**LASOUCHE:** That is too long a story to tell you right now. One part used to be turned into ammunition <note>original: "cartridges"</note>, which created the most beautiful smoke in the world. Another part went to pay the soldiers who had been maimed in foreign wars. Then, when this spending brought an invasion upon us, our "polite friend," the enemy, was unwilling to leave us without carrying away some of our money as a souvenir, and this money had to be borrowed.
**JACQUES:** And what benefit do I get from this now?
**LASOUCHE:** The satisfaction of saying—
> How proud I am to be French
> When I look upon the column! <note>original: "Que je suis fier d'être Français / Quand je regarde la colonne!"; a reference to the Vendôme Column in Paris, which celebrated Napoleon's military victories.</note>
**JACQUES:** And the humiliation of leaving to my heirs an estate burdened with a permanent debt <note>original: "perpetual rent-charge"</note>. Still, it is necessary to pay one's debts, no matter what foolish use was made of the money. That explains the disposal of one cask; but what about the five others?
**LASOUCHE:** One goes to support the public service, the <term>Civil List</term> <note>The list of people, including the head of state, whose salaries are paid by the government.</note>, the judges who protect your property when your neighbor tries to wrongfully take it, the <term>gendarmes</term> <note>Armed police officers.</note> who protect you from robbers while you sleep, the <term>cantonnier</term> <note>A laborer responsible for maintaining a specific stretch of road.</note> who maintains the highways, the <term>curé</term> <note>A parish priest.</note> who baptizes your children, and the school—

**Taxgatherer:** Your refusal comes too late. Your *representative* has agreed to the whole quantity I demand.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Jacques Bonhomme and the Taxgatherer. Jacques has just expressed frustration that his wine is being taken to fund colonial infrastructure and wars in North Africa, which offer him no personal benefit.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

By making him my proxy, I was guilty of a foolish mistake; for what do a general officer and a poor <term>vine-dresser</term> <note>original: "vine-dresser"; a person who prunes, trains, and cultivates grapevines.</note> have in common?

**Taxgatherer:** Oh, yes; there is something in common, namely, the wine which he has voted to give himself in your name.

**Jacques:** You have every right to laugh at me, Mr. Taxgatherer, for I richly deserve it. But be reasonable. Leave me at least the sixth <term>cask</term> <note>original: "tun"; a large wine cask, historically a measure of capacity equal to about 252 gallons.</note>. You have already secured payment for the interest on the national debt, and provided for the <term>civil list</term> <note>The "civil list" refers to the annual grant of money by Parliament for the personal expenses of the monarch and the maintenance of the royal household.</note> and the public service, besides funding the ongoing war in Africa. What more could you want?

**Taxgatherer:** There is no point in haggling with me. Share your views with the General, your representative. For the moment, he has voted away your entire <term>vintage</term> <note>The season's yield of grapes or the wine produced from them.</note>.

**Jacques:** Damn that man! But tell me, what do you intend to do with this last cask, the best of my whole stock? Wait, taste this wine. Look how ripe, mellow, and full-bodied it is!

**Taxgatherer:** Excellent! Delicious! It will suit Mr. D., the cloth-manufacturer, perfectly.

**Jacques:** Mr. D., the cloth-manufacturer? What do you mean?

**Taxgatherer:** I mean that he will reap the benefit of it.

**Jacques:** How? What? I’ll be damned if I understand you!

**Taxgatherer:** Don't you know that Mr. D. has started a grand project which will supposedly prove very useful to the country, but which, when everything is accounted for, causes a massive financial loss every year?

**Jacques:** I am sorry to hear it, but what can I do about it?

**Taxgatherer:** The <term>Chamber</term> <note>original: "The Chamber"; referring to the Chamber of Deputies, the lower house of the French parliament.</note> has concluded that if this state of affairs continues, Mr. D. will be forced to either work more profitably or shut down his factory altogether.

**Jacques:** But what do Mr. D.'s losing investments have to do with my wine?

**Taxgatherer:** The Chamber has discovered that by handing over to Mr. D. some wine taken from your cellar, some grain taken from your neighbor's barns, and some money taken from workers' wages, the losses of that "enterprising patriot" can be turned into profits.

**Jacques:** That recipe is as foolproof as it is clever. But, for heaven's sake!

<summary>In this satirical dialogue, the Taxgatherer explains to Jacques Bonhomme that his private property is being confiscated not just for government expenses, but to subsidize the failing business of a politically connected manufacturer.</summary>
<keywords>Frederic Bastiat, Jacques Bonhomme, taxation, subsidies, protectionism, crony capitalism, economic satire, Chamber of Deputies</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Jacques Bonhomme and the Taxgatherer. Jacques has just discovered that his final cask of wine is being seized to benefit Mr. D., a wealthy cloth manufacturer, under the guise of national interest.</meta>

-># THE TAXGATHERER.<-

**Jacques:** It is terribly unfair. Is Mr. D. <note>original: "Mons. D."; "Mons." is an abbreviation for "Monsieur" (Mr.). In this context, he represents a protected industrialist who benefits from government subsidies.</note>, for heaven's sake, supposed to make up for his losses by seizing my wine?

**Taxgatherer:** Not exactly the wine itself, but its value. This is what we call *incentive premiums* <note>original: "premiums of encouragement"; these were government subsidies or "bounties" paid to domestic industries to help them compete or stay profitable.</note>, or bounties. Don't you see the great service you are providing to the country?

**Jacques:** You mean the service I am providing to Mr. D.?

**Taxgatherer:** To the country. Mr. D. assures us that his manufacturing business thrives because of this arrangement, and in this way, he considers the country to be enriched. He said as much the other day in the Chamber <note>The "Chamber of Deputies," which was the lower house of the French parliament at the time.</note>, of which he is a member.

**Jacques:** This is a miserable excuse! A speculator <note>original: "speculator"; here meaning a businessman or investor taking a financial risk.</note> enters into a losing trade and wastes his capital; then he extorts wine and corn of sufficient value from me and my neighbors—not only to repair his losses but to grant him a profit—and this is described as a gain for the country at large.

**Taxgatherer:** Since your representative has come to this conclusion, you have nothing left to do but hand over to me the six casks <term>tuns</term> <note>original: "tuns"; a large wine cask, historically a measure of capacity equal to about 252 gallons.</note> of wine which I demand, and sell the remaining fourteen casks for the best price you can get.

**Jacques:** That is my business.

**Taxgatherer:** It will be a shame if you do not earn a high price for them.

**Jacques:** I will think about that.

**Taxgatherer:** A higher price will enable you to buy more of other things.

**Jacques:** I am well aware of that, Sir.

**Taxgatherer:** In the first place, if you buy iron to replace your ploughs and your spades, the law decrees that you must pay the ironmaster double what the product is actually worth.

**Jacques:** Yes, that is very comforting.

**Taxgatherer:** Then you have need of coal, of butcher's meat, of cloth, of oil, of wool, and of sugar; and for each of these goods <term>commodities</term>, the law makes you pay double.

**Jacques:** It is horrible, frightening, and disgusting!

**Taxgatherer:** Why should you complain? You yourself, through your representative...

**Jacques:** Say nothing more of my representative. I am poorly represented, it is true. But they will not trick me a second time. Next time, I shall be represented by a good and honest peasant.

<sig>M</sig>

<summary>Jacques Bonhomme debates the Taxgatherer on the logic of "bounties," realizing that protectionist laws force him to subsidize failing industries and pay double for essential goods. He concludes that his political representative has betrayed his interests, vowing to elect a fellow peasant instead.</summary>

### XI.

## THE UTOPIAN FREE-TRADER

"If I were only one of His Majesty’s ministers! . . ."

"Well, what would you do?"

"I would begin by—by—to be honest <note>original: "faith"; used here as a mild exclamation like "by my faith."</note>, by being completely at a loss. For it is clear I could only be a minister because I had the majority in my favor. I could only have the majority in my favor by winning the popular vote <term>suffrage</term> <note>original: "suffrage"; the right to vote in political elections.</note>. And I could reach that goal—honestly, at least—only by governing in accordance with public opinion. If I tried to carry out my own private opinions, I would no longer have the majority; and if I lost the support of the majority, I would no longer be one of His Majesty’s ministers."

"But suppose you were already a minister, and that you experienced no opposition from the majority. What would you do then?"

"I would inquire where *justice* lay."

"And then?"

"I would inquire where *utility* <note>In 19th-century economics, "utility" refers to the practical benefit, usefulness, or well-being of the public.</note> lay."

"And then?"

"I would inquire whether justice and utility were in harmony, or if they ran counter to one another."

<summary>Jacques Bonhomme expresses frustration over his wine being seized for subsidies before the text shifts to a philosophical dialogue about the challenges a minister faces when balancing public opinion, justice, and utility.</summary>
<keywords>General Bugeaud, taxation, suffrage, public opinion, justice vs. utility, free trade, political majority</keywords>

“And if you found they were not in harmony?”

->The rhyme is not rich, and the style is old;<-
->But do you not see that this is worth much more<-

->And that *honesty* speaks there with pure sincerity.” <note>These lines are a parody of a famous scene in Molière's play *The Misanthrope*, where the character Alceste prefers a simple, honest old song over a complex, superficial modern poem.</note><-

“But if you found that the *just* and the *useful* were one and the same thing?”

“Then I should go straight forward.”

“True; but to realize utility by means of justice, a third thing is needed.”

“What?”

“Possibility.”

“You granted me that.”

“When?”

“Just now.”

“How?”

“In assuming that I had the majority on my side.”

“A most dangerous concession, I fear; for it implies that the majority see clearly what is just, see clearly what is useful, and see clearly that both are in perfect harmony.”

“And if they see all this clearly, good results will work themselves out, so to speak, of their own accord.”

“You always bring me back to this: that no reform is possible apart from the progress of general intelligence.”

“Assuming this progress, every needed reform will infallibly follow.”

“True; but this presupposed progress is a work of time. Suppose it were accomplished—what would you do? I am anxious to see you actually and practically at work.”

“I should begin by reducing the rate of postage to a penny.”

“I have heard you speak of a halfpenny.” <note>See chapter 12 of *Economic Sophisms*, second series, later in this volume.</note>

“Yes, but as I have other reforms in view, I should proceed prudently, in the first instance, to avoid any risk of a deficit <term>deficit</term> <note>A shortfall in the government's budget when spending exceeds income.</note>.”

“Fine prudence, to be sure! You have already landed yourself in a deficit of 30 million francs.”

“Then I should reduce the salt tax <term>salt tax</term> <note>original: "salt-tax"; known in France as the *gabelle*, it was a highly unpopular and regressive tax on salt.</note> to 10 francs.”

He argues that justice and economic utility are naturally aligned when the majority of people are well-informed.</summary>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

“Good. Then you find yourself with a deficit of another thirty million. You have surely invented a new tax?”

“Heaven forbid! And besides, I do not claim to possess an inventive genius.”

“It will be very necessary, however... Ah! I see. What was I thinking? You simply intend to reduce spending. I did not think of that.”

“You are not alone in that. I shall come to that; but for now, that is not the strategy I am relying on.”

“What! You are going to decrease revenue without reducing spending, and still avoid a deficit!”

“Yes, by reducing other taxes at the same time.”

(At this point the person he was speaking to, raising his right forefinger to his forehead, shook his head as if searching for ideas.)

“By my faith! A most ingenious process. I pay over 100 francs to the Treasury; you reduce my burden by 5 francs on salt and 5 francs on postage; and in order for the Treasury to still receive 100 francs, you reduce my burden by 10 francs on some other tax?”

“Exactly; I see you understand what I mean.”

“The idea seems so strange that I am not quite sure I even heard you correctly.”

“I repeat: I balance one *tax reduction* <note>original: "dégrèvement"</note> with another.”

“Well, I happen to have a few minutes to spare, and I would very much like to hear you explain this paradox.”

“Here is the whole mystery. I know of a tax which costs the taxpayer 20 francs, of which not one cent <note>original: "farthing"</note> ever reaches the Treasury. I relieve you of half of it, and I ensure that the other half finds its way to the *Ministry of Finance* <note>original: "Hôtel des Finances"</note>.”

“Truly, you are an unrivaled financier. And what tax, tell me, do I pay that does not reach the Treasury?”

“How much did this coat cost you?”

“100 francs.”

“And if you had obtained the cloth from Verviers <note>A city in Belgium famous at the time for its wool and textile industry.</note>, how much would it have cost you?”

“80 francs.”

“Then why didn't you order it from Verviers?”

“Because that is forbidden.” <note>This refers to protectionist trade laws (tariffs or prohibitions) that forced French citizens to buy more expensive domestic cloth instead of cheaper imports.</note>

<summary>In this dialogue, the author explains a "paradox" where cutting a protectionist tax (tariffs) can actually increase government revenue and save citizens money by eliminating the "hidden tax" of high prices caused by trade prohibitions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the "Utopian" reformer and a skeptic. -># THE UTOPIAN FREE-TRADER.<-

“And why is it forbidden?”

“In order that the coat may cost 100 instead of 80 francs.”

“This prohibition, then, costs you 20 francs.”

“Undoubtedly.”

“And where do these 20 francs go?”

“Where should they go, but into the pocket of the cloth manufacturer?”

“Well, then, give me 10 francs for the Treasury; I will abolish the prohibition, and you will still be a winner by 10 francs.”

“Oh! I begin to follow you. The account with the Treasury will then stand like this: The revenue loses 5 francs on salt and 5 on postage, and gains 10 francs on cloth. The one balances the other.”

“And your own account stands like this: You gain 5 francs on salt, 5 francs on postage, and 10 francs on cloth.”

“Total, 20 francs. I like your plan; but what happens to the poor cloth manufacturer?”

“Oh! I have not lost sight of him. I manage to compensate him as well by means of tax reductions <note>original: "dégrèvements"</note> which are profitable to the revenue. What I have done for you regarding cloth, I will do for him regarding wool, coal, machinery, and so on, so that he is enabled to reduce his price without being a loser.”

“But are you sure that the one will balance the other?”

“The balance will be in his favor. The 20 francs which I enable you to gain on cloth will be increased by the amount I enable you to save on corn, meat, fuel, and other goods. This will add up to a large sum, and a similar saving will be realized by each of your 35 million fellow-countrymen <note>A reference to the approximate population of France in the mid-19th century.</note>. In this way, you will find the means to buy all the cloth produced at Verviers and Elbeuf <note>Verviers (in modern-day Belgium) and Elbeuf (in France) were major centers of the textile industry.</note>. The nation will be better clothed; that is all.”

“I shall think it over; for all this, I confess, is a bit confusing to me.”

“After all, regarding clothing, the main consideration is to be clothed. Your limbs are your own, and not the property of the manufacturer. To protect them from the cold is your business and not his! If the law takes his side against you, the law is unjust; and we have been reasoning so far on the assumption <note>original: "hypothesis"</note> that what is unjust is harmful.”

<summary>The Utopian explains how removing trade prohibitions benefits consumers through lower prices and the state through new tariff revenue, while compensating manufacturers by lowering taxes on their raw materials. He concludes that the primary purpose of clothing is to serve the wearer, not the producer, and that protectionist laws are inherently unjust.</summary>

“Perhaps I am being a bit too forward with you, but I ask you to finish explaining your financial plan.”

“I will introduce a new Customs law.”

“In two massive volumes?”

“No, in just two articles.”

“For once, then, we can do without repeating that famous legal fiction: ‘No one is supposed to be ignorant of the law’ <note>original: *Nul n'est censé ignorer la loi*</note>. Let us see your proposed tariff.”

“Here it is:

‘**Article 1.**—All imported goods shall pay a duty of 5 percent of their value <note>original: *ad valorem*, a Latin legal term meaning "according to value"</note>.’”

“Even raw materials?”

“Except for those that have no *value*.”

“But they all have some value, more or less.”

“In that case, they must pay a duty, more or less.”

“How do you expect our manufacturers to compete with foreign manufacturers who get their raw materials tax-free?”

“Given the current spending of the State, if we close off this source of revenue, we must open another. That will not change the fact that our manufacturers are at a relative disadvantage, and we would simply have to create and pay for even more government officials to manage it.”

“True. I am reasoning as if the goal were to eliminate taxes altogether, rather than simply swapping one tax for another. I will think about it. What is your second article?”

“‘**Article 2.**—All goods exported shall pay a duty of 5 percent of their value <note>original: *ad valorem*</note>.’”

“Good heavens, Mr. Utopian! <note>original: *Monsieur l' Utopiste*</note> You are going to get yourself pelted with stones—and if necessary, I will throw the first one myself!”

“We have assumed that the majority of people are well-informed.”

“Well-informed! Can you really argue that *export duties* will not be burdensome?”

“All taxes are burdensome, but this one will be less so than others.”

“The carnival season justifies many strange ideas. Please, try to make this new contradiction <note>original: "paradox"</note> seem plausible, if that is even possible.”

“How much do you pay for this wine?”

“One franc per liter <note>original: "litre," a metric unit of volume slightly larger than a quart</note>.”

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Utopian reformer and a skeptic regarding a proposed 5 percent tax on both imports and exports. The Utopian is currently defending the controversial idea of taxing exports by explaining who truly bears the burden of taxes.</meta>

# THE UTOPIAN FREE-TRADER

“How much would you have paid for it outside the city gates?”

“Half a franc.”

“What is the reason for this difference?”

“Ask the city toll office <note>original: *octroi*, a local tax collected on consumer goods—especially wine and food—brought into a town or city</note>, which has imposed a tax of half a franc upon it.”

“And who established this toll office?”

“The Paris City Council <note>original: *Commune of Paris*, referring here to the municipal government</note>, to enable them to pave and light the streets.”

“It resolves itself, then, into an import tax. But if the neighboring districts <note>original: *communes*</note> had established the toll for their own profit, what would have been the consequence?”

“I would still have paid one franc for wine worth only half a franc, and the other half-franc would have gone to pave and light Montmartre and the Batignolles <note>districts on the outskirts of Paris that were separate municipalities at the time</note>.”

“So, in effect, it is the consumer who pays the tax.”

“That is beyond all doubt.”

“Then, by imposing an export tax, you make the foreigner contribute to your public expenses.”

“Pardon me, but that is *unjust*.”

“Why? Before any product <note>original: *commodity*</note> can be produced in a country, we must assume that certain things already exist in that country: education, security, and roads, which all cost money. Why then should the foreigner not bear the costs necessary to produce the goods of which he is ultimately the consumer?”

“That goes against common opinion.”

“Not in the least. The final buyer must bear the entire cost of production, both direct and indirect.”

“It is useless to argue this point. It is self-evident that such a measure would cripple trade and shut all foreign markets against us.”

“This is a mistake. If you paid this tax on top of all others, you might be right. But if the 100 million francs raised by this tax relieved the taxpayer of other burdens to the same extent, you would reappear in the foreign market with all your usual advantages—and even greater advantages, if this tax results in less complexity and lower administrative costs.”

“I shall think it over. And now that we have settled the matter of salt,

<summary>The Utopian reformer argues that since the costs of government services like roads and security are baked into the price of goods, an export tax is a fair way to ensure foreign consumers contribute to the infrastructure that made their purchases possible.</summary>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

"...postage and <term>customs duties</term> on a new footing, does this end your projected reform?"

"On the contrary, we are only beginning."

"Please give me some account of your other utopian schemes."

"We have already given up 60 million <term>francs</term> <note>the currency of France at the time</note> on salt and postage. The <term>Customs Office</term> <note>original: Customhouse</note> provides compensation, but it also gives something far more precious."

"And what is that, if you please?"

"International relations founded on justice, and a probability of peace nearly equal to a certainty. I will disband the army."

"The whole army?"

"Except for the specialized branches, which will be recruited voluntarily like all other professions. You thus see <term>mandatory military service</term> <note>original: conscription</note> abolished."

"Please, sir, use the word recruitment."

"Ah! I had forgotten; how easy it is in some countries to perpetuate and hand down the most unpopular things by changing their names!"

"In this way, *<term>consolidated taxes</term>* <note>original: *droits réunis*, a group of unpopular indirect taxes on consumer goods</note> have become *<term>indirect taxes</term>* <note>original: *contributions indirectes*</note>."

"And *<term>armed police</term>* <note>original: *gendarmes*</note> have taken the name of *<term>municipal guards</term>* <note>original: *gardes municipaux*</note>."

"In short, you would disarm the country based on a utopian theory."

"I said that I would disband the army—not that I would disarm the country. On the contrary, I intend to give it invincible force."

"And how can you make sense of this mass of contradictions?"

"I should call upon all citizens to take part in the service."

"It would be well worth while to excuse the services of some of them, only to enroll everyone."

"You surely did not make me a minister in order to leave things as they are. Upon my taking power, I should say, like Richelieu <note>Cardinal Richelieu (1585–1642), the chief minister to King Louis XIII, famous for his "Reason of State" philosophy</note>, 'The principles of statecraft have changed.' And my first principle, the one I would use as the basis of my administration, would be this: Every citizen must prepare for two things—to provide for his own livelihood <note>original: subsistence</note>, and to defend his country."

"It appears to me, at first glance, that there is some show of common sense in what you say."

<summary>The Utopian reformer proposes replacing a professional standing army and mandatory conscription with a citizen-led defense force, arguing that true national security comes from universal participation rather than a specialized military class.</summary>

“Consequently, I should base the law of national defense on these two enactments:

“**ARTICLE 1.**—Every able-bodied citizen shall remain in military service <note>original: *sous les drapeaux*, literally "under the colors"</note> for four years—namely, from age 21 to 25—for the purpose of receiving military instruction.”

“A fine economy, truly! You disband four hundred thousand soldiers only to create ten million.”

“**ARTICLE 2.**—The first article is waived unless it is proved that at 21 years of age the citizen already perfectly knows the basic infantry training <note>original: platoon drill</note>.”

“I don't stop there. It is certain that in order to be excused from four years’ service, there would be a fierce competition among our youth to learn the ‘right flank’ <note>original: *par le flanc droit*</note> and the ‘loading in twelve steps’ <note>original: *charge en douze temps*, a standard musket drill of the era</note>. The idea is whimsical.”

“It is better than that. For without bringing grief to families, and without infringing on equality, would it not secure for the country—in a simple and inexpensive manner—ten million defenders capable of defying all the standing armies of the world?”

“Really, if I were not on my guard, I would end up taking a serious interest in your fantasies.”

*The Utopian free-trader gets excited:* “Thank Heaven! Here is my budget relieved of 200 million <term>francs</term>. I abolish the local tolls <note>original: *octroi*, a tax on goods brought into a town</note>. I remodel indirect taxes. I . . .”

“Oh, Mr. Utopian!”

*The Utopian free-trader gets more and more excited:* “I should proclaim freedom of worship, freedom of teaching, and find new resources. I would buy up the railways, pay off the public debt, and starve out the financial speculators <note>original: stockjobbers</note>.”

“Mr. Utopian!”

“Freed from a multitude of worries, I would concentrate all the powers of government on the suppression of fraud and the administration of prompt and cheap justice; I . . .”

“Mr. Utopian, you undertake too many things; the nation will not support you!”

“You have granted me a political majority.”

“I withdraw it.”

“So be it. Then I am no longer a minister, and my projects will continue to be what they were—UTOPIAS.”
