<meta>This page begins a new section titled "Economic Sophisms," transitioning from the dialogue of the Utopian reformer to a specific critique of French fiscal policy regarding the salt tax, postage, and tariffs.</meta>

->ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.<-

# XII.

## THE SALT TAX, POSTAGE RATES, AND CUSTOMS DUTIES.

Some time ago, we expected to see our political system <note>original: representative machinery</note> produce a brand-new result—one whose manufacture had not yet been attempted: namely, *the relief of the taxpayer*.

Everyone was full of expectation. The experiment was as interesting as it was new. The movement of this political machine disturbed no one. In this regard, its performance was wonderful, regardless of when, where, or under what circumstances it was set in motion.

But regarding those reforms meant to simplify, equalize, and lighten public burdens, no one has yet been able to discover what has actually been achieved.

People said: "You will soon see; wait a little; this popular outcome requires the work of *four legislative sessions*." The year 1842 gave us railways; 1846 is supposed to give us a reduction in the salt tax and postage rates; in 1850, we are to have a reform of the tariff and of indirect taxes. This fourth session is meant to be the *jubilee* <term>jubilee</term> <note>A year of celebration or release from debt, traditionally every 50 years in ancient Hebrew law.</note> of the taxpayer.

Men were full of hope because everything seemed to favor the experiment. The *Moniteur* <note>The official government newspaper of France at the time.</note> had announced that tax revenue would continue to increase every quarter. What better use could be made of these unexpected profits than to give the villager a little more salt for his *warm water* <note>original: *eau tiède*; likely referring to a thin, simple soup</note>, and perhaps an extra letter now and then from the battlefield where his son was risking his life?

But what has happened? Like two sugar mixtures that are said to prevent each other from crystallizing, or the legendary Kilkenny cats—who fought so fiercely that nothing remained of them but their tails—the two promised reforms have swallowed each other up. Nothing remains of them but the tails. By that, I mean we have *draft bills* <note>original: *projets de lois*</note>, *statements of purpose* <note>original: *exposés des motifs*</note>, reports, statistical data, and schedules. In these, we have the small comfort of seeing our sufferings philanthropically acknowledged and *homeopathically* <note>original: homœopathically; referring to medicine given in extremely tiny, diluted amounts</note> calculated. But as for the reforms themselves—

<summary>The author critiques the French government's failure to deliver promised tax reforms, noting that despite increased revenue, the salt tax and postage reductions have been buried in bureaucracy.</summary>
<keywords>salt tax, postage rates, tax reform, fiscal policy, Moniteur, bureaucracy, indirect taxation</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the metaphorical "experiment" of French fiscal reform. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how the reforms, like a chemical experiment, have failed to produce results for the taxpayer.</meta>

themselves, they have not crystallized. Nothing has come out of the melting pot <note>original: crucible</note>, and the experiment has been a failure.

The chemists <note>The politicians and economic theorists conducting these legislative experiments.</note> will eventually come before the jury <note>The public or the voting citizenry.</note> and explain the causes of the breakdown.

One will say, "*I proposed* a postal reform; but the Chamber <note>The French Chamber of Deputies, the legislative body at the time.</note> wished first of all to rid us of the salt tax, and I gave it up."

Another will say, "*I voted* for doing away with the salt tax, but the Minister had proposed a postal reform, and my vote went for nothing."

And the jury, finding these reasons satisfactory, will begin the experiment anew on the same data, and hand the work back to the same chemists.

This proves that it would be well for us, despite the origins of the idea, to adopt the practice introduced half a century ago across the English Channel <note>original: on the other side of the Channel</note> of pursuing only one reform at a time. It is slow, and it is wearisome; but it leads to some result.

Here, we have a dozen reforms on the anvil at the same time. They jostle one another, like the ghosts at the Gate of Oblivion <note>A reference to the underworld in classical mythology, where souls are forgotten.</note>, where no one enters.

-> "Alas! How weary I am!" <-
-> "One at a time, for mercy's sake!" <note>original Italian: *Ohimè ! che lasso ! Una a la volta, per carità.* This is a famous line from Figaro's aria in Rossini's opera, *The Barber of Seville*.</note> <-

Here is what *Jacques Bonhomme* <note>The traditional personification of the French common man or peasant, similar to "Joe Public."</note> said in a dialogue with *John Bull* <note>The personification of the United Kingdom or the typical Englishman.</note>, and it is worth being reported:

### JACQUES BONHOMME AND JOHN BULL

JACQUES BONHOMME: Oh! Who will deliver me from this hurricane of reforms? My head is in a whirl. A new one seems to be invented every day: university reform, financial reform, sanitary reform, parliamentary reform, electoral reform, commercial reform, social reform, and, last of all, comes *postal reform!*

JOHN BULL: As far as the last one is concerned, it is so easy and so useful—as we have found by experience—that I venture to give you some advice on the subject.

JACQUES: We are told that postal reform has turned out poorly in England, and that the Treasury <note>original: Exchequer</note> has lost half a million pounds.

JOHN: But it has benefited the public by ten times that sum.

JACQUES: No doubt of that.

<summary>Bastiat uses a dialogue between personified nations to argue that France's attempt to pass too many reforms at once leads to failure, contrasting it with Britain's focused approach to postal reform.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Jacques Bonhomme and John, discussing the necessary reforms of the French postal system by comparing it to the successful British "Penny Post" model.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

**JOHN:** We have every sign by which public satisfaction can be proved. The nation—following the lead of Sir Robert Peel <note>A British statesman who served as Prime Minister and supported free trade and postal reform.</note> and Lord John Russell—has given Rowland Hill <note>The teacher and social reformer who campaigned for the "Penny Post," a flat rate for mail regardless of distance.</note>, in true British fashion, substantial marks of public gratitude. Even the poorer classes show their satisfaction by sealing their letters with stickers <note>original: wafers; these were small disks of dried paste used to seal letters before gummed envelopes became common.</note> bearing this inscription: "*Public gratitude for postal reform.*" The leaders of the Anti-Corn-Law League <note>A powerful political movement led by Richard Cobden that successfully campaigned to remove tariffs on imported grain.</note> have proclaimed aloud in Parliament that without cheap postage, thirty years would have been required to accomplish their great undertaking, which aimed to remove taxes on the food of the poor. The officers of the Board of Trade have declared it unfortunate that English currency does not allow for an even greater reduction! What more proof do you need?

**JACQUES:** But what about the Treasury? <note>The government’s department in charge of public finances and tax collection.</note>

**JOHN:** Don’t the Treasury and the public sail in the same boat?

**JACQUES:** Not quite. And besides, is it entirely clear that our postal system needs to be reformed?

**JOHN:** That is the question. Let us see how things stand now. What happens to the letters that are put into the post office?

**JACQUES:** The routine is very simple. The postmaster opens the letterbox at a certain hour and takes out, let’s say, a hundred letters.

**JOHN:** And then?

**JACQUES:** Then he inspects them one by one. With a geographical table before him and a letter-scale in his hand, he assigns each letter to its proper category according to weight and distance. There are currently eleven postal zones or districts and eleven degrees of weight.

**JOHN:** That makes 121 combinations for every single letter.

**JACQUES:** Yes; and we must double that number, because the letter may or may not belong to the rural delivery service <note>original: *service rural*</note>.

**JOHN:** There are, then, 24,200 things to be checked regarding every hundred letters. And how does the postmaster proceed after that?

**JACQUES:** He marks the weight on one corner of the letter and the postage cost in the middle of the address, using a coded symbol <note>original: hieroglyphic; before stamps, postal workers wrote complex symbols to indicate how much the recipient had to pay.</note> agreed upon at headquarters.

<summary>John and Jacques discuss the success of Rowland Hill’s postal reform in England, contrasting it with the administrative complexity and inefficiency of the current French postal system.</summary>
<keywords>Rowland Hill, postal reform, Anti-Corn-Law League, Sir Robert Peel, service rural, bureaucracy, taxation, economic efficiency</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Jacques Bonhomme and John regarding the inefficiencies of the current French postal system compared to the proposed reforms.</meta>

### POSTAL REFORM

**JOHN:** And then?

**JACQUES:** He stamps the letters and arranges them into ten parcels corresponding to the other post offices with which he is in communication. He adds up the total postage costs of the ten parcels.

**JOHN:** And then?

**JACQUES:** Then he enters the ten sums into a register with counterfoils <note>The part of a check or receipt kept by the issuer as a record for accounting purposes.</note>.

**JOHN:** And then?

**JACQUES:** Then he writes a letter to each of his ten corresponding postmasters, telling them which sums he is charging to their accounts.

**JOHN:** And what if the letters are prepaid?

**JACQUES:** Then, I admit, the service becomes somewhat complicated. In that case, he must receive the letter, weigh it, and assign it to its proper category as before. He must receive payment and give change, select the appropriate stamp from among thirty others, and mark the letter with its number, weight, and postage. He must transcribe the full address—first in one register, then in a second, then in a third, and finally on a detached slip of paper. He wraps the letter in that slip and sends the whole package, secured tightly with string, to the corresponding postmaster. Finally, he must enter each of these details in a dozen columns, selected from fifty other columns, which indicate the mailbag in which prepaid letters are placed.

**JOHN:** And all this for forty centimes (about 4 pence)!

**JACQUES:** Yes, on average.

**JOHN:** I see now that the *dispatch* <note>original: "despatch"</note> of letters is simple enough. Let us see now what takes place upon their *arrival*.

**JACQUES:** The postmaster opens the mailbag.

**JOHN:** And then?

**JACQUES:** He reads the ten invoices from his correspondents.

**JOHN:** And after that?

**JACQUES:** He compares the totals on the invoices with the totals produced by each of the ten parcels of letters.

**JOHN:** And after that?

**JACQUES:** He brings the whole to a grand total to find out with what sum, as a whole <note>original: *en bloc*; a French term meaning altogether or as a single unit.</note>, he is to charge each letter carrier.

**JOHN:** And after that?

**JACQUES:** After that, with a table of distances and letter—

<summary>Jacques describes the incredibly complex and repetitive clerical tasks required of postmasters to process and track mail under the current system. The dialogue highlights the absurdity of such a high level of bureaucracy for a relatively small fee.</summary>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Jacques Bonhomme and John detailing the administrative inefficiencies of the postal system upon the arrival of mail, contrasting it with the simplicity of the proposed reforms.</meta>

### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

**JACQUES:** Holding the scales in his hand, he verifies or corrects the postage of each letter.

**JOHN:** And after that?

**JACQUES:** He enters the results in register after register, and in column after column, whether they are large or small.

**JOHN:** And after that?

**JACQUES:** He communicates with the ten postmasters—his correspondents—to inform them of errors of 10 or 20 centimes <note>A French unit of currency; 100 centimes equaled 1 franc.</note> (a penny or twopence).

**JOHN:** And then?

**JACQUES:** He collects and arranges all the letters he has received to hand them to the postman.

**JOHN:** And after that?

**JACQUES:** He calculates the total postage that each postman is responsible for collecting.

**JOHN:** And after that?

**JACQUES:** The postman verifies, or debates, the meaning <note>original: "signification"</note> of the hieroglyphics <note>The scribbled markings made by postal clerks to indicate weight and cost, which were often difficult to read.</note>. The postman finally pays the total amount in advance and sets out.

**JOHN:** Go on.

**JACQUES:** The postman goes to the person to whom a letter is addressed and knocks at the door. A servant opens it. There are six letters for that address. The postage costs are added up—separately at first, then all together. They amount to 2 francs 70 centimes (2 shillings, 3 pence).

**JOHN:** Go on.

**JACQUES:** The servant goes to find his master. The master then proceeds to verify the hieroglyphics. He mistakes the *threes* for *twos* and the *nines* for *fours*. He has doubts about the weights and distances. In short, he has to ask the postman to come upstairs, and on the way, he tries to identify the signatures on the letters, thinking it may be wise to refuse some of them.

**JOHN:** Go on.

**JACQUES:** Once the postman has gone upstairs, he argues the case for the post office. They debate, they examine, they weigh, and they calculate distances—at length, the person agrees to receive five of the letters and *refuses* one.

**JOHN:** Go on.

<summary>Jacques describes the tedious, multi-step process required for a postman to deliver letters and collect payment, highlighting the wasted time and potential for disputes over postage costs.</summary>

## POSTAL REFORM

**JACQUES:** All that remains is to pay the postage. The servant is sent to the grocer to get change. After a delay of twenty minutes he returns, and the postman is finally released and rushes from door to door, to go through the same process <note>original: "ceremony"</note> at each.

**JOHN:** Go on.

**JACQUES:** He returns to the post office. He counts and recounts the money with the postmaster. He returns the letters that were rejected <note>original: "refused"</note> and receives a refund for the money he paid in advance for them. He reports the objections raised by the recipients regarding the weight and distance of the letters.

**JOHN:** Go on.

**JACQUES:** The postmaster has to consult the registers, letter bags, and special slips in order to create an account of the letters which have been *refused*.

**JOHN:** Go on, if you please.

**JACQUES:** I am thankful I am not a postmaster. We now come to accounts in dozens and scores <note>A score is a group of twenty.</note> at the end of the month. We must deal with systems invented not only to establish, but to check and control a detailed accountability <note>original: "minute responsibility"</note>. This involves a total of 50 million francs, made up of postage fees amounting on average to 43 centimes each (less than 4 1/2 pence), and 116 million letters, each of which may belong to one of 242 different categories.

**JOHN:** A very complicated "simplicity," truly! The man who has solved this problem must have a hundred times more genius than your Monsieur Piron <note>Likely referring to a French postal administrator or critic of the era.</note> or our Rowland Hill <note>Sir Rowland Hill (1795–1879) was the English social reformer who campaigned for the "penny post" and invented the postage stamp.</note>.

**JACQUES:** Well, you seem to laugh at our system. Would you explain yours to me?

**JOHN:** In England, the government sells stamps, envelopes, and covers <note>Paper wrappers used for mailing.</note> at a penny each all over the country, wherever it is considered useful.

**JACQUES:** And after that?

**JOHN:** You write your letter, fold it, put it in the envelope, and drop it into the post office.

**JACQUES:** And after that?

**JOHN:** “After that”—why, that is the whole affair. We have nothing to do with distances, bulletins, registers, controls, or accounting. We have no money to give or to receive, and no concern with hieroglyphics <note>The scribbled markings indicating cost and weight.</note>, discussions, interpretations, etc., etc.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Jacques and John regarding the merits of the British "Penny Post" system versus the complex French postal administration described on the previous page.</meta>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

**JACQUES:** Truly, this is very simple. But is it not too simple? A child might understand it. But such reforms as you describe stifle the genius of great administrators. For my own part, I stick to the French way of doing things. And besides, your *uniform rate* has the greatest of all faults: it is unjust.

**JOHN:** *How so?*

**JACQUES:** Because it is unjust to charge as much for a letter addressed to the immediate neighborhood as for one which you carry three hundred miles.

**JOHN:** In any case, you will allow that the injustice goes no further than the extent of a penny.

**JACQUES:** No matter—it is still an injustice.

**JOHN:** Besides, the injustice—which at most cannot exceed a penny in any particular case—disappears when you consider the entire correspondence of any individual citizen, who sends his letters sometimes to a great distance and sometimes to places in the immediate vicinity.

**JACQUES:** I stick to my opinion. The injustice is lessened—infinitely lessened, if you will; it is barely noticeable, infinitesimal, homeopathic <note>original: "homœopathic"; referring to an extremely small, diluted amount.</note>; but it exists.

**JOHN:** Does your government make you pay more for an ounce of tobacco which you buy in the Rue de Clichy than for the same quantity sold on the Quai d’Orsay? <note>Both locations are in Paris; the Quai d'Orsay is near the center, while the Rue de Clichy is further north.</note>

**JACQUES:** What connection is there between these two things?

**JOHN:** In both cases, the cost of transport must be taken into account. Mathematically, it would be just for each pinch of snuff to be more expensive in the Rue de Clichy than on the Quai d’Orsay by the millionth part of a farthing <note>A farthing was a British coin worth one-quarter of a penny.</note>.

**JACQUES:** True; I don't dispute that it may be so.

**JOHN:** Let me add that your postal system is just only in appearance. Two houses may stand side by side, but one of them happens to be within, and the other just outside, a certain *zone* or postal district. The one pays a penny more than the other—an amount equal to the entire postage cost in England. You see, then, that with you, injustice is committed on a much greater scale than with us.

**JACQUES:** That is true. My objection does not amount to much; but the loss of tax revenue <note>original: "revenue"</note> still remains to be taken into account.

<summary>John defends the uniform "Penny Post" system against Jacques's claims of injustice, arguing that small discrepancies in transport costs are negligible and that the French zone-based system creates even greater inequalities.</summary>

# POSTAL REFORM.

At this point, I stopped listening to the two speakers. It turned out, however, that Jacques Bonhomme was completely converted; for some days later, after the report by Mr. Vuitry had been published, Jacques wrote the following letter to that honorable legislator:

> ->**J. Bonhomme to Mr. de Vuitry, Deputy, Reporter of the Commission charged to examine the *proposed bill* <note>original: "projet de loi"</note> regarding the Postage of Letters.**<-
> 
> "Sir <note>original: "Monsieur"</note>,—Although I am aware of the extreme discredit one faces by advocating for an *absolute theory*, I believe it is my duty not to abandon the cause of a *uniform postage rate, reduced to a simple payment for the service actually performed*.
> 
> "My writing to you will no doubt be seen as a joke. On one side is a hot-headed, armchair reformer <note>original: "closet-reformer"; a person who proposes theories without practical experience.</note> who talks of overturning an entire system all at once and without any gradual transition; a dreamer who has perhaps never even glanced at that mountain of laws, ordinances, tables, schedules, and statistical details which accompany your report—in a word, a *theorist*. On the other side is a serious, prudent, and moderate legislator who has weighed and compared all interests, showing due respect for them; who has rejected all *systems* or, what amounts to the same thing, has built a system of his own by borrowing from all the others. The outcome of such a struggle cannot be doubted.
> 
> "Nevertheless, as long as the question is undecided, everyone has a right to express their opinions. I know that mine are firm enough to make me look ridiculous. All I ask of the reader of this letter is not to dismiss me with ridicule—if indeed there is cause for it—before hearing my reasons, rather than after.
> 
> "For I, too, can appeal to *experience*. A great nation has tried this experiment. What was the result? We cannot deny that those people are knowledgeable in such matters, and that their opinion carries weight.
> 
> "Well, there is not a man in England whose voice is not in favor of *postal reform*. Witness the fundraiser that has been started for a testimonial to Mr. Rowland Hill <note>Sir Rowland Hill (1795–1879) was the English social reformer who campaigned for the "Penny Post" system, which revolutionized communication.</note>. Witness the way John Bull <note>The national personification of the United Kingdom, similar to Uncle Sam for the United States.</note> shows his gratitude. Witness—"

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

> ...the oft-repeated declaration of the Anti-Corn-Law League <note>A powerful British political movement led by Richard Cobden that successfully campaigned to abolish tariffs on imported grain.</note>: "Without the penny postage <note>The introduction of a flat one-penny rate for letters in Britain in 1840, which revolutionized communication.</note>, we should never have developed that public opinion which has overturned the system of protection <note>original: "system of protection"; referring to protectionist trade tariffs that restricted imports.</note>." All this is confirmed by what we read in a work originating from an official source:
>
> "The rates of postage should be regulated, not with a view to generating revenue, but for the sole purpose of covering the expense."
>
> To which Mr. Macgregor <note>John MacGregor (1797–1857), a Scottish statistician and diplomat involved in trade reform.</note> adds:
>
> "It is true that since the rate has dropped to our smallest coin, we cannot lower it further, even though it does yield some revenue. But this source of revenue, which will go on constantly increasing, must be used to improve the service and to develop our system of mail steamers all over the world."
>
> This leads me to examine the primary idea of the commission, which is, on the other hand, that the rate of postage should be a source of revenue for the government.
> 
> This idea runs through your entire report, and I admit that, under the influence of this bias, you could arrive at nothing great or comprehensive. You are fortunate if, in trying to reconcile these two systems, you have not fallen into the errors and drawbacks of both.
> 
> The first question we have to consider is this: Is the correspondence that passes between individual citizens a proper subject for taxation?
> 
> I shall not rely on abstract principles, or remind you that because the very essence of society is the communication of ideas, the object of every government should be to facilitate—and not impede—this communication.
> 
> Let us look at the actual facts.
> 
> The total length of our highways and local roads extends to a million kilometers (625,000 miles). Supposing that each has cost 100,000 francs (£4,000), this makes a capital of 100 billion <note>original: "milliards"; an older term for a thousand million.</note> francs (£4,000,000,000) expended by the State to facilitate the transport of passengers and goods.
> 
> Now, consider this: what if one of your honorable colleagues asked the Chamber <note>The Chamber of Deputies, the lower house of the French parliament at the time.</note> for permission to introduce a bill written like this:
> 
> "From and after January 1st next, the Government will levy a tax upon all travelers. This tax will be sufficient not only to cover the expense of maintaining the highways, but to return to the Treasury <note>original: "Exchequer"</note> four or five times the amount of that expense. . . ."

# POSTAL REFORM

> "Would you not feel such a proposal to be anti-social and monstrous?
> 
> "How is it that this focus on *profits*—or even simply covering costs—never occurs to us regarding the movement of goods, yet seems so natural regarding the movement of ideas?
> 
> "Perhaps it is simply a matter of habit. If we were creating a postal system from scratch today, it would surely seem monstrous to design it as a *source of government revenue* <note>In this context, the author is arguing against the government using the postal service as a "tax" on communication.</note>.
> 
> "Yet notice that the injustice is even more obvious in this case than in others.
> 
> "When the government builds a new road, it forces no one to use it. (It would undoubtedly do so if the use of the road were taxed.) But as long as Post Office regulations are enforced, no one is allowed to send a letter by any other means—not even to their own mother.
> 
> "Therefore, the cost of postage should, in principle, simply cover the expenses <note>original: "remunerative"</note> and, for that same reason, be uniform for everyone.
> 
> "If we start with this idea, the reform I am proposing becomes marvelously simple and effective!
> 
> "Here is the entire plan, almost ready to be written into law:
> 
> " ' ARTICLE 1. Starting January 1st of next year, *stamped envelopes and covers* will be sold in every location the government deems necessary, at the price of a halfpenny or a penny.
> 
> " ' 2. Every letter placed in one of these envelopes (not exceeding half an ounce), and every newspaper or *printed item* <note>original: "print"</note> placed in one of these covers (not exceeding the weight of . . . ), will be sent and delivered to its address at no additional cost.
> 
> " ' 3. All Post Office bookkeeping <note>original: "accounting"</note> is completely eliminated.
> 
> " ' 4. All fines and punishments <note>original: "pains and penalties"</note> regarding the private delivery of letters are abolished.'
> 
> "I admit this is very simple—perhaps too simple—and I expect many objections.
> 
> "The question is not whether my proposed system has drawbacks, but whether the current system has even more.
> 
> "Quite seriously, can the two systems even be compared (except when it comes to generating government income)?
> 
> "Examine both. Compare them in terms of ease, convenience, speed, simplicity, order, economy, justice, equality, the volume of business, public satisfaction, and moral and in-

### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

...intellectual development and its civilizing influence; tell me honestly if it is possible to hesitate for a moment.

"I shall not stop to expand on each of these considerations—I am giving you the headings of twelve chapters, which I leave blank, convinced that no one can fill them up better than yourself.

"But since there is one objection—namely, *government revenue*—I must say a word on that subject.

"You have constructed a table in order to show that even at twopence, the revenue would suffer a loss of £880,000.

"At a penny, the loss would be £1,120,000, and at a half-penny, a loss of £1,320,000; these are scenarios so terrifying that you do not even lay them out in detail.

"But allow me to say that the figures in your report dance about with a little too much freedom. In all your tables and in all your calculations, you make the silent assumption that *all other things will remain equal* <note>original: "cæteris paribus"</note>. You assume that the cost of administration will be the same under a simple system as under a complicated one. You assume that the number of letters will be the same with the current average postage of 4½d. as with the uniform rate of twopence. You limit yourself to this 'rule of three' <note>A mathematical method of calculating a fourth value based on the ratio of three known values; here, the author critisizes the government for using simple multiplication that ignores how price changes affect demand.</note>: if 87 million letters at 4½d. yield so much, then at 2d. the same number will yield this much—only allowing for certain distinctions when they work against our proposed reform.

"In order to estimate the real sacrifice of revenue, we must, first of all, calculate the savings in the service that will be achieved; then, we must calculate in what proportion the amount of correspondence will be increased. We only take this last piece of data into account because we cannot suppose that the savings in costs that will be realized will not be offset by the need for more staff <note>original: "personnel"</note> required by a more extended service.

"Undoubtedly, it is impossible to fix the exact amount of increase in the circulation of letters which the reduction of postage would cause, but in such matters, a reasonable analogy has always been accepted.

"You yourself admit that in England, a reduction of seven-eighths in the rate caused an increase of correspondence to the extent of 360 percent.

"Here, the lowering to 5 centimes (a halfpenny) of the rate—which is at present at an average of something less than...

<summary>The author critiques the government's financial objections to postal reform, arguing that their projections fail to account for the massive increase in mail volume and administrative savings that follow a price reduction.</summary>

# POSTAL REFORM

A rate of four and a half pence <note>original: "4½d."; "d" was the abbreviation for the British penny, derived from the Latin "denarius."</note> would likewise represent a reduction of seven-eighths. We may therefore be allowed to expect the same result—that is to say, 417 million letters instead of 116 million.

"But let us count on only 300 million.

"Is there any exaggeration in assuming that with a postage rate one-half lower, we shall reach an average of 8 letters for each inhabitant, especially when in England they have already reached 13?

"Now I ask whether the Government—which makes a *positive* sacrifice of 800 million francs (£32,000,000) per year in order to facilitate the *free* transport of passengers—should not make a *negative* sacrifice of 17 million in order to avoid making a *profit* on the transmission and circulation of ideas?

"But I am aware that the Treasury has its own habits. With whatever satisfaction it sees its receipts increase, it feels a proportional disappointment in seeing them diminished by even a single farthing <note>The smallest British coin, worth a quarter of a penny.</note>. It seems to be equipped with those admirable valves which, in the human body, allow the blood to flow in one direction but prevent its return. So be it. The Treasury is perhaps a little too old for us to quicken its pace. We have no hope, therefore, that it will yield to us. But what will be said if I, *Jacques Bonhomme* <note>The traditional name for the French "common man" or typical citizen, used here as a pseudonym for the author.</note>, show it a way that is simple, easy, convenient, and essentially practical for doing a great service to the country without it costing a single farthing?"

<summary>The author provides a financial model showing that a drastic reduction in postage rates would significantly increase letter volume and, while reducing the Treasury's net profit by 17 million, would benefit the circulation of ideas. He concludes by promising a practical solution that costs the government nothing.</summary>

"Give me power to modify the customs tariff in such a way *that I shall be strictly forbidden from increasing any duty, but that I may lower duties as I please.*
"And I, Jacques Bonhomme <note>The personification of the common French man, similar to "John Bull" in England.</note>, guarantee you a revenue, not of 280 million, but of 300 million. Two hundred French bankers will be my guarantors, and all I ask for my reward is whatever these three taxes produce over and above 300 million.
"Is it necessary for me to list the advantages of my proposal?
"1. The people will receive all the benefits resulting from the cheap price of a basic necessity—salt.
"2. Fathers will be able to write to their sons, and mothers to their daughters. No longer will men’s affections and sentiments, and the endearments of love and friendship, be blocked and driven back into their hearts, as they are now, by the hand of the tax collector.
"3. To carry a letter from one friend to another will no longer be listed in our law books as a crime.
"4. Trade will revive with this new liberty, and our merchant shipping will recover from its current low state.
"5. The Treasury will gain *twenty million* at first; later, it will gain everything that flows into the revenue from other sources because of the savings each citizen realizes on salt, postage, and other things where duties have been lowered.

If my proposal is rejected, what am I to conclude? Provided the bankers I represent offer sufficient security, under what excuse can my proposal be refused? It is impossible

# POSTAL REFORM.

...to appeal to the *balance* <note>original: "equilibrium"</note> of the budget. It would indeed be disrupted, but in such a way that the revenues would exceed the expenses. This is not a matter of theory, systems, statistics, probability, or guesswork; it is a firm offer, much like a company seeking a contract <note>original: "concession"</note> to build a railway line. The Treasury tells me what it earns from postage, the salt tax, and customs. I offer to give it *more*. Therefore, the objection cannot come from the Treasury. I offer to reduce the rates for salt, postage, and customs, and I promise not to raise them; therefore, the objection cannot come from the taxpayers. From whom does it come, then? From the monopolists? It remains to be seen whether their voices will be allowed in France to drown out the voices of the Government and the people. To prove this to us, I beg you to pass my proposal to the Council of Ministers.
-> JACQUES BONHOMME. <-

> "P.S.—Here is the text of my offer:
> "I, Jacques Bonhomme, representing a group of bankers and investors who are ready to provide all necessary guarantees and deposit whatever security is required;
> "Having learned that the Government derives only 280 million francs from customs duties, postage, and the salt tax under the current fixed rates;
> "I offer to give the Government 300 million francs from the total revenue <note>original: "gross produce"</note> of these three sources;
> "And I will do this while reducing the salt tax from 30 francs to 10 francs;
> "Reducing the rate of postage from an average of 42½ centimes to a uniform rate of between 5 and 10 centimes;
> "On the sole condition that I am permitted—not to raise (which will be strictly forbidden)—but to *lower* the customs duties as much as I please.
> -> JACQUES BONHOMME." <-

"You are a fool," I said to Jacques Bonhomme when he read me his letter. "You can do nothing in moderation. The other day you cried out against the *hurricane* of reforms, and now I find you demanding three at once, making one of them a condition for the other two. You will ruin yourself."

"Be quiet," he said, "I have done all my calculations. I only wish they would be accepted. But they will not be accepted."

With that we parted, his head full of figures and mine full of reflections which I will not impose <note>original: "forbear to inflict"</note> upon the reader.

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

### XIII.

## PROTECTION; OR, THE THREE CITY MAGISTRATES.

### A DEMONSTRATION IN FOUR SCENES <note>original: "Tableaux"</note>.

### *SCENE I.—The house of Mr. Peter.—A window looking out on a fine park.—Three gentlemen seated near a warm fire.*

PETER: Bravo! There is nothing like a good fire after a good dinner. It feels so comfortable. But, alas! how many honest folks are like the King of Yvetot <note>original: "Roi d’ Yvetot," a reference to a popular French song by Pierre-Jean de Béranger about a simple, legendary king.</note>, who:

> ->“Blow on their fingers,<-
> ->For lack of wood.”<-
> <note>original: "Soufflent, faute de bois, / Dans leurs doigts."</note>

Miserable creatures! A charitable thought has just come into my head. You see these fine trees; I am about to cut them down and distribute the timber among the poor.

PAUL and JOHN: What! For free <note>original: "gratis"</note>?

PETER: Not exactly. My charitable works would soon come to an end if I were to waste <note>original: "dissipate"</note> my fortune. I estimate my park is worth £1000. By cutting down the trees, I shall pocket a good sum.

PAUL: Wrong. Your wood, as it stands, is worth more than that of the neighboring forests, for it provides you with services that they cannot. When it is cut down, it will only be good for firewood, like any other, and it will not bring in a single penny more per load.

PETER: Oh! Oh! Mr. Theorist, you forget that I am a practical man. My reputation as a speculator is well enough established, I believe, to prevent me from being taken for a fool <note>original: "noodle"</note>. Do you imagine I am going to amuse myself by selling my timber at the price of <term>float-wood</term> <note>Wood transported by river currents, usually cheaper than locally sourced wood.</note>?

PAUL: It would seem so.

PETER: Simpleton! And what if I can stop float-wood from being brought into Paris?

PAUL: That changes things. But how can you manage that?

PETER: Here is the whole secret. You know that float-wood, upon entering the city, pays a tax of 5 pence <note>original: "5d."</note> per load. Tomorrow, I will persuade...

<summary>In this satirical dialogue, Bastiat introduces "Mr. Peter," a wealthy man who plots to increase the value of his own timber by using his influence as a magistrate to block competing wood from entering the city.</summary>
<keywords>protectionism, monopoly, Frédéric Bastiat, economic satire, timber trade, market manipulation, trade barriers</keywords>

## PROTECTION; OR, THE THREE CITY MAGISTRATES.

the local council <note>original: "commune"</note> to raise the duty to £4, £8, £12—in short, high enough to prevent the entry of even a single log. Now, do you follow me? If the good people are not to die of cold, they will have no choice but to come to my woodyard. They will bid against each other for my wood, and I will sell it at a high price. This "act of charity," successfully carried out, will put me in a position to perform even more acts of charity.

**PAUL:** What a clever invention, truly! It suggests to me another plan of the same kind.

**JOHN:** And what is that? Is philanthropy to be brought into play once again?

**PAUL:** How do you like this Normandy butter?

**JOHN:** It is excellent.

**PAUL:** Until now, I thought it was acceptable. But don’t you find it catches in your throat? I could make better butter right here in Paris. I shall keep four or five hundred cows and distribute milk, butter, and cheese among the poor.

**PETER AND JOHN:** What! As charity?

**PAUL:** Bah! Let us always put "charity" in the foreground. It is such a noble concept that even its mask serves as a good passport for our plans. I shall give my butter to the people, and they will give me their money. Is that what you call selling?

**JOHN:** No; at least not according to the character in *The Bourgeois Gentleman* <note>original: "Bourgeois Gentilhomme," a play by Molière. In it, the protagonist is told his father wasn't a "merchant" who sold cloth, but a "gentleman" who gave it to friends in exchange for money.</note>. But call it whatever you like—you will ruin yourself. How can Paris ever compete with Normandy in dairy production?

**PAUL:** I shall be able to save the cost of shipping.

**JOHN:** Perhaps. But even after paying for shipping, the Normans can still beat the Parisians in price.

**PAUL:** To provide a man with something at a lower price—is that what you call "beating" him?

**JOHN:** It is the usual phrase; and you will always find yourself beaten by their lower prices.

**PAUL:** Yes; beaten in the same way *Don Quixote* was. The blows will actually fall upon *Sancho* <note>In Cervantes' novel, the servant Sancho Panza often suffers the physical consequences of his master Don Quixote's delusions.</note>. John, my friend, you are forgetting the <term>octroi</term>.

**JOHN:** The <term>octroi</term> <note>original: "octroi," a local tax collected on various consumer goods, especially food and drink, brought into a city or town.</note>! What does that have to do with your butter?

**PAUL:** Tomorrow, I shall demand <term>protection</term> and persuade the city council to prohibit butter from being brought into Paris from Normandy and Brittany. The people must then either go without it or purchase mine—and at whatever price I choose to set.

<summary>Paul proposes a plan to create a dairy monopoly in Paris by using city taxes and import bans to block superior, cheaper butter from Normandy and Brittany. He justifies this scheme using the same "charitable" rhetoric Peter used for his wood monopoly.</summary>

### ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

**JOHN:** Upon my honor, gentlemen, your philanthropy has completely converted me.

->“One learns to howl, as they say, with the wolves.”<-
<note>original: "On apprend à hurler, dit l’autre, avec les loups." A French proverb meaning that one must adapt to the customs—or even the vices—of those around them.</note>

My mind is made up. I shall not be thought unworthy of my colleagues. Peter, this sparkling fire has inflamed your soul. Paul, this butter has lubricated the gears of your intelligence. I, too, feel stimulated by this piece of salted pork <note>original: "powdered pork"; meat preserved with salt or "powdered" with salt crystals.</note>; and tomorrow I shall vote, and convince others to vote, for the exclusion of all swine, whether dead or alive. Once that is done, I shall build magnificent sheds in the heart of Paris,

->“For the filthy animal forbidden to the Hebrews.”<-
<note>original: "Pour l’animal immonde aux Hébreux défendu." A quote from Jean Racine’s play *Athalie*, referring to the pig, which is considered non-kosher in the Hebrew tradition.</note>

I shall become a pig-driver and a pork-butcher. Let us see how the good people of Paris can avoid coming to buy their supplies at my shop!

**PETER:** Not so fast, my good friends; if you raise the price of butter and salted meat to such an extent, you will cut down the profit I expect from my wood before I even see it.

**PAUL:** And my business will no longer be so wonderfully profitable if I am overcharged for my firewood and bacon.

**JOHN:** And what shall I gain by overcharging you for my sausages if you overcharge me for my firewood, bread, and butter?

**PETER:** Very well, let us not quarrel. Let us instead put our heads together and make mutual concessions. Moreover, it is not good to consult one's self-interest exclusively—we must practice humanity and ensure that the people do not go without fuel.

**PAUL:** Quite right; and it is only proper that the people should have butter for their bread.

**JOHN:** Undoubtedly; and a bit of bacon for the cooking pot.

->**ALL:** Three cheers for charity! Three cheers for philanthropy! Tomorrow we shall take the city gates by assault!<-
<note>The original refers to the "octroi," a local tax or toll collected on consumer goods as they entered a city or town.</note>

**PETER:** Ah! I forgot. One more word; it is essential. My good friends, in this age of selfishness <note>original: "egotism"</note>, the world is suspicious, and the purest intentions are often misunderstood. Paul, you shall take the role of pleading for the wood; John will do the same for the butter; and I shall devote myself to the *home-bred* pig. It is necessary to prevent any malicious suspicions.

<summary>In this concluding dialogue, the three magistrates agree to support each other’s protectionist schemes, pretending to advocate for each other's interests to hide their own greed from the public.</summary>

# PROTECTION; OR, THE THREE CITY MAGISTRATES.

PAUL and JOHN (*leaving*): On my word, that is a clever fellow.

### SCENE II.—*The Council Chamber.*

PAUL: My dear colleagues <note>original: "Mes chers collégues"</note>, every day great piles of firewood are brought into Paris, which drain away huge sums of money. At this rate, we will all be ruined within three years, and what will become of the poorer classes? (*Cheers.*) We must prohibit <term>foreign timber</term> <note>In this context, "foreign" refers to wood brought in from outside the city of Paris, even from other parts of France.</note>. I am not speaking for my own benefit, for all the wood I own would not even make a toothpick. Therefore, in what I mean to say, I am entirely free from any personal interest or bias. (*Hear, hear.*) 

But here is my friend Peter, who owns a park; he will guarantee a sufficient supply of fuel to our fellow citizens, who will no longer be dependent on the charcoal-burners of the Yonne <note>The Yonne is a river and department south of Paris, traditionally a major source of the city's wood supply.</note>. Have you ever considered the risk we run of dying from the cold if the owners of foreign forests should decide to send no more firewood to Paris? Let us impose a ban, then, on importing wood. By this means, we shall stop the draining away of our money, create an independent industry charged with supplying the city with firewood, and open up a new source of employment and pay for our workmen. (*Cheers.*)

JOHN: I support the proposal of my honorable friend, the previous speaker, which is at once so charitable and—as he himself has explained—so entirely selfless. It is indeed high time that we should put an end to this insolent <term>free passage</term> <note>original: "laissez passer," a French phrase meaning "let it pass," used here to describe the policy of allowing goods to enter the city without taxes or restrictions.</note>, which has brought excessive competition into our markets. It has reached such an extent that whenever a province has a special advantage for providing us with any product, it immediately floods our market, undersells us, and ruins the Parisian workman. 

It is the duty of the Government to level the playing field by setting <term>duties</term> <note>taxes on imported goods</note> wisely adapted to each case. We should not allow anything to enter from the outside that is not more expensive than it is in Paris; this will relieve us from an unfair struggle. How, for example, can we possibly produce milk and butter in Paris with Brittany and Normandy right at our door? Remember, gentlemen, that the farmers of Brittany have cheaper land, a more abundant supply of hay, and manual labor on much cheaper terms.

<summary>In this scene, the magistrates Paul and John argue for banning imported wood and taxing dairy products from other regions, claiming these trade barriers will protect Parisian jobs and ensure the city's independence.</summary>

## ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

Doesn’t common sense tell us that we must level the playing field by a protective <term>octroi</term> <note>An *octroi* was a local tax collected on consumer goods—such as food and fuel—brought into a city or town.</note> tariff? I demand that the duty on milk and butter be increased by 1,000 percent, and even higher if necessary. The workman’s breakfast will cost a little more, but look at how much his wages will rise! We will see cow sheds, dairies, and butter churns appearing all around us, laying the foundations for new branches of industry. It is not that I have any personal interest in this proposal. I am not a dairy farmer, nor do I have any wish to be one. The only motive that drives me is the desire to be useful to the working classes. (*Applause.*)

PETER: I am delighted to see in this assembly such pure, enlightened, and devoted statesmen who care for the best interests of the people. (*Cheers.*) I admire their lack of self-interest, and I can do no better than to follow the noble example they have set. I support their motions, and I will only add one more: a ban on bringing the pigs of Poitou <note>Poitou is a historic province in west-central France, known for its agriculture.</note> into Paris. I assure you, I have no desire to become a pig-driver or a butcher. In that case, I would have felt it a matter of conscience to remain silent. But is it not shameful, gentlemen, that we should be paying tribute to the peasants of Poitou? They have the audacity to come into our own market and take over a branch of industry that we ourselves have no way of maintaining. After they have flooded us with their hams and sausages, do they perhaps take nothing from us in return? In any case, who can say that the <term>balance of trade</term> <note>The difference in value between a country's (or city's) imports and exports; Peter is using the mercantilist argument that buying from others "drains" wealth.</note> is not in their favor, and that we are not forced to pay them a tribute in hard cash? Is it not obvious that if the pig industry of Poitou were moved to Paris, it would create a steady demand for Parisian labor? And then, gentlemen, is it not very possible—as Mr. Lestiboudois <note>Charles Lestiboudois (1797–1876) was a French physician and politician whose protectionist views Bastiat frequently satirized.</note> has so rightly remarked—that we may be buying the salt pork of Poitou with our capital rather than our income? Where will that lead us? Let us not allow rivals who are at once greedy, grasping, and treacherous to come here and undercut us, making it impossible for us to provide these same goods for ourselves. Gentlemen, Paris has placed its trust in you; it is up to you to justify that trust. The people are unemployed; it is your job to create jobs for them. And if salt pork costs them a slightly higher price, we will have, at least, the conscious—

**A VOICE:** I have heard much talk of the poor; but under the pretext of providing them with jobs, you begin by taking away what is more valuable than employment itself: namely, butter, firewood, and meat.

->### SCENE III.—*Twenty years later.*<-

**SON:** Father, make up your mind; we must leave Paris. No one can live here anymore—there is no work, and everything is expensive.

**FATHER:** You do not know, my son, how much it hurts a man to leave the place where he was born.

**SON:** The worst thing of all is to die of starvation.

**FATHER:** You go, then, and look for a more welcoming country. As for me, I will not leave the place where the graves of your mother, and of your brothers and sisters, lie. I long to find with them that peace which has been denied to me in this city of desolation.

**SON:** Take courage, father; we will find work somewhere else—in Poitou, Normandy, or Brittany. <note>These are agricultural provinces of France. The son is suggesting they move to the very places Paris formerly traded with.</note> They say that all the manufacturing industries of Paris are gradually moving to these distant provinces.

**FATHER:** And it is only natural that they should. Since the people of those provinces can no longer sell us their firewood and food, they have stopped producing more than they need for themselves. The time and money they have available are now spent making for themselves the products we used to provide for them.

**SON:** Just as in Paris, people have given up the manufacture of elegant clothing and furniture, and have turned instead to planting trees and raising pigs and cows. Although I am still young, I have lived to see vast warehouses, luxurious neighborhoods, and <term>quays</term> <note>Stone platforms or wharves built along the edge of the water for loading and unloading ships.</note> that were once bustling with life and ani[mation]... <note>The text cuts off mid-word "animation."</note>

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS

...ing to the banks of the Seine, which have been turned into meadows and woods <note>original: "copses"</note>.

**FATHER:** While towns are spreading across the provinces, Paris is being turned into green fields. What a deplorable revolution! And this terrible calamity has been brought upon us by three magistrates, backed by public ignorance.

**SON:** Please tell <note>original: "Pray relate"</note> me the history of this change.

**FATHER:** It is short and simple. Under the pretext of establishing in Paris three new branches of industry, and by this means giving employment to the working classes, these men convinced the city council <note>original: "commune"</note> to prohibit the entry into Paris of firewood, butter, and meat. They claimed for themselves the right of providing these things for their fellow citizens. These commodities rose at first to outrageous <note>original: "exorbitant"</note> prices. No one earned enough to afford <note>original: "procure"</note> them, and the few who could afford them spent all their income on them, leaving no money to buy anything else. This caused a collapse in all other branches of industry and production—occurring all the more quickly because the provinces no longer provided a market. Poverty, death, and emigration then began to depopulate Paris.

**SON:** And when is this to stop?

**FATHER:** When Paris has become a forest and a prairie.

**SON:** The three magistrates must have made a large fortune?

**FATHER:** At first they realized enormous profits, but at length they fell into the same poverty as everyone else.

**SON:** How did that happen?

**FATHER:** Look at that ruin. That was a magnificent mansion surrounded by a beautiful park. If Paris had continued to grow <note>original: "progress"</note>, Master Peter <note>One of the magistrates from the previous scene, who advocated for the firewood monopoly.</note> would have earned more interest than his entire capital is worth now.

**SON:** How can that be, seeing he has got rid of competition?

**FATHER:** Competition in selling has disappeared, but competition in *buying* has disappeared also. It will continue to disappear every day more and more until Paris becomes a bare field, and until the woods of Master Peter have no more value than the woods of an equal amount of land in the Forest of Bondy <note>A large forest outside Paris, historically known for being a wild area and a haunt for bandits; the Father implies the land has become worthless wilderness.</note>. It is in this way that monopoly, like every other system of injustice, carries within itself its own punishment.

**SON:** That does not seem very clear to me, but the decline...

<summary>In this satirical dialogue, Bastiat illustrates the ultimate failure of protectionism: by banning imports to "protect" local sellers, the magistrates accidentally destroy the city's economy, leading to depopulation and making the land itself worthless.</summary>

<keywords>Frederic Bastiat, protectionism, monopoly, competition, Paris, economic collapse, Forest of Bondy, trade barriers</keywords>

# PROTECTION; OR, THE THREE CITY MAGISTRATES.

...of Paris is an undeniable fact. Is there no way, then, to counteract this strange measure that Peter and his colleagues had adopted twenty years ago?

**FATHER:** I am going to tell you a secret. I am staying in Paris for a reason. I will call on the people for help. It is up to them to return the <term>octroi</term> <note>original: "octroi"; a local tax collected on consumer goods—like food and fuel—brought into a city</note> to its original purpose. They must rid themselves of that deadly principle that was grafted onto it—a principle that still grows there like a parasitic fungus.

**SON:** You must succeed in this immediately.

**FATHER:** On the contrary, the work will be difficult and exhausting. Peter, Paul, and John understand each other perfectly. They will do anything to stop firewood, butter, and meat from entering Paris. They have the people on their side, because the people clearly see the jobs these three protected industries provide. They know exactly how much employment the <term>cowfeeders</term> <note>original: "cowfeeders"; dairymen who kept cows within or near the city to supply milk</note> and wood merchants provide; however, they have no clear idea of the jobs that would be created in the fresh air of freedom.

**SON:** If that is all, you will soon enlighten them.

**FATHER:** At your age, my son, you have no doubts. If I write, the people will not read; for, to support their miserable existence, they have very little time at their disposal. If I speak, the magistrates will silence me. Therefore, the people will remain trapped in this deadly mistake for a long time. Political parties, who rely on stirring up popular passions, will not try to clear up these prejudices, but will instead try to profit from them. I will have to fight against the powerful men of the day, the people, and their leaders all at once. In truth, I see a terrifying storm ready to break over the head of any brave man who dares to protest against an injustice so deeply rooted in this country.

**SON:** You will have truth and justice on your side.

**FATHER:** And they will have force and slander <note>original: "calumny"</note> on theirs. If only I were young again! But age and suffering have drained my strength.

**SON:** Very well, father; devote whatever strength you have left to the service of our country. Begin this work of liberation <note>original: "enfranchisement"</note>, and leave the task of finishing it to me.

### SCENE IV.—*The Protest.*

JACQUES BONHOMME <note>original: "Jacques Bonhomme"; a traditional name for the French "Everyman" or the common working person</note>: Parisians, let us insist on a reform of the <term>octroi</term> duties <note>original: "octroi"; a local tax collected on consumer goods—like food and fuel—brought into a city</note>; let us demand that they be immediately reduced to their former rate. Let every citizen be FREE to buy his firewood, butter, and meat wherever he sees fit.

THE PEOPLE: Long live, long live LIBERTY! <note>original: "Vive, vive LA LIBERTÉ!"</note>

PETER: Parisians, do not allow yourselves to be seduced by that word, "liberty." What good can come from the liberty to buy things if you lack the money to pay for them—in other words, if you are unemployed? Can Paris produce firewood as cheaply as the Forest of Bondy? Can it produce meat as cheaply as the region of Poitou, or butter as cheaply as Normandy? If you open your gates freely to these rival products, what will become of the dairy farmers, woodcutters, and pork butchers? They cannot survive without protection.

THE PEOPLE: Long live, long live PROTECTION! <note>original: "Vive, vive LA PROTECTION!"</note>

JACQUES BONHOMME: Protection! But who protects you, the workers? Do you not compete with one another for jobs? Let the wood merchants, then, be subject to competition in their turn. They should not have a legal right to raise the price of firewood unless the law also raises your wages. Are you no longer in love with equality?

THE PEOPLE: Long live, long live EQUALITY! <note>original: "Vive, vive L'EGALITÉ!"</note>

PETER: Do not listen to these agitators. It is true that we have raised the price of firewood, meat, and butter; but we have done so for the specific purpose of being able to give good wages to the workmen. We are motivated by charity.

THE PEOPLE: Long live, long live CHARITY! <note>original: "Vive, vive LA CHARITÉ!"</note>

JACQUES BONHOMME: Make the wages rise by using the <term>octroi</term> tax if you can, or else stop using that same tax to raise the prices of goods. We Parisians do not ask for charity—we demand justice.

THE PEOPLE: Long live, long live JUSTICE! <note>original: "Vive, vive LA JUSTICE!"</note>

PETER: It is exactly the high price of goods which will lead, indirectly <note>original: "par ricochet"; literally "by ricochet," meaning an indirect or knock-on effect</note>, to a rise in wages.

THE PEOPLE: Long live, long live HIGH PRICES! <note>original: "Vive, vive LA CHERTÉ!"</note>

JACQUES BONHOMME: If butter is expensive, it is not because you...

<summary>Jacques Bonhomme argues for free trade and lower taxes to help Parisian consumers, but he is repeatedly countered by Peter, who uses protectionist rhetoric to convince the public that high prices actually benefit them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the satirical debate between Jacques Bonhomme and Peter regarding the economic effects of city taxes and trade barriers on the people of Paris. Jacques argues for free trade, while Peter defends protectionism by appealing to the fears of the workers.</meta>

### PROTECTION; OR, THE THREE CITY MAGISTRATES.

...pay high wages to the workmen, it is not even because you make exorbitant profits; it is solely because Paris is poorly suited for that branch of industry. It is because you wish to make in the town what should be produced in the country, and in the country what should be produced in the town. The people do not have more employment—they only have employment of a different kind. They have no higher wages, yet they can no longer buy commodities as cheaply as they once could.

THE PEOPLE: Long live, long live CHEAPNESS! <note>original: "Vive, vive LE BON MARCHÉ!"</note>

PETER: This man seduces you with fine words. Let us look at the question in all its simplicity. Is it, or is it not, true that if we admit firewood, meat, and butter freely or at a lower tax rate, our markets will be flooded? Believe me, there is no other way to protect ourselves from this new kind of invasion but to keep the door shut, and so maintain the prices of goods by making them artificially scarce.

SOME VOICES IN THE CROWD: Long live, long live SCARCITY! <note>original: "Vive, vive LA RARETÉ!"</note>

JACQUES BONHOMME: Let us put the question to the simple test of truth. You cannot divide goods among the people of Paris that are not actually in Paris. If there is less meat, less firewood, and less butter, the share falling to each person will be smaller. Now, there must be less if we prohibit what should be allowed to enter the city. Parisians, abundance for each of you can be secured only by general abundance.

THE PEOPLE: Long live, long live ABUNDANCE! <note>original: "Vive, vive L’ABONDANCE!"</note>

PETER: It is in vain that this man tries to persuade you that it is in your interest to be subjected to unbridled competition.

THE PEOPLE: Down with, down with COMPETITION! <note>original: "A bas, à bas LA CONCURRENCE!"</note>

JACQUES BONHOMME: It is in vain that this man tries to make you fall in love with trade restrictions.

THE PEOPLE: Down with, down with RESTRICTION! <note>original: "A bas, à bas LA RESTRICTION!"</note>

PETER: I declare, for my own part, if you deprive the poor cow-feeders and pig-drivers of their daily bread, I can no longer be responsible for public order. Workmen, do not trust that man. He is the agent of treacherous Normandy <note>Normandy was a major agricultural region whose cheap imports Peter feared</note>, and he derives his inspiration from the provinces. He is a traitor; down with him! (*The people remain silent.*)

JACQUES BONHOMME: Parisians, what I have told you today, I told you twenty years ago, when Peter first set himself to work...

## XIV.

# SOMETHING ELSE.

“What is restriction?”
“It is partial prohibition.”
“What is prohibition?”
“Absolute restriction.”
“So that what holds true of the one, holds true of the other?”
“Yes; the difference is only one of degree. There is between them the same relation as there is between a circle and the arc of a circle.”
“Then, if prohibition is bad, restriction cannot be good?”
“No more than the arc can be correct if the circle is irregular.”
“What is the name which is common to restriction and prohibition?”
“Protection.”
“What is the definitive effect of protection?”
“To demand from men a greater amount of labor for the same result.”
“Why are men attached to the system of protection?”
“Because as liberty enables us to obtain the same result *with less labor*, this apparent loss of employment frightens them.”
“Why do you say *apparent*?”
“Because all labor saved can be applied to *something else*.”
“To what?”
“That I cannot specify, nor is there any need to specify it.”

<summary>Jacques Bonhomme concludes his argument by stating that the real issue is the policy of trade restriction itself, not the individuals involved. The text then defines protectionism as a system that forces people to perform more labor for the same results, noting that the fear of job loss from efficiency ignores the fact that saved labor is redirected toward new production.</summary>
<keywords>octroi, trade restriction, prohibition, protectionism, labor efficiency, employment, economic logic, Jacques Bonhomme</keywords>

## XIV.

# SOMETHING ELSE.

"Why?"
"Because if the sum of satisfactions <note>original: "satisfactions"; here meaning the fulfillment of needs and desires through goods and services</note> which the country currently enjoys could be obtained with one-tenth less labor, no one can count the new enjoyments which men would desire to obtain from the labor left available. One man would desire to be better clothed, another better fed, another better educated, another better entertained."
"Explain to me the mechanism and the effects of protection."
"That is not an easy matter. Before entering on consideration of the more complicated cases, we must study it in a very simple one."
"Take as simple a case as you choose."
"You remember how Robinson Crusoe <note>The hero of Daniel Defoe's 1719 novel; economists often use "Crusoe economics" to simplify complex theories by imagining a single person on an island.</note> managed to make a plank when he had no saw."
"Yes; he felled a tree, and then, cutting the trunk left and right with his hatchet, he reduced it to the thickness of a board."
"And that cost him much labor?"
"Fifteen whole days' work."
"And what did he live on during that time?"
"He had provisions."
"What happened to the hatchet?"
"It was blunted by the work."
"Yes; but you perhaps do not know this: that at the moment when Robinson was beginning the work, he noticed a plank washed up by the tide upon the seashore."
"A happy accident! He of course ran to grab it?"
"That was his first impulse; but he stopped short, and began to reason thus with himself:—
" 'If I take this plank, it will cost me only the trouble of carrying it, and the time needed to go down and climb back up the cliff.
" 'But if I make a plank with my hatchet, first of all, it will provide me fifteen days' employment; then my hatchet will get blunt, which will furnish me with the additional employment of sharpening it; then I shall consume my stock of provisions, which will be a third source of employment in replacing them. Now, *labor is wealth*. It is clear that I should ruin myself by taking the shipwrecked plank. I must protect my *per-* <note>The text cuts off mid-word here; it likely intended "personal labor" or "productive labor."</note>

<summary>The author uses a parable of Robinson Crusoe to satirize protectionist logic, showing how it mistakenly treats the struggle of labor as more valuable than the result of the labor itself. Crusoe "reasons" that finding a finished plank is bad because it robs him of the "opportunity" to work for fifteen days.</summary>

personal labor; and, now that I think of it, I can even increase that labor by throwing the other plank back into the sea.' "

"But this reasoning was absurd."

"No doubt. It is nevertheless the reasoning of every nation which *protects* itself by prohibition. It throws back the plank which is offered to it in exchange for a small amount of labor in order to exert a greater amount of labor. It is not in the labor of the customs <note>original: "Customhouse"</note> officials that it discovers a gain. That gain is represented by the effort which Robinson takes to return the gift that the waves had offered him. Consider the nation as a collective being, and you will not find an atom of difference between its reasoning and that of Robinson."

"Did Robinson not see that he could devote the time saved to *something else?*"

"What *else?*"

"As long as a man has needs to satisfy and time at his disposal, there is always *something* to be done. I am not bound to specify the kind of labor he would undertake in such a case."

"I see clearly what labor he could have escaped."

"And I maintain that Robinson, with incredible blindness, confused the labor with its result, the end with the means, and I am going to prove to you . . . ."

"There is no need. Here we have the system of restriction or prohibition in its simplest form. If it appears absurd to you when put this way, it is because the two roles of producer and consumer are in this case combined in the same individual."

"Let us pass on, therefore, to a more complicated example."

"With all my heart. Some time afterward, Robinson having met with Friday, they united their labor in a common work. In the morning they hunted for six hours, and brought home four baskets of game. In the evening they worked in the garden for six hours, and obtained four baskets of vegetables.

“At these words Robinson and Friday retired to consult, and the argument that passed is too interesting not to be reported *in full* <note>original: "in extenso"</note>.

“FRIDAY: What do you think of it?

“ROBINSON: If we close with the proposal, we are ruined.

“F.: Are you sure of that? Let us consider.

“R.: The case is clear. Crushed by competition, our hunting as a branch of industry is annihilated.

“F.: What does it matter, if we have the game?

“R.: *Theory!* it will no longer be the product of our labor.

“F.: I beg your pardon, sir; for in order to have game we must part with vegetables.

“R.: Then, what shall we gain?

“F.: The four baskets of game cost us six hours' work. The foreigner gives them to us in exchange for two baskets of vegetables, which cost us only three hours' work. This places three hours at our disposal.

“R.: Say, rather, which are subtracted from our exertions. Our loss will consist of this. *Labor is wealth*, and if we lose a fourth part of our time, we shall be less rich by a fourth.

“F.: You are greatly mistaken, my good friend. We shall have as much game, and the same quantity of vegetables, and three hours at our disposal into the bargain. This is progress, or there is no such thing in the world.

“R.: You lose yourself in generalities! What should we do with these three hours?

“F.: We would do *something else*.

“R.: Ah! I understand you. You cannot come to particulars. *Something else, something else*—this is easily said.

“F.: We can fish, we can ornament our cottage, we can read the Bible.

“R.: Utopia! Is there any certainty that we should do either the one or the other?

“F.: Very well, if we have no needs to satisfy we can rest. Is rest nothing?

“R.: But while we rest we may die of hunger.

“R.: It is very clear, Friday, that you have not been educated in Europe, and that you have never read the *Moniteur Industriel* <note>A prominent 19th-century French journal that advocated for protectionist economic policies.</note>. If you had, it would have taught you this: that all time saved is a total loss. The important thing is not to eat or consume, but to work. Everything we consume, if it is not the direct product of our labor, counts for nothing. Do you want to know whether you are rich? For myself, as one who has no claim to be a theorist, the only thing I look at is the loss of our hunting.

“F.: What a strange jumble of ideas! but . . .

“R.: I will have no *buts*. Moreover, there are political reasons for rejecting the self-interested offers of the treacherous foreigner.

“F.: Political reasons!

“R.: Yes, he only makes us these offers because they are advantageous to him.

“F.: So much the better, since they are to our advantage as well.

“R.: Then by this trade we would place ourselves in a situation of dependence upon him.

“F.: And he would place himself in dependence on us. We would have need of his game, and he of our vegetables, and we would live on terms of friendship.

“R.: Theory! Do you want me to shut your mouth?

“F.: We shall see about that. I have yet to hear a good reason.

“R.: Suppose the foreigner learns to cultivate a garden, and that his island should prove more fertile than ours. Do you see the consequence?

“F.: Yes; our relations with the foreigner would cease. He would send us no more vegetables, since he could have them at home with less labor. He would take no more game from us, since we would have nothing to give him in exchange, and we would then be in exactly the situation that you want us in now.

<summary>Robinson Crusoe uses protectionist logic to argue that labor is more important than consumption and that trade creates dangerous dependence. Friday counters that mutual trade creates beneficial interdependence and that the isolation Robinson desires would be the natural result if trade ever became inefficient.</summary>

“The debate was prolonged for a long time, and, as often happens, each remained fixed in his own opinion. But Robinson, possessing great influence over Friday, his opinion prevailed; and when the foreigner arrived to demand a reply, Robinson said to him—

“‘Stranger, in order to persuade us to accept your proposal, we must be certain of two things:

“‘The first is, that your island is no better stocked with game than ours, for we want to fight only with *equal* weapons.

“‘The second is, that you will lose by the bargain. For, as in every exchange there is necessarily a gaining and a losing party, we would be fools if you were not the loser. What do you have to say?’

“‘Nothing,’ replied the foreigner; and, bursting out laughing, he returned to his canoe.”

“The story would not be bad, if Robinson were not made to argue so very absurdly.”

“He does not argue more absurdly than the committee of the Rue Hauteville <note>The Rue Hauteville in Paris was the headquarters of the Odier Committee, a group of industrialists who strongly advocated for protectionist trade policies in the 19th century.</note>.”

The division of labor and the intervention of merchants and money change the state of the question significantly.”

“That may complicate transactions, but it does not change their nature.”

<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS. 216</header>

its own nature is identical to commerce, just as labor on a small scale is identical to labor on a large scale, or as the law of gravity which moves an atom is identical to that same law of gravity which moves a world.”

“So, according to you, these arguments, which are so weak coming from Robinson, are equally weak when urged by our protectionists.”

“Yes; only the error is better concealed under a complication of circumstances.”

“Then, please, let us have an example taken from the current state of affairs.”

“With pleasure. In France, owing to the demands of climate and habits, cloth is a useful thing. Is the essential thing to make it, or to obtain it?”

“A very sensible question, truly! In order to have it, you must make it.”

“Not necessarily. For you to have it, someone must make it, that is certain; but it is not at all necessary that the same person or the same country which consumes it should also produce it. You did not make that fabric which clothes you so well. France does not produce the coffee on which our citizens breakfast.”

“But I buy my cloth, and France buys her coffee.”

“Exactly so; and with what?”

“With money.”

“But neither you nor France produce the material of money.”

“We buy it.”

“With what?”

“With our products, which are sent to Peru.”

“It is then, in fact, your labor which you exchange for cloth, and French labor which is exchanged for coffee.”

“Undoubtedly.”

“It is not absolutely necessary, therefore, to manufacture what you consume.”

“No; if we manufacture *something else* which we give in exchange.”

“In other words, France has two ways of obtaining a given quantity of cloth. The first is to make it; the second is to make something else, and to exchange this something else with foreigners for cloth. Of these two means, which is the best?”

<summary>The author argues that international trade operates on the same principles as individual labor, where it is often more efficient to produce one good to exchange for another rather than manufacturing everything domestically.</summary>
