# SOMETHING ELSE.

“I don’t very well know.”

“Is it not that which, *for a specific amount of labor, obtains the greater quantity of cloth?*”

“It seems so.”

“And which is better for a nation: to have the choice between these two methods, or for the law to prohibit one of them on the chance of stumbling upon the better of the two?”

“It appears to me that it is better for the nation to have the choice, since in such matters it invariably chooses *correctly.*”

“The law that prohibits the importation of foreign cloth decides, then, that if France wishes to have cloth, she must produce it directly, and that she is prohibited from making the *something else* with which she could purchase foreign cloth.”

“True.”

“And since the law obliges us to make the cloth and forbids us from making the *something else*—precisely because that *something else* would require less labor (the very reason the law would otherwise not interfere with it)—the law effectively decrees that for a specific amount of labor, France shall only have one yard of cloth, when for the same amount of labor she might have two yards by applying that labor to *something else.*”

“But the question returns: ‘*What else?*’”

“And my question returns: ‘*What does it matter?*’ Having the choice, she will only make the *something else* to the extent that there is a *demand for it.*”

“That is possible; but I cannot rid myself of the idea that the foreigner will send us his cloth and not take the *something else* from us, in which case we would be trapped. In any case, this is the objection even from your own point of view. You admit that France could make this *something else* to exchange for cloth with less expenditure of labor than if she had made the cloth itself?”

“Undoubtedly.”

“There would, then, be a certain amount of her labor made idle?”

“Yes; but without her being less well provided with clothes—a small detail that makes all the difference. Robinson lost sight of this, and our protectionists either do not see it, or

ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

...pretend not to see it. The shipwrecked plank rendered fifteen days of Robinson’s labor inert, insofar as that labor was applied to making a plank, but it did not deprive him of it. Distinguish, then, between these two kinds of diminished labor: the diminution which results in *deprivation*, and that which is caused by *satisfaction*. These two things are very different, and if you mix them up, you reason as Robinson did. In the most complicated, as in the most simple cases, the sophism consists in this: *Judging the utility of labor by its duration and intensity, and not by its results; which gives rise to this economic policy: To reduce the results of labor for the purpose of increasing its duration and intensity.* <note>See chapters 2 and 3 of *Sophisms*, first series; and *Economic Harmonies*, chapter 6.</note>

### XV.

## THE LITTLE ARSENAL OF THE FREE-TRADER.

If anyone tells you that there are no absolute principles, no inflexible rules; that prohibition may be bad and yet that restriction may be good,
Reply: “Restriction *prohibits* everything that it prevents from being imported.”
If anyone says that agriculture is the nursing mother of the country,
Reply: “What nourishes the country is not exactly agriculture, but *grain*.”
If anyone tells you that the basis of the people’s food is agriculture,
Reply: “The basis of the people’s food is *grain*. This is the reason why a law which gives us, through agricultural labor, *two* quarters of grain, when we could have obtained *four* quarters without such labor by means of labor applied to manufactures, is a law not for feeding, but for starving the people.”

**Reply** : “It induces men to sow grain on comparatively barren and unproductive soils. To milk a cow and go on milking her puts a little more into the pail, for it is difficult to say when you will come to the last drop. But that drop costs dearly.”
If anyone tells you that when bread is expensive, the agriculturist, having become rich, enriches the manufacturer,
**Reply** : “Bread is expensive when it is scarce, and then men are poor, or, if you like it better, they become rich *starvelings*.”
If you are further told that when bread gets more expensive, wages rise,
**Reply** by pointing out that, in April 1847, five-sixths of our workmen were receiving charity.
If you are told that the wages of labor should rise with the increased price of provisions,
**Reply** : “This is as much as to say that in a ship without provisions, everyone will have as much biscuit as if the vessel were fully supplied.”
If you are told that it is necessary to secure a good price to the man who sells grain,
**Reply** : “That in that case it is also necessary to secure good wages to the man who buys it.”
If it is said that the owners, who make the laws, have raised the price of bread, without taking thought about wages, because they know that when bread prices rise, wages *naturally* rise,
**Reply** : “Upon the same principle, when the workmen come to make the laws, do not blame them if they fix a high rate of wages without busying themselves about protecting grain, because they know that when wages rise, provisions *naturally* rise also.”
If you are asked what, then, is to be done?
**Reply** : “Be just to everybody.”
If you are told that it is essential that every great country should produce iron,
**Reply** : “What is essential is, that every great country should *have* iron.”

If it is said that labor is wealth,
*Reply*: “This is not true.”
And, by way of improvement, add: “Phlebotomy <note>The practice of bloodletting</note> is not health, and the proof of it is that bleeding is used for the purpose of restoring health.”
If it is said: “To force men to cultivate rocks, and extract an ounce of iron from a hundredweight of ore, is to increase their labor and consequently their wealth,”
*Reply*: “To force men to dig wells by prohibiting them from taking water from the brook is to increase their *useless* labor, but *not* their wealth.”
If you are told that the sun gives you its heat and light without payment,
*Reply*: “So much the better for me, for it costs me nothing to see clearly.”
And if you are answered that industry in general loses what would have been paid for artificial light,
*Rejoin*: “No; for having paid nothing to the sun, what it saves me enables me to buy clothes, furniture, and candles.”
In the same way, if you are told that these rascally English possess capital which is *dormant*,
*Reply*: “So much the better for us; they will not make us pay interest for it.”
If it is said: “These perfidious English find coal and iron in the same pit,”
*Reply*: “So much the better for us; they will charge us nothing for bringing them together.”
If you are told that the Swiss have rich pastures, which cost little:
*Reply*: “The advantage is ours, for they will demand a smaller amount of our labor in return for giving an impetus to our agriculture and supplying us with provisions.”
If they tell you that the lands of the Crimea have no value and pay no taxes,
*Reply*: “The profit is ours, who buy corn <note>grain</note> free from such charges.”

If they tell you that under free trade we are about to be inundated with bread, *beef stew*, coal, and winter clothing,

If they ask how we are to pay for these things?

*Reply*: “Neither money nor coffee grows in the fields of *Beauce*, nor are they produced by the workshops of *Elbeuf*. As far as we are concerned, paying the foreigner with money is the same thing as paying him with coffee.”

# ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.

...thing, and foresee everything, in order to direct the people, and that the people have simply to allow themselves to be led,

*Reply by asking*: “Is there a state apart from the people? Is there human foresight apart from humanity? Archimedes might repeat every day of his life, ‘With a fulcrum and lever I can move the world;’ but he never did move it, for lack of a fulcrum and lever. The lever of the state is the nation; and nothing can be more foolish than to base so many hopes upon the state, which is simply to take for granted the existence of collective knowledge and foresight, after having started with the assumption of individual incompetence and lack of foresight.”

If anyone says, “I ask no favor, but only such a duty on bread and meat as shall compensate for the heavy taxes to which I am subjected; only a small duty equal to what the taxes add to the cost price of my grain,”

*Reply*: “A thousand pardons; but I also pay taxes. If, then, the protection which you vote in your own favor has the effect of burdening me, as a purchaser of grain, with exactly your share of the taxes, your modest demand amounts to nothing less than establishing this arrangement as formulated by you: ‘Seeing that the public charges are heavy, I, as a seller of grain, am to pay nothing, and you, my neighbor, as a buyer of grain, are to pay double—namely, your own share and mine into the bargain.’ Mr. Grain-merchant, my good friend, you may have force at your command, but assuredly you have not reason on your side.”

If anyone says to you, “It is, however, exceedingly hard upon me, who pay taxes, to have to compete in my own market with the foreigner, who pays none,”

*Reply*:
“*1st*, In the first place, it is not *your* market, but *our* market. I, who live upon grain and pay for it, should surely be taken into account.
“*2nd*, Few foreigners at the present day are exempt from taxes.
“*3rd*, If the taxes you vote provide you with roads, canals, security, etc., more than they cost you, you are not justified in repelling, at my expense, the competition of foreigners, who, if they do not pay taxes, do not have the advantages you enjoy in roads, canals, and security. You might as well say, ‘I demand a...”

# THE LITTLE ARSENAL OF THE FREE-TRADER.

...compensating duty because I have finer clothes, stronger horses, and better plows than the hard-working peasant of Russia.”

“4th, If the tax does not repay you for what it costs, do not vote for it.

“5th, In short, after having voted for the tax, do you wish to get free from it? Try to draft a law which will shift it onto the foreigner. But your tariff makes your share of it fall upon me, who already have my own burden to bear.”

If anyone says, “For the Russians, free trade is necessary to enable them to exchange their products with advantage” (*Opinion of M. Thiers in the Committees, April 1847*),

*Reply:* “Liberty is necessary everywhere, and for the same reason.”

If you are told, “Each country has its needs, and we must be guided by that in what we do” (M. Thiers),

*Reply:* “Each country acts this way of its own accord, if you do not place obstacles in the way.”

If they tell you, “We have no sheet iron, and we must allow it to be imported” (M. Thiers),

*Reply:* “Many thanks.”

If you are told, “We have no freight for our merchant shipping. The lack of return cargoes prevents our ships from competing with foreigners” (M. Thiers),

*Reply:* “When a country wishes to have everything produced at home, there can be no freight for either exports or imports. It is just as absurd to desire to have a merchant marine under a system of prohibition as it would be to have carts when there is nothing to carry.”

If you are told that, assuming protection to be unjust, everything has been arranged on that basis; capital has been invested; rights have been acquired; and the system cannot be changed without causing suffering to individuals and classes,

*Reply:* “All injustice benefits someone (except, perhaps, restriction, which in the long run benefits no one). To argue from the disruption that the end of injustice may cause the person who profits from it is the same as saying that a system of injustice, for no other reason than that it has existed for a time, ought to exist forever.”

## XVI.

# THE RIGHT HAND AND THE LEFT.

### REPORT ADDRESSED TO THE KING.

Sire,

When we observe these free-trade advocates boldly spreading their doctrines, and maintaining that the right of buying and selling is implied in the right of property (as has been urged by M. Billault in the true style of a special pleader <note>A "special pleader" refers to someone who uses clever but potentially misleading or technical legal arguments.</note>), we may be permitted to feel serious alarm as to the fate of our *national labor*; for what would Frenchmen make of their heads and their hands were they left to their own resources?

The administration which you have honored with your confidence has turned its attention to this grave state of things, and has sought in its wisdom to discover a species of protection which may be substituted for that which appears to be falling out of favor. They propose a law TO FORBID YOUR FAITHFUL SUBJECTS FROM USING THEIR RIGHT HANDS.

Sire, we beg you not to do us the injustice of supposing that we have adopted lightly and without due deliberation a measure which at first sight may appear somewhat whimsical. A profound study of the *system of protection* has taught us this syllogism, upon which the whole doctrine rests:

> The more men work, the richer they become;
> The more difficulties there are to be overcome, the more work;
> *Therefore*, the more difficulties there are to be overcome, the richer they become.

In fact, what is protection, if it is not an ingenious application of this reasoning—reasoning so tight and conclusive as to baffle the subtlety of M. Billault himself?

Let us personify the country, and regard it as a collective being with thirty million mouths, and, as a natural consequence, with sixty million hands. Here is a man who makes a French clock, which he can exchange in Belgium for ten hundredweights <note>A unit of weight equal to 112 pounds in the UK or 100 pounds in the US.</note> of iron. But we tell him to make the iron himself. He replies, "I cannot, it would occupy too much of my

<summary>In this satirical report, the author proposes a law forbidding the use of right hands to create more "work," mocking the protectionist logic that increasing difficulties leads to greater wealth. The text argues that if protectionism aims to create labor by making tasks harder, then disabling the right hand is a logical extension of that policy.</summary>
<keywords>protectionism, free trade, labor, M. Billault, economic satire, syllogism, French industry, Belgium, trade barriers</keywords>

Sire, it cannot have escaped your wisdom that this is effectively the same as if we were to say to the country, “ *Work with your left hand, and not with the right.*”

To create obstacles in order to provide labor with an opportunity to develop itself was the principle of the old system of restriction, and it is likewise the principle of the new system which is now being inaugurated. Sire, to regulate industry in this way is not to innovate, but to persevere.

As for the efficiency of the measure, it is incontestable. It is difficult—much more difficult than one would suppose—to do with the left hand what we have been accustomed to do with the right. You will be convinced of this, Sire, if you will condescend to try our system in a process that must be familiar to you; for example, in shuffling a deck of cards. For this reason, we flatter ourselves that we are opening an unlimited career to labor.

When workmen in all departments of industry are thus confined to the use of the left hand, we can imagine, Sire, the immense number of people that will be needed to supply current consumption, assuming it remains constant—as we always do when we compare two different systems of production with one another. Such a prodigious demand for manual labor cannot fail to cause a great rise in wages, and poverty will disappear as if by magic.

Sire, your fatherly heart will rejoice to think that our new law will extend its benefits to that interesting part of the community whose destinies occupy all your concern. What is the current fate of women in France? The bolder and hardier sex gradually drives them out of every department of industry.

Formerly, they had the resource of the lottery offices. These offices have been closed by a pitiless philanthropy, and on what pretext? “To save the money of the poor.” Alas! the poor man never obtained, for a small sum of money, enjoyments as

> ->“ *It is still something to have a beautiful dream!* ”<-

The lottery was the poetry of the poor, and we have lost it. Now that the lottery is gone, what means do we have of providing for our *protégés*? Tobacco shops and the post office.

Tobacco is fine; its use progresses, thanks to the *distinguished* habits which noble examples have skillfully introduced among our fashionable youth.

The post office! . . . We shall say nothing of it, as we mean to make it the subject of a special report.

Except, then, for the sale of tobacco, what employment remains for your female subjects? Embroidery, lacework, and sewing—melancholy resources, which the barbarous science of mechanics continues to limit more and more.

But the moment your new law comes into operation—the moment right hands are amputated or tied up—the face of everything will be changed. Twenty times, or thirty times the number of embroiderers, polishers, laundresses, seamstresses, milliners, and shirtmakers will not be sufficient to supply the needs of the kingdom, always assuming, as before, that consumption remains the same.

This assumption may very likely be disputed by some cold theorists, for clothing and everything else will then be more expensive. The same thing may be said of the iron which we extract from our own mines, compared with the iron we could obtain in exchange for our wines. This argument, therefore, does not argue more against <term>gaucherie</term> <note>The state of being left-handed; clumsiness</note> than against protection, for this very dearness is the effect and the sign of an excess of work and effort, which is precisely the basis upon which, in both cases, we contend that the prosperity of the working classes is founded.

Yes, we shall soon be favored with a touching picture of the prosperity of the millinery business. What movement! What

No young woman will be idle, and we have no need, Sire, to indicate to your insight the moral consequences of this great revolution. Not only will there be more young women employed, but each of them will earn more, for they will be unable to supply the demand; and if competition shall again show itself, it will not be among the seamstresses who make the dresses, but among the fine ladies who wear them.

You must see then, Sire, that our proposal is not only in strict conformity with the economic traditions of the government, but is in itself essentially moral and popular.

To appreciate its effects, let us suppose the law passed and in operation—let us transport ourselves in imagination into the future—and assume the new system to have been in operation for twenty years. Idleness is banished from the country; ease and harmony, contentment and morality, have, with employment, been introduced into every family—no more poverty, no more vice. The left hand being very visible in all work, employment will be abundant, and the compensation adequate. Everything is arranged on this footing, and the workshops in consequence are full. If, in such circumstances, Sire, Utopian dreamers were all at once to agitate for the right hand being again set free, would they not throw the whole country into alarm? Would such a pretended reform not overturn the whole existing state of things? Then our system must be good, since it could not be put an end to without universal suffering.

And yet we confess we have the gloomy premonition (so great is human perversity) that some day there will be formed an association for right-hand freedom.

We think that already we hear the free *Dexteristes* <note>Proponents of the use of the right hand; a satirical reference to free-trade advocates</note>, assembled in the *Salle Montesquieu*, holding this language:—

> “Good people, you think yourselves richer because the use of one of your hands has been denied you; you take account only of the additional employment which that brings you. But consider also the high prices which result from it, and the forced reduction of consumption. That measure has not made capital more abundant, and capital is the fund from which wages are paid. Fortunately, Sire, an association has been formed *in defense of left-hand labor*, and the *Sinistristes* <note>Advocates for the left-handed system; from the Latin 'sinister' for left.</note> will have no difficulty in demolishing all these generalities, suppositions, abstractions, fantasies, and utopias. They only have to dig up the *Moniteur Industriel* <note>A prominent protectionist journal of the era.</note> for 1846, and they will find ready-made arguments against freedom of trade, which refute so admirably all that has been urged in favor of *right-hand liberty* that it is only necessary to substitute one word for the other.

> “The Parisian *free-trade league* has no doubt of securing the cooperation of the workmen. But the workmen are no longer men who can be led by the nose. They have their eyes open, and they know political economy better than our professors. *Free trade*, they say, will deprive us of employment, and labor is our wealth. *With employment, with abundant employment, the price of goods never places them beyond our reach.* Without employment, even if bread were a halfpenny a pound, the workman would die of hunger. Now your doctrines, instead of increasing the present amount of employment, would diminish it, that is to say, would reduce us to poverty.

> “When there are too many goods in the market, their price falls, no doubt. But as wages always fall when goods are cheap, the result is that, instead of being in a position to purchase more, we are no longer able to buy anything. It is when goods are cheap that the workman is worst off.”

It will not be amiss for the *Sinistristes* to mix some threats with their theories. Here is a model for them:—

> “What! you desire to substitute right-hand for left-hand labor, and thus force down, or perhaps destroy wages, the sole resource of the great bulk of the nation!

> “And, at a time when a poor harvest is imposing painful hardships on the workman, you wish to make him anxious about his future, and make him more susceptible to bad advice, and more

<header>THE RIGHT HAND AND THE LEFT.</header>

ready to abandon that wise line of conduct that has distinguished him until now.”

After such conclusive reasoning as this, we have a confident hope, Sire, that if the battle once begins, the left hand will emerge victorious.

Perhaps an association may be formed for the purpose of inquiring whether both the right hand and the left are wrong, and whether a third hand cannot be found to satisfy everyone.

After depicting the *Dexteristes* <note>Advocates for the use of the right hand; from the Latin 'dexter' for right.</note> as being seduced by *the apparent liberality of a principle, the soundness of which experience has not yet proven*, and the *Sinistristes* <note>Advocates for the left-handed system; from the Latin 'sinister' for left.</note> as maintaining the position they have gained, they go on to say:

> “We deny that there is any third position that it is possible to take up in the middle of the battle! Is it not evident that the workmen have to defend themselves at one and the same time against those who desire to change nothing in the present situation, because they benefit from it, and against those who dream of an economic revolution whose direction and extent they have not calculated?”

We cannot, however, hide from your Majesty that our project has a vulnerable side; for it may be said that twenty years from now, left hands will be as skillful as right hands are currently, and that then you could no longer rely on *gaucherie* <note>Clumsiness or awkwardness; used here to refer to the use of the left hand.</note> for an increase in national employment.

To that we reply that, according to the most learned physicians, the left side of the body has a natural weakness, which is quite reassuring regarding the labor of the future.

If your Majesty consents to pass the measure now proposed, a great principle will be established: *All wealth proceeds from the intensity of labor.* It will be easy for us to extend and vary the applications of this principle. We may decree, for example, that it shall no longer be permitted to work with anything but the foot; for this is no more impossible (as we have seen) than extracting iron from the mud of the Seine. You see then, Sire, that the means of increasing national labor can never fail. And after everything else has been tried, we still have the practically inexhaustible resource of amputation.

To conclude, Sire, if this report were not intended for publicity,

<summary>The author concludes his satirical petition by arguing that because the left hand is naturally weaker, it will always provide more "employment" than the right, and suggests that if more work is ever needed, the government could mandate working with feet or even resort to amputation.</summary>

### XVII.

# DOMINATION BY LABOR.

> "In the same way that in time of war we achieve mastery through superiority in arms, do we not, in time of peace, achieve dominance through superiority in labor?"

This is a question of the highest interest at a time when there seems to be no doubt that in the field of industry, as in the field of battle, the stronger crushes the weaker.

To reach this conclusion, we must have discovered a somber and discouraging analogy between the labor applied to goods and the violence exercised upon men; for why should these two kinds of operations be thought to have identical effects, if they are essentially different in their own nature?

And if it is true that in industry, as in war, dominance is the necessary result of superiority, what is the point of progress or social economy? After all, we would be inhabiting a world where everything has been so arranged by Providence that the exact same effect—namely, oppression—necessarily proceeds from two opposite principles.

Regarding England’s new policy of commercial freedom, many people raise this objection, which I am convinced has taken hold of the most sincere minds among us:
> "Is England doing anything other than pursuing the same goal by different means? Does she not always aspire to universal supremacy? Confident in her superiority in capital and labor, does she not invite free competition in order to stifle Continental industry, and thus put herself in a position to reign as a

<summary>The author transitions from his satire on "left-handed" labor to a serious discussion on whether industrial superiority leads to national oppression. He addresses the fear that England's move toward free trade is merely a new strategy for achieving global dominance.</summary>
<keywords>economic competition, industrial superiority, free trade, England, protectionism, national dominance, social economy</keywords>

--- Page 244 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on whether England's shift toward free trade is a strategy for industrial dominance or a benefit to all nations, specifically addressing the fears of protectionists.</meta>

<page-num>231</page-num>
<header>DOMINATION BY LABOR.</header>

sovereign, having conquered the privilege of feeding and clothing the population she has ruined?"

It would not be difficult to demonstrate that these alarms are imaginary; that our alleged inferiority is much exaggerated; that our major branches of industry not only maintain their ground, but are actually developed under the influence of external competition, and that the certain effect of such competition is to bring about an increase in general consumption, capable of absorbing both domestic and foreign products.

At present, I wish to offer a direct answer to the objection, granting it all the advantage of the ground chosen by the objectors. Setting aside for the moment the specific case of England and France, I shall inquire in a general way whether, when a nation—through its superiority in one branch of industry—manages to outrival and shut down a similar branch of industry existing among another people, the former has advanced one step toward domination, or the latter toward dependence. In other words, I will ask whether both nations do not gain by the operation, and whether it is not the nation that is outrivaled that gains the most.

If we saw in a product nothing more than *an opportunity to provide labor*, the alarms of the protectionists would undoubtedly be well-founded. Were we to consider iron, for example, only in its relations with iron producers, we might be led to fear that competition from a country where iron is the free gift of nature would extinguish the furnaces of another country where both ore and fuel are scarce.

But is this a complete view of the subject? Does iron have relations only with those who make it? Does it have no relations with those who use it? Is its sole and final purpose merely to be produced? And if it is useful, not because of the labor to which it gives employment, but because of the qualities it possesses—of the numerous purposes to which its durability and malleability adapt it—does it not follow that the foreigner cannot reduce its price, even so far as to render its production at home unprofitable, without doing us more good in this last respect than harm in the other?

Please consider how many things there are which foreigners, by reason of the natural advantages by which they are

<summary>The author argues that industrial competition benefits both nations and that being outrivaled in production can actually lead to greater gains for the consumer. Using the iron industry as an example, he explains that the value of a product lies in its utility to the user rather than just the labor required to produce it.</summary>
<keywords>industrial competition, protectionism, free trade, labor, iron industry, economic dominance, consumer benefit</keywords>

--- Page 245 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that international competition and foreign superiority in specific industries do not lead to national ruin or dependence, but rather to increased wealth and efficiency by freeing up resources for other uses.</meta>

<page-num>232</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

surrounded, prevent us from producing directly, and with reference to which we are placed *in reality* in the hypothetical position we have been examining with reference to iron. We produce neither tea, coffee, gold, nor silver at home. Is our industry *as a whole* diminished as a result? No; only in order to create the equivalent value of these imported commodities, and to acquire them by means of exchange, we set aside from our national labor a portion *smaller* than would be required to produce these things ourselves. More labor thus remains to be devoted to obtaining other enjoyments. We are so much the richer and so much the stronger. All that external competition can do, even in cases where it puts an end absolutely to a specific branch of industry, is to save labor and increase our productive power. Is this, in the case of the foreigner, the road to *domination*?

If we were to find a gold mine in France, it does not follow that it would be in our interest to work it. Indeed, it is certain that the enterprise would be neglected if each ounce of gold required more of our labor than an ounce of gold purchased from abroad with cloth. In this case, we would do better to find our mines in our workshops. And what is true of gold is true of iron.

The illusion stems from our failure to see one thing: that foreign superiority never stops national industry, except in a specific form, and in that form only makes it unnecessary by placing at our disposal the result of the very labor thus replaced. If men lived in diving bells under water and had to provide themselves with air by means of a pump, this would be a great source of employment. To throw obstacles in the way of such employment, *as long as men were left in this condition*, would be to inflict upon them a frightful injury. But if the labor ceases because the necessity for its effort no longer exists—because men are placed in an environment where air is introduced into their lungs without effort—then the loss of that labor is not to be regretted, except by those who stubbornly persist in seeing labor as nothing but labor in the abstract.

It is exactly this kind of labor which machinery, commercial freedom, and progress of every kind gradually replace;

<summary>This page argues that foreign competition and technological progress do not destroy industry but rather save labor, allowing a nation to redirect its efforts toward new areas of productivity and wealth. Using the analogy of a diving bell, the author explains that losing a job because the underlying need for it has been eliminated (through efficiency or nature) is a benefit, not a loss.</summary>
<keywords>national labor, exchange, external competition, productive power, gold mining, foreign superiority, commercial freedom, Economic Sophisms, labor efficiency</keywords>

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that international competition and foreign superiority in specific industries do not lead to national ruin or dependence, but rather to increased wealth and efficiency by freeing up resources for other uses.</meta>

<page-num>233</page-num>
<header>DOMINATION BY LABOR.</header>

not useful labor, but labor that has become superfluous, without a goal, and without results. On the contrary, protectionism sets that sort of useless labor to work; it places us under water again to bring the air pump into play; it forces us to seek gold from an inaccessible national mine rather than from national workshops. The entire effect is expressed by the words, *waste of forces*.

It should be understood that I am speaking here of general effects, not of the temporary inconvenience that is always caused by the transition from a bad system to a good one. A momentary disruption necessarily accompanies all progress. This may be a reason for making the transition gently and gradually. It is no reason for systematically stopping all progress, and even less for misunderstanding it.

Industry is often represented as a struggle. That is not a true representation of it, or it is only true when we limit ourselves to considering each branch of industry in its effects on similar branches, looking at them both in thought apart from the interests of the rest of humanity. But there is always something else to be considered, namely, the effects on consumption and on general prosperity.

It is an error to apply phrases that are applicable to war to trade, as is done only too often.

In war, the stronger overcomes the weaker.

In industry, the stronger *gives strength to the weaker*. This entirely does away with the analogy.

Let the English be as powerful and skillful as they are represented; let them possess as large an amount of capital and have as great a command of the two major agents of production—iron and fuel—as they are supposed to have. All this simply means *cheapness*. And who gains by the cheapness of products? The person who buys them.

It is not in their power to destroy any part whatsoever of our national labor. All they can do is to make it unnecessary in the production of what is acquired through exchange—to provide us with air without the help of a pump—thereby increasing our available forces and making their alleged dominance more impossible as their superiority becomes more undeniable.

<summary>The author argues that protectionism wastes national labor on inefficient tasks, whereas free trade treats foreign efficiency as a benefit that lowers prices for consumers. Unlike war, where the strong crush the weak, in industry the efficiency of a stronger neighbor provides strength to the trading partner by freeing up their resources.</summary>

<keywords>protectionism, free trade, national labor, economic progress, competition vs war, consumption, efficiency, Bastiat</keywords>

--- Page 247 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that industrial competition differs from military conflict because it results in the economization of labor and increased public abundance, rather than destruction and loss.</meta>

<page-num>234</page-num>
<header>ECONOMIC SOPHISMS.</header>

Thus, by a rigorous and comforting demonstration, we arrive at this conclusion: that *labor* and *violence*, which are so opposite in their nature, are no less opposite in their effects.

All we are called upon to do is to distinguish between *labor annihilated* and *labor economized*.

To have less iron *because* we work less, and to have less iron *although* we work less, are not only different but opposite things. The protectionists confuse them; we do not. That is all.

We may be very certain of one thing: if the English employ a large amount of activity, labor, capital, intelligence, and natural forces, it is not done for show. It is done in order to procure a multitude of enjoyments in exchange for their products. They most certainly expect to receive at least as much as they give. *What they produce at home is destined to pay for what they purchase abroad.* If they flood us with their products, it is because they expect to be flooded with ours in return. That being so, the best means of having much for ourselves is to be free to choose between these two modes of acquisition: immediate production and mediate production. British *Machiavellianism* cannot force us to make a wrong choice.

Let us give up, then, the childishness of applying phrases applicable to war to industrial competition—a way of speaking that is only plausible when applied to competition between two rival trades. The moment we take into account the effect produced on the general prosperity, the analogy disappears.

In a battle, everyone who is killed diminishes the strength of the army by that much. In industry, a workshop is shut up only when what it produced is obtained by the public from another source and in greater abundance. Imagine a state of things where for every one man killed on the spot, two would rise up full of life and vigor. Were such a state of things possible, war would no longer deserve its name.

This, however, is the distinctive character of what is so absurdly called *industrial war*.

Let the Belgians and the English lower the price of their iron ever so much; let them, if they will, send it to us for nothing; this might extinguish some of our blast furnaces; but imme-

<meta>catchword: imme-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that industrial competition should not be compared to war, as it results in increased abundance and "economized labor" rather than destruction. He highlights that trade is a mutual exchange of products and that foreign imports allow a nation to choose the most efficient way to acquire goods.</summary>
<keywords>labor, industrial competition, protectionism, free trade, industrial war, production, iron industry, economic sophisms</keywords>

--- Page 248 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument from the previous page, explaining that industrial efficiency in one nation creates new opportunities in others, rather than leading to economic "domination."</meta>

<page-num>235</page-num>
<header>DOMINATION BY LABOR.</header>

immediately, and as a *necessary* consequence of this very cheapness, a thousand other branches of industry would rise up, more profitable than the one which had been superseded.

We arrive, then, at the conclusion that domination by labor is impossible and a contradiction in terms, seeing that all superiority which manifests itself among a people means cheapness, and tends only to impart strength to all other nations. Let us banish, then, from political economy all terms borrowed from the military vocabulary: *to fight with equal weapons, to conquer, to crush, to stifle, to be beaten, invasion, tribute, etc.* What do such phrases mean? Squeeze them, and you obtain nothing. . . Yes, you do obtain something; for from such words proceed absurd errors, and fatal and harmful prejudices. Such phrases tend to hinder the fusion of nations, are hostile to their peaceful, universal, and indissoluble alliance, and retard the progress of the human race.

# WORKS ON POLITICAL ECONOMY,

->By P. J. STIRLING, LL.D., F.R.S.E.<-

### THE PHILOSOPHY OF TRADE; or, Outlines of a Theory of PROFITS and PRICES: Including an Examination of the Principles which determine the relative Value of Corn, Labor, and Currency. 8vo, 10s. 6d.

> “We would be greatly mistaken about the rank or character of this treatise if we regarded it only as a successful explanation of its basic principles for the benefit of those who are still schoolboys on the subject. It is something far higher than an exposition—it is a correction of fundamental principles; for the author grapples with some of the primary and basic doctrines of those who have been considered the greatest masters in the science and, in our view, overturns them. The changes he advocates in the basic elements of the science lead to different interpretations and lessons throughout, and will help establish a different school of thought from those that are currently most valued and used.”—DR. CHALMERS, IN NORTH BRITISH REVIEW.

---

### THE AUSTRALIAN AND CALIFORNIAN GOLD DISCOVERIES and their PROBABLE CONSEQUENCES; or, an Inquiry into the Laws which determine the Value and Distribution of the Precious Metals: With Historical Notices of the Effects of the American Mines on European Prices in the Sixteenth, Seventeenth, and Eighteenth Centuries. Post 8vo, 5s.

> “All questions of fact and doctrine relating to the discovery of the new gold mines have been studied by Mr. Stirling with wisdom and sobriety. His book is one of the best collections of facts and ideas published on this question.”—JOURNAL DES ÉCONOMISTES.

> “Mr. Stirling points out that the depreciation of precious metals is determined less by the quantities extracted than by the reduction in production costs. This demonstration is the original contribution of his book.”—REVUE DES DEUX MONDES.
